## THE TIBETAN
## BOOK OF THE DEAD

<term>The Tibetan Book of the Dead</term> <note>The original Tibetan title is Bardo Thodol, which translates more literally as "Liberation Through Hearing during the Intermediate State." It is a funerary text intended to guide the consciousness of the deceased through the interval between death and the next rebirth.</note>

<summary>The title page of the manuscript, identifying the work as the Tibetan Book of the Dead, a foundational text of Himalayan Buddhism regarding the afterlife.</summary>

<image-desc>
This plate presents two rectangular folios <term>folio: a single leaf of a manuscript, consisting of two pages (front and back)</term> from a Tibetan manuscript of the Bardo Thödol, likely woodblock printed or hand-illuminated.

The upper folio, identified as Folio 35A, is dominated by a central mandala <term>mandala: a symbolic diagram representing the universe or a sacred space for deities</term>. This mandala consists of a central figure within a circle, surrounded by an inner ring of eight smaller circular vignettes and an outer ring of twelve. Horizontal blocks of Tibetan script in the *dbu-can* <note>pronounced "U-chen"; the formal, "headed" style of Tibetan writing used for printed books and formal manuscripts</note> style are located above and below the mandala. Seated deity figures on lotus thrones are illustrated in the top corners and along the bottom margin.

The lower folio, identified as Folio 67A, features a similar layout with a central mandala containing a central figure and numerous smaller figures in surrounding concentric circles. Blocks of Tibetan text are arranged above and below the diagram. Seated deity figures are depicted in the upper-right and lower-right corners of the folio.
</image-desc>

> "You shall understand that it is a science most profitable, and surpassing all other sciences, to learn how to die. For a man to know that he shall die is common to all men; inasmuch as there is no man who may live forever, nor has he hope or trust of doing so; but you shall find very few who have the skill to learn how to die. . . I shall give you the mystery of this doctrine; which shall profit you greatly toward the beginning of spiritual health <note>original: "ghostly health"; in Middle English, "ghostly" refers to the spirit or soul</note>, and to a stable foundation of all virtues."—*The Clock of Wisdom* <note>original: "Orologium Sapientiae," a famous 14th-century Latin treatise on mystical devotion by Henry Suso</note>.

> "Against his will he dies who has not learned to die. Learn to die and you shall learn to live, for none shall learn to live who has not learned to die."—*Tower of all Towers: and Teaches a Man how to Die*.

->*The Book of the Craft of Dying* <note>This refers to the "Ars Moriendi," a genre of Christian literature providing guidance on a "good death"</note> (Comper's Edition).<-

> "Whatever is here, that is there; what is there, the same is here. He who sees things here as different, meets death after death.
> "By the mind alone this is to be realized, and [then] there is no difference here. From death to death he goes, who sees as if there is a difference here."—*Katha Upanishad*, iv. 10-11 <note>An ancient Sanskrit text exploring the nature of the soul and the afterlife</note> (Swami Sharvananda's Translation).

## PREFACE TO THE THIRD EDITION

It is with a consciousness of the deepest gratitude that I write this Preface. No greater honor could be shown by the Western World to this Tibetan treatise on the Science of Death and Rebirth than that shown by the most illustrious of the West’s psychologists, Dr. Carl G. Jung, in his Psychological Commentary to it, first published in the Swiss edition of *The Tibetan Book of the Dead* <note>original: "Das Tibetanische Totenbuch"</note>, by Rascher Verlag, Zürich, 1938, and herein presented in English translation for the first time. And no exposition of the arcane <note>secret or mysterious</note> significance of the book’s teachings could have been written more knowledgeably than that set forth in the Introductory Foreword hereto, in its original English form, by the learned Lāma Anagarika Govinda.

The Editor and all who read this book are indebted to Dr. Jung and to Lāma Govinda for having made this enlarged and greatly enriched edition possible, and to Mr. R. F. C. Hull, also, for his able translation from the German of Dr. Jung’s Psychological Commentary. Grateful acknowledgement, too, is here made to the Bollingen Foundation <note>An educational foundation founded in 1945, dedicated to the dissemination of Jung's work and the humanities</note> for the granting of permission to publish the English translation of the Psychological Commentary.

To each member of the One Human Family, now incarnate here, on the planet Earth, this book bears the greatest of all great messages. It reveals to the peoples of the Occident <note>The West</note> a science of death and rebirth such as only the peoples of the Orient <note>The East</note> have heretofore known.

Inasmuch as all mankind must relinquish their fleshly bodies and experience death, it is supremely profitable that they should know how rightly to meet death when it comes. Lāma Govinda makes comprehensible, as the Ancient Mysteries did, and as the Upanishads <note>Ancient Sanskrit texts of spiritual teaching and philosophy</note> declare, that the unenlightened meet death after death unceasingly.

According to the Avatāra <note>A divine incarnation</note> Krishna, in the *Bhagavad-Gītā* <note>The "Song of God," a central Hindu scripture</note>, only the Awakened Ones remember their many deaths and births. The Buddha sets forth the <term>yogic</term> <note>relating to Yoga, specifically the disciplined practice of spiritual realization</note> method whereby all who doubt these teachings, concerning a plurality of births and deaths, can prove them to be true, as He did, by self-realization.

<summary>The editor introduces the third edition of the Tibetan Book of the Dead, highlighting the inclusion of a psychological commentary by C.G. Jung and a foreword by Lāma Govinda. The text frames the work as a universal guide to the science of death and rebirth, bridging Eastern spiritual wisdom with Western psychology.</summary>
<keywords>Carl G. Jung, Tibetan Book of the Dead, Lāma Anagarika Govinda, reincarnation, death and rebirth, Bollingen Foundation, Bhagavad-Gītā, Upanishads, Buddhism</keywords>

# PREFACE TO THE THIRD EDITION

The argument of the unenlightened man <note>The "unenlightened man" refers to one who lacks spiritual insight or "vidya," remaining trapped in worldly ignorance.</note>, that, merely because he himself has no conscious memory of his many births and deaths, the teachings are untrue, is scientifically untenable. The field of the normal man’s sense perceptions is, as can be demonstrated, narrowly circumscribed and extremely limited. There are objects and colors he cannot see, sounds he cannot hear, odors he cannot smell, tastes he cannot taste, and feelings he cannot feel. And beyond his work-a-day consciousness, which he assumes to be his only consciousness, there are other consciousnesses, of which <term>yogins</term> <note>Practitioners of yoga who seek spiritual liberation and mental control.</note> and saints have cognizance, and of which psychologists are beginning to glean some, but as yet very little, understanding. As Lama Govinda <note>Anagarika Govinda (1898–1985), a German-born Buddhist lama and scholar.</note> concisely explains, there exists, in completeness, in a potentially realizable consciousness, the memory of a forgotten past, in which each of us now incarnate shares.

In his Psychological Commentary, Dr. Jung <note>Carl Gustav Jung, the Swiss psychiatrist and founder of analytical psychology.</note> points out that although Freud’s <note>Sigmund Freud, the founder of psychoanalysis.</note> ‘is the first attempt made by the West to investigate, as if from below, from the animal sphere of instinct, the psychic territory that corresponds in Tantric Lamaism, to the *Sidpa Bardo*’ <note>original: "Sidpa Bardo"; the intermediate state of seeking rebirth or the "Bardo of Becoming."</note>, or state of reincarnating, ‘a very justifiable fear of metaphysics prevented Freud from penetrating into the sphere of the “occult”’. In this, Freud was typically non-Oriental, and fettered by his own self-imposed limitations. But such self-imposed limitations of Western Science, which are very much like those that Western Theology imposed upon itself by refusing to take into proper account the <term>esoteric</term> <note>Hidden or inner teachings intended for a small group of initiates.</note> in Christian tradition, cannot always hold back psychological research. Dr. Jung himself has, indeed, gone far beyond these limitations of Freud, his predecessor. ‘It is therefore not possible’, Dr. Jung states, ‘for Freudian theory to reach anything except an essentially negative valuation of the unconscious’—wherein are stored, apparently imperishably, as Dr. Jung holds, the records, in completeness, of mankind’s past. At a conclusion parallel to this of Western Science, Lama Govinda arrived by means of Eastern Science.

Dr. Jung reports that ‘psychoanalysts even claim to have probed back to memories of intra-uterine origin’ <note>Memories relating to the time spent inside the womb before birth.</note>; and that had Freudian psychoanalysis succeeded in pursuing these so-called intra-uterine experiences still further back, ‘it would surely have

<summary>This page defends the concept of reincarnation by comparing the limitations of ordinary human perception with the insights of Eastern yogins and Western psychologists like C.G. Jung. It highlights the transition from Freud's instinct-based theories to Jung's broader exploration of the unconscious and the "Sidpa Bardo."</summary>

<keywords>reincarnation, consciousness, C.G. Jung, Sigmund Freud, Lama Govinda, Sidpa Bardo, unconscious, Western Science, Eastern Science, sense perception</keywords>

# PREFACE TO THE THIRD EDITION

come out beyond the *Bardo of Seeking Rebirth* <note>original: "Sidpa Bardo"; the state where the soul seeks a new body</note> and penetrated from behind into the lower reaches of the *Bardo of Experiencing Reality* <note>original: "Chönyid Bardo"; the state where the deceased encounters the luminous nature of the mind</note>.' But, as he points out, 'with the equipment of our existing biological ideas such a venture would not have been crowned with success; it would have needed a wholly different kind of philosophical preparation from that based on current scientific assumptions. But, had the journey back been consistently pursued, it would undoubtedly have led to the postulate of a pre-uterine existence, a true *Intermediate State* <note>original: "Bardo"</note> life, if only it had been possible to find at least some trace of an experiencing subject.'

Western psychologists have, therefore, advanced appreciably beyond Freud in the study of the psychic life of man, and will advance much further when they no longer allow the Freudian fear of metaphysics <note>the branch of philosophy dealing with the fundamental nature of reality and being</note> to bar their entrance into the realm of the occult. This finds ample support in Dr. Jung's further pronouncement:

'I think, then, we can state it as a fact that with the aid of psychoanalysis the rationalizing mind of the West has pushed forward into what one might call the neuroticism of the *Rebirth* <note>original: "Sidpa"</note> state, and has there been brought to an inevitable standstill by the uncritical assumption that everything psychological is subjective and personal. Even so, this advance has been a great gain, inasmuch as it has enabled us to take one more step behind our conscious lives.'

Thus it is of far-reaching historical importance that the profound doctrine of pre-existence and rebirth, which many of the most enlightened men in all known epochs have taught as being realizable, is now under investigation by our own scientists of the West. And some of these scientists seem to be approaching that place, on the path of scientific progress, where, as with respect also to other findings by the Sages of Asia long before the rise of Western Science, East and West appear to be destined to meet in mutual understanding.

Apparently, however, before this much-desired understanding can be attained, there must be, as Dr. Jung observes, 'a wholly different kind of philosophical preparation' from that based upon the West's 'existing biological ideas'. May it not be that 'heretical' Western psychologists who are prepared to blaze a new path of research will eventually find the lacking complement

->PREFACE TO THE THIRD EDITION<-

to their at present inadequate methods in the psychological techniques of oriental <term>yoga: a Sanskrit term referring to a range of physical, mental, and spiritual disciplines originating in ancient India</term>, such as those referred to in Lāma Govinda's <note>Anagarika Govinda (1898–1985) was a German-born scholar and monk who became a prominent teacher of Tibetan Buddhism</note> Foreword? At least the writer believes that they will. According to his view, that much sought-after higher understanding of the human psyche will be won not by these admittedly inadequate Freudian <note>referring to the methods of Sigmund Freud, the founder of psychoanalysis</note> methods, now in vogue, of ' psychoanalyzing ' a subject, but by meditation and an integrating self-analysis, such as the master ***yogins*** <term>yogins: practitioners of advanced yoga and meditation</term> employ and the Buddha prescribes. He believes, too, that thereby Western Science and Eastern Science will, at last, attain at-one-ment <note>the author uses this hyphenated spelling to emphasize the state of being "at one" or in total harmony</note>.

Then, when that long-awaited at-one-ment shall have been consummated, there will no longer be doubt, nor fallacious argumentation, nor unwise and unscientific Church-Council anathematizations <note>formal denunciations or excommunications by a religious authority</note> directed against that paramount doctrine of pre-existence and re-birth, upon which the ***Bardo Thödol*** <note>original Tibetan: bar do thos grol; the "Liberation Through Hearing in the Intermediate State," popularly known in the West as the Tibetan Book of the Dead</note> is based. Then, too, not only will Pythagoras and Plato and Plotinus <note>ancient Greek philosophers who advocated for the immortality and transmigration of the soul</note>, and the Gnostic Christians <note>early Christian groups who emphasized spiritual knowledge and, in some cases, the pre-existence of the soul</note>, and Krishna <note>a major deity in Hinduism, considered an avatar of Vishnu</note> and the Buddha be vindicated in their advocacy of the doctrine, but, equally, the Hierophants <note>priests or officials who interpreted sacred mysteries in ancient Greece</note> of the Ancient Mysteries of Egypt and Greece and Rome, and the Druids <note>the learned priestly class of the ancient Celts</note> of the Celtic World. And Western man will awaken from that slumber of Ignorance which has been hypnotically induced by a mistaken Orthodoxy. He will greet with wide-opened eyes his long unheeded brethren, the Wise Men of the East.

As set forth in my first important work, *The Fairy-Faith in Celtic Countries*, forty-four years ago, the postulate of rebirth implies a scientific extension and correction of Darwin's <note>Charles Darwin, the naturalist famous for the theory of biological evolution</note> conception of evolutionary law,—that alone through traversing the Cycle of Death and Birth <note>often referred to in Eastern traditions as Samsara</note>, as taught by our revered ancestors, the Druids of Europe, twenty-five and more centuries ago, man attains in the spiritual and psychic sphere that destined perfection which all life's processes and all living things exhibit at the end of their evolutionary course, and from which at present man is so far removed.

May this third edition of the first volume of the Oxford Tibetan Series bear to all who read it the good wishes of its compilers, not only of those of them who dwell in far-away Tibet and Hindustan <note>a historical and geographical term for the Indian subcontinent</note>, but, also, of those of them who dwell in the Western World. And may we heed the solemn admonition set forth in

### BONDAGE TO REBIRTH

> 'As a man's desire is, so is his destiny. For as his
> desire is, so is his will; and as his will is, so is his
> deed; and as his deed is, so is his reward, whether
> good or bad.
> ' A man acteth according to the desires to which he
> clingeth. After death he goeth to the next world
> bearing in his mind the subtle impressions of his
> deeds; and, after reaping there the harvest of his
> deeds, he returneth again to this world of action.
> Thus he who hath desire continueth subject to
> <term>rebirth</term><note>The cyclical process of being born, dying, and being born again, driven by one's actions and attachments.</note>.'

->*<term>Brihadaranyaka Upanishad</term><note>original: "Brihadaranyaka Upanishad"; one of the oldest and most significant philosophical texts of ancient India, focusing on the nature of the self and the universe.</note>.*<-

### FREEDOM FROM REBIRTH

> ' He who lacketh <term>discrimination</term><note>In this context, the spiritual capacity to distinguish between the eternal soul and the temporary physical world.</note>, whose mind is
> unsteady and whose heart is impure, never reacheth
> the goal, but is born again and again. But he who
> hath discrimination, whose mind is steady and
> whose heart is pure, reacheth the goal, and having
> reached it is born no more.'

->*<term>Katha Upanishad</term><note>original: "Katha Upanishad"; a sacred Hindu text that takes the form of a dialogue between a young seeker named Nachiketa and Yama, the god of death.</note>.*<-
->(Swami Prabhavananda's and Frederick<-
->Manchester's Translations).<-

# PREFACE TO THE SECOND EDITION

---

## THE MESSAGE OF THIS BOOK

As this, the second, edition of *The Tibetan Book of the Dead* was about to be published, its editor was invited to explain, by means of an additional Preface, what the essential message is that the book holds for peoples so enamoured of this world's utilitarianism and physical existence and so fettered to bodily sensuousness as are the peoples of the <term>Occident</term><note>A term traditionally used to refer to the Western world, particularly Europe and the Americas, often contrasted with the "Orient" or the East.</note>.

The message is, that the **Art of Dying** is quite as important as the **Art of Living** (or of Coming into Birth), of which it is the complement and summation; that the future of being is dependent, perhaps entirely, upon a rightly controlled death, as the second part of this volume, setting forth the **Art of Reincarnating**, emphasizes.

The **Art of Dying**, as indicated by the death-rite associated with initiation into the <term>Mysteries of Antiquity</term><note>Secret religious rituals of the ancient Greek and Roman world, such as the Eleusinian Mysteries, which often involved a symbolic death and rebirth to gain spiritual knowledge.</note>, and referred to by <term>Apuleius</term><note>Lucius Apuleius (c. 124 – c. 170 AD), a Roman philosopher and writer who famously described spiritual initiation in his work "The Golden Ass."</note>, the Platonic philosopher, himself an initiate,

¹ Of this pre-mortem experiencing of death, presumably while out of the body, Apuleius states, in his *Metamorphoses* (XI, 23): 'I drew nigh to the confines of death. I trod the threshold of <term>Proserpine</term><note>The Roman goddess of the Underworld, equivalent to the Greek Persephone.</note> [in the realm of the dead]. I was borne through all the elements, and I returned to Earth again.' <term>Cf.</term><note>original: "Cf."; an abbreviation for the Latin "confer," meaning "compare."</note> translation by H. E. Butler (Oxford, Clarendon Press, 1910).

The art of going out from the body, or of transferring the consciousness from the earth-plane to the after-death plane, or to any other plane, is still practised, in Tibet, where it is known as <term>Pho-wa</term><note>original: "Pho-wa"; a Tibetan Buddhist meditation practice regarding the "transference of consciousness" at the moment of death to a chosen state of existence.</note>. See W. Y. Evans-Wentz, *Tibetan Yoga and Secret Doctrines* (Oxford University Press, London, 1935), pages 169-70, 246-76.

<summary>In this preface, the editor introduces the central theme of the book: that mastering the "Art of Dying" is essential for spiritual progress and a controlled rebirth, drawing parallels between Tibetan practices and the ancient Greek and Roman Mysteries.</summary>
<keywords>Art of Dying, Art of Living, Reincarnation, Occident, Apuleius, Pho-wa, Transference of Consciousness, Mysteries of Antiquity</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the Preface to the Second Edition, moving from the introduction of the "Art of Dying" to a comparison between ancient Mediterranean wisdom—such as that found in Egypt and Greece—and the limitations of modern materialistic medicine. It emphasizes that death, when approached as an initiation, can be consciously controlled.</meta>

and by many other illustrious initiates¹, and as *The Egyptian Book of the Dead* suggests, appears to have been far better known to the ancient peoples inhabiting the Mediterranean countries than it is now by their descendants in Europe and the Americas.

To those who had passed through the secret experiencing of **pre-mortem death** <note>The experience of "dying before one dies," referring to spiritual initiations where the soul is said to leave the body and return, removing the fear of physical death.</note>, right dying is initiation, conferring, as does the initiatory death-rite, the power to control consciously the process of death and regeneration. Throughout the Middle Ages, and during the Renaissance that followed, Europe still retained enough of the <term>Mystery teachings</term><note>Secret religious doctrines of antiquity that provided maps of the afterlife and instructions for the soul’s journey.</note> concerning death to understand the paramount importance of knowing how to die; and many treatises, hereinafter referred to, on the **Art of Dying** <note>original: "Ars Moriendi," a body of Christian literature providing guidance on how to die a "good death" according to medieval values.</note> were then current there. Various primitive Churches of Christendom, notably the Roman, Greek, Anglican, Syrian, Armenian, and Coptic, and other of the Churches dating from Reformation days, wisely incorporated into their rituals and observances many principles of this pre-Christian Art of Dying. And to-day, in their efforts thus to aid the dying, these Churches are in outstanding contrast, sociologically and culturally, to an Earth-limited medical science which has no word of guidance to convey to the dying concerning the after-death state, but which, on the contrary, frequently augments rather than ameliorates, by its questionable practices, the unfounded fears and often extreme unwillingness to die of its death-bed patients, to whom it is likely to have administered stupefying drugs and injections.

As *The Tibetan Book of the Dead* teaches, the dying should face death not only calmly and clear-mindedly and heroically,

---

¹Among these illustrious initiates, who, in their various extant writings, make reference similar to that by <term>Apuleius</term><note>A Roman writer and philosopher (2nd century AD) whose work "The Golden Ass" describes the experience of initiation into the mysteries of Isis.</note> to this death-rite, but usually in language more veiled than his, may be mentioned <term>Aeschylus</term><note>The famous Greek playwright (c. 525–456 BC), known as the father of tragedy.</note>, the founder of Greek drama, <term>Pindar</term><note>An ancient Greek lyric poet (c. 518–438 BC).</note>, the Greek poet, <term>Plato</term><note>The Athenian philosopher (c. 427–347 BC) whose works often explore the immortality of the soul.</note>, the disciple of Pythagoras, <term>Plutarch</term><note>A Greek philosopher and biographer (c. 46–120 AD).</note>, the Greek biographer, <term>Cicero</term><note>A Roman statesman and philosopher (106–43 BC) who wrote extensively on duty and the nature of the gods.</note>, the Roman orator and statesman, <term>Plotinus</term><note>A major Hellenistic philosopher (c. 204–270 AD), the founder of Neoplatonism.</note>, the Neo-Platonist, and his disciples <term>Porphyry</term><note>A Phoenician Neoplatonic philosopher (c. 234–305 AD).</note> and <term>Iamblichus</term><note>A Neoplatonist philosopher (c. 245–325 AD) who wrote on theurgy and the Egyptian mysteries.</note>. Cicero, rejoicing in his initiation-acquired enlightenment, writes: "We at last possess reasons why we should live; and we are not only eager to live, but we cherish a better hope in death" (*On the Laws* <note>original: "De Legibus"</note>, II, 14; translation by A. Moret, in his *Kings and Gods of Egypt*, New York and London, 1912, page 194). In the same context, A. Moret states: "The same sentiment is found in the inscription of an <term>Eleusinian initiate</term><note>A person who had undergone the secret rites of Demeter and Persephone at Eleusis, which promised a happy afterlife.</note>: 'Behold! it is a fair mystery that comes unto us from the Blessed; for mortals, death is no more an evil, but a bliss'". And Plutarch, in his *Immortality of the Soul*, refers to "the crowd of the folk who are not initiated and purified, and who throng to the mud-pit <note>A symbolic reference to the state of those obsessed with physical/sensual desires after death.</note> [of sensuality] and flounder in the darkness, and through fear of death cling to their woes, not trusting in the bliss of the hereafter" (Compare A. Moret, in the work cited <note>original: "op. cit."</note>, page 195¹).

It lists numerous classical philosophers and writers who viewed death not as an evil, but as a hopeful transition for the prepared soul.</summary>

->xv<-

-># PREFACE TO THE SECOND EDITION<-

...but with a mind properly trained and directed, rising above physical suffering and bodily weakness if necessary. They would be able to do this if they had effectively practiced the **Art of Living** during their active years, and the **Art of Dying** when the time came to pass away. When Milarepa <note>An 11th-century Tibetan poet and saint who reached enlightenment in a single lifetime.</note>, Tibet's holy master of **Spiritual Discipline** <term>Yoga</term><note>A system of physical and mental practices intended to achieve spiritual union.</note>, was preparing to die, he chose not only a favorable external setting in the Cave of Brilche, in Chubar, Tibet, but also an internal state of mental balance suited to his approaching **Ultimate Liberation** <term>Nirvāna</term><note>The final release from the cycle of suffering and rebirth.</note>. With unshakable control over his body—which had been poisoned by an enemy and was weakened by disease and racked with pain—he welcomed death with song, viewing it as natural and unavoidable. After delivering his final testament and parting advice to his gathered disciples, he spontaneously composed a remarkable hymn of grateful praise for his teacher, Marpa <note>The "Translator," who brought Buddhist teachings from India to Tibet and founded the Kagyu lineage.</note>, which is still preserved in his *Biography*. Then, when Milarepa had finished singing the hymn, he entered the quiet state of **Deep Meditative Absorption** <term>Samādhi</term><note>A state of profound, focused concentration where the mind is perfectly still.</note> and left his physical form. Thus, Milarepa died triumphantly, just as the saints and sages of all religions that offer salvation have done throughout the ages.¹

In the West, however, where the **Art of Dying** is little known and rarely practiced, there is a common resistance to death. As the ritual of the **Intermediate State** <term>Bardo</term><note>The transitional state of consciousness between death and the next rebirth.</note> suggests, this resistance produces harmful results. Here in America, every effort is typically made by a modern medical science—often focused purely on the physical—to postpone and thus interfere with the natural process of dying. Very often, the dying person is not allowed to die in their own home or in a calm mental state. In a hospital, they may be under the mind-numbing influence of pain-killing drugs, or else stimulated by injections to force the body to cling to life as long as possible. This creates a very undesirable death, as traumatic as that of a shell-shocked soldier on a battlefield. Just as the normal result of a birth can be ruined by malpractice, so can the normal result of the death-process be ruined.

The Eastern Sages believe that despite these unfortunate circumstances that now surround a person at the time of death, Western...

xvi

# PREFACE TO THE SECOND EDITION

As a person grows in right understanding, they will recognize that everywhere throughout the all-embracing universe—the vastness of which is measured in millions of light years—there is the reign of unerring Law. The <term>Cycle of Necessity</term> <note>Also known as the "Circle of Existence" in old Druidic faith</note>, the Round of Life and Death, will be known as a universal truth. Worlds and suns, no less than humans and every living thing, repeatedly enter into the illusory manifestation of physical embodiment. Each of these many manifestations is concluded by what the Lāmas of Tibet call the <term>Bardo</term> <note>Tibetan: བར་དོ་; the state intervening between death and rebirth</note>.

If the suggestive observations presented here in this new Preface—which are born from the doctrines contained in the translated texts of this book—can aid even slightly in awakening the Western world to the extreme dangers it faces, they will have achieved their purpose. These dangers result largely from a medical science that is mostly ignorant of the <term>Art of Dying</term>. By addressing this, these writings further the prayers of the Lāmas by helping to dispel that "Darkness of Ignorance" which, as the Buddha realized, enshrouds the world. As the Fully Enlightened One and all the Supreme Guides of Humanity have taught, it is only by the inner Light of Wisdom—'the true Light, which lighteth every man that cometh into the world'¹—that this darkness can be dispersed.

*The Egyptian Book of the Dead*, when correctly titled, is called *The Coming Forth by Day* <note>original: *Per em hru*</note>². This title refers to the sacred Egyptian art of passing from this life into another. Similarly, *The Tibetan Book of the Dead*, in the original Tibetan, is the *Bardo Thödol*, which means 'Liberation by Hearing on the After-Death Plane.' This implies a yogic method of passing into <term>Nirvānic Liberation</term> <note>the state of being free from the cycle of suffering and rebirth</note>, beyond the Cycle of Birth and Death. Each of these two books concerning death teaches, through its own unique method, an "Art of Dying" and a way of "Coming Forth into a New Life." However, they do so in a more symbolic and deeply spiritual manner than the treatises of medieval Christian Europe. Among those European works, the <term>Craft of Dying</term> <note>original Latin: *Ars Moriendo* (commonly *Ars Moriendi*)</note> may be seen as a typical example of this different, less esoteric approach.

It was the fervent hope of the late Lāma Kazi Dawa-Samdup,

---

¹ See the Gospel of St. John, Chapter I, Verse 9.
² See H. M. Tirard, *The Book of the Dead* (London, 1910), pages 48-49.

<summary>In this preface, the author compares the Tibetan "Bardo Thödol" with Egyptian and medieval Christian texts, arguing that death is a universal transition governed by law that requires a spiritual "Art of Dying."</summary>

<meta>This page concludes the author's preface to the second edition. It synthesizes the Tibetan Buddhist teachings on death with Western Christian concepts of resurrection, suggesting that death is not an end but a transition to be met with joy rather than fear.</meta>

xvii

# PREFACE TO THE SECOND EDITION

The translator, and the other learned <term>Lāmas</term> <note>Tibetan Buddhist spiritual masters</note> who directed the editor's Tibetan research, shared a hope—one which the editor also shares—that the <term>Occident</term> <note>The Western world</note> might find a way to reformulate and practice an *Art of Dying* and an *Art of Living*. This could be achieved by drawing on the *Mystery* teachings and the Western world's own Christianized versions of those same principles. For the peoples of the West, just as it was for the spiritual initiates of ancient times and remains for the peoples of the <term>Orient</term> <note>The Eastern world</note>, the transition from the human level of consciousness in the process called death can and should be accompanied by a solemn joy. Eventually, as the master <term>yogins</term> <note>Advanced practitioners of meditation and spiritual disciplines</note> declare, when humanity has grown spiritually strong, death will be experienced ecstatically in that state known to them as meditative ecstasy <note>original: "Samādhi"; a state of profound concentration and union with the divine.</note>. By the right practice of a trustworthy Art of Dying, death will then truly have lost its sting and been "swallowed up in victory."

As this Preface is being written, it is Easter in California. Just as it was the custom in many great civilizations of the past, so here today, from hilltops and mountains, people are paying respect to the new-born Sun at dawn with prayer and joyful singing. This occurs amidst the fresh, glistening greenery of newly-born leaves, the fragrance of blossoms, and the joy of Spring. It is, truly, the ever-returning Resurrection—the coming forth into a new life of things that had died. In the same manner, those who have "fallen asleep" in the Christ <note>original: "Christos"; the Greek term for the 'Anointed One'.</note> are to be empowered to rise from their tombs. Over the heart of the Earth-Mother, in pulsating, radiant, and energizing vibrations, flows the eternal Stream of Life. Whoever has the power of "right-seeing" understands that for beings who are not yet spiritually liberated, death is nothing more than the necessary, Law-directed prelude to birth.

->W. Y. E-W.<-

->San Diego, California,<-
->Easter 1948.<-

<summary>W. Y. Evans-Wentz concludes his preface by advocating for a Western 'Art of Dying' that mirrors Eastern wisdom, viewing death as a joyful transition and a necessary prelude to rebirth, much like the symbolism of Easter.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the editor’s preface, moving from the celebratory themes of Easter and spiritual rebirth mentioned on page xvii to the specific mechanics of the "Art of Dying" as understood in Eastern traditions.</meta>

xviii

### RIGHT DIRECTING OF THOUGHT WHEN DYING

Buddhists and Hindus alike believe that the last thought at the moment of death determines the character of the next incarnation <note>The rebirth of a soul or consciousness into a new physical body.</note>. As the *Liberation Through Hearing in the Between-State* <note>original: "Bardo Thödol"; the Tibetan title of the work popularly known as the Tibetan Book of the Dead.</note> teaches, so have the Sages of India long taught, that the thought-process of a dying person should be rightly directed, preferably by the dying person if he or she has been initiated or psychically trained to meet death, or, otherwise, by a <term>guru</term> <note>A spiritual teacher or guide who provides instruction and initiation.</note> or a friend or relative versed in the science of death.

Sri Krishna, in the *Song of God* <note>original: "Bhagavad Gītā" (viii, 6); a 700-verse Hindu scripture that is part of the epic Mahabharata.</note>, says to Arjuna, 'One attaineth whatever state [of being] one thinketh about at the last when relinquishing the body, being ever absorbed in the thought thereof.'

Our past thinking has determined our present status, and our present thinking will determine our future status; for man is what man thinks. In the words of the opening verse of the *Path of Truth* <note>original: "Dhammapāda"; a foundational collection of sayings of the Buddha.</note>, 'All that we are is the result of what we have thought: it is founded on our thoughts, it is made up of our thoughts.'

Likewise did the Hebrew Sages teach, as in *Proverbs* xxiii, 7, 'As a man thinketh in his heart, so is he'.

<summary>This page explains the pivotal importance of the "last thought" at the moment of death, illustrating how Buddhist, Hindu, and Hebrew traditions all agree that our state of mind shapes our future existence.</summary>
<keywords>last thought, reincarnation, Bardo Thödol, Bhagavad Gītā, Dhammapāda, spiritual direction, guru, mental state at death</keywords>

# PREFACE TO THE FIRST EDITION

In this book I am seeking—so far as possible—to suppress my own views and to act simply as the mouthpiece of a Tibetan sage, of whom I was a recognized disciple.

He was quite willing that I should make known his interpretation of the higher *lāmaic* <note>Relating to the system of Tibetan Buddhism and its spiritual leaders, the Lamas.</note> teachings and of the subtle esotericism underlying the *Bardo Thödol* <note>original: "Bar-do Thos-grol"; This is the Tibetan title of the work popularly known as the "Tibetan Book of the Dead," which literally means "Liberation Through Hearing in the Between-State."</note>, following the private and orally transmitted instructions which he as a young man had received when living the life of an ascetic with his late <term>hermit-guru</term> <note>A spiritual teacher who lives in seclusion to focus on intensive meditation and inner realization.</note> in Bhutan <note>A Buddhist kingdom located in the eastern Himalayas.</note>. Being himself a man who possessed a considerable amount of Western learning, he took great trouble to enable me to reproduce Oriental ideas in a form which would be intelligible to the European mind. If, in amplification, I have frequently referred to Occidental <note>Western</note> parallels of various mystic or occult doctrines current in the Orient, I have done so largely because in my wanderings there, chiefly in the high Himalayas and on the Tibetan frontiers of Kashmir, Garhwal, and Sikkim, I had come across learned philosophers and holy men who have found or thought they had found beliefs and religious practices—some recorded in books, some preserved by oral tradition alone—not only analogous to their own, but so closely akin to those of the Occident as to imply some historical connexion therewith. Whether the supposed influence passed from East to West or from West to East, was not so clear to their minds. A certain similarity does, however, seem to attach to the culture of these geographically divided provinces.

I have spent more than five years in such research, wandering from the palm-wreathed shores of Ceylon <note>The former name of the island nation of Sri Lanka.</note>, and thence through the wonder-land of the Hindus, to the glacier-clad heights of the Himalayan Ranges, seeking out the Wise Men of the East. Sometimes I lived among city dwellers, sometimes in jungle and mountain solitudes among *yogis* <note>Practitioners of yoga, specifically those who seek spiritual union through disciplined physical and mental practices.</note>, sometimes in

<summary>The author explains his role as a translator for a Tibetan sage, aiming to present the esoteric teachings of the "Tibetan Book of the Dead" to a Western audience. He describes his five years of travel and research across South Asia and the Himalayas in search of shared spiritual truths.</summary>

<keywords>preface, Bardo Thödol, Tibetan Buddhism, Bhutan, Himalayas, yoga, East-West philosophy, spiritual research</keywords>

The Introduction—which in its unusual lengthiness is intended to serve as a very necessary commentary to the translation—and the annotations to the text record the more important results of this research, more especially in relation to <term>Mahāyāna Buddhism</term> <note>Literally the "Great Vehicle," this is one of the two main branches of Buddhism, prevalent in Tibet, China, and Japan, which emphasizes the path of the Bodhisattva and the universal potential for enlightenment.</note>.

Nevertheless, I have been really little more than a compiler and editor of ‘The Tibetan Book of the Dead’. To the deceased translator—who combined in himself a greater knowledge of the Occult Sciences of Tibet and of Western Science than any Tibetan scholar of this epoch—the chief credit for its production very naturally belongs.

In addition to that greatest of all debts which the student ever owes to his preceptor, I acknowledge my indebtedness to each of my many good friends and helpers who have personally aided me herewith. Some of them are of one Faith, some of another; some are far away in Japan and in China, some in the land of my birth, America; many are in Ceylon and in India; a few are in Tibet.

I am also very grateful to him for the Foreword.

### II. VICTORY

> 'But anguish crept upon me, even me,

> Ah me ! how have I come into this evil road,

> Brief is the span of life yet left to me ;
> Old age, disease, hang imminent to crush.

# ILLUSTRATIONS

I. THE TRANSLATOR AND THE EDITOR . . *preceding Plate II*
From a photograph of the Translator and the Editor in <note>A traditional style of clothing consisting of long robes (chuba) and sashes.</note> Tibetan dress, taken in Gangtok, <note>A Himalayan region located between Nepal, Tibet, and Bhutan; it was an independent kingdom in 1919.</note> Sikkim, during the year 1919.

II. FOLIOS 35a AND 67a OF THE <term>Bardo Thödol: Literally "Liberation Through Hearing in the Intermediate State," commonly known as the Tibetan Book of the Dead.</term> *BARDO THÖDOL* MS.
*Frontispiece*

A photographic reproduction (about two-thirds of the original size). In the original the illuminations are in colour (now much faded) painted on the folios (cf. p. 68).

The painting on the upper folio illustrates, with the colours, emblems, and orientation in strict accord with the traditions of Tibetan monastic art, the description in the text of the united <term>Maṇḍalas: Sacred geometric diagrams or "circles" that represent the residence of divine beings and the structure of the universe.</term> *maṇḍalas*, or divine conclaves, of the Peaceful Deities of the First to the Sixth Day of the *Bardo* that dawn thus in one complete conclave on the Sixth Day (cf. pp. 118-26). In the central circle (Centre) is the <note>One of the five primary "Meditation Buddhas," representing the source of the universe and the transmutation of ignorance into wisdom.</note> Dhyānī Buddha Vairochana, embraced by his <term>Shakti: The personification of divine feminine creative power, often depicted as a spouse or consort to a male deity.</term> *shakti*, or divine spouse, the Mother of Infinite Space. In the next circle, each likewise embraced by his *shakti*, are the four Dhyānī Buddhas, who, with Vairochana, constitute the *maṇḍala* of the Five Dhyānī Buddhas. In the outermost circle are typical <note>Enlightened beings who have postponed their own entry into Nirvana to help all other living beings achieve liberation.</note> Bodhisattvas and other deities who accompany the Five Dhyānī Buddhas (cf. pp. 118-20); and in the four small outer circles the four female Door-Keepers of the complete conclave (cf. p. 120).

The painting on the lower folio similarly illustrates, in colours, emblems, and orientation, the united *maṇḍalas* of the Wrathful Deities of the Eighth to the Fourteenth Day that dawn thus in one complete conclave on the Fourteenth Day (cf. pp. 143-6). In the cruciform design at the centre are the three-headed <term>Herukas: Fierce, powerful manifestations of enlightened energy that appear in terrifying forms to help destroy the ego and overcome spiritual obstacles.</term> Herukas of the Buddha, Vajra, Ratna, Padma, and Karma Order, each with his *shakti*, that dawn, *maṇḍala* by *maṇḍala*, from the Eighth to the Twelfth Day (cf. pp. 136-41). The outer circle contains representations of the various animal-headed deities that dawn on the Thirteenth and the Fourteenth Day (cf. pp. 141-6). In the four small outer circles are the <note>Powerful female spiritual beings or "Sky-Goers" who protect and guide practitioners on the path to enlightenment.</note> Four Yoginīs of the Door (cf. pp. 145-6).

The translation of the text on the two folios is indicated by special markings on pages 120 and 144.

III. EFFIGY OF THE DEAD PERSON . . . . . p. 21
A reproduction (slightly reduced) of a copy of a Tibetan printed <term>Chang-ku: A printed paper effigy used in funeral rites to represent the deceased person; the paper is eventually burned as part of the purification ceremony.</term> *Chang-ku* paper.

# ILLUSTRATIONS

### IV. THE GREAT <term>MAṆḌALA: A sacred geometric diagram or "circle" representing the divine realm and the structure of the universe.</term> MAṆḌALA OF THE PEACEFUL DEITIES
->facing p. 118<-

This and the companion illustration, number V, following, are photographic reproductions (about one-fourth of the original size) of two paintings in colour, on heavy cotton cloth, made in the chief monastery of Gyantse, Tibet, on the instructions of Major W. L. Campbell, to illustrate our <term>Bardo Thödol: Literally "Liberation Through Hearing in the Intermediate State," commonly known as the Tibetan Book of the Dead.</term> *Bardo Thödol* translation (see Preface, p. xi). The colours, emblems, and orientations, as in the two manuscript illuminations described above, are in accord with the strict conventions of the religious art of Tibet. The correlations, too, between the text and the deities depicted, as brought out in the description of the two manuscript illuminations, also apply to these two more elaborate paintings.

Innermost circle (representing the Centre of the orientation): at the centre, <term>Vairochana: One of the five primary "Meditation Buddhas," representing the source of the universe and the transmutation of ignorance into wisdom.</term> Vairochana (white) and <term>shakti: The personification of divine feminine creative power, often depicted as a spouse or consort to a male deity.</term> *shakti*, on lion throne (cf. pp. 105-6); at the top, <term>Samanta-Bhadra: The "All-Good," representing the primordial, pure state of the mind.</term> Samanta-Bhadra (blue) and *shakti*; in subordinate circle on the left, <note>original: "Chenrazee"</note> <term>Avalokiteshvara: The Bodhisattva who embodies the compassion of all Buddhas.</term> (above), <term>Mañjushrī: The Bodhisattva of transcendent wisdom, usually depicted with a sword to cut through ignorance.</term> Mañjushrī (below, on left), <term>Vajra-Pāṇi: The Bodhisattva representing spiritual power, often called the "Wielder of the Scepter."</term> Vajra-Pāṇi (below, on right); in subordinate circle on the right, Tsoṅ Khapa, a famous Tibetan <term>guru: A spiritual teacher or guide.</term> *guru* (above), and his two chief <note>original: "shishyas"</note> disciples, Gendundub (below, on left), and Gyalshabje (below, on right).

Lower circle (East): at the centre, <term>Vajra-Sattva: The "Diamond-Being," a deity associated with the purification of the mind.</term> Vajra-Sattva (blue), the reflex of <term>Akṣhobhya: The "Immovable" Buddha, who represents the transformation of anger into mirror-like wisdom.</term> Akṣhobhya, and *shakti*, on elephant throne; Pushpā (above); Lāsyā (below); and Bodhisattvas (on left and right). Cf. pp. 108-9.

Left circle (South): at the centre, <term>Ratna-Sambhava: The "Jewel-Born" Buddha, representing the transformation of pride into the wisdom of equality.</term> Ratna-Sambhava (yellow) and *shakti*, on horse throne; Dhūpa (above); Mālā (below); and Bodhisattvas (on left and right). Cf. pp. 110-11.

Upper circle (West): at the centre, <term>Amitābha: The Buddha of "Infinite Light," who presides over the Western Paradise.</term> Amitābha (red) and *shakti*, on peacock throne; Āloka (above); Gīta (below); and Bodhisattvas (on left and right). Cf. p. 113.

Right circle (North): at the centre, <term>Amogha-Siddhi: The Buddha of "Unfailing Success," representing the transformation of envy into all-accomplishing wisdom.</term> Amogha-Siddhi (green) and *shakti*, on <note>A mythical creature, often depicted as half-human and half-bird.</note> harpy throne; Naivedya (above); Gandha (below); and Bodhisattvas (on left and right). Cf. pp. 115-6.

Occupying the four corners of the great circle are the four chief Door-Keepers (cf. p. 120) of the *Maṇḍala*, each pair on a fire-enhaloed lotus throne: upper left, Yamāntaka (yellow) and *shakti*, the Door-Keepers of the South; upper right, Hayagrīva (red) and *shakti*, the Door-Keepers of the West; lower right, Amṛitā-Dhāra (white) and *shakti*, the Door-Keepers of the North; lower left, Vijaya (green) and *shakti*, the Door-Keepers of the East. At the bottom, in the centre, <term>Padma Sambhava: The 8th-century Indian master who established Buddhism in Tibet, also known as the Precious Guru.</term> Padma Sambhava, the Great Human *Guru* of the *Bardo Thödol* Doctrine, in royal robes and pandit head-dress, holding a skull filled with blood, symbolical of renunciation of life, in his left hand, and a <term>dorje: A ritual object representing a thunderbolt or diamond, symbolizing the indestructible nature of reality and spiritual power.</term> *dorje*, symbolical of mastery over life, in his right. At his feet lie

### V. THE GREAT <term>SACRED DIAGRAM</term> <note>original: "MAṆḌALA"</note> OF THE <term>KNOWLEDGE-HOLDERS: Semi-wrathful enlightened beings who "hold" the power of secret wisdom.</term> <note>original: "Vidyadhara"</note> AND WRATHFUL DEITIES . . . . . *facing p. 136*

At the bottom, in the centre (Centre) the supreme Knowledge-Holding Deity, the Lotus Lord of Dance (red, for the five colours of text) and <term>consort: The female partner of a deity, representing the union of wisdom and compassionate action.</term> <note>original: "shakti"</note>. In the four corners, his four companion deities: lower left (East), the Earth-Abiding Knowledge-Holder (white) and <term>consort</term> <note>original: "shakti"</note>; upper left (South), the Knowledge-Holder Having Power Over Duration of Life (yellow) and <term>consort</term> <note>original: "shakti"</note>; upper right (West), the Knowledge-Holding Deity of the Great Symbol (red) and <term>consort</term> <note>original: "shakti"</note>; lower right (North), the Self-Evolved Knowledge-Holder (green) and <term>consort</term> <note>original: "shakti"</note>. Each pair of deities of this <term>sacred circle: A geometric and symbolic diagram representing the celestial abode of a deity.</term> <note>original: "maṇḍala"</note>, that dawns intermediately (i. e. between the <term>sacred circles</term> <note>original: "maṇḍalas"</note> of the Peaceful Deities and the <term>sacred circles</term> <note>original: "maṇḍalas"</note> of the Wrathful Deities) on the Seventh Day (cf. pp. 126-8), are in peaceful aspect, on an enhaloed lotus and lunar throne, performing a mystic dance which is Tantric.

At the top, in the centre, presiding over the whole greater <term>sacred circle</term> <note>original: "maṇḍala"</note>, is <term>The All-Good: The primordial Buddha who represents the fundamental, unchanging nature of reality.</term> <note>original: "Samanta-Bhadra"</note> (dark blue), the <term>Primordial Buddha: The 'First Buddha,' representing the essence of enlightenment itself.</term> <note>original: "Ādi-Buddha"</note>, and <term>consort</term> <note>original: "shakti"</note> (white), in peaceful aspect, on a lotus and lunar throne, enhaloed in rainbow colours, with lotuses and the moon (white) on his right and lotuses and the sun (gold) on his left.

### VI. THE TIBETAN JUDGEMENT . . . . . . *facing p. 166*

A photographic reproduction (about one-fourth of the original size) of a monastic painting in colour, on heavy cotton cloth, made on the instructions of the editor, in Gangtok, Sikkim, by a Tibetan artist named Lharipa-Pempa-Tendup-La, to illustrate the Judgement (see p. 37).

Occupying the central position is <term>The King of Truth: The title given to the judge of the dead, who weighs the virtues and vices of the deceased.</term> <note>original: "Dharma-Rāja"</note>, the King of Truth, or Administrator of Truth and Justice, otherwise called <term>The Lord of Death: The sovereign of the hell realms and the judge of karma.</term> <note>original: "Yama-Rāja"</note>, the King and Judge of the Dead. He is the wrathful aspect of <term>The Compassionate One: The patron deity of Tibet, known for his infinite mercy toward all beings.</term> <note>original: "Chenrazee"</note>, the National Divine Protector of Tibet. The third eye of spiritual insight is in his forehead. He stands enhaloed in flames of wisdom, on a solar throne supported by a lotus throne, treading under foot a <term>demon: A manifestation of the obstacles and desires that trap beings in the cycle of suffering.</term> <note>original: "māra"</note> form, symbolic of the <term>illusion: The deceptive nature of reality that leads beings to mistake the temporary for the eternal.</term> <note>original: "māyā"</note> (i. e. illusionary) nature of human existence. His head-dress is adorned with human skulls, and a serpent forms his necklace. His necklet is a human hide, the head of which protrudes from behind his right side, and a hand and foot hang down over the centre of his breast. A girdle of human heads surrounds his waist. His pavilion and the walls of his Court

There is written on the mirror, in Tibetan, '<term>Hri</term> <note>The sacred seed-syllable associated with compassion.</note>,' the <term>seed-syllable</term> <note>original: "bija"</note>, or chief, <term>sacred chant</term> <note>original: "mantra"</note> of <term>the Bodhisattva of Compassion</term> <note>original: "Chenrazee," the Tibetan name for Avalokiteshvara.</note>.

Directly in front of the <term>King of Truth</term> <note>original: "Dharma-Rāja," the Lord of Death who judges the deceased.</note> is the <term>Monkey-Headed One</term> <note>original: "Sprehu-gochan" (Tib. On the right of the Lord of Death stands the Little White God, emptying a sack of white pebbles; on the other side, the Little Black God, emptying a sack of black pebbles (cf. p. 166).

Guarding the weighing are the <term>Bull-Headed One</term> <note>original: "Lang-gochan" (Tib. Glang-mgo-chan)</note>, holding another mirror of past deeds, and the <term>Serpent-Headed One</term> <note>original: "Dul-gochan" (Tib. Sbrul-mgo-chan)</note>, holding a scourge and a noose.

A yellow deity, on the right of the King of Truth, holding a writing-tablet and a stilus, and a brown deity, on the left, holding a sword and a noose, are the two Advocates. The yellow advocate is the defender, the brown advocate is the accuser. The six deities, five of whom are animal-headed, sitting in the Court of Judgement, three on either side, like a jury of subordinate judges, supervise the proceedings in order to ensure regularity of procedure and impartial justice (cf. pp. 35-7). The first above on the right holds a mirror of deeds and a skull-cup of blood, the second a battle-axe and a skull-cup of blood, the third a noose. The first above on the left holds a battle-axe and skull-cup of blood, the second a small vase of blossoms in his right hand, the third a <term>thunderbolt scepter</term> <note>original: "dorje"</note> and a skull-cup of blood.

Near the gate on the left and the gate on the right stands one of the avenging furies who act as warders. There are ten Tibetans in the foreground of the Court awaiting judgement. The one with a conical head-dress (in red) is a <term>priest of the Red-Hat sect</term> <note>original: "red-cap lāma"</note>; the one with a round head-dress (in yellow) is a government official. The others are ordinary people. The three gates, through which the condemned enter the Hells below, are guarded by three animal-headed porters, each holding a noose.

Issuing from the Court, at either side of the Judge, are the Six <term>Pathways of Action</term> <note>original: "Karmic Pathways"</note>, leading to the Six Buddhas of the Six <term>Realms of Existence</term> <note>original: "Lokas"</note>, in whom the Pathways end, each Pathway and Buddha in appropriate colour (cf. p. 124 and Illustration IV). Traversing the Pathways are twelve of the dead who have recently been judged. The highest one, upon the left, on the white light-path approaching the Buddha of the <term>God Realm</term> <note>original: "Deva Loka"</note>, is a <term>priest of the Yellow-Hat sect</term> <note>original: "yellow-cap lāma"</note>; next to him is a government official

on the yellow light-path approaching the Buddha of the <term>Human Realm</term> <note>original: "Human Loka"</note>; the highest upon the right, on the green light-path approaching the Buddha of the <term>Titan Realm</term> <note>original: "Asura Loka," the realm of jealous demigods</note>, is a red-cap <term>Lama</term> <note>original: "lāma," a spiritual teacher</note>.

In the Lower World, at the bottom of the painting, typical punishments in various Hells are depicted, none of which, however, are everlasting. On the left, in the upper corner, where two sinners are immersed in a glacial region, the <term>Eight Cold Hells</term> are suggested. Near the edge of the painting, on the opposite side, a sinner amidst flames suggests the <term>Eight Hot Hells</term>. The commission of any of the ten impious acts, deliberately and from selfish motives, leads to purgation in the Cold Hells; any of the same acts done through anger lead to purgation in the Hot Hells.

Just below the Cold Hells is the <term>Hell of the Spiked Tree</term> or <term>Hill of Spikes</term> <margin>Tibetan: *Shal-ma-ri*</margin>, in which an evil-doer has been quartered and affixed to the spikes. Beside it, in charge of a hell-fury, is the <term>Doorless Iron House</term> <margin>Tibetan: *Lchags-khang-sgo-med*</margin>. Next to this there are four <term>Lamas</term> held under the mountainous weight of an enormous Tibetan sacred book; they are being punished thus for having in their earth-life hurried through and skipped passages when reading religious texts. The triangle, in which an evil-doer is fixed, symbolizes the terrible <term>Avichi Hell</term> <note>original: "Avitchi," the hell of uninterrupted suffering</note>, wherein one guilty of a heinous sin, such as using sorcery to destroy enemies or deliberate failure to fulfil <term>Tantric vows</term> <note>Sacred promises made during esoteric Buddhist initiations.</note>, endures punishment for ages which are almost immeasurable. Close to the triangle, a hell-fury is pouring spoonfuls of molten metal into a woman condemned for prostitution. The person next to her, bowed under the weight of a heavy rock tied to his back, is being punished in that manner for having killed small living creatures like vermin or other insects. The sinner whom a hell-fury is holding stretched out on a floor of spiked iron while another hell-fury is preparing to hack him to pieces (cf. p. 166) has been found guilty of another of the ten impious acts. So also has been the woman who is about to be sawn in two lengthwise; her sin has been murder. As in Dante’s <term>Inferno</term> <note>original: "Inferno," a reference to the 'Hell' described in the Divine Comedy.</note>, other evil-doers, incapable, as our text explains, of succumbing to the process (cf. p. 166), are being cooked in the iron cauldron at the lower right-hand corner. Three hell-furies (one brown, one yellow, one blue in the original) are to be seen holding by the end of nooses and leading and dragging along (cf. p. 166) to appropriate punishments three of the dead who have just been cast into Hell.

At the top of the picture, in the centre, on an enhaloed lotus and lunar throne, with the moon (white) at his right and the sun (gold) at his left, presiding over all, is <term>Vajradhara</term> <note>original: "Dorje Chang" (blue), the primordial Buddha and "Holder of the Thunderbolt."</note>, the <term>Divine Teacher</term> <note>original: "Guru"</note> of the Red-Hat School of <term>Padmasambhava</term> <note>original: "Padma Sambhava," the 8th-century master who brought Buddhism to Tibet.</note>; for he is held to be the Ever-living and Spiritual Source whence continue to emanate, as in the days of the <term>Shakyamuni Buddha</term> <note>original: "Buddha Shakya Muni," the historical Buddha.</note>, all the <term>Esoteric Doctrines</term> <note>Secret or inner teachings intended for initiated students.</note>

## EMBLEMS

1. THE INDIAN <term>WHEEL OF THE LAW</term> <note>original: *Dharma-Chakra*</note>
->*on back of book*<-

From designs sculptured on the <term>Great Stupas of Sanchi</term> <note>original: "Sanchi Topes," ancient Buddhist monuments in India</note>, dating from about 500 B.C. to 100 A.D.

2. THE <term>TIBETAN BUDDHIST</term> <note>original: *Lāmaic*</note> CROSSED <term>SCEPTRE</term> <note>original: *Dorje*</note> . . . *on front cover of book*
Symbolical of equilibrium, immutability, and almighty power. (Cf. pp. 63, 116^1.)

3. THE TIBETAN <term>WHEEL OF THE LAW</term> <note>original: *Ch'os-'k'or-bskor*</note>
->*facing p. 119*<-

The Eight-Spoked Wheel (cf. p. 106), on a lotus throne and enhaloed by Flames of Wisdom, is representative of the Thousand-Spoked Wheel of the Good Law of the Buddha, symbol of the symmetry and completeness of the Sacred Law of the <term>Truth</term> <note>original: *Dharma*</note>, or Scriptures. The design at the centre, called in Tibetan the <term>Wheel of Joy</term> <note>original: *rgyan-'k'yil*</note>, composed of three whirling segments, symbolizes—as does the <term>symbol of well-being</term> <note>original: *svastika*</note> at the centre of the Indian Wheel of the Law—the <term>Cycle of Suffering</term> <note>original: *Sangsāra*</note>, or ceaseless change or ‘becoming’.

4. THE <term>SCEPTRE</term> <note>original: *Dorje*</note>, THE <term>TIBETAN BUDDHIST</term> <note>original: *Lāmaic*</note> STAFF . . . *facing p. 137*
A type of the Thunderbolt of Indra, the Indian Jupiter, used in most <term>Tibetan Buddhist</term> <note>original: *lāmaic*</note> rituals (cf. pp. 10, 108^2, 137-8, 142-5), symbolical of dominion over <term>worldly</term> <note>original: *sangsāric*</note> existence.

<note>^1 It should be noted that each of the dead possesses a body suited to the paradise realm or hell-world in which <term>the results of past actions</term> <note>original: *karma*</note> brings about birth; and that when any of the after-human-death states of existence ends there is again a death process and a casting off of a body (cf. pp. 155-8, and Book II *passim*). The <term>Intermediate State</term> <note>original: *Bardo*</note> is the state between death and rebirth whence one may be reborn in this world in a human body, or in the ghost-world in a ghost body, or in one of the paradise realms, such as the <term>realm of the gods</term> <note>original: *deva-loka*</note>, in a god body, or in the <term>realm of the titans</term> <note>original: *asura-loka*</note> in a titan body, or in one of the hells in a body capable of enduring suffering and incapable of dying there until the purification is complete. Following death in a hell, or in any other of the after-human-death states, the normal process is to be reborn on earth as a human being. The True Goal, as the *Bardo Thödol* repeatedly explains, is beyond all states of embodiment, beyond all hells, worlds, and heavens, beyond the <term>Cycle of Rebirth</term> <note>original: *Sangsāra*</note>, beyond Nature; it is called <term>Liberation</term> <note>original: *Nirvāṇa*</note> (Tib. <term>Passed Beyond Sorrow</term> <note>original: *Myang-hdas*</note>). See Addenda, V, pp. 224-32.</note>

<meta>This page marks a transition from the physical and symbolic descriptions of the manuscript's emblems to the intellectual framing of the text. # THE TIBETAN BOOK OF THE DEAD

---

## PSYCHOLOGICAL COMMENTARY

### By Dr. C. G. Jung¹ <note>Carl Gustav Jung (1875–1961) was a Swiss psychiatrist who founded analytical psychology. His interest in this text helped bridge the gap between Eastern spirituality and Western science.</note>

->Translated by R. F. C. Hull from *The Tibetan Book of the Dead* <note>original: *Das Tibetaniſche Totenbuch*</note><-

Before embarking upon the psychological commentary, I should like to say a few words about the text itself. *The Tibetan Book of the Dead*, or the *Bardo Thödol* <note>Literally, "Liberation Through Hearing in the Intermediate State."</note>, is a book of instructions for the dead and dying. Like *The Egyptian Book of the Dead*, it is meant to be a guide for the dead man during the period of his <term>Intermediate State</term> <note>original: *Bardo*; the state of existence between death and the next rebirth.</note> existence, symbolically described as an intermediate state of forty-nine days’ duration between death and rebirth. The text

¹ To one of Dr. Jung’s most successful disciples, Dr. James Kirsch, Analytical Psychologist, of Los Angeles, California, who has discussed this Psychological Commentary with Dr. Jung in Zürich and aided in its English translation, the Editor is indebted for the important prefatory admonition which follows, addressed to the <term>Eastern</term> <note>original: *Oriental*</note> reader:—
*This book addresses itself primarily to the <term>Western</term> <note>original: *Occidental*</note> reader, and attempts to describe important <term>Eastern</term> <note>original: *Oriental*</note> experiences and conceptions in <term>Western</term> <note>original: *Occidental*</note> terms. Dr. Jung seeks to facilitate this difficult undertaking by his Psychological Commentary. It is, therefore, unavoidable that, in so doing, he employs terms which are familiar to the <term>Western</term> mind but which are, in some instances, objectionable to the <term>Eastern</term> mind.*
*One such objectionable term is “soul”. According to Buddhistic belief, the “soul” is ephemeral, is an illusion, and, therefore, has no real existence. The Germanic word “Seele” <note>The German term for soul, which carries connotations of the entire psyche and life-force.</note>, as employed in the original German version of this Psychological Commentary, is not synonymous with the English word “Soul” although commonly so translated. “Seele” is an ancient word, sanctioned by Germanic tradition and used, by outstanding German mystics like <term>Eckhart</term> <note>Meister Eckhart (c. 1260–1328), a German theologian and philosopher known for his work on mysticism.</note> and great German poets like <term>Goethe</term> <note>Johann Wolfgang von Goethe (1749–1832), Germany's most famous literary figure.</note>, to signify the Ultimate Reality, symbolized in feminine, or <term>divine energy</term> <note>original: *shakti*; the personification of divine feminine creative power.</note>, aspect. Herein, Dr. Jung uses it poetically with reference to the “Psyche” as the Collective Psyche. In psychological language it represents the <term>Collective Unconscious</term> <note>Jung's concept of a part of the deepest unconscious mind that is genetically inherited and not shaped by personal experience.</note>, as being the matrix of everything. It is the womb of everything, even of the <term>Truth Body of the Buddha</term> <note>original: *Dharma-Kaya*; the unmanifested aspect of the Buddha from which all things arise.</note>; it is the <term>Truth Body</term> itself.*
*Accordingly, <term>Eastern</term> readers are invited to put aside, for the time being, their understanding of “soul” and to accept Dr. Jung’s use of the word, in order to be able to follow him with an open mind into the depths where he seeks to build a bridge from the Shore of the <term>East</term> to the Shore of the <term>West</term>, and to tell of the various paths leading to the Great Liberation, the <term>One Salvation</term> <note>original: *Una Salus*; Latin for 'the only salvation' or 'single health'.</note>.*

<summary>This page introduces Dr. C. G. Jung’s psychological commentary on the Tibetan Book of the Dead, including a crucial footnote explaining the linguistic and philosophical differences between the Western concept of the "soul" and Eastern Buddhist thought.</summary>
<keywords>C. G. Jung, Bardo Thödol, Psychological Commentary, Soul vs Seele, Collective Unconscious, Dharma-Kaya, James Kirsch, Buddhism and Psychology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Dr. C. G. Jung's psychological commentary on the Tibetan Book of the Dead. The previous page introduced the text as a guide for the "Intermediate State" between death and rebirth. This section breaks down the three stages of this journey and reflects on the text's reception in the West.</meta>

<page-num>xxxvi</page-num>

### PSYCHOLOGICAL COMMENTARY

falls into three parts. The first part, called the *Transitional State of the Moment of Death* <note>original: *Chikhai Bardo*</note>, describes the psychic happenings at the moment of death. The second part, or the *Transitional State of Reality* <note>original: *Chönyid Bardo*</note>, deals with the dream-state which supervenes immediately after death, and with what are called 'karmic <note>Relating to the law of cause and effect based on one's past actions.</note> illusions'. The third part, or the *Transitional State of Rebirth* <note>original: *Sidpa Bardo*</note>, concerns the onset of the birth-instinct and of prenatal events. It is characteristic that supreme insight and illumination, and hence the greatest possibility of attaining liberation, are vouchsafed during the actual process of dying. Soon afterward, the 'illusions' begin which lead eventually to reincarnation, the illuminative lights growing ever fainter and more multifarious, and the visions more and more terrifying. This descent illustrates the estrangement of consciousness from the liberating truth as it approaches nearer and nearer to physical rebirth. The purpose of the instruction is to fix the attention of the dead man, at each successive stage of delusion and entanglement, on the ever-present possibility of liberation, and to explain to him the nature of his visions. The text of the *Liberation through Hearing in the Intermediate State* <note>original: *Bardo Thödol*</note> is recited by the *spiritual teacher* <note>original: *lāma*</note> in the presence of the corpse.

I do not think I could better discharge my debt of thanks to the two previous translators of the *Bardo Thödol*, the late teacher <note>original: *Lāma*</note> Kazi Dawa-Samdup and Dr. Evans-Wentz, than by attempting, with the aid of a psychological commentary, to make the magnificent world of ideas and the problems contained in this treatise a little more intelligible to the Western mind. I am sure that all who read this book with open eyes, and who allow it to impress itself upon them without prejudice, will reap a rich reward.

The *Bardo Thödol*, fitly named by its editor, Dr. W. Y. Evans-Wentz, 'The Tibetan Book of the Dead', caused a considerable stir in English-speaking countries at the time of its first appearance in 1927. It belongs to that class of writings which are not only of interest to specialists in <term>Mahāyāna Buddhism</term> <note>A major branch of Buddhism focused on the ideal of the Bodhisattva—attaining enlightenment for the sake of all beings.</note>, but which also, because of their deep humanity and their still deeper insight into the secrets of the human psyche, make an especial appeal to the layman who is seeking to broaden his knowledge of life. For years, ever since it was first published, the *Bardo Thödol* has been my constant companion, and to it I owe not only many stimulating ideas and discoveries, but also many fundamental insights. Unlike *The Egyptian Book of the Dead* <note>original: *The Egyptian Book of the Dead*; historically known as the *Book of Coming Forth by Day*</note>, which always prompts one

<summary>This page outlines the three stages of the Bardo—death, reality, and rebirth—explaining how liberation is most accessible at the moment of death. Jung expresses his personal gratitude to the translators and notes the profound psychological impact the text has had on Western readers.</summary>

<keywords>Chikhai Bardo, Chönyid Bardo, Sidpa Bardo, Bardo Thödol, Mahāyāna Buddhism, C. G. Jung, spiritual teacher, liberation, reincarnation, psychology</keywords>

--- Page 38 ---
<meta>This page continues Dr. C. G. Jung’s psychological commentary. The previous page ended mid-sentence as Jung began to explain his approach to the text, describing the three stages of the transition between death and rebirth.</meta>

<page-num>xxxvii</page-num>

### PSYCHOLOGICAL COMMENTARY

to say too much or too little, the <term>Bardo Thödol<gloss>Literally "Liberation Through Hearing in the Intermediate State," popularly known in the West as the Tibetan Book of the Dead.</gloss></term> offers one an intelligible philosophy addressed to human beings rather than to gods or primitive savages. Its philosophy contains the quintessence of Buddhist psychological criticism; and, as such, one can truly say that it is of an unexampled superiority. Not only the ‘wrathful’ but also the ‘peaceful’ deities are conceived as <term>sangsāric<gloss>Derived from "Samsara," the cycle of birth, suffering, death, and rebirth in Buddhist and Hindu traditions.</gloss></term> projections of the human psyche, an idea that seems all too obvious to the enlightened European, because it reminds him of his own banal simplifications. But though the European can easily explain away these deities as projections, he would be quite incapable of positing them at the same time as real. The *Bardo Thödol* can do that, because, in certain of its most essential metaphysical premises, it has the enlightened as well as the unenlightened European at a disadvantage. The ever-present, unspoken assumption of the *Bardo Thödol* is the <term>antinominal<gloss>Likely "antinomial," referring to a paradox or a contradiction between two laws or principles that are both equally true or necessary.</gloss></term> character of all metaphysical assertions, and also the idea of the qualitative difference of the various levels of consciousness and of the metaphysical realities conditioned by them. The background of this unusual book is not the niggardly European ‘either-or’, but a magnificently affirmative ‘both-and’. This statement may appear objectionable to the Western philosopher, for the West loves clarity and unambiguity; consequently, one philosopher clings to the position, ‘God is’, while another clings equally fervently to the negation. ‘God is not’. What would these hostile brethren make of an assertion like the following:

> ‘Recognizing the <term>voidness<gloss>original: "Sunyata." A central Buddhist concept referring to the lack of inherent, independent existence in all phenomena.</gloss></term> of thine own intellect to be <term>Buddhahood<gloss>The state of perfect enlightenment; the realization of one's true nature.</gloss></term>, and knowing it at the same time to be thine own consciousness, thou shalt abide in the state of the divine mind of the Buddha.’

Such an assertion is, I fear, as unwelcome to our Western philosophy as it is to our theology. The *Bardo Thödol* is in the highest degree psychological in its outlook; but, with us, philosophy and theology are still in the mediaeval <note>A British/older spelling of "medieval."</note>, pre-psychological stage where only the assertions are listened to, explained, defended, criticized and disputed, while the authority that makes them has, by general consent, been deposed as outside the scope of discussion.

Metaphysical assertions, however, are *statements of the psyche*, and are therefore psychological. To the Western mind, which compensates its well-known feelings of resentment by a slavish

<summary>Dr. Jung argues that the Bardo Thödol offers a superior psychological insight by treating deities as both psychic projections and realities simultaneously. He contrasts the Eastern "both-and" logic with the Western tendency toward binary "either-or" thinking in philosophy and religion.</summary>
