### PSYCHOLOGICAL COMMENTARY

regard for ‘rational’ explanations, this obvious truth seems all too obvious, or else it is seen as an inadmissible negation of metaphysical ‘truth’. Whenever the Westerner hears the word ‘psychological’, it always sounds to him like ‘only psychological’. For him the ‘soul’ is something pitifully small, unworthy, personal, subjective, and a lot more besides. He therefore prefers to use the word ‘mind’ instead, though he likes to pretend at the same time that a statement which may in fact be very subjective indeed is made by the ‘mind’, naturally by the ‘Universal Mind’, or even—at a pinch—by the ‘Absolute’ itself. This rather ridiculous presumption is probably a compensation for the regrettable smallness of the soul. It almost seems as if Anatole France <note>Anatole France (1844–1924) was a Nobel Prize-winning French author known for his irony and skepticism.</note> had uttered a truth which were valid for the whole Western world when, in his *Penguin Island*, Cathérine d’Alexandrie offers this advice to God: ‘Give them a soul, but a little one!’ <note>original: "Donnez leur une ame, mais une petite"</note>

It is the soul which, by the divine creative power inherent in it, makes the metaphysical assertion; it posits the distinctions between metaphysical entities. Not only is it the condition of all metaphysical reality, it is that reality.¹

With this great psychological truth the <term>Bardo Thödol<gloss>Literally "Liberation Through Hearing in the Intermediate State," known in the West as the Tibetan Book of the Dead.</gloss></term> opens. The book is not a ceremonial of burial, but a set of instructions for the dead, a guide through the changing phenomena of the <term>Bardo<gloss>The "intermediate" or transitional state between death and the next rebirth.</gloss></term> realm, that state of existence which continues for 49 days after death until the next incarnation. If we disregard for the moment the supra-temporality of the soul—which the East accepts as a self-evident fact—we, as readers of the <term>Bardo Thödol</term>, shall be able to put ourselves without difficulty in the position of the dead man, and shall consider attentively the teaching set forth in the opening section, which is outlined in the quotation above. At this point, the following words are spoken, not presumptuously, but in a courteous manner:—

> ‘O nobly-born (so and so), listen. Now thou art experiencing the Radiance of the Clear Light of Pure Reality. Recognize it. O nobly-born, thy present intellect, in real nature void, not

# PSYCHOLOGICAL COMMENTARY

formed into anything as regards characteristics or colour, naturally void, is the very Reality, the <term>All-Good<gloss>A translation of the name of the primordial Buddha, Samantabhadra (Tibetan: Kuntuzangpo), representing the fundamental nature of reality.</gloss></term>.

> ' Thine own intellect, which is now voidness, yet not to be regarded as of the voidness of nothingness, but as being the intellect itself, unobstructed, shining, thrilling, and blissful, is the very consciousness, the All-good Buddha.'

This realization is the <term>Dharma-Kāya<gloss>The 'Truth Body' or 'Reality Body,' the highest of the three bodies (trikaya) of a Buddha, representing the unmanifested, absolute essence of the universe.</gloss></term> state of perfect enlightenment; or, as we should express it in our own language, the creative ground of all metaphysical assertion is consciousness, the invisible, intangible manifestation of the soul. The 'Voidness' is the state transcendent over all assertion and all predication. The fulness of its discriminative manifestations still lies latent in the soul.

The text continues:—
> ' Thine own consciousness, shining, void, and inseparable from the Great Body of Radiance, hath no birth, nor death, and is the Immutable Light—Buddha <term>Amitābha<gloss>The Buddha of 'Infinite Light,' one of the five principal Wisdom Buddhas, associated with the western direction and the transformation of desire into discriminating wisdom.</gloss></term>.'

The soul [or, as here, one's own consciousness] is assuredly not small, but the radiant Godhead itself. The West finds this statement either very dangerous, if not downright blasphemous, or else accepts it unthinkingly and then suffers from a theosophical inflation. Somehow we always have a wrong attitude to these things. But if we can master ourselves far enough to refrain from our chief error of always wanting to do something with things and put them to practical use, we may perhaps succeed in learning an important lesson from these teachings, or at least in appreciating the greatness of the <term>Bardo Thödol<gloss>Literally 'Liberation Through Hearing in the Intermediate State,' the Tibetan funerary text describing the experiences of the soul after death.</gloss></term>, which vouchsafes to the dead man the ultimate and highest truth, that even the gods are the radiance and reflection of our own souls. No sun is thereby eclipsed for the Oriental as it would be for the Christian, who would feel robbed of his God; on the contrary, his soul is the light of the Godhead, and the Godhead is the soul. The East can sustain this paradox better than the unfortunate <note>Angelus Silesius (1624–1677) was a German mystic and poet known for his bold, paradoxical verses about the intimate union between God and the human soul.</note> *Angelus Silesius*, who even today would be psychologically far in advance of his time.

It is highly sensible of the *Bardo Thödol* to make clear to the dead man the primacy of the soul, for that is the one thing which

# PSYCHOLOGICAL COMMENTARY

life does not make clear to us. We are so hemmed in by things which jostle and oppress that we never get a chance, in the midst of all these 'given' things, to wonder by whom they are 'given'. It is from this world of 'given' things that the dead man liberates himself; and the purpose of the instruction is to help him towards this liberation. We, if we put ourselves in his place, shall derive no lesser reward from it, since we learn from the very first paragraphs that the 'giver' of all 'given' things dwells within us. This is a truth which in the face of all evidence, in the greatest things as in the smallest, is never known, although it is often so very necessary, indeed vital, for us to know it. Such knowledge, to be sure, is suitable only for contemplatives who are minded to understand the purpose of existence, for those who are <term>Gnostics<gloss>Followers of ancient religious movements who sought direct, mystical knowledge of the divine as a means of salvation.</gloss></term> by temperament and therefore believe in a saviour who, like the saviour of the <term>Mandaeans<gloss>A Gnostic sect from the Middle East that emphasizes the soul's journey back to a world of light.</gloss></term>, calls himself '**knowledge of life**' <note>original: "manda d'hajie"</note>.

Perhaps it is not granted to many of us to see the world as something 'given'. A great reversal of standpoint, calling for much sacrifice, is needed before we can see the world as 'given' by the very nature of the soul. It is so much more straight-forward, more dramatic, impressive, and therefore more convincing, to see that all the things happen to me than to observe how I make them happen. Indeed, the animal nature of man makes him resist seeing himself as the maker of his circumstances. That is why attempts of this kind were always the object of secret initiations, culminating as a rule in a figurative death which symbolized the total character of this reversal. And, in point of fact, the instruction given in the <term>Bardo Thödol<gloss>The Tibetan Book of the Dead; literally, "Liberation Through Hearing in the Intermediate State."</gloss></term> serves to recall to the dead man the experiences of his initiation and the teachings of his <term>guru<gloss>A spiritual teacher or guide who imparts sacred knowledge.</gloss></term>, for the instruction is, at bottom, nothing less than an initiation of the dead into the *Bardo* life, just as the initiation of the living was a preparation for the Beyond. Such was the case, at least, with all the <term>mystery cults<gloss>Secret religious systems of the ancient world that used rituals to reveal sacred truths to initiates.</gloss></term> in ancient civilizations from the time of the <term>Egyptian<gloss>Referring to the rituals concerning Osiris and the journey of the soul after death.</gloss></term> and <term>Eleusinian mysteries<gloss>Ancient Greek initiation ceremonies for the cult of Demeter, focused on the cycle of life and the afterlife.</gloss></term>.

In the initiation of the living, however, this 'Beyond' is not a world beyond death, but a reversal of the mind's intentions and outlook, a psychological 'Beyond' or, in Christian terms, a 'redemption' from the trammels of the world and of sin. Redemption is a separation and deliverance from an earlier condition of darkness and unconsciousness, and leads to a condition

# PSYCHOLOGICAL COMMENTARY

...of illumination and releasedness, to victory and transcendence over everything 'given'.

Thus far the *Bardo Thödol* <note>The Tibetan Book of the Dead</note> is, as Dr. Evans-Wentz also feels, an initiation process whose purpose it is to restore to the soul the divinity it lost at birth. Now it is a characteristic of Oriental religious literature that the teaching invariably begins with the most important item, with the ultimate and highest principles which, with us, would come last—as for instance in the writings of Apuleius, where the character Lucius is worshipped as <term>Helios<gloss>The Greek personification of the Sun, representing the highest state of enlightenment.</gloss></term> only right at the end. Accordingly, in the *Bardo Thödol*, the initiation is a series of diminishing climaxes ending with rebirth in the womb. 

The only 'initiation process' that is still alive and practised today in the West is the analysis of the unconscious as used by doctors for therapeutic purposes. This penetration into the ground-layers of consciousness is a kind of rational <term>maieutics<gloss>From the Greek word for midwifery; a method of bringing out ideas or latent knowledge into the light of consciousness.</gloss></term> in the Socratic sense, a bringing forth of psychic contents that are still germinal, <term>subliminal<gloss>Existing or operating below the threshold of consciousness.</gloss></term>, and as yet unborn. Originally, this therapy took the form of Freudian psychoanalysis and was mainly concerned with sexual fantasies. This is the realm that corresponds to the last and lowest region of the *Bardo*, known as the *Sidpa Bardo* <note>The stage of the "Bardo of Seeking Rebirth".</note>, where the dead man, unable to profit by the teachings of the *Chikhai* <note>The "Bardo of the Moments of Death".</note> and *Chönyid Bardo* <note>The "Bardo of Experiencing Reality".</note>, begins to fall a prey to sexual fantasies and is attracted by the vision of mating couples. Eventually he is caught by a womb and born into the earthly world again. 

Meanwhile, as one might expect, the <term>Oedipus complex<gloss>A psychological theory suggesting children experience unconscious desire for the opposite-sex parent and hostility toward the same-sex parent.</gloss></term> starts functioning. If his <term>karma<gloss>The law of cause and effect resulting from one's past actions, which determines the nature of one's future rebirth.</gloss></term> destines him to be reborn as a man, he will fall in love with his mother-to-be and will find his father hateful and disgusting. Conversely, the future daughter will be highly attracted by her father-to-be and repelled by her mother. The European passes through this specifically Freudian domain when his unconscious contents are brought to light under analysis, but he goes in the reverse direction. He journeys back through the world of infantile-sexual fantasy to the womb. It has even been suggested in psychoanalytical circles that the ultimate trauma <note>original: "trauma par excellence"</note> is the birth-experience itself—nay more, psychoanalysts even claim to have probed back to memories of <term>intra-uterine<gloss>Occurring or existing within the uterus or womb.</gloss></term> origin. Here Western reason reaches its limit, unfortunately. I say 'unfortunately', because one rather wishes that Freudian psychoanalysis could have happily pursued these so-

called intra-uterine <note>Relating to the time spent inside the womb before birth.</note> experiences still further back; had it succeeded in this bold undertaking, it would surely have come out beyond the **Bardo of Becoming** <note>original: "Sidpa Bardo"; the final stage of the afterlife where the soul is drawn back into a physical body.</note> and penetrated from behind into the lower reaches of the **Bardo of Experiencing Reality** <note>original: "Chönyid Bardo"; the middle stage where the deceased encounters the projections of their own mind.</note>. It is true that with the equipment of our existing biological ideas such a venture would not have been crowned with success; it would have needed a wholly different kind of philosophical preparation from that based on current scientific assumptions. But, had the journey back been consistently pursued, it would undoubtedly have led to the postulate of a pre-uterine existence, a true **Bardo** life, if only it had been possible to find at least some trace of an experiencing subject. As it was, the psychoanalysts never got beyond purely conjectural traces of intra-uterine experiences, and even the famous 'birth trauma' <note>A concept introduced by Otto Rank, suggesting that the psychological shock of being born is the primary source of human anxiety.</note> has remained such an obvious truism that it can no longer explain anything, any more than can the hypothesis that life is a disease with a bad prognosis because its outcome is always fatal.

Freudian psychoanalysis, in all essential aspects, never went beyond the experiences of the **Bardo of Becoming** <note>original: "Sidpa Bardo"</note>; that is, it was unable to extricate itself from sexual fantasies and similar 'incompatible' tendencies which cause anxiety and other affective states. Nevertheless, Freud's theory is the first attempt made by the West to investigate, as if from below, from the animal sphere of instinct, the psychic territory that corresponds in **Tantric Buddhism** <note>original: "Tantric Lāmaism"; a tradition of Buddhism that utilizes complex rituals and meditation to achieve enlightenment.</note> to the **Bardo of Becoming** <note>original: "Sidpa Bardo"</note>. A very justifiable fear of metaphysics prevented Freud from penetrating into the sphere of the 'occult'. In addition to this, the **Becoming** <note>original: "Sidpa"</note> state, if we are to accept the psychology of the **Bardo of Becoming** <note>original: "Sidpa Bardo"</note>, is characterized by the fierce wind of **consequence** <note>original: "karma"; the law of cause and effect resulting from one's past actions.</note>, which whirls the dead man along until he comes to the '**womb-door**' <term>womb-door<gloss>The symbolic entrance to a new physical life or reincarnation.</gloss></term>. In other words, the **Becoming** <note>original: "Sidpa"</note> state permits of no going back, because it is sealed off against the **Reality** <note>original: "Chönyid"</note> state by an intense striving downwards, towards the animal sphere of instinct and physical rebirth. That is to say, anyone who penetrates into the unconscious with purely biological assumptions will become stuck in the instinctual sphere and be unable to advance beyond it, for he will be pulled back again and again into physical existence. It is therefore not possible for Freudian theory to reach anything except an essentially negative valuation of the unconscious. It is a 'nothing

...but. At the same time, it must be admitted that this view of the **psyche** <note>The human soul, mind, or spirit as a functional whole.</note> is typically Western, only it is expressed more blatantly, more plainly, and more ruthlessly than others would have dared to express it, though at bottom they think no differently. As to what 'mind' means in this connection, we can only cherish the hope that it will carry conviction. But, as even **Max Scheler** <note>A German philosopher (1874–1928) who argued that the human "spirit" is essentially powerless compared to biological and social drives.</note> noted with regret, the power of this 'mind' is, to say the least of it, doubtful.

I think, then, we can state it as a fact that with the aid of **psychoanalysis** <note>A clinical method for treating psychological issues through the investigation of the unconscious mind.</note> the rationalizing mind of the West has pushed forward into what one might call the neuroticism of the **Bardo of Becoming** <note>original: "Sidpa"; the final stage of the afterlife where the soul is drawn back into a physical body.</note> state, and has there been brought to an inevitable standstill by the uncritical assumption that everything psychological is subjective and personal. Even so, this advance has been a great gain, inasmuch as it has enabled us to take one more step behind our conscious lives. This knowledge also gives us a hint of how we ought to read the **Tibetan Book of the Dead** <note>original: "Bardo Thödol"; literally, "Liberation Through Hearing in the Intermediate State."</note>—that is, backwards. If, with the help of our Western science, we have to some extent succeeded in understanding the psychological character of the **Bardo of Becoming**, our next task is to see if we can make anything of the preceding **Bardo of Experiencing Reality** <note>original: "Chönyid Bardo"; the middle stage of the afterlife where the deceased encounters the projections of their own mind in the form of deities.</note>.

The **state of experiencing reality** <note>original: "Chönyid"</note> is one of **illusion caused by past actions** <note>original: "karmic illusion"; the belief that our current perceptions are shaped by the mental imprints of previous deeds.</note>—that is to say, illusions which result from the psychic residua of previous existences. According to the Eastern view, **the law of cause and effect** <note>original: "karma"</note> implies a sort of psychic theory of heredity based on the hypothesis of **reincarnation** <note>The rebirth of a soul in a new body.</note>, which in the last resort is an hypothesis of the **existence of the soul beyond time** <note>original: "supratemporality of the soul"</note>. Neither our scientific knowledge nor our reason can keep in step with this idea. There are too many if's and but's. Above all, we know desperately little about the possibilities of continued existence of the individual soul after death, so little that we cannot even conceive how anyone could prove anything at all in this respect. Moreover, we know only too well, on **philosophical** <note>original: "epistemological"; relating to the theory of knowledge and how we distinguish justified belief from opinion.</note> grounds, that such a proof would be just as impossible as the proof of God. Hence we may cautiously accept the idea of **the law of past actions** <note>original: "karma"</note> only if we understand it as *psychic heredity* in the very widest sense of the word. Psychic heredity does exist—that is to say, there is inheritance of psychic characteristics such as predisposition to disease, traits of character, special gifts, and so forth. It does no violence to the psychic

He argues that certain psychic structures are not just personal or cultural, but are universal human inheritances.</meta>

# PSYCHOLOGICAL COMMENTARY

...nature of these complex facts if natural science reduces them to what appear to be physical aspects (nuclear structures in cells, and so on). They are essential phenomena of life which express themselves, in the main, psychically, just as there are other inherited characteristics which express themselves, in the main, physiologically, on the physical level. Among these inherited psychic factors there is a special class which is not confined either to family or to race. These are the universal dispositions of the mind, and they are to be understood as analogous to Plato’s **forms** <note>original: "eidola"; Greek for "images" or "ideal forms." In Plato's philosophy, these are the perfect, eternal patterns that underlie the physical world.</note>, in accordance with which the mind organizes its contents. One could also describe these forms as **categories** <term>categories<gloss>Fundamental concepts or mental frameworks—such as time, space, and causality—that allow the mind to organize and make sense of raw sensory information.</gloss></term> analogous to the logical categories which are always and everywhere present as the basic postulates of reason. Only, in the case of our ‘forms’, we are not dealing with categories of reason but with categories of the **imagination**. As the products of imagination are always in essence visual, their forms must, from the outset, have the character of images and moreover of *typical* images, which is why, following **St. Augustine** <note>A 4th-century philosopher and theologian who influenced Western thought by describing "primordial ideas" existing within the Divine mind.</note>, I call them ‘**archetypes**’ <note>Universal, inherited patterns of thought and imagery that are present in every human being's unconscious mind.</note>. Comparative religion and mythology are rich mines of archetypes, and so is the psychology of dreams and psychoses. 

The astonishing parallelism between these images and the ideas they serve to express has frequently given rise to the wildest migration theories <note>Historical theories suggesting that similar myths or symbols appeared in different cultures only because people traveled and physically shared them.</note>, although it would have been far more natural to think of the remarkable similarity of the human **psyche** <note>The human soul or mind as a functional whole, including both conscious and unconscious parts.</note> at all times and in all places. Archetypal fantasy-forms are, in fact, reproduced spontaneously anytime and anywhere, without there being any conceivable trace of direct transmission. The original structural components of the psyche are of no less surprising a uniformity than are those of the visible body. The archetypes are, so to speak, organs of the pre-rational psyche. They are eternally inherited forms and ideas which have at first no specific content. Their specific content only appears in the course of the individual’s life, when personal experience is taken up in precisely these forms. If the archetypes were not pre-existent in identical form everywhere, how could one explain the fact, postulated at almost every turn by the **Tibetan Book of the Dead** <note>original: "Bardo Thödol."</note>, that the dead do not know that they are dead, and that this assertion is to be met with just as often in the dreary, half-baked literature of European and American **Spiritualism** <note>A 19th and 20th-century movement based on the belief that the spirits of the dead can communicate with the living through mediums.</note>? Although we find the same...

<meta>This page continues Carl Jung’s psychological analysis of the "Bardo Thödol." Having previously discussed the universal dispositions of the mind—which he likens to Plato's "forms"—Jung now argues that these structures, or "archetypes," are the reason why similar spiritual ideas, such as those found in Swedenborg’s writings and the Tibetan Book of the Dead, appear across different cultures independently.</meta>

# PSYCHOLOGICAL COMMENTARY

assertion in **Swedenborg** <note>Emanuel Swedenborg (1688–1772) was a Swedish scientist and mystic who claimed to have talked with spirits and described the afterlife in great detail.</note>, knowledge of his writings can hardly be sufficiently widespread for this little bit of information to have been picked up by every small-town 'medium'. And a connection between Swedenborg and the **Liberation Through Hearing in the Intermediate State** <note>original: "Bardo Thödol"; the Tibetan Book of the Dead.</note> is completely unthinkable. It is a primordial, universal idea that the dead simply continue their earthly existence and do not know that they are disembodied spirits—an archetypal idea which enters into immediate, visible manifestation whenever anyone sees a ghost. It is significant, too, that ghosts all over the world have certain features in common. I am naturally aware of the unverifiable spiritualistic hypothesis, though I have no wish to make it my own. I must content myself with the hypothesis of an omnipresent, but differentiated, psychic structure which is inherited and which necessarily gives a certain form and direction to all experience. For, just as the organs of the body are not mere lumps of indifferent, passive matter, but are dynamic, functional complexes which assert themselves with imperious urgency, so also the **archetypes** <term>archetypes<gloss>Universal, inherited patterns of thought or symbolic imagery that Jung believed were present in the "collective unconscious" of all humans.</gloss></term>, as organs of the psyche, are dynamic, instinctual complexes which determine psychic life to an extraordinary degree. That is why I also call them *dominants* of the unconscious. The layer of unconscious psyche which is made up of these universal dynamic forms I have termed the *collective unconscious*.

So far as I know, there is no inheritance of individual prenatal, or pre-uterine, memories, but there are undoubtedly inherited archetypes which are, however, devoid of content, because, to begin with, they contain no personal experiences. They only emerge into consciousness when personal experiences have rendered them visible. As we have seen, **Rebirth** <note>original: "Sidpa"</note> psychology consists in wanting to live and to be born. (The **Intermediate State of Rebirth** <note>original: "Sidpa Bardo"</note> is the 'Bardo of Seeking Rebirth'.) Such a state, therefore, precludes any experience of transubjective psychic realities, unless the individual refuses categorically to be born back again into the world of consciousness. According to the teachings of the **Liberation Through Hearing**, it is still possible for him, in each of the **Intermediate** <note>original: "Bardo"</note> states, to reach the **Truth-Body of the Buddha** <term>Truth-Body<gloss>original: "Dharma-Kāya"; the ultimate, absolute nature of reality or enlightenment, beyond form and physical existence.</gloss></term> by transcending the four-faced **Cosmic Mountain** <note>original: "Mount Meru"; the sacred mountain at the center of the universe in Buddhist and Hindu cosmology.</note>, provided that he does not yield to his desire to follow the 'dim lights' <note>In the Tibetan tradition, "dim lights" represent the paths leading back to the cycle of suffering and rebirth in the six realms, as opposed to the "clear light" of liberation.</note>. This is as much as to say that the dead man must desperately resist the dictates of reason, as we understand it,

<summary>Jung explores the concept of the "collective unconscious," arguing that universal spiritual ideas like those in the Bardo Thödol are inherited "archetypes" rather than learned information. He explains that the drive for rebirth (Sidpa) can only be overcome by resisting worldly desires to achieve the ultimate reality of the Dharma-Kāya.</summary>

<keywords>Carl Jung, Swedenborg, Bardo Thödol, archetypes, collective unconscious, Sidpa Bardo, Dharma-Kāya, Mount Meru, rebirth, psychic structure</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Carl Jung’s psychological analysis, moving from the concept of archetypes as inherited structures to the practical—and often perilous—process of surrendering the ego to the unconscious. Jung compares the Tibetan descriptions of the afterlife to modern psychological states like "psychosis" and warns against the Western tendency to underestimate the power of these spiritual practices.</meta>

->xlvi<-

# PSYCHOLOGICAL COMMENTARY

...and give up the supremacy of **egohood** <term>egohood<gloss>The state of identifying oneself primarily through the conscious "I" or ego, which Jung argues must be transcended to reach deeper levels of the mind.</gloss></term>, regarded by reason as sacrosanct. What this means in practice is complete capitulation to the objective powers of the **psyche** <term>psyche<gloss>The totality of the human mind, including both conscious and unconscious elements.</gloss></term>, with all that this entails; a kind of symbolical death, corresponding to the **Judgement of the Dead** in the **Intermediate State of Rebirth** <note>original: "Sidpa Bardo"</note>. It means the end of all conscious, rational, morally responsible conduct of life, and a voluntary surrender to what the **Liberation Through Hearing in the Intermediate State** <note>original: "Bardo Thödol," commonly known in the West as the Tibetan Book of the Dead.</note> calls '**karmic illusion**'. 

Karmic illusion springs from belief in a visionary world of an extremely irrational nature, which neither accords with nor derives from our rational judgements but is the exclusive product of uninhibited imagination. It is sheer dream or '**fantasy**', and every well-meaning person will instantly caution us against it; nor indeed can one see at first sight what is the difference between fantasies of this kind and the **phantasmagoria** <note>A shifting series of deceptive or imaginary figures, like those seen in a dream or a fever.</note> of a lunatic. Very often only a slight **lowering of the mental level** <note>original: "abaissement du niveau mental," a psychological term for a loss of mental energy that allows the unconscious to flood into the conscious mind.</note> is needed to unleash this world of illusion. 

The terror and darkness of this moment has its equivalent in the experiences described in the opening sections of the **Intermediate State of Rebirth** <note>original: "Sidpa Bardo"</note>. But the contents of this **Intermediate State** also reveal the **archetypes**, the karmic images which appear first in their terrifying form. The **State of Reality** <note>original: "Chönyid"</note> is equivalent to a deliberately induced **psychosis** <term>psychosis<gloss>A severe mental condition where the mind loses touch with external reality, often resulting in hallucinations or delusions.</gloss></term>.

One often hears and reads about the dangers of **yoga** <term>yoga<gloss>From the Sanskrit for "union"; a group of ancient Indian physical, mental, and spiritual practices.</gloss></term>, particularly of the ill-reputed **Coiled Power** yoga <note>original: "Kundalinī yoga," a practice focused on awakening a potent energy at the base of the spine.</note>. The deliberately induced psychotic state, which in certain unstable individuals might easily lead to a real psychosis, is a danger that needs to be taken very seriously indeed. These things really are dangerous and ought not to be meddled with in our typically Western way. It is a meddling with fate, which strikes at the very roots of human existence and can let loose a flood of sufferings of which no sane person ever dreamed. These sufferings correspond to the hellish torments of the **State of Reality** <note>original: "Chönyid"</note>, described in the text as follows:—

> 'Then the **Lord of Death** will place round thy neck a rope and drag thee along; he will cut off thy head, tear out thy heart, pull out thy intestines, lick up thy brain, drink thy blood, eat thy flesh, and gnaw thy bones; but thou wilt be incapable of dying. Even when thy body is hacked to pieces, it will revive again. The repeated hacking will cause intense pain and torture.'

<summary>Jung discusses the psychological risks of spiritual practice, comparing the Tibetan "Intermediate States" to a controlled surrender of the ego that resembles psychosis. He specifically warns Westerners that practices like Kundalinī yoga can trigger terrifying unconscious imagery and profound suffering if approached without proper understanding.</summary>

<keywords>egohood, psyche, Sidpa Bardo, Bardo Thödol, karmic illusion, abaissement du niveau mental, archetypes, Chönyid, yoga, Kundalini yoga, psychosis, Lord of Death</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Carl Jung’s psychological analysis of the Tibetan Book of the Dead. He is explaining that the "tortures" described in the afterlife are actually metaphors for the psychological process of the mind breaking apart when it confronts the unconscious without the protection of the ego.</meta>

->xlvii<-

# PSYCHOLOGICAL COMMENTARY

These tortures aptly describe the real nature of the danger: it is a disintegration of the wholeness of the **Intermediate State** <note>original: "Bardo"</note> body, which is a kind of **subtle body** <term>subtle body<gloss>A "spiritual" body that acts as the vehicle for the soul or mind, bridging the gap between the physical and the purely mental.</gloss></term> constituting the visible envelope of the psychic self in the after-death state. The psychological equivalent of this dismemberment is **psychic dissociation** <term>psychic dissociation<gloss>A mental process where a person's thoughts, memories, or sense of identity become disconnected.</gloss></term>. In its harmful form it would be **schizophrenia** (split mind). This most common of all mental illnesses consists essentially in a marked **lowering of the mental level** <note>original: "abaissement du niveau mental"</note> which abolishes the normal checks imposed by the conscious mind and thus gives unlimited scope to the play of the unconscious **dominants** <term>dominants<gloss>Jung’s term for archetypes; powerful, universal patterns in the unconscious that can overwhelm the personality.</gloss></term>.

The transition, then, from the **State of Becoming** <note>original: "Sidpa"</note> state to the **State of Reality** <note>original: "Chönyid"</note> state is a dangerous reversal of the aims and intentions of the conscious mind. It is a sacrifice of the **ego's** <term>ego<gloss>The conscious part of the mind that represents our sense of "I" and personal identity.</gloss></term> stability and a surrender to the extreme uncertainty of what must seem like a chaotic riot of phantasmal forms. When Freud coined the phrase that the ego was "the true seat of anxiety," he was giving voice to a very true and profound intuition. Fear of self-sacrifice lurks deep in every ego, and this fear is often only the precariously controlled demand of the unconscious forces to burst out in full strength. No one who strives for **individuation** <term>individuation<gloss>The lifelong process of self-development where a person integrates different parts of their psyche to become a whole, unique individual.</gloss></term> is spared this dangerous passage, for that which is feared also belongs to the wholeness of the self—the sub-human, or supra-human, world of psychic **dominants** from which the ego originally emancipated itself with enormous effort, and then only partially, for the sake of a more or less illusory freedom. This liberation is certainly a very necessary and very heroic undertaking, but it represents nothing final: it is merely the creation of a **subject** <note>The "I" or observer.</note>, who, in order to find fulfilment, has still to be confronted by an **object** <note>The "other" or the world.</note>. This, at first sight, would appear to be the world, which is swelled out with **projections** <term>projections<gloss>A psychological defense mechanism where one attributes their own unconscious impulses or traits to other people or the outside world.</gloss></term> for that very purpose. Here we seek and find our difficulties, here we seek and find our enemy, here we seek and find what is dear and precious to us; and it is comforting to know that all evil and all good is to be found out there, in the visible object, where it can be conquered, punished, destroyed or enjoyed. But nature herself does not allow this paradisal state of innocence to continue for ever. There are, and always have been, those who cannot help but see that the world and its experiences are in the nature of a symbol, and that it really reflects something that lies hidden in the subject himself, in his own **beyond-the-subjective** <note>original: "transubjective"</note>

<summary>Jung compares the Tibetan "Bardo" visions to psychological dissociation and schizophrenia, warning that the journey toward self-wholeness (individuation) requires a dangerous surrender of the ego. He explains that we often project our internal struggles onto the external world to avoid facing the deep-seated forces of the unconscious.</summary>

reality. It is from this profound intuition, according to **Tibetan Buddhist** <note>original: "lāmaist"</note> doctrine, that the **State of Reality** <note>original: "Chōnyid"</note> state derives its true meaning, which is why the **Intermediate State of Reality** <note>original: "Chōnyid Bardo"</note> is entitled 'The **Intermediate State** <note>original: "Bardo"</note> of the Experiencing of Reality'.

The reality experienced in the **State of Reality** <note>original: "Chōnyid"</note> state is, as the last section of the corresponding **Intermediate State** <note>original: "Bardo"</note> teaches, the reality of thought. The 'thought-forms' appear as realities, fantasy takes on real form, and the terrifying dream evoked by **karma** <term>karma<gloss>The law of cause and effect where a person's intent and actions influence their future; here, Jung sees it as the momentum of one's own psychological history.</gloss></term> and played out by the unconscious **archetypes** <note>original: "dominants"</note> begins. The first to appear (if we read the text backwards) is the all-destroying God of Death, the epitome of all terrors; he is followed by the 28 'power-holding' and sinister goddesses and the 58 'blood-drinking' goddesses. In spite of their daemonic aspect, which appears as a confusing chaos of terrifying attributes and monstrosities, a certain order is already discernible. We find that there are companies of gods and goddesses who are arranged according to the four directions and are distinguished by typical mystic colours. It gradually becomes clearer that all these deities are organized into **sacred circles** <term>maṇḍalas<gloss>Geometric designs representing the universe or the self, used in meditation to bring order to the psyche.</gloss></term>, or circles, containing a cross of the four colours. The colours are co-ordinated with the four aspects of wisdom:

(1) White = the light-path of the **mirror-like wisdom**;
(2) Yellow = the light-path of the **wisdom of equality**;
(3) Red = the light-path of the **discriminative wisdom**;
(4) Green = the light-path of the **all-performing wisdom**.

On a higher level of insight, the dead man knows that the real thought-forms all emanate from himself, and that the four light-paths of wisdom which appear before him are the radiations of his own psychic faculties. This takes us straight to the psychology of the **Tibetan Buddhist** <note>original: "lāmaistic"</note> **sacred circle** <note>original: "maṇḍala"</note>, which I have already discussed in the book I brought out with the late Richard Wilhelm, *The Secret of the Golden Flower*.

Continuing our ascent backwards through the region of the **Intermediate State of Reality** <note>original: "Chōnyid Bardo"</note>, we come finally to the vision of the Four Great Ones: the green **Amogha-Siddhi** <note>The Buddha of "Infallible Success."</note>, the red **Amitābha** <note>The Buddha of "Infinite Light."</note>, the yellow **Ratna-Sambhava** <note>The Buddha of "Jewel-Born" richness.</note>, and the white **Vajra-Sattva** <note>The Buddha of "Diamond-Being" or purity.</note>. The ascent ends with the effulgent blue light of the **Truth-Dimension** <term>Dharma-Dhātu<gloss>The ultimate realm or essence of reality, representing the mind in its purest state.</gloss></term>, the Buddha-body, which glows in the midst of the **sacred circle** <note>original: "maṇḍala"</note> from the heart of **Vairochana** <note>The "Illuminator," the central Buddha who represents the integration of all wisdom.</note>.

With this final vision the **karmic** <note>original: "karmic"</note> illusions cease; consciousness,

# PSYCHOLOGICAL COMMENTARY

weaned away from all form and from all attachment to objects, returns to the timeless, inchoate state of the **Body of Reality** <note>original: "Dharma-Kāya"; in Buddhist thought, this is the ultimate, unmanifested nature of the enlightened mind</note>. Thus (reading backwards) the **Moment of Death** <note>original: "Chikhai"</note> state, which appeared at the moment of death, is reached.

I think these few hints will suffice to give the attentive reader some idea of the psychology of the **Liberation through Hearing in the Intermediate State** <note>original: "Bardo Thödol"</note>. The book describes a way of initiation in reverse, which, unlike the eschatological <note>concerning the final destiny of the soul and humankind</note> expectations of Christianity, prepares the soul for a descent into physical being. The thoroughly intellectualistic and rationalistic worldly-mindedness of the European makes it advisable for us to reverse the sequence of the **Tibetan Book of the Dead** <note>original: "Bardo Thödol"</note> and to regard it as an account of Eastern initiation experiences, though one is perfectly free, if one chooses, to substitute Christian symbols for the gods of the **Intermediate State of Reality** <note>original: "Chönyid Bardo"</note>. At any rate, the sequence of events as I have described it offers a close parallel to the phenomenology of the European unconscious when it is undergoing an 'initiation process', that is to say, when it is being analyzed. The transformation of the unconscious that occurs under analysis makes it the natural analogue of the religious initiation ceremonies, which do, however, differ in principle from the natural process in that they forestall the natural course of development and substitute for the spontaneous production of symbols a deliberately selected set of symbols prescribed by tradition. We can see this in the **Spiritual Exercises** <note>original: "Exercitia"</note> of Ignatius Loyola, or in the **meditative practice** <note>original: "yoga"</note> meditations of the Buddhists and **Tantric practitioners** <note>original: "Tantrists"</note>.

The reversal of the order of the chapters, which I have suggested here as an aid to understanding, in no way accords with the original intention of the **Tibetan Book of the Dead** <note>original: "Bardo Thödol"</note>. Nor is the psychological use we make of it anything but a secondary intention, though one that is possibly sanctioned by **Tibetan Buddhist** <note>original: "lāmaist"</note> custom. The real purpose of this singular book is the attempt, which must seem very strange to the educated European of the twentieth century, to enlighten the dead on their journey through the regions of the **Intermediate State** <note>original: "Bardo"</note>. The Catholic Church is the only place in the world of the white man where any provision is made for the souls of the departed. Inside the Protestant camp, with its world-affirming optimism, we only find a few mediumistic 'rescue circles', whose main concern is to make the dead aware of the fact that they are dead. But, generally speaking, we

He contrasts the Eastern focus on guiding the dead with the modern Western, particularly Protestant, neglect of the soul's journey after death.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Carl Jung’s psychological analysis, moving from the philosophical mechanics of the "Body of Reality" to a comparison between Western and Eastern approaches to death. Jung argues that while the West has rationalized away the spiritual needs of the dead, the Tibetan tradition provides a sophisticated psychological map for the transition beyond life.</meta>

# PSYCHOLOGICAL COMMENTARY

We have nothing in the West that is in any way comparable to the **Liberation through Hearing in the Intermediate State** <note>original: "Bardo Thödol"; popularly known as the Tibetan Book of the Dead</note>, except for certain secret writings which are inaccessible to the wider public and to the ordinary scientist. According to tradition, the **Liberation through Hearing** <note>original: "Bardo Thödol"</note>, too, seems to have been included among the 'hidden' <term>terma (sacred treasures hidden by past masters to be discovered at an auspicious time)</term> books, as Dr. Evans-Wentz <note>the scholar who first brought this text to the English-speaking world</note> makes clear in his Introduction. As such, it forms a special chapter in the magical 'care of the soul' <note>original: "Seelsorge"; the practice of providing spiritual or psychological guidance</note> which extends even beyond death.

This cult of the dead is rationally based on the belief in the soul existing beyond time, but its irrational basis is to be found in the psychological need of the living to do something for the departed. This is an elementary need which forces itself upon even the most 'enlightened' individuals <note>those who consider themselves purely rational or scientific</note> when faced by the death of relatives and friends. That is why, enlightenment or no enlightenment, we still have all manner of ceremonies for the dead. If Vladimir Lenin <note>the Soviet leader whose body was preserved in a mausoleum</note> had to submit to being embalmed and put on show in a sumptuous mausoleum like an Egyptian pharaoh, we may be quite sure it was not because his followers believed in the resurrection of the body.

Apart, however, from the Masses <note>the liturgical service for the dead in the Catholic tradition</note> said for the soul in the Catholic Church, the provisions we make for the dead are rudimentary and on the lowest level—not because we cannot convince ourselves of the soul's immortality, but because we have rationalized the above-mentioned psychological need out of existence. We behave as if we did not have this need, and because we cannot believe in a life after death we prefer to do nothing about it. Simpler-minded people follow their own feelings, and, as in Italy, build themselves funeral monuments of gruesome beauty. The Catholic Masses for the soul are on a level considerably above this, because they are expressly intended for the psychic welfare of the deceased and are not a mere gratification of tearful sentiments. 

But the highest application of spiritual effort on behalf of the departed is surely to be found in the instructions of the **Liberation through Hearing** <note>original: "Bardo Thödol"</note>. They are so detailed and thoroughly adapted to the apparent changes in the dead man's condition that every serious-minded reader must ask himself whether these wise old <term>lāmas (Tibetan Buddhist spiritual masters)</term> might not, after all, have caught a glimpse of a higher dimension and pulled back the veil from the greatest of life's secrets.

If the truth is always doomed to be a disappointment, one almost feels tempted to concede at least that much reality to the—

# PSYCHOLOGICAL COMMENTARY

vision of life in the **Intermediate State** <note>original: "Bardo"</note>. At any rate, it is unexpectedly original, if nothing else, to find the state after death—of which our religious imagination has formed the most grandiose conceptions—painted in lurid colors as a terrifying dream-state of a progressively decaying character. The supreme vision comes not at the end of the **Intermediate State**, but right at the beginning, in the moment of death; what happens afterward is an ever-deepening descent into illusion and darkness, down to the ultimate degradation of a new physical birth. The spiritual climax is reached at the moment when life ends. Human life, therefore, is the vehicle of the highest perfection it is possible to attain; it alone generates the **action and intent** <term>karma (the law of cause and effect where a person's intent and actions influence their future)</term> that makes it possible for the dead man to abide in the perpetual light of the **Void** <note>original: "Sunyata"; the ultimate nature of reality, which is empty of independent existence</note> without clinging to any object, and thus to rest on the hub of the wheel of rebirth, freed from all illusion of birth and decay. Life in the **Intermediate State** <note>original: "Bardo"</note> brings no eternal rewards or punishments, but merely a descent into a new life which shall bear the individual nearer to his final goal. But this **final destiny** <term>eschatological (relating to the ultimate fate of the soul or the world)</term> goal is what he himself brings to birth as the last and highest fruit of the labors and aspirations of earthly existence. This view is not only lofty, it is manly and heroic.

The decaying character of life in the **Intermediate State** is supported by the spiritualistic literature of the West, which again and again gives one a sickening impression of the utter pointlessness and banality of communications from the "spirit world." The scientific mind does not hesitate to explain these reports as outpourings from the **unconscious** <note>the part of the mind containing feelings and memories of which the individual is not aware</note> of the "mediums" and of those taking part in the session, and even to extend this explanation to the description of the Hereafter given in the **Liberation through Hearing in the Intermediate State** <note>original: "Bardo Thödol"; popularly known as The Tibetan Book of the Dead</note>. And it is an undeniable fact that the whole book is created out of the **archetypal** <term>archetypal (universal patterns or symbols residing in the collective human psyche)</term> contents of the unconscious. Behind these there lie—and in this our Western reason is quite right—no physical or metaphysical realities, but "merely" the reality of **psychic facts** <note>internal mental experiences that are treated as real because they are experienced</note>, the data of psychic experience. Now whether a thing is "given" internally or externally, the fact remains that it *is*. The **Liberation through Hearing** <note>original: "Bardo Thödol"</note> says no more than this, for its five **Meditation Buddhas** <term>Dhyāni Buddhas (five representations of the qualities of the Buddha used in meditation)</term> are themselves no more than psychic data. That is just what the dead man has to recognize, if it has not already become clear to him during life that his own psychic self and the giver of all data are one and the same. The world of gods and spirits is truly

<summary>Jung argues that the Tibetan Book of the Dead presents a "manly and heroic" view where the highest spiritual peak occurs at death, followed by a descent into psychic illusions. He compares these visions to modern psychological concepts, suggesting they are not external realities but projections of the human unconscious.</summary>

"nothing but" the **collective unconscious** <term>collective unconscious (a concept in Jungian psychology referring to the part of the mind containing memories and impulses shared by all human beings)</term> inside me. To turn this sentence around so that it reads: The **collective unconscious** is the world of gods and spirits outside me, no intellectual acrobatics are needed, but a whole human lifetime, perhaps even many lifetimes of increasing *completeness*. Notice that I do not say "of increasing perfection," because those who are "perfect" make another kind of discovery altogether. <note>Jung often contrasted 'completeness'—the integration of all parts of the self—with 'perfection,' which he viewed as an impossible and often harmful moral ideal.</note>

->* * *<-

The **Great Liberation Through Hearing in the Intermediate State** <note>original: "Bardo Thödol"</note> began by being a "closed" book, and so it has remained, no matter what kind of commentaries may be written upon it. For it is a book that will only open itself to spiritual understanding, and this is a capacity which no man is born with, but which he can only acquire through special training and special experience. It is good that such to all intents and purposes "useless" books exist. They are meant for those "queer folk" who no longer set much store by the uses, aims, and meaning of present-day "civilization." <note>Jung uses 'useless' ironically here, targeting a modern society that values only material or economic utility over spiritual depth.</note>

<summary>Jung concludes that the "world of gods" is identical to the collective unconscious and argues that the Bardo Thödol remains "closed" to those without direct spiritual experience. He critiques modern civilization's focus on utility over the "completeness" of the soul.</summary>

<meta>This page transitions from the psychological commentary provided by Carl Jung to the formal Introductory Foreword by Lāma Anagarika Govinda. While Jung analyzed the text through the lens of the human psyche and the collective unconscious, Govinda begins to ground the work in the traditional Tibetan perspective on death, rebirth, and the continuity of consciousness.</meta>

# INTRODUCTORY FOREWORD
## By Lāma Anagarika Govinda <note>Lāma Anagarika Govinda (1898–1985) was a German-born scholar and monk who became one of the most influential figures in introducing Tibetan Buddhism to the West.</note>

It may be argued that nobody can talk about death with authority who has not died; and since nobody, apparently, has ever returned from death, how can anybody know what death is, or what happens after it?

The Tibetan will answer: ‘There is not *one* person, indeed, not *one* living being, that has *not* returned from death. In fact, we all have died many deaths, before we came into this incarnation. <note>In this context, "incarnation" refers to the Buddhist belief in the cycle of rebirth, where a being is born into a new body after death.</note> And what we call birth is merely the reverse side of death, like one of the two sides of a coin, or like a door which we call "entrance" from outside and "exit" from inside a room.’

It is much more astonishing that not everybody remembers his or her previous death; and, because of this lack of remembering, most persons do not believe there was a previous death. But, likewise, they do not remember their recent birth—and yet they do not doubt that they were recently born. They forget that active memory is only a small part of our normal consciousness, and that our subconscious memory registers and preserves every past impression and experience which our waking mind fails to recall.

There are those who, in virtue of concentration and other **yogic** practices <term>yogic (referring to the system of Yoga, which involves mental and physical disciplines intended to achieve spiritual insight and control over the mind and body)</term>, are able to bring the subconscious into the realm of discriminative consciousness and, thereby, to draw upon the unrestricted treasury of subconscious memory, wherein are stored the records not only of our past lives but the records of the past of our race, the past of humanity, and of all pre-human forms of life, if not of the very consciousness that makes life possible in this universe.

If, through some trick of nature, the gates of an individual's subconsciousness were suddenly to spring open, the unprepared mind would be overwhelmed and crushed. Therefore, the gates of the subconscious are guarded, by all initiates, and hidden behind the veil of mysteries and symbols.

For this reason, the **Great Liberation Through Hearing in the Intermediate State** <note>original: "Bardo Thödol"</note>, the Tibetan book vouch-

He explains that while most people do not remember their past lives, spiritual practitioners can access these subconscious memories through disciplined meditation.</summary>

# INTRODUCTORY FOREWORD

...ensuring liberation from the intermediate state <note>original: *bardo*</note> between life and re-birth—which state men call death—has been couched in symbolical language. It is a book which is sealed with the seven seals of silence—not because its knowledge should be withheld from the uninitiated, but because its knowledge would be misunderstood, and, therefore, would tend to mislead and harm those who are unfitted to receive it. But the time has come to break the seals of silence; for the human race has come to the juncture where it must decide whether to be content with the subjugation of the material world, or to strive after the conquest of the spiritual world, by subjugating selfish desires and transcending self-imposed limitations.

According to Tibetan tradition, the *Liberation Through Hearing in the Intermediate State* <note>original: *Bardo Thödol*</note> is one of those works of the Lotus-Born Guru <note>original: Padma-Sambhava, the 8th-century Indian master who established Buddhism in Tibet</note> which were secretly hidden in order to preserve them for later generations, and which were to be revealed to the world when the time was ripe. However this may be, it is a fact that during the persecution of Buddhism by King Langdarma <note>The last emperor of the Tibetan Empire, known for his anti-Buddhist policies</note>, at the beginning of the ninth century, A.D., innumerable books of the earliest period of Tibetan Buddhism were concealed under rocks, in caves, and other places, to prevent their destruction. Since all members of the Buddhist Order and their supporters were either killed or driven out of Tibet, most of these buried scriptures remained where they had been hidden. Many of them were recovered during the succeeding centuries and designated **Hidden Treasures** <note>original: *Termas*, a term derived from the Tibetan word *Gter*, pronounced *Ter*</note>, meaning 'Treasure'. Those who discovered these spiritual treasures and propagated their teachings were called **Treasure-Revealers** <note>original: *Tertöns*, from Tibetan *Gter-bston*, pronounced *Tertön*</note>, meaning 'Revealer of Treasure'.

This seems to me a far more reasonable explanation for the tradition of the **Treasure-Revealers**, which, significantly, is held in the oldest Schools of Tibetan Buddhism, like the Ancient School <note>original: Nyingmapa</note> and the Oral Lineage School <note>original: Kargyütpa</note>, than the theory advanced by certain Western critics, that these scriptures had been 'faked' by people who wanted to pass off their own ideas under the guise of ancient revelations. Such critics underestimate the religious sincerity and the deep respect for the sanctity of spiritual tradition which is engrained in every Tibetan, layman and spiritual teacher <note>original: *lāma*</note> alike. To add to or omit from the Sacred Scriptures a single word or letter has ever been looked...

<summary>This page explains that the Bardo Thödol was written in symbolic language and hidden for centuries to protect it from persecution and misuse. It describes the tradition of "Hidden Treasures" (Termas) and their recovery by "Treasure-Revealers" (Tertöns), defending the authenticity of these texts against Western skepticism.</summary>

<keywords>Bardo Thödol, Padma-Sambhava, Langdarma, Termas, Tertöns, Nyingmapa, Kargyütpa, hidden treasures, Tibetan history, spiritual tradition</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Lāma Anagarika Govinda’s foreword, addressing the authenticity of the "Hidden Treasures" (Termas) and the integration of local Tibetan deities into the Buddhist framework by Padma-Sambhava.</meta>

# INTRODUCTORY FOREWORD

...upon by Tibetans as a heinous sin, which even the most impious would fear to commit.

Furthermore, these same critics underestimate the difficulties of forging and issuing such scriptures, for the forging would require a technical and critical knowledge of history and linguistics such as was not only unknown in Tibet, but such as would have required a master-mind for its execution. Had a genius of that sort existed in Tibet, he would have had no need to resort to the subterfuge of forgery, for he could have stood on his own feet, as did many scholarly geniuses who wrote and taught in their own name. Nor is it likely that men who could create and propagate such profound thoughts and lofty ideals as the *Hidden Treasures* <note>original: *Termas*</note> contain would stoop so low as to deceive their fellow-men. And when we consider that the literature in question is not a matter of a few isolated treatises but of about a hundred big volumes (according to tradition 108 volumes), running into tens of thousands of folios, then the theory of wilful deception becomes not only improbable, but absurd.

In considering the influences on the *Liberation Through Hearing in the Intermediate State* <note>original: *Bardo Thödol*</note> of the pre-Buddhistic religion of Tibet, namely that of the Bön-pos <note>The indigenous shamanistic and animistic religion of Tibet that existed before Buddhism</note>, there must be taken into account the fact that all of those *Hidden Treasures* attributed to the Lotus-Born Guru <note>original: Padma-Sambhava</note> declare, in no uncertain terms, their adherence to him, the very personage who opposed and defeated the Bön-pos. These recovered scriptures cannot, therefore, be regarded as propagating Bön ideas.

Even though Padma-Sambhava did adopt into the Buddhist system some of the local Tibetan deities, to serve as guardians of the Faith, in doing so he did not give up one inch of Buddhist ground to the Bön-pos, but acted in perfect conformity with the principles of orthodox Buddhism, wherein, in all Buddhist countries, the deities of the Earth and of space have always been honoured and propitiated, as being protectors of the *Universal Law* <note>original: *Dharma*</note>. Thus, the following ancient Indian <note>original: Pāli</note> verses are still recited, in the course of the regular *ceremonial worship* <note>original: *pūjā*</note>, by the followers of the School of the Elders <note>original: Theravāda Buddhism</note>, in Ceylon, Burma, Siam, Cambodia, and elsewhere:—

> "May the deities and dragon-spirits <term>nāgā: powerful serpent-like beings in Buddhist and Hindu mythology</term> of great power, residing in the sky and on the earth, having shared in this merit, protect the Teachings for a long time." <note>original Pāli: "Ākāsaṭṭhā ca bhummaṭṭhā, devā nāgā mahiddhikā, Puññantam anumoditvā, ciraṃ rakkhantu sāsanam."</note>

These verses may be rendered into English as follows:—

<margin>✓</margin>
> ' May the beings of space and of the Earth,
> Gods and serpent-spirits <note>original: *Devas* and *Nāgas*</note> of great
> power,
> After having shared in the merit [of this ritual worship <note>original: *pūjā*</note>],

Any cultural influence, as between Buddhism and the Bön religion <term>Bönism: The indigenous shamanistic and animistic religion of Tibet that existed before the arrival of Buddhism</term>, was more in the nature of a one-way traffic than a mutual exchange of ideas; for the followers of Bön <note>original: Bön-pos</note>, who had no literature of their own, took over Buddhist concepts and symbols on a vast scale, and thereby created a literature and an iconography which so greatly resemble those of the Buddhists as to be almost indistinguishable to the casual observer.

<margin>✓</margin>
There is also current the wholly arbitrary assertion that it was the Bön influence which encouraged laxity in the observance of Buddhist monastic rules in Tibet and led to a general decline in the standard of Tibetan learning and morality. Whoever has had the opportunity to stay for even a short time in one of the still existing Bön monasteries of Tibet, will have noticed, with surprise, that the rules of celibacy and monastic discipline are stricter there than in most Buddhist monasteries, and that for many of the major scriptures of the Tibetan Buddhist Canon <term>Canon: The official collection of sacred primary texts</term> a parallel can be found in the scriptures of the Bön followers. They have their Perfection of Wisdom Discourses <note>original: *Prajñāpāramitā Sūtras*</note>, their principle of Dependent Origination <note>original: *Pratītyasamutpāda*</note> (represented in a Wheel of Life of thirteen divisions), their esoteric ritual texts <note>original: *Tantras*</note> and sacred chants <note>original: *Mantras*</note>; and their deities more or less correspond to the various Buddhas, enlightened beings <term>Bodhisattva: A person who is able to reach nirvana but delays doing so out of compassion in order to save suffering beings</term>, minor deities <note>original: *Devatas*</note>, and protectors of the teaching <note>original: *Dharmapālas*</note> of Buddhism.

<margin>✓</margin>
It may seem paradoxical, but it is a fact, that whereas the older Schools of Tibetan Buddhism, despite their tolerance of local deities, succeeded in breaking the power of the Bön religion, it was the Virtuous Ones <note>original: Gelugpas</note>, the youngest and most vigorously reformed School, which re-introduced one of the most influential institutions of the Bön followers, namely, State Oracles in Oracle-Temples, in all important monasteries of the Yellow Sect <term>Yellow Sect: A common name for the Gelug school, named after their distinctive yellow hats worn during ceremonies</term>. The deities who are invoked in these Oracle-Temples are exclusively of Bön origin. Among the older Buddhist sects, and especially among the followers of the Oral Lineage <note>original: Kargyütpas</note>, no such Oracle-Temples exist. This shows that the Old Schools, contrary to common belief, are less under the influence of the Bön religion than the reformed Virtuous Ones, in spite of the latter's reforms and stricter

### INTRODUCTORY FOREWORD

monastic discipline. This stricter monastic discipline of the <term>Gelugpas: The 'Yellow Hat' school of Tibetan Buddhism, known for its emphasis on logic and monastic ethics</term> really brings them nearer to the above-mentioned puritanism of the <term>Bön-pos: Followers of the Bön religion, Tibet's indigenous faith</term>.

We must, therefore, beware of sweeping statements, as to what can be attributed to the influence of <term>Bönism: The indigenous religious tradition of Tibet</term> and what not. Especially is this so because we do not know of what the teachings of Bön consisted before the advent of Buddhism, although we can safely assume that they were <term>animistic: The belief that all natural objects, such as trees and mountains, possess a spiritual soul</term>, the spiritualised forces of man and nature being worshipped, chiefly in their awe-inspiring and terrifying aspects; and certain rituals were performed for the benefit and the guidance of the dead. Such religious practices as these are commonly found in almost all early civilizations; and they prevailed in India as much as they did in Tibet. This ' animism ' permeates all Buddhistic texts, wherein every tree and grove, and every locality, is held to have its own peculiar deities; and the <term>Buddha: The 'Awakened One,' Siddhartha Gautama</term> is represented as discoursing with gods and other spiritual beings, inhabiting the Earth and the realms beyond, as if that were a most natural procedure. Only a completely intellectualized and Westernized Buddhism, which attempts to separate the rational thought-content of Buddhism from its equally profound mythological elements, can deny this animistic background and with it the metaphysical foundations of Buddhism.

The Buddhist universe is alive through and through; it has no room for inert matter and mere mechanism. And what is more, the Buddhist is alert to all possibilities of existence and to all aspects of reality. If we have read of the fearful apparitions which surrounded the Buddha during the night preceding His <term>Enlightenment: The attainment of ultimate truth and the end of suffering</term>, we need not search for Bön influences in relation to the animal-headed monsters that appear from the abyss of the subconscious mind in the hour of death, or in the visions of meditation. Wrathful deities, demons in animal form, and gods in demonical guise are as much at home in Indian as in Tibetan tradition. Despite the popular usages to which the ***Bardo Thödol*** <note>original: *Bardo Thödol* — literally 'Liberation Through Hearing in the Intermediate State,' popularly known as the Tibetan Book of the Dead</note> has been put in connection with the death rituals—and herein, probably, is discernible the only trace of Bön influence worth considering—the central idea and the profound symbolism of the ***Bardo Thödol*** are genuinely Buddhistic.

The Tibetans themselves have put forth considerable effort

<meta>This page continues the introductory foreword by Lāma Anagarika Govinda. This page moves into the history of how Tibetan authorities worked to standardize these teachings through strict translation rules and the advent of woodblock printing.</meta>

### INTRODUCTORY FOREWORD

to free their Scriptures from errors and non-Buddhistic accretions, and to ensure the correctness and reliability of their traditions. After the rules for the translation of Sanskrit texts and the necessary corresponding Tibetan terminology had been established by the early Tibetan translators and pioneers of the <term>Dharma: The teachings of the Buddha and the universal laws of reality</term>, translators were explicitly forbidden to coin new terms. When this was unavoidable, they were directed to report the matter to a special Tribunal, called "the Tribunal of the Doctrine of the Blessed One," attached to the royal palace. The translation of 
<margin>/</margin> <term>Tantric: Esoteric or ritual-based spiritual practices</term> works could be undertaken with the king's permission only. These rules were promulgated by King Ti-de Song-tsen (known as Ral-pa-can, 817-36 A.D.) and have been followed by all Tibetan translators ever since.¹

With the advent of wooden block-prints <note>Xylography, or printing from hand-carved wooden blocks, which became the standard for reproducing texts in Tibet</note>, similar precautions were taken, not only with regard to translations, but with regard to all religious literature. Thus it became a rule that no religious book could be published without the sanction of the highest spiritual authorities, who appointed qualified proof-readers and scholars to prevent faulty renderings or unwarranted interpolations. This, however, did not interfere with the diversity of interpretations by the various acknowledged Schools and their Teachers. The chief purpose was to prevent the degeneration of established traditions either through carelessness or ignorance of unqualified copyists and interpreters.

It is for this reason that the authorized block-prints contain the most reliable versions of the generally accepted traditional sacred texts. But hand-written books, although sometimes suffering from mistakes in spelling and from other errors of the copyist, who often shows lack of understanding of the archaic or classical language of the text, are, nevertheless, valuable, especially if they go back to originals of greater antiquity than those of the current block-prints, or if they represent some lesser known tradition handed down from <term>guru: A spiritual teacher</term> to <term>chela: A student or disciple</term> through many generations.

If, therefore, I direct the reader's attention to certain differences between the officially accepted version of the block-print and that of the manuscript, which formed the basis of Lāma Kazi Dawa <note>Kazi Dawa Samdup (1868–1922), a scholar and translator instrumental in bringing Tibetan texts to the West</note>

->### INTRODUCTORY FOREWORD<-

Regarding <term>Samdup: Kazi Dawa-Samdup (1868–1922), the primary Tibetan translator of this text</term>'s translation, I do not wish to question the value of the manuscript, but merely to throw light upon some important points of <term>Buddhist tradition: The long-standing customs and spiritual lineages derived from the teachings of the Buddha</term>, which may lead to a deeper understanding, not only from the historical, but, likewise, from a spiritual point of view.

Indeed, it is the spiritual point of view that makes this book so important for the majority of its readers. If the *<term>Bardo Thödol: Literally, 'Liberation Through Hearing in the Intermediate State'; popularly known as the Tibetan Book of the Dead</term>* were to be regarded as being based merely upon folklore, or as consisting of religious speculation about death and a hypothetical after-death state, it would be of interest only to anthropologists and students of religion. But the *Bardo Thödol* is far more. It is a key to the innermost recesses of the human mind, and a guide for initiates, and for those who are seeking the spiritual path of liberation.

Although the *Bardo Thödol* is at the present time widely used in <term>Tibet: The high-altitude region in the Himalayas where Vajrayana Buddhism flourished</term> as a <term>breviary: A book of prayers or rites intended for daily use</term>, and read or recited on the occasion of death, —for which reason it has been aptly called 'The Tibetan Book of the Dead'—one should not forget that it was originally conceived to serve as a guide not only for the dying and the dead, but for the living as well. And herein lies the justification for having made *The Tibetan Book of the Dead* accessible to a wider public.

Notwithstanding the popular customs and beliefs which, under the influence of age-old traditions of pre-Buddhist origin <note>This likely refers to the Bön religion, the indigenous spiritual tradition of Tibet that existed before the arrival of Buddhism</note>, have grown around the profound revelations of the *Bardo Thödol*, it has value only for those who practise and realize its teaching during their life-time.

There are two things which have caused misunderstanding. One is that the teachings seem to be addressed to the dead or the dying; the other, that the title contains the expression "Liberation through Hearing" (in Tibetan, <note>original: "Thos-grol"</note>). As a result, there has arisen the belief that it is sufficient to read or to recite the *Bardo Thödol* in the presence of a dying person, or even of a person who has just died, in order to effect his or her liberation.

Such misunderstanding could only have arisen among those who do not know that it is one of the oldest and most universal practices for the <term>initiate: One who has been formally admitted into a spiritual tradition and secret knowledge</term> to go through the experience of death before he can be spiritually reborn. Symbolically he must die to his past, and to his old ego, before he can take his

<summary>Lāma Anagarika Govinda argues that the Bardo Thödol is a profound psychological guide for the living and spiritual initiates rather than just a collection of funerary folklore. He clarifies that "Liberation through Hearing" requires active practice during one's lifetime, comparing the process to a symbolic spiritual rebirth.</summary>
<keywords>Bardo Thödol, Kazi Dawa-Samdup, spiritual point of view, Liberation through Hearing, initiation, psychological guide, spiritual rebirth, Tibetan Buddhism</keywords>

->### INTRODUCTORY FOREWORD<-

place in the new spiritual life into which he has been initiated.

The dead or the dying person is addressed in the *<term>Bardo Thödol: 'Liberation Through Hearing in the Intermediate State,' the Tibetan title of this work</term>* mainly for three reasons: (1) the earnest practitioner of these teachings should regard every moment of his or her life as if it were the last; (2) when a follower of these teachings is actually dying, he or she should be reminded of the experiences at the time of initiation, or of the words (or *<term>mantra: A sacred sound or phrase used as a focus for the mind</term>*) of the *<term>guru: A spiritual teacher or guide</term>*, especially if the dying one's mind lacks alertness during the critical moments; and (3) one who is still incarnate <note>living in a physical body</note> should try to surround the person dying, or just dead, with loving and helpful thoughts during the first stages of the new, or after-death, state of existence, without allowing emotional attachment to interfere or to give rise to a state of morbid mental depression. Accordingly, one function of the *Bardo Thödol* appears to be more to help those who have been left behind to adopt the right attitude towards the dead and towards the fact of death than to assist the dead, who, according to <term>Buddhist philosophy: The system of thought based on the Buddha's insights into the nature of reality and the path to enlightenment</term> belief, will not deviate from their own *<term>karmic: Related to the law of action and consequence, where one's past deeds shape future experiences</term>* path.

In applying the *Bardo Thödol* teachings, it is ever a matter of remembering the right thing at the right moment. But in order so to remember, one must prepare oneself mentally during one's life-time; one must create, build up, and cultivate those faculties which one desires to be of deciding influence at death and in the after-death state,—in order never to be taken unawares, and to be able to react, spontaneously, in the right way, when the critical moment of death has come.

This is clearly expressed in the Root Verses <note>Foundational poetic lines that contain the core essence of the teachings</note> of the *Bardo Thödol* as rendered in *The Tibetan Book of the Dead*:—
> ['O] procrastinating one, who thinketh not of the coming of death,
> Devoting thyself to the useless doings of this life,
> Improvident art thou in dissipating thy great opportunity;
> Mistaken, indeed, will thy purpose be now if thou returnest
> ->empty-handed [from this life].<-
> Since the Holy <term>Dharma: The sacred teachings and the universal truth taught by the Buddha</term> is known to be thy true need,
> Wilt thou not devote [thyself] to the Holy Dharma even now?'

It is recognized by all who are acquainted with Buddhist philosophy that birth and death are not phenomena which happen only once in any given human life; they occur uninterruptedly. At every moment something within us dies and something is

<summary>This page explains that the Bardo Thödol is a psychological tool for the living to prepare for death, emphasizing that the text serves to comfort the bereaved and guide the practitioner toward spiritual mindfulness.</summary>
<keywords>Bardo Thödol, mindfulness of death, karma, guru, mantra, Holy Dharma, spiritual preparation, Buddhist psychology</keywords>
