<meta>This page continues the introductory foreword by Lāma Anagarika Govinda. The previous page explained that the Bardo Thödol serves to orient the practitioner within their spiritual life and assists the living in maintaining a proper attitude toward death.</meta>

lxi

->### INTRODUCTORY FOREWORD<-

reborn. The different *intermediate states* <note>original: *bardos*</note>, therefore, represent different states of consciousness in our life: the state of waking consciousness, the normal consciousness of a being born into our human world, known in Tibetan as the *intermediate state of living* <note>original: *skyes-nas bardo*</note>; the state of dream-consciousness <note>original: *rmi-lam bardo*</note>; the state of *meditative absorption* <note>original: *dhyāna*</note>, or trance-consciousness, in profound meditation <note>original: *bsam-gtan bardo*</note>; the state of the experiencing of death <note>original: *hchhi-kha bardo*</note>; the state of experiencing of Reality <note>original: *chhos-nyid bardo*</note>; the state of rebirth-consciousness <note>original: *srid-pa bardo*</note>.

All this is clearly described in *The Root-Verses of the Six Intermediate States*, which, together with *The Paths of Good Wishes*, form the authentic and original nucleus of the *Liberation Through Hearing in the Intermediate State* <note>original: *Bardo Thödol*</note>, around which the prose parts crystallized as commentaries. This proves that we have to do here with life itself and not merely with a mass for the dead, to which the *Bardo Thödol* was reduced in later times.

The *Bardo Thödol* is addressed not only to those who see the end of their life approaching, or who are very near death, but to those who still have years of incarnate <note>living in a physical body</note> life before them, and who, for the first time, realize the full meaning of their existence as human beings. To be born as a human being is a privilege, according to the Buddha's teaching, because it offers the rare opportunity of liberation through one's own decisive effort, through a 'turning-about in the deepest seat of consciousness,' as the *Sutra of the Descent into Lanka* <note>original: *Lankāvatāra Sūtra*</note> puts it.

Accordingly, *The Root-Verses of the Six Intermediate States* open with the words:

> 'O that now, when the *Intermediate State of Life*¹ is dawning upon me,
> —After having given up indolence, since there is no time to
> waste in life—
> May I undistractedly enter the path of listening, reflecting,
> and meditating,
> So that, . . . once having attained human embodiment,
> No time may be squandered through useless distractions.'

---

¹Lāma Kazi Dawa-Samdup has here ‘ Birthplace Intermediate State ’. Apparently his manuscript has ‘ *skyes-gnas* ’ instead of ‘ *skyes-nas* ’, which is found in the block-print. The latter means, literally, ‘ having been born ’, that is, having been born into the state men call life. ‘ *Skyes-gnas* ’ refers to the womb, the ‘ place ’ <note>original: *gnas*</note> of birth; and this is the subject of the sixth verse, dealing with the *intermediate state* <note>original: *bardo*</note> of rebirth, which, therefore, cannot be meant here, for otherwise there would be only five *intermediate states* instead of six.

<summary>This page clarifies that the "intermediate states" (bardos) are not just for the dead but represent various states of consciousness during life, including waking, dreaming, and meditation. It argues that the Bardo Thödol is a guide for the living to use their precious human life for spiritual liberation.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the introductory foreword by Lāma Anagarika Govinda, moving from the definition of the various intermediate states to the practice of discipleship and the psychological foundations of the Bardo Thödol teachings.</meta>

lxii

->### INTRODUCTORY FOREWORD<-

Listening, reflecting, and meditating are the three stages of discipleship. The Tibetan word for 'listening', or 'hearing', <term>listening <note>original: *thos*</note></term> in this connection, as well as in the expression '<term>Liberation through Hearing <note>original: *Thödol* or *thos-grol*</note></term>', cannot be confused with the mere physical sense-awareness of hearing, as may be seen from the Tibetan term <term>hearer <note>original: *nyan-thos*</note></term>, the equivalent of the Sanskrit word <term>disciple <note>original: *sravaka*; literally "one who hears"</note></term>, referring to a 'disciple,' and, more particularly, to a personal disciple of the Buddha, and not merely to one who by chance happened to hear the Buddha's teaching. It refers to one who has accepted this teaching in his heart and has made it his own. Thus the word 'listening,' in this connection, implies 'hearing with one's heart,' that is, with sincere <term>faith <note>original: *sraddha*</note></term>. This represents the first stage of discipleship. In the second stage, this intuitive attitude is transformed into understanding through reason; while, in the third stage, the disciple's intuitive feeling, as well as intellectual understanding, are transformed into living reality through direct experience. Thus intellectual conviction grows into spiritual certainty, into a knowing in which the knower is *one* with the known.

This is the high spiritual state vouchsafed by the teachings set forth in the <term>Liberation Through Hearing in the Intermediate State <note>original: *Bardo Thödol*</note></term>. Thereby the initiated disciple attains dominion over the realm of death, and, being able to perceive death's illusory nature, is freed from fear. This illusoriness of death comes from the identification of the individual with his temporal, transitory form, whether physical, emotional, or mental, whence arise the mistaken notion that there exists a personal, separate egohood of one's own, and the fear of losing it. If, however, the disciple has learned, as the <term>Liberation Through Hearing in the Intermediate State <note>original: *Bardo Thödol*</note></term> directs, to identify himself with the Eternal, the <term>Universal Truth <note>original: *Dharma*</note></term>, the Imperishable Light of <term>Enlightened Mind <note>original: *Buddhahood*</note></term> within, then the fears of death are dissipated like a cloud before the rising sun. Then he knows that whatever he may see, hear, or feel, in the hour of his departure from this life, is but a reflection of his own conscious and subconscious mental content; and no mind-created illusion can then have power over him if he knows its origin and is able to recognize it. The illusory <term>intermediate state <note>original: *Bardo*</note></term> visions vary, in keeping with the religious or cultural tradition in which the percipient has grown up, but their underlying motive-power is the same in all human beings. Thus it is that the profound psychology set forth by the <term>Liberation Through Hearing in the Intermediate State <note>original: *Bardo Thödol*</note></term>

<summary>This page details the three stages of discipleship—listening, reflecting, and meditating—emphasizing that true "hearing" is a spiritual engagement of the heart. It explains that the Bardo Thödol aims to free the practitioner from the fear of death by teaching them to recognize death's visions as mere mental projections.</summary>

<keywords>discipleship, hearing with the heart, faith, sraddha, sravaka, Bardo Thödol, Dharma, Buddhahood, psychology of death, illusory visions</keywords>

--- Page 64 ---
<meta>This page concludes a sentence from page lxii regarding the spiritual mastery of the disciple, transitioning into a critique of modern academic translation versus the traditional Tibetan method of transmission.</meta>

lxiii

->### INTRODUCTORY FOREWORD<-

constitutes an important contribution to our knowledge of the human mind and of the path that leads beyond it. Under the guise of a science of death, the <term>Liberation Through Hearing in the Intermediate State <note>original: *Bardo Thödol*</note></term> reveals the secret of life; and therein lies its spiritual value and its universal appeal.

The <term>Liberation Through Hearing in the Intermediate State <note>original: *Bardo Thödol*</note></term> is a treatise which needs more than philological knowledge for its translation and interpretation, namely, a thorough knowledge of its traditional background and of the religious experience of one who either has grown up in the tradition or who has imbibed its tradition from a competent living <term>spiritual teacher <note>original: *guru*</note></term>. In times of old it was not considered that the mere knowledge of language sufficed to make a man a "translator" in any serious sense of the word; no one would have undertaken to translate a text who had not studied it for long years at the feet of a traditional and authoritative exponent of its teaching, and much less would anyone have thought himself qualified to translate a book in the teachings of which he did not believe.^1

Our modern attitude, unfortunately, is a complete reversal of this: a scholar is regarded as being all the more competent ('scholarly') the less he believes in the teachings which he has undertaken to interpret. The sorry results are only too apparent, especially in the realm of <term>Tibetan studies <note>original: *Tibetology*</note></term>, which such scholars have approached with an air of their own superiority, thus defeating the very purpose of their endeavours.

Lāma Kazi Dawa-Samdup and Dr. Evans-Wentz were the first to re-establish the ancient method of <term>translators of sacred texts <note>original: *Lotsavas*</note></term>. They approached their work in the spirit of true devotion and humility, as a sacred trust that had come into their hands through generations of initiates, a trust which had to be handled with the utmost respect for even the smallest detail. At the same time, they did not regard their translation as final, or infallible, but rather like the pioneer translations of the *Bible*, that is, as being a starting-point for ever deeper and more perfect renderings in accordance with our growing acquaintance with the sources of Tibetan tradition.

The latter is probably the best and most readable introduction to Tibetan Buddhism which so far has been written.

->INTRODUCTORY FOREWORD<-

Such an attitude is not only the hallmark of spiritual understanding and true scholarship, but it makes even the reader feel that he is treading on sacred ground. This explains the deep impression which <term>The Tibetan Book of the Dead <note>original: *Bardo Thödol*</note></term>, as well as the other complementary volumes of the Oxford Tibetan Series, have made upon thoughtful readers all over the world. The outstanding success of these works was due to their convincing sincerity and seriousness of purpose. Indeed, the world owes a great debt of gratitude to these two devoted scholars <note>Referring to the primary translator, Lāma Kazi Dawa-Samdup, and the editor, W. Y. Evans-Wentz.</note>. <note>original Pali: *Sabbadānaṃ dhammadānaṃ jināti*</note> 'The best of all gifts is the gift of Truth.'¹

# FOREWORD

## BY SIR JOHN WOODROFFE

### THE SCIENCE OF DEATH¹

> ‘Strive after the Good before thou art in danger, before pain masters thee and thy mind loses its keenness.’—*Kulārnava Tantra*, I. 27. <note>The Kulārnava Tantra is a significant Hindu text of the Kaula school, emphasizing the preciousness of human life for spiritual practice.</note>

THE thought of death suggests two questions. The first is: ‘How may one avoid death, except when death is desired as in <term>Death-at-will <note>original Sanskrit: *Ichchhāmṛityu*</note></term>?’ The avoidance of death is the aim when <term>Hathayoga <note>A system of physical techniques and breath control used to master the body and extend its lifespan.</note></term> is used to prolong present life in the flesh. This is not, in the Western sense, a ‘yea-saying’ to ‘life’, but, for the time being, to a particular form of life. Dr. Evans-Wentz <note>The American scholar who edited this volume.</note> tells us that according to popular Tibetan belief no death is natural. This is the notion of most, if not of all, primitive peoples. Moreover, physiology also questions whether there is any ‘natural death’, in the sense of death through mere age without lesion or malady. This Text, however, in the language of the renouncer of fleshly life the world over, tells the nobly-born that Death comes to all, that human kind are not to cling to life on earth with its <term>ceaseless wandering in the Worlds of birth and death <note>original Tibetan: *Sangsāra*</note></term>. Rather should they implore the aid of the Divine Mother for a safe passing through the fearful state following the body’s dissolution, and that they may at length attain all-perfect Buddhahood.

The second question then is: ‘How to accept Death and die?’ It is with this that we are now concerned. Here the technique of dying makes Death the entrance to good future lives, at first out of, and then again in, the flesh, unless and until <term>liberation <note>original Sanskrit: *Nirvāṇa*</note></term> from the <term>wandering <note>original Tibetan: *Sangsāra*</note></term> is attained.

---

¹ As to the title of this Foreword, ‘The Science of Death’, see *Thanatology* <note>The scientific study of death and the losses resulting from it.</note>, by Dr. Roswell Parks, in *The Journal of the American Medical Association* April 27, 1912.

<summary>Sir John Woodroffe introduces the "Science of Death," exploring the yogic desire to prolong life versus the spiritual necessity of preparing for the inevitable transition beyond the physical body.</summary>

lxvi

# FOREWORD

This Book, which is of extraordinary interest, both as regards Text and Introduction, deals with the period (longer or shorter according to the circumstances) which, commencing immediately after death, ends with 'rebirth'. In the Buddhists' view, Life consists of a series of successive states of consciousness. The first state is the Birth-Consciousness; the last is the consciousness existing at the moment of death, or the Death-Consciousness. The interval between the two states of Consciousness, during which the transformation from the 'old' to a 'new' being is effected, is called the <term>Intermediate State <note>original Tibetan: *Bardo*; original Sanskrit: *Antarābhāva*</note></term>, divided into three stages, called <term>the Bardo of the Moment of Death <note>original Tibetan: *Chikhai Bardo*</note></term>, <term>the Bardo of the Experiencing of Reality <note>original Tibetan: *Chönyid Bardo*</note></term>, and <term>the Bardo of Seeking Rebirth <note>original Tibetan: *Sidpa Bardo*</note></term> respectively.

This Manual, common in various versions throughout Tibet, is one of a class amongst which Dr. Evans-Wentz includes the Egyptian Book of the Dead, a guide for the use of the <term>Spirit-Double <note>original Egyptian: *Ka*</note></term> or so-called 'Double', the <term>*On the Art of Dying* <note>original Latin: *De Arte Moriendi*</note></term> and other similar medieval treatises on the craft of dying, to which may be added the Orphic Manual called *The Descent into Hades* (compare 'He descended into Hell') and other like guide-books for the use of the dead, the <term>Section on the Departed <note>original Sanskrit: *Pretakhanda*</note></term> of the Hindu <term>*Garuda Purāna* <note>A classic Hindu scripture detailing the soul's journey after death and the rites performed by survivors.</note></term>, Swedenborg's <term>*On Heaven and Hell* <note>original Latin: *De Coelo et de Inferno*</note></term>, Rusca's <term>*On Hell* <note>original Latin: *De Inferno*</note></term>, and several other works on the final stages of life and the afterlife both ancient and modern. Thus, the *Garuda Purāna* deals with the rites used over the dying, the death-moment, the funeral ceremonies, the building up, by means of the <term>Rite for the Departed <note>original Sanskrit: *Pretashrāddha*</note></term>, of a new body for the <term>Deceased Spirit <note>original Sanskrit: *Preta*</note></term> in lieu of that destroyed by fire, the Judgement, and thereafter (chapter five) the various states through which the deceased passes until he is reborn again on earth.

Both the original text and Dr. Evans-Wentz's Introduction form a very valuable contribution to the Science of Death from the standpoint of the Tibetan <term>Great Vehicle <note>original Sanskrit: *Mahāyāna*</note></term> Buddhism of the so-called <term>Ritualistic <note>original Sanskrit: *Tantrik*</note></term> type. The book is welcome not merely in virtue of its particular subject-matter, but because the ritual works of any religion enable us more fully to comprehend the philosophy and psychology of the system to which they belong.

The Text has three characteristics. It is, firstly, a work on

->REINCARNATION *v.* RESURRECTION<-

the Art of Dying; for Death, as well as Life, is an Art, though both are often enough muddled through. There is a Bengali saying, "Of what use are <term>Repetition of Prayer <note>original: *Japa*; the practice of meditative repetition of a mantra or divine name</note></term> and <term>Spiritual Discipline <note>original: *Tapas*; refers to burning off karma through self-discipline or austerity</note></term> if one knoweth not how to die?" Secondly, it is a manual of religious therapeutic for the last moments, and a <term>Psychic Direction <note>original: *psychurgy*; the use of ritual or mental power to influence the soul’s journey</note></term> exorcising, instructing, consoling, and fortifying by the rites of the dying, him who is about to pass on to another life. Thirdly, it describes the experiences of the deceased during the intermediate period, and instructs him in regard thereto. It is thus also a Traveller’s Guide to Other Worlds.

The doctrine of "Reincarnation" on the one hand and of "Resurrection" on the other is the chief difference between the four leading Religions—Brahmanism, Buddhism, Christianity, and Islam. Christianity, in its orthodox form, rejects the most ancient and widespread belief of the <term>Cycle of Becomings <note>original Greek: *Kúklos geneseōn*</note></term>, or <term>The Wandering <note>original Sanskrit: *Sangsarā*</note></term>, or "Reincarnation," and admits one universe only—this, the first and last—and two lives, one here in the natural body and one hereafter in the body of Resurrection.

It has been succinctly said that as <term>The Migration of Souls <note>original Greek: *Metempsychosis*</note></term> makes the same soul, so Resurrection makes the same body serve for more than one Life. But the latter doctrine limits man’s lives to two in number, of which the first or present determines for ever the character of the second or future.

Brahmanism and Buddhism would accept the doctrine that "as a tree falls so shall it lie," but they deny that it so lies for ever. To the adherents of these two kindred beliefs this present universe is not the first and last. It is but one of an infinite series, without absolute beginning or end, though each universe of the series appears and disappears. They also teach a series of successive existences therein until morality, devotion, and knowledge produce that high form of detachment which is the cause of Liberation from the cycle of birth and death called <term>The Wandering <note>original Sanskrit: *Sangsarā*</note></term>. Freedom is the attainment of the Supreme State called the Void, <term>The State of Extinction <note>original Sanskrit: *Nirvāṇa*; the liberation from the cycle of suffering</note></term>, and by other names. They deny that there is only one universe, with one life for each of its human units, and then a division of men for all eternity into those who are saved in Heaven or are in Limbo and those who are lost in

FOREWORD

Hell. Whilst they agree in holding that there is a suitable body for enjoyment or suffering in Heaven and Hell, it is not a resurrected body, for the fleshly body on death is dissolved for ever.

The need of some body always exists, except for the non-dualist who believes in a <term>Bodiless <note>original Sanskrit: *Videha*; referring to an existence without any physical or subtle form</note></term> <term>Liberation <note>original Sanskrit: *Mukti*; the final release from the cycle of death and rebirth</note></term>;
<margin>✓</margin>
and each of the four religions affirms that there is a subtle and death-surviving element—vital and psychical—in the physical body of flesh and blood, whether it be a permanent entity or Self, such as the Brahmanic <term>True Self <note>original Sanskrit: *Atmā*; the eternal, unchanging essence of an individual</note></term>, the Moslem <term>Spirit <note>original Arabic: *Ruh*; the vital breath or soul</note></term>, and the Christian 'Soul', or whether it be only a complex of activities (or <term>Aggregates <note>original Sanskrit: *Skandha*; in Buddhist thought, the five bundles of mental and physical phenomena that constitute a sentient being</note></term>), psychical and physical, with life as their function—a complex in continual change, and, therefore, a series of physical and psychical momentary states, successively generated the one from the other, a continuous transformation, as the Buddhists are said to hold. Thus to none of these Faiths is death an absolute ending, but to all it is
<margin> [Passage marked with a vertical line in the margin] </margin>
only the separation of the <term>Soul <note>original Greek: *Psyche*; the life-force or mind</note></term> from the gross body. The former then enters on a new life, whilst the latter, having lost its principle of animation, decays. As Dr. Evans-Wentz so concisely says, Death disincarnates the 'soul-complex', as Birth incarnates it. In other words, Death is itself only an initiation into another form of life than that of which it is the ending.

On the subject of the physical aspect of Death, the attention of the reader is drawn to the remarkable analysis here given of symptoms which precede it. These are stated because it is necessary for the dying man and his helpers to be prepared for the final and decisive moment when it comes.¹ Noteworthy, too, is the description of sounds heard as (to use Dr. Evans-Wentz's language) 'the psychic resultants of the disintegrating process called death'. They call to mind the humming, rolling, and crackling noises heard before and up to fifteen hours after death, which, recognized by Greunwaldi in 1618 and referred to by later writers, were in 1862 made the subject of special study by Dr. Collingues.

->CONSCIOUSNESS TRANSFERENCE<-

But it is said that the chain of conscious states is not always broken by death, since there is <term>Consciousness Transference <note>original Tibetan: *Phowa*; a meditation technique where a practitioner intentionally directs their consciousness out of the body at the moment of death</note></term>, or the power to project consciousness and enter the body of another.^1 Indian occultism speaks of the same power of <term>Voluntarily Leaving the Body <note>original Sanskrit: *Svechhhotkrānti*</note></term>, which, according to <term>The King of Tantras <note>original Sanskrit: *Tantrarāja*; a foundational text of Hindu Tantric practice</note></term> (chapter 27, verses 45-7, 72-80), is accomplished through the <term>Concentration of Vital Energies <note>original Sanskrit: *Vāyudhāraṇa*</note></term> of the <term>Life-Breath <note>original Sanskrit: *Vāyu*</note></term> in thirty-eight <term>Vital Junctions <note>original Sanskrit: *Marma*; specific points in the body where physical and spiritual energies intersect</note></term> of the body. How, it may be asked, does this practice work in with the general doctrine of 'reincarnation'? We should have been glad if Dr. Evans-Wentz had elucidated this point. On principle, it would seem that in the case of entry into an unborn body such entry may be made into the <term>Womb <note>original Latin: *Matrix*</note></term> in the same way as if it had occurred after a break of consciousness in death. But in the case of entry into beings already born, the operation of the <term>Spiritual Power <note>original Sanskrit: *Siddhi*</note></term> would appear to be by the way of <term>Possession <note>original Sanskrit: *Āvesha*</note></term> by one consciousness of the consciousness and body of another, differing from the more ordinary case by the fact that the possessing consciousness does not return to its body, which <term>by the very nature of the assumption <note>original Latin: *ex hypothesi*</note></term> is about to die when the consciousness leaves it.

If transference of consciousness is effected, there is, of course, no <term>Intermediate State <note>original Tibetan: *Bardo*; the transitional period between death and the next rebirth</note></term>, which involves the break of consciousness by death. Otherwise, the Text is read.

Then, as the breathing is about to cease, instruction is given and the arteries are pressed. This is done to keep the dying person conscious with a consciousness rightly directed. For the nature of the Death-consciousness determines the future state of the 'soul-complex', existence being the continuous transformation of one conscious state into another. Both in Catholic and Hindu ritual for the dying there is constant prayer and repetition of the sacred names.

The pressing of the arteries regulates the path to be taken by the outgoing <term>Life-Force <note>original Sanskrit: *Prāṇa*</note></term>. The proper path is that which passes through <term>The Opening of Brahma <note>original Sanskrit: *Brāhmarandhra*</note></term>, or <term>The Foramen of Monro <note>An anatomical passage in the brain used here to provide a physical location for a spiritual exit point</note></term>. This notion appears to have been widely held (to quote an instance) even in so remote and primitive a spot as San Cristoval in the Solomon Islands (see *Threshold of the*

### FOREWORD

*Pacific*, by C. E. Fox). The function of a holed-stone in a <term>Dolmen <note>A prehistoric stone tomb consisting of a large flat stone laid on upright ones.</note></term> found there (reminiscent of the **dolmen with a pierced slab** <note>original French: *Dolmen à dalle percée*</note> common in the Marne district of Western Europe, in South Russia, and in Southern India) is 'to allow the free passage to its natural seat, the head, of the dead man's *adaro*, or "double"'.

According to Hindu belief (see the **Section on the Departed** <note>original Sanskrit: *Pretakhanda*</note> of the **Garuda Purāna** <note>A classical Hindu text dealing with death, funerals, and reincarnation.</note>) there are nine apertures of the body which are the means of experience, and which, in the divine aspect, are the **Lords** <note>original Sanskrit: *Nātha*</note> or **Gurus**.¹ A good exit is one which is above the navel. Of such exits the best is through the fissure on the top of the cranium called the <term>Opening of Brahma <note>original Sanskrit: *Brahmarandhra*</note></term>. This is above the physical **brain** <note>original Latin: *cerebrum*</note> and the **Yoga** centre called the <term>Thousand-Petaled Lotus <note>original Sanskrit: *Sahasrāra Padma*</note></term>, wherein Spirit is most manifest, since it is the seat of Consciousness. Because of this, the orthodox Hindu wears a <term>Crest-lock <note>original Sanskrit: *Shikhā*; a tuft of hair left on the crown of the head</note></term> at this spot; not, as some have absurdly supposed, so that he may thereby be gripped and taken to Heaven or Hell, but because the **Crest-lock** is, as it were, a flag and its staff, raised before and in honour of the abode of the Supreme Lord, Who is Pure Consciousness itself. (The fancy-picture in a recent work by C. Lancelin, *Posthumous Life* <note>original French: *La Vie posthume*</note>, p. 96, does not show the aperture of exit, which is given in Plate 8 of the second edition of Arthur Avalon's *Serpent Power*, p. 93.)

Whatever be the ground for the belief and practice of primitive peoples, according to **Yoga** doctrine, the head is the chief centre of consciousness, regulating other subordinate centres in the spinal column. By withdrawal of the vital current through the **central** <note>original Sanskrit: *Sushumnā*</note> **nerve** <note>original Sanskrit: *nādi*</note>, the lower parts of the body are devitalized, and there is vivid concentrated functioning at the cerebral centre.

Exotericism speaks of the 'Book of Judgement'. This is an objective symbol of the 'Book' of Memory. The 'reading' of that 'Book' is the recalling to mind by the dying man of the whole of his past life on earth before he passes from it.²

---

¹ Cf. A. Avalon's *Tantrik Texts*, vol. viii, p. 2.
² That such a review of earth-life is experienced by the dying has been frequently attested by persons who had begun to die, as, for example, in drowning, and then been resuscitated.—W. Y. E-W.

<meta>This page continues the philosophical introduction to the Tibetan concepts of death and the intermediate state, specifically focusing on the transition of consciousness into the "Clear Light."</meta>

-># THE CLEAR LIGHT OF THE VOID<-

The vital current at length escapes from the place where it last functioned. In <term>Yoga (spiritual disciplines focusing on the union of mind and body)</term>, thought and breathing being interdependent, exit through the <term>Brahmarandhra (the ‘aperture of the Creator,’ located at the crown of the head)</term> connotes previous activity at the highest centre. Before such exit, and whilst self-consciousness lasts, the mental contents are supplied by the ritual, which is so designed as to secure a good death, and, therefore (later on), birth-consciousness.

At the moment of death the empiric consciousness, or consciousness of objects, is lost. There is what is popularly called a ‘swoon’, which is, however, the corollary of super-consciousness itself, or the Clear Light of the Void; for the swoon is in, and of, the Consciousness as knower of objects <note>original: "Vijñāna Skandha" (the Buddhist term for the aggregate of consciousness or discernment)</note>. This empiric consciousness disappears, unveiling Pure Consciousness, which is ever ready to be ‘discovered’ by those who have the will to seek and the power to find It.

That clear, colourless Light is a sense-symbol of the formless Void, ‘beyond the Light of Sun, Moon, and Fire’, to use the words of the Indian <term>Gita (a sacred Hindu scripture)</term>. It is clear and colourless, but <term>mayik (relating to Maya, the world of illusion and form)</term> bodies are coloured in various ways. For colour implies and denotes form. The Formless is colourless. The use of psycho-physical chromatism <note>The study of how colors relate to mental and physical states.</note> is common to the Hindu and Buddhist <term>Tantras (esoteric scriptures focusing on ritual and meditation)</term>, and may be found in some Islamic mystical systems also.

What then is this Void? It is not absolutely ‘nothingness’. It is the Alogical, to which no categories drawn from the world of name and form apply. But whatever may have been held by the <term>Madhyamika Bauddha (followers of the 'Middle Way' school of Buddhist philosophy)</term>, a <term>Vedantist (a follower of the Hindu philosophy of non-dualism)</term> would say that ‘Being’, or ‘Is-ness’, is applicable even in the case of the Void, which is experienced as ‘is’ <note>original: "asti" (it exists)</note>. The Void is thus, in this view, the negation of all determinations, but not of ‘Is-ness’ as such, as has been supposed in accounts given of Buddhist ‘Nihilism’; but it is nothing known to finite experience in form, and, therefore, for those who have had no other experience, it is no-thing.

A description of Buddhist <term>Mahayana (the 'Great Vehicle' branch of Buddhism)</term> teaching which is at once more succinct and clear than, to my knowledge, any other, is given in the Tibetan work, *The Path of Good Wishes of <term>Samanta Bhadra (the 'All-Good' primordial Buddha)</term>*, which I have published in the seventh

<summary>This page describes the transition of consciousness during death, where the individual moves from objective experience into a "swoon" that reveals the Clear Light of the Void. It clarifies that the "Void" is not literal nothingness but a state beyond human categories of form and color.</summary>
<keywords>Clear Light, the Void, death process, Yoga, Mahayana Buddhism, consciousness, Brahmarandhra, Samantabhadra</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the introduction to the philosophical foundations of the text, transitioning from the specific experience of the "Clear Light" at death to a broader explanation of the relationship between the cycle of suffering (Samsara) and ultimate liberation (Nirvana).</meta>

# FOREWORD

volume of *Tantric Texts* (p. xxi and the following pages <note>original: "et seq."</note>) and here summarize and explain.

All is either the Cycle of Suffering <term>Sangsāra (the worldly cycle of birth, death, and rebirth)</term> or Liberation <term>Nirvāṇa (the state of being released from suffering and the cycle of rebirth)</term>. The first is finite experience in the ‘Six Worlds’ or <term>Loka (various planes of existence in Buddhist cosmology)</term>—a word which means ‘that which is experienced’ <note>original: "Lokyante"</note>. The second, or Liberation <term>Nirvāṇa</term>, is, negatively speaking, release from such experience, that is from the worlds of Birth and Death and their pains. The Void cannot even be strictly called Liberation, for this is a term relative to the world, and the Void is beyond all relations. Positively, and at the same time as such release, it is the Perfect Experience which is Buddhahood, which, again, from the cognitive aspect, is Consciousness unobscured by the darkness of Unconsciousness—that is to say, Consciousness freed of all limitation. From the emotional aspect, it is pure Bliss unaffected by sorrow; and from the volitional aspect, it is freedom of action and Unfailing Success <term>Amogha-Siddhi (one of the five Great Wisdom Buddhas, representing the power of infallible action)</term>. Perfect Experience is an eternal or, more strictly speaking, a timeless state. Imperfect Experience is also eternal in the sense that the series of universes in which it is undergone is infinite. The religious—that is, practical—problem is then how from the lesser experience to pass into that which is complete, called by the Indian philosophical scriptures <term>Upanishads (ancient Sanskrit texts exploring the nature of reality and the self)</term> ‘the Whole’ or ‘Fullness’ <term>Pūrṇa</term>. This is done by the removal of obscuration. At base, the two are one—the Void, uncreated, independent, uncompounded, and beyond mind and speech. If this were not so, Liberation would not be possible. Man is in fact liberated, but does not know it. When he realizes it, he is freed. The great saying of the Buddhist work the Perfection of Wisdom <term>Prajñā-Pāramitā (a fundamental collection of Mahayana Buddhist scriptures)</term> runs thus: ‘Form <term>Rūpa</term> is the Void and the Void is Form.’¹ Realization of the Void is to be a Buddha, or ‘Knower’, and not to realize it is to be an ‘ignorant being’ in the Cycle of Suffering <term>Sangsāra</term>. The two paths, then, are Knowledge and Ignorance. The first path leads to—and, as actual realization, is—Liberation <term>Nirvāṇa</term>. The second means continuance of fleshly life as man or brute, or as a denizen of the other four Worlds <term>Lokas</term>. Ignorance in the individual is in its cosmic aspect Illusion <term>Māyā (the deceptive power that makes the phenomenal world appear real)</term>, which in Tibetan <term>sGyuma</term> means a magical show. In its most generic form,

-># STATE OF LIBERATION<-

->k<-

the former is that which produces the pragmatic, but, in a transcendental sense, the ‘unreal’ notion of self and otherness. This is the root cause of error (whether in knowing, feeling, or action) which becomes manifest as the ‘Six Poisons’ (which Hindus call the ‘Six Enemies’) of the <term>Six Lokas (the six realms of existence, ranging from heavens to hells)</term> of <term>Sangsāra (the cycle of birth, death, and rebirth)</term> (of which the Text gives five only)—pride, jealousy, sloth (or ignorance), anger, greed, and lust. The Text constantly urges upon the dying or ‘dead’ man to recognize in the apparitions, which he is about to see or sees, the creatures of his own <term>māyā (the power of the mind to create illusion)</term>-governed mind, veiling from him the <term>Clear Light of the Void (the pure, unconditioned reality that appears at the moment of death)</term>. If he does so, he is liberated at any stage.

This philosophical scheme has so obvious a resemblance to the Indian <term>Māyāvāda Vedānta (a school of Hindu philosophy that views the world as an illusion)</term> that the Vaishnava <term>Padma Purāna (an ancient Hindu religious text)</term> dubs that system ‘a bad scripture and covert Buddhism’ <note>original: "māyāvādam asachchāstram prachchhannam bauddham"</note>. Nevertheless, its great scholastic, ‘the incomparable <term>Shangkarāchāryya (Shankara, a famous 8th-century Indian philosopher who revitalized Hindu thought)</term>’, as Sir William Jones calls him, combated the Buddhists in their denial of a permanent Self (<term>Ātmā (the eternal soul or individual spirit)</term>), as also their subjectivism, at the same time holding that the notion of an individual self and that of a world of objects were pragmatic truths only, superseded by and on the attainment of a state of Liberation which has little, if anything, to distinguish it from the Buddhist Void. The difference between the two systems, though real, is less than is generally supposed. This is a matter, however, which it would be out of place to discuss further here.

However this may be, the after-death apparitions are ‘real’ enough for the deceased who does not, as and when they appear, recognize their unsubstantiality and cleave his way through them to the Void. The <term>Clear Light</term> is spoken of in the <term>Bardo Thödol (The Tibetan Book of the Dead, or "Liberation Through Hearing in the Intermediate State")</term> as such a Dazzlement as is produced by an infinitely vibrant landscape in the springtide. This joyous picture is not, of course, a statement of what It is in itself, for It is not an object, but is a translation in terms of objective vision of a great, but, in itself, indescribable joyful inner experience. My attention was drawn, in this connexion, to a passage in a paper on the <term>Avatamsaka Sūtra (The Flower Ornament Scripture, a central text in East Asian Buddhism)</term> (ch. XV), by Mr. Hsu, a Chinese scholar, which says, ‘The

<summary>This page discusses how the ego and the "Six Poisons" lead to the cycle of rebirth, and explains that the visions seen after death are projections of the mind that must be recognized to achieve liberation. It also notes the deep similarities between Tibetan Buddhist philosophy and the Indian Vedanta school regarding the illusory nature of the world.</summary>

--- Page 75 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction to the philosophical foundations of the Tibetan Book of the Dead, building upon the discussion of how the concept of self-illusion obscures the Clear Light of reality at the moment of death.</meta>

-># FOREWORD<-

The <term>Bodhisattva (an enlightened being who postpones their own final liberation to help others)</term> emits the light called "Seeing the Buddha" in order to make the dying think about the <term>Tathāgata (a title for the Buddha, meaning "one who has thus gone")</term> and so enable them to go to the pure realms of the latter after death.

The dying or deceased person is urged to recognize the **Clear Light** and thus liberate themselves. If they do so, it is because they are themselves spiritually ready for the liberated state which is thus presented to them. If they do not (as is commonly the case), it is because the pull of worldly tendency (<term>Sangskāra (mental impressions or habits formed by past actions)</term>) draws them away. They are then presented with the secondary **Clear Light**, which is the first, somewhat dimmed to them by the general <term>Māyā (the cosmic illusion that makes the temporary world seem real)</term>. If the mind does not find its resting-place here, the first or <term>Chikhai Bardo (the intermediate state of the moment of death)</term>, which may last for several days, or "for the time that it takes to snap a finger" (according to the state of the deceased), comes to an end.

In the next stage (<term>Chönyid Bardo (the intermediate state of experiencing reality)</term>) there is a recovery of the **Death-Consciousness** of objects. In one sense, that is compared with a swoon; it is a reawakening. But it is not a waking-state such as existed before death. The "soul-complex" emerges from its experience of the **Void** into a state like that of a dream. This continues until it attains a new fleshly body and thus really awakes to earth-life again. For this world-experience is life in such a body.

When I first read the account of the fifteen days following recovery from the "swoon," I thought it was meant to be a scheme of gradual arising of limited consciousness, analogous to that described in the thirty-six <term>Tattvas (the basic principles or "levels" of reality)</term> by the Northern <term>Shaivāgama (sacred texts dedicated to the god Shiva)</term> and its <term>Tantras (esoteric spiritual scriptures)</term>, a process which is given in its ritual form in the Tantric <term>Bhūtashuddhi (the ritual purification of the elements in the body)</term> rite and in <term>Laya or Kuṇḍalinī Yoga (meditative practices focused on dissolving the ego or awakening internal energy)</term>. <note>The author is comparing the Tibetan process of death to similar Hindu rituals that "rehearse" the dissolution of the physical body back into spiritual energy.</note> But on closer examination I found that this was not so. After the ending of the first *Bardo* the scheme commences with the complete recovery, without intermediate stages, of the **Death-Consciousness**. The psychic life is taken up and continued from that point, that is from the stage immediately prior to the "swoon."¹ Life immediately after death is, according to this view, as <term>Spiritists (those who believe in communication with the dead)</term> assert, similar to, and a continuation of, the life preceding it.

---

¹ Compare with the <term>Yogavāshishṭha (a Hindu philosophical text explaining the illusory nature of the world)</term>, chapter 160, verse 41.

->## AFTER-DEATH EXISTENCE<-

lxxv
k*
<meta>3009</meta>

As in Swedenborg's account <note>Emanuel Swedenborg (1688–1772) was a Swedish scientist and mystic who wrote extensively about his visions of the spirit world.</note>, and in the recent play *Outward Bound* <note>A 1923 play by Sutton Vane where passengers on a ship gradually realize they are dead.</note>, the deceased does not at first know that he is 'dead'. Swedenborg, who also speaks of an intermediate state, says that, except for those immediately translated to Heaven or Hell, the first state of man after death is like his state in the world, so that he knows no other, believing that he is still in the world notwithstanding his death.<sup>1</sup>

Two illustrations may be given of the doctrine of the continuity and the similarity of experience before and immediately after death. In India, on the one hand, there are reports of hauntings by unhappy ghosts or <term>Pretas (in Sanskrit, "the departed"; spirits who suffer from hunger or unfulfilled desires)</term>, which hauntings are said to be allayed by the performance of the <term>Preta Shrāddha (a Hindu ritual meant to provide a temporary body for the deceased to help them transition to the next world)</term> rite at the sacred town of Gaya. On the other hand, I have heard of a case in England where it was alleged that a haunting ceased on the saying of a <term>Requiem Mass (a Catholic religious service offered for the rest of the soul of a deceased person)</term>. In this case, it was supposed that a Catholic soul in <term>Purgatory (an intermediate state after death for expiatory purification)</term> felt in need of a rite which in its earth-life it had been taught to regard as bringing peace to the dead. The Hindu ghost craves for the Hindu rite which gives to it a new body in lieu of that destroyed on the funeral pyre. These souls do not (in an Indian view) cease to be Hindu or Catholic, or lose their respective beliefs because of their death. Nor (in this view) do those who have passed on necessarily and at once lose any habit, even though it be drinking and smoking. But in the after-death state the 'whisky and cigars' of which we have heard are not gross, material things. Just as a dream reproduces waking experiences, so in the after-death state a man who was wont to drink and smoke imagines that he still does so. We have here to deal with 'dream-whisky' and 'dream-cigars' which, though imaginary, are, for the dreamer, as real as the substances he drank and smoked in his waking state.<sup>2</sup>

Some years afterwards, a friend of the planter found the grave carefully fenced in and covered with empty whisky and beer bottles. At a loss to understand such an unusual sight, he asked for an explanation, and was told that the dead sahib's ghost had caused much trouble and that no way had been <unclear>found to pacify him other than offering what he loved in life</unclear>.

-># FOREWORD<-

lxxvi

Subsequently, the deceased becomes aware that he is 'dead'. But as he carries over with him the recollection of his past life, he, at first, still thinks that he has such a physical body as he had before. It is, in fact, a dream-body, such as that of persons seen in dreams. It is an imagined body, which, as the Text says, is neither reflected in a mirror nor casts a shadow, and which can do such wonders as passing through mountains and the like, since Imagination is the greatest of magicians. Even in life on earth a man may imagine that he has a limb where he has none. Long after a man's leg has been amputated above the knee he can 'feel his toes', or is convinced that the soles of his feet (buried days before) are tickling. In the after-death state the deceased imagines that he has a physical body, though he has been severed therefrom by the high surgery of death. In such a body the deceased goes through the experiences next described.

In the First <term>Bardo (the intermediate state between death and rebirth)</term> the deceased glimpses the Clear Light, as the <term>Truth Body (the ultimate, formless essence of the universe; original: 'Dharma-Kāya')</term>, called by Professor Sylvain Lévy <note>Sylvain Lévy (1863–1935) was a famous French scholar of Oriental studies.</note> the 'Essential Body'. This, which is beyond form <note>original: 'Arūpa'</note>, is the <term>Matrix of Reality (the fundamental source of all phenomena; original: 'Dharma-Dhātu')</term>, or Matrix of Truth-substance, whence all the <term>Blessed Ones (those who have attained enlightenment; original: 'Tathāgatas')</term>, issue. This is the body of a Buddha in <term>Nirvāṇa (the state of final liberation from suffering and rebirth)</term>. The second body, or <term>Body of Bliss (a celestial form of enlightenment visible to advanced beings; original: 'Sambhoga-Kāya')</term>, has such subtle form <note>original: 'Rūpavān'</note> as is visible to the <term>Bodhisattvas (beings who have vowed to attain enlightenment for the sake of all others)</term>, and is an intermediate manifestation of the Matrix of Reality. In the third body, or <term>Emanation Body (the physical form a Buddha takes to help worldly beings; original: 'Nirmāṇa-Kāya')</term>, the Void, or State of Buddhahood, is exteriorized into multiple individual appearances more material, and, therefore, visible to the gross senses of men, such as the forms in which the manifested Buddhas (for there are many and not, as some think, only one, or <note>Gautama refers to Siddhartha Gautama, the historical Buddha who lived roughly 2,500 years ago.</note> Gautama) have appeared on earth. If the deceased recognizes the Clear Light of the First intermediate state, he is liberated in the

<header>PATHS TO LIBERATION lxxvii</header>

<term>Truth Body (the ultimate, formless essence of reality; original: 'Dharma-Kāya')</term>. In the Second <term>Intermediate State (the period between death and rebirth; original: 'Bardo')</term>, Liberation is into the <term>Enjoyment Body (the state of heavenly bliss and celestial forms; original: 'Sambhogha-Kāya')</term> (the passage touching the Paradise Realms is not, I think, meant to conflict with this); and in the Third <term>Intermediate State (original: 'Bardo')</term> Liberation is experienced in the <term>Emanation Body (the physical form assumed to help others; original: 'Nirmāṇa-Kāya')</term>.

During the Second and Third <term>Intermediate State (original: 'Bardo')</term> the deceased is in the <term>world of illusion (original: 'Māyik-world')</term>, and if Liberation is then attained it is <term>with form (original: 'Rūpavān')</term>. The deceased being thus in the world of duality, we find that from this point onwards there is a double parallel presentation to his consciousness. There is firstly a line of <term>Nirvāṇa (ultimate peace and liberation)</term>, comprising the Five <term>Wisdom Buddhas (the primary celestial buddhas of meditation; original: 'Dhyāni Buddhas')</term> of the <term>Enjoyment Body (original: 'Sambhogha-Kāya')</term>, symbolized by various dazzling colors, with certain Divinities, peaceful and wrathful, emanating from them; and, secondly, a line of <term>Samsāra (the cycle of worldly suffering; original: 'Sangsāric')</term>, consisting of the Six <term>Realms (states of existence; original: 'Lokas')</term>. These latter, with one exception (if it be one and not due to corruption of text, viz. the association of the smoky or black light of Hell with the blue <term>Indestructible Being (original: 'Vajra-Sattva')</term>), have the same color as their <term>Nirvāṇic</term> counterparts, but of a dull hue. With the <term>Realms (original: 'Lokas')</term> are given their ‘Poisons’, or the sinful characteristics of their inhabitants. The ‘soul-complex’ is then adjured, on the one hand, to seek Liberation through the compassionate grace of the <term>Nirvāṇic</term> line of Buddhas and <term>Divinities (original: 'Devatās')</term>, and, on the other hand, to shun the particular <term>Realm (original: 'Loka')</term> which is concomitantly presented to his mental vision. 

With these Buddhas, <term>Divinities (original: 'Devatās')</term>, and <term>Realms (original: 'Lokas')</term> are associated certain <term>Causal Connections (the twelve links of dependent origination; original: 'Nidānas')</term>, <term>Constituent Factors (the five aggregates that make up a person; original: 'Skandhas')</term>, material elements, and the colors of the latter. This account appears to have suffered from corruption of the Text. Thus the <term>Causal Connections (original: 'Nidānas')</term> and <term>Constituent Factors (original: 'Skandhas')</term> are not complete. Logically, the <term>Aggregate of Consciousness (original: 'Vijñāna Skandha')</term> should go first with <term>the Illuminator (one of the five Wisdom Buddhas; original: 'Vairochana')</term>, and <term>Mind-and-Body (literally 'name and form'; original: 'Nāma-rūpa')</term> with the <term>Indestructible Being (original: 'Vajra-Sattva')</term>. Only four out of the five elements are mentioned. Ether, which is omitted, should be associated with <term>the Illuminator (original: 'Vairochana')</term> and Consciousness. The colors of the elements accord with those given in the <term>Hindu Esoteric Texts (original: 'Hindu Tantras')</term> except as regards ‘air’, to which is assigned a green color, appropriate for <term>Demigod (the jealous titans; original: 'Asuric')</term> jealousy, though it is not that of the Hindu coloration, which is smoky grey. Again, the order of the Six <term>Realms (original: 'Lokas')</term> is not the usual one, viz. first the better <term>Realms (original: 'Lokas')</term>, of <term>Gods (original: 'Devas')</term>, <term>Demigods (original: 'Asuras')</term>,

and Men, and then the <term>Worlds (realms of existence; original: 'Lokas')</term> of <term>Hungry Ghosts (spirits driven by unquenchable desire; original: 'Pretas')</term>, Brutes <note>referring to the animal kingdom</note>, and Hell. Each <term>World (original: 'Loka')</term> is characterized by its 'poison' or besetting sin, but, of these, five only are mentioned. The editor has, however, referred to corruption in the Text in some of these matters, and others I have noted on a careful analysis of the translated Text.

The peaceful <term>Deities (divine beings; original: 'Devatās')</term> follow on the sixth and seventh day, and the wrathful <term>Deities (original: 'Devatās')</term> on the eighth and subsequent days. The latter are of the terrific type, characteristic both of the Buddhist and Hindu <term>Shakta Tantras (scriptures concerning the feminine divine power; original: 'Shākta Tantras')</term>, with their <term>Fierce Forms (terrifying aspects of the divine; original: 'Bhairavas' and 'Bhairavīs')</term>, <term>Sky-goers (female spirits of wisdom and transformation; original: 'Dākinīs')</term>, <term>Female Practitioners (original: 'Yoginīs')</term>, and so on. Hinduism also makes this distinction in the nature of Divinities and interprets the wrathful orders as representative of the so-called 'destructive' power of the Supreme Lord and of his lesser manifestations; though, in truth, "God never destroys" <note>original: 'na devo nāshakah kvachit'</note>, but withdraws the Universe to Himself.

But Power, which thus dissolves the world, is ever terrible to those who are attached to the world. All <term>unrighteous action (action contrary to the cosmic law; original: 'Adharma')</term>, too, is dissolvent; and, according to the Text, the deceased's evil <term>Karma (the law of cause and effect; original: 'Karma')</term> in the <term>Cycle of Existence (the world of birth and death; original: 'Sangsāra')</term> is reflected in the <term>Liberated (relating to ultimate peace; original: 'Nirvānic')</term> line in its forms as Divinities of the Lower <term>Intermediate State (the state between death and rebirth; original: 'Bardo')</term>, who so terrify the deceased that he flees from them and sinks therefore more and more into such a state as will eventually bring him birth in one or other of the <term>Worlds (original: 'Lokas')</term>.

The Peaceful <term>Deities (original: 'Devatās')</term> are said to issue from the heart, and the Wrathful from the head. I do not, however, think that this statement necessarily lets in the <term>Yoga (spiritual discipline; original: 'Yoga')</term> doctrine of the <term>Serpent Power (the dormant spiritual energy at the base of the spine; original: 'Kundalini')</term> and the <term>Six Centers (energy points within the subtle body; original: 'Chakras')</term>, which the editor has shortly set out in Part II of the Addenda, assuming (a matter of which I have no personal knowledge) that the Tibetans both practice this <term>Yoga (original: 'Yoga')</term> and teach it in its Indian form. I myself think that the mention of the heart and head does not refer to these places as <term>Yoga (original: 'Yoga')</term>-centers, but possibly to the fact that the Peaceful Deities reflect, as stated in the Text, the love of the deceased which springs from his heart.

I make a reservation also as regards the subject of <term>Sacred Chants (spiritual syllables used in meditation; original: 'Mantras')</term>, dealt with in Part III of the Addenda. No doubt the Tibetans employ Sanskrit <term>Sacred Chants (original: 'Mantras')</term>, but such <term>Chants (original: 'Mantras')</term> are often found in

-># STATE WHEN SEEKING REBIRTH<-

...a sadly corrupt form in their books—a fact which suggests that the Tibetans feel little appreciation of the supposed sound-value of <term>Mantras (sacred sounds or chants)</term>. But whether their theory on this subject is the same in all respects as that of the Hindus I cannot say.¹ The Hindu theory, which I have elsewhere endeavoured to elucidate (compare the work *Garland of Letters*), is still on several points obscure; the subject being perhaps the most difficult of any in Hinduism. Even though Tibetan Buddhism may have <term>Mantra practice (the disciplined use of sacred sound; original: 'Mantra-Sādhana')</term>, the presentment of it is likely to differ as much as does the general substance of these two Faiths.

About the fifteenth day, passage is made into the <term>Third Intermediate State (the stage of seeking rebirth; original: 'Third Bardo')</term>, in which the deceased, if not previously liberated, seeks ‘Rebirth’. His past life has now become dim. That of the future is indicated by certain premonitory signs which represent the first movements of desire towards fulfilment. The ‘soul-complex’ takes on the colour of the <term>World (realm of existence; original: 'Loka')</term> in which it is destined to be born. If the deceased’s <term>Karma (the law of cause and effect; original: 'Karma')</term> leads him to Hell, thither he goes after the Judgement, in a subtle body which cannot be injured or destroyed, but in which he may suffer atrocious pain. Or he may go to the Heaven-world or other <term>World (original: 'Loka')</term>, to return at length and in all cases (for neither punishment nor reward are eternal) to earth, whereon only can new <term>Karma</term> be made. Such return takes place after expiation of his sins in Hell, or the expiration of the term of enjoyment in Heaven which his <term>Karma</term> has gained for him. If, however, the lot of the deceased is immediate rebirth on earth, he sees visions of mating men and women. He, at this final stage towards the awakening to earth-life, now knows that he has not a gross [body]...

<note>¹ Just as the Tibetans took over <term>Tantra (esoteric ritual practices; original: 'Tantricism')</term> from India, so, as the well-known Tibetan *Biography of Jetsün Milarepa* (Tibet’s most famous <term>Practitioner of Yoga (original: 'Yogi')</term> and Saint), for example, makes clear, they appear also to have derived various systems of <term>Union (spiritual discipline; original: 'Yoga')</term> from India, including <term>Yoga of Dissolution or Serpent Power (practices focusing on internal energy centers; original: 'Laya' or 'Kundalini Yoga')</term>. While it is undoubtedly true that many <term>Mantras</term> likewise derived from India have grown hopelessly corrupt in the Tibetan language itself, the practice of <term>Yoga of Dissolution</term> or <term>Serpent Power Yoga</term> by Tibetans seems to have been kept fairly pure, largely through oral transmission from <term>teacher (spiritual guide; original: 'guru')</term> to <term>teacher</term> rather than through written records, except for Tibetanized terminologies and methods of application. Certain Tibetan treatises on <term>Yoga</term> which the editor possesses, both in the original and in English translation, suggest this. —W. Y. E-W.</note>

# FOREWORD

body of flesh and blood. He urgently desires to have one, in order that he may again enjoy physical life on the earth-world.

The <term>Freudian psycho-analyst (a follower of Sigmund Freud's theories of the unconscious mind)</term> will find herein a remarkable passage supporting his doctrine of the aversion of the son for the father <note>This refers to the "Oedipus complex," a theory suggesting children have unconscious desires for one parent and rivalry with the other</note>. The passage says that, if the deceased is to be born as a male, the feeling of its being a male comes upon the knower, and a feeling of intense aversion for the father and attraction for the mother is begotten, and vice versa as regards birth as a female. This is, however, an old Buddhist doctrine found elsewhere. Professor <note>Louis de La Vallée Poussin (1869–1938), a renowned Belgian scholar of Buddhism</note> De la Vallée Poussin cites the following passage:

> "The mind, troubled by the desire for love, goes to the place of its destiny. Even from a great distance, it sees—through the eye born of the power of its past actions—the place of its birth; seeing there its father and mother joined together, it conceives a desire for the mother if it is male, and a desire for the father if it is female, and, conversely, hatred" <note>original French: 'L'esprit troublé par désir d'amour, il va au lieu de sa destinée...'</note> (*Buddhism: Studies and Materials*, *The Treasury of Higher Knowledge (Abhidharmakosha)*, iii. 15, p. 25).

The work cited also contains other interesting details concerning the embryo. (See, too, the same author's *The Theory of the Twelve Causes*.)

At length the deceased passes out of the <term>Bardo (the intermediate state between death and rebirth)</term> dream-world into a womb of flesh and blood, issuing thence once more into the waking state of earth-experience. This is what in English is called Re-incarnation, or Re-birth in the flesh. The Sanskrit term is <term>Saṅsāra (the continuous cycle of birth, death, and rebirth)</term>, that is, ‘rising and rising again’ (<term>Punarutpatti (the process of being born again)</term>) in the worlds of birth and death. Nothing is permanent, but all is transitory. In life, the ‘soul-complex’ is never for two consecutive moments the same, but is, like the body, in constant change. There is thus a <term>series (Santāna; a stream or continuum of consciousness)</term> of successive, and, in one sense, different states, which are in themselves but momentary. There is still a unifying bond in that each momentary state is a present transformation representative of all those which are past, as it will be the generator of all future transformations potentially involved in it.

This process is not interrupted by death. Change continues in the <term>Skandhas (the five aggregates or components that make up a person's physical and mental existence)</term> other than

# CONTINUITY OF TRANSFORMATION

the gross body which has been cast off and which undergoes changes of its own. But there is this difference: the after-death change is merely the result of the action of accumulated past <term>Karma (the law of cause and effect, where one's actions and intentions influence future outcomes)</term> and does not, as in earthly life, create new Karma, for which a physical body is necessary. (Buddhism, Hinduism, and Christianity are in agreement in holding that man's destiny is decided on Earth, though the last differs from the first two, as explained above, on the question whether there is more than one life on Earth.) There is no <term>breach <note>original Sanskrit: 'Uchchheda'</note> (the philosophical concept of total annihilation or a gap in the stream of being)</term> of consciousness, but a continuity of transformation. The Death-Consciousness is the starting-point, followed by the other states of consciousness already described. Karma at length generates a fully-formed desire or mental action. This last is followed by the consciousness taking up its abode in a suitable <note>In this context, a womb or generative environment</note> matrix, whence it is born again as a Birth-Consciousness. What is so born is not altogether different from what has gone before, because it is the present transformation of it; and has no other independent existence.

There are thus successive births of (to use Professor <note>Louis de La Vallée Poussin (1869–1938), a prominent Belgian scholar of Buddhist philosophy</note> de la Vallée Poussin's term) a 'fluid soul-complex', because the series of psychic states continues at intervals of time to enter the physical womb of living beings. It has been said by the authority cited (*Way to Nirvāṇa*, p. 85) that the birth-consciousness of a new celestial or infernal <note>Beings reborn in heavenly or hellish realms, rather than the human world</note> being makes for itself and by itself, out of unorganized matter, the body it is to inhabit. Therefore the birth of such beings will follow immediately after the death of the being which is to be reborn as an infernal or celestial being. But the case is said to be different, as a rule, where there is to be 'reincarnation', that is 'rebirth' in the flesh. Conception and birth then presuppose physical circumstances that may not be realized at the moment of the death of the being to be 're-incarnated'. In these cases and others it is alleged that the dying consciousness cannot be continued at once into the birth-consciousness of a new being. The Professor says that this difficulty is solved by those Schools which, maintaining the <term>intermediate existence <note>original Sanskrit: 'Antarābhāva'</note> (the state of being between death and the next rebirth, often called the Bardo in Tibetan)</term>, hold that the dying consciousness is continued into a short-lived

# FOREWORD

being called <term>*Gandharva* (the intermediate state or being that exists between death and rebirth)</term>, which lasts for seven days, or seven times seven days (referring to the forty-nine days of the <term>*Bardo* (the Tibetan state of existence between death and a new life)</term>). This *Gandharva* creates, with the help of the conceptional elements <note>Likely referring to the 'skandhas' or mental aggregates that make up a person's personality</note>, an embryo as soon as it can find opportunity. This doctrine, if it has been rightly understood, is apparently another and cruder version of the *Bardo* doctrine. There cannot, in any philosophic view of the doctrine of <term>*Karma* (the law of cause and effect, where one's moral actions shape future destiny)</term>, be any ‘hold up’ of what is a continuous life-process. Such process does not consist of independent sections waiting upon one another. And so a ‘soul-complex’ cannot be ready to reincarnate before the circumstances are fit for it. The law which determines that a being shall incarnate is the same as that which provides the means and conditions by, and under, which the incarnation is to take place. Nor is the body of the infernal or celestial being gross matter. This is clear from the present Text.

Dr. Evans-Wentz <note>Walter Evans-Wentz (1878–1965), the primary editor and scholar responsible for bringing these Tibetan texts to the Western world</note> raises again the debated question of the transmigration <note>The movement of a soul from one body to another after death</note> of human ‘souls’ into sub-human bodies, a process which this Text, viewed as an outward or popular teaching, seems to assume, and which is, as he points out, the general Hindu and Buddhist belief. It seems to be an irrational, though it may be a popular, belief that a human ‘soul’ can permanently inhabit a sub-human body as its own. For the body cannot exist in such disagreement with its occupant. The right doctrine appears to be that, as man has evolved through the lowest forms of being (Hinduism speaks of 8,400,000 graded kinds of births culminating in man),¹ so by misconduct and neglect to use the opportunity of manhood there can, equally, be a descent along the ‘downward path’ to the same low forms of being from which humanity has, with difficulty, emerged. The Sanskrit term <term>*Durlabham* (meaning ‘difficult to get’)</term> refers to this difficulty of securing human birth. But such descent involves (as Dr. Evans-Wentz says) the loss of the human nature and the enormous lengths of time of a creation epoch.

If the series <term>*Santāna* (the continuous flow or stream of conscious states)</term> of conscious states are determined

<page-type>text</page-type>
->WORKINGS OF ACTIONS AND CONSEQUENCES<-
<page-num>lxxxiii</page-num>

If everything is determined by past actions and their inevitable consequences <term>(*Karma*)</term>, one might ask how the freedom of choice exists which the entire text assumes by its commands to the deceased to perform certain actions or avoid others. Undoubtedly, even within a single individual, there are various competing mental tendencies <term>(*Sangskāra*)</term>. Yet the question remains: if the actions ready to bear fruit determine one's next move, then giving advice to the departed is useless. If the "soul" is truly free to choose, then it cannot be strictly determined by its past. Hinduism maintains that, despite the heavy influence of past deeds, the inner Self <term>(*Ātmā*)</term> remains essentially free. 

The answer here appears to be twofold. Aside from what follows, the instructions provided in this book may, through the power of suggestion, awaken one of several dormant tendencies that aligns with the suggested path. Furthermore, this system allows for the belief that one "soul" can assist another. Because of this, we find prayers and the transfer of spiritual merit to the deceased, much like the funeral rites for spirits <term>(*Pretashrāddha*)</term> in Hinduism, the Mass for the dead <term>(*Requiem* Mass)</term> in Catholicism, and the recitation of the opening prayer <note>original: "Fatika", likely referring to the *Al-Fatiha*, the first chapter of the Quran recited by Muslims for the deceased</note> in Islam. In these and other matters, it is claimed that one mind can influence another through ways other than the physical senses, whether before or after death. There is also a tendency in these teachings to overlook collective consequences <note>original: "collective Karma"</note> and their effects. An individual is shaped not only by their own deeds but by those of the community to which they belong. A broader question arises regarding the meaning of the doctrine of rebirth <note>original: "Re-incarnation Doctrine"</note> itself, but this is not the place to debate it.

There are many other points of interest in this remarkable book, but I must now conclude and allow the reader to discover them personally. I would, however, like to add a word regarding how this work was created. This text was fortunate to find its editor in Dr. Evans-Wentz <note>Walter Evans-Wentz (1878–1965), a scholar who spent years studying in India and Tibet</note>, whose deep knowledge and sympathy for the subject have allowed him to provide us with a very clear account. He, in turn, was fortunate to have as his teacher the translator, the late Priest Moon-Wise-Accomplisher <note>original Tibetan: *Zla-va-bsam-hgrub*, known as Lama Kazi Dawa-Samdup</note>. When I first met him, he served as the Chief Interpreter for the Great Minister <term>(*Lonchen*)</term> Satra, the Tibetan diplomat to the Government of India. He was also a member of the political staff of the Great Ocean-like Teacher <term>(*Dalai Lāma*)</term> during the latter's visit to India. At the time of—

<page-type>preface</page-type>
<page-num>lxxxiv</page-num>
# FOREWORD

At the time of his untimely and deeply mourned death, <term>Lāma</term> <note>A title for a teacher of the Dharma in Tibetan Buddhism</note> Kazi Dawa-Samdup served as the Lecturer in Tibetan at the University of Calcutta. This role, and the other appointments the translator held (which Dr. Evans-Wentz has already mentioned), clearly establish his high level of skill in both the Tibetan and English languages. I should also add that he possessed a knowledge of <term>Sanskrit</term> <note>The ancient classical language of India used in religious and philosophical texts</note>, which I found very useful when discussing the meanings of technical terms used in <term>Tibetan-Buddhist</term> doctrine and ritual.

I can, therefore, speak from personal experience regarding his scholarly attainments. I spent a great deal of time with him while he was preparing a translation for me of the <term>*Shrīchakrasambhāra Tantra*</term> <note>A foundational text of the "Highest Yoga" class of Buddhist Tantra, centered on the deity Chakrasamvara</note>, which I published as the seventh volume in the <term>*Tantrik Texts*</term> <note>A series of scholarly editions of esoteric Hindu and Buddhist scriptures published by Woodroffe</note> series (Luzac & Co.). From my own knowledge, I also wish to echo everything Dr. Evans-Wentz has said regarding this remarkable man. May their joint work achieve the success it deserves, and thus encourage Dr. Evans-Wentz to publish at least some of the other texts he tells me he has ready in his collection.

->JOHN WOODROFFE.<-

OXFORD,
*October 3, 1925.*

# INTRODUCTION <note>original: 1</note>

> 'The phenomena of life may be likened unto a dream, a phantasm, a bubble, a shadow, the glistening dew, or lightning flash; and thus they ought to be contemplated.'—The Buddha, in *The Immutable Sutra*. <note>original: "The Immutable Sutra" — likely referring to the *Vajracchedikā Prajñāpāramitā Sūtra*, commonly known as the Diamond Sutra.</note>

### I. THE IMPORTANCE OF THE *BARDO THÖDOL*

As a contribution to the science of death and of the existence after death, and of rebirth, *The Tibetan Book of the Dead*,

<note>This Introduction is—for the most part—based upon and suggested by explanatory notes which the late <term>Lāma</term> <note>A title for a high-ranking spiritual teacher in Tibetan Buddhism</note> Kazi Dawa-Samdup, the translator of the *Bardo Thödol*, dictated to the editor while the translation was taking shape, in Gangtok, Sikkim. The Lāma was of the opinion that his English rendering of the *Bardo Thödol* ought not to be published without his <term>exegetical</term> <note>Interpretive or explanatory</note> comments on the more abstruse and figurative parts of the text. This, he thought, would not only help to justify his translation, but, moreover, would accord with the wishes of his late <term>guru</term> <note>A personal spiritual guide or teacher</note> (see p. 80) with respect to all translations into a European tongue of works expository of the <term>esoteric</term> <note>Intended for or likely to be understood by only a small number of people with specialized knowledge</note> lore of the <term>Great Perfectionist School</term> <note>A translation of *Dzogchen*, the central teaching of the Nyingma school of Tibetan Buddhism, focusing on the natural, primordial state of the mind</note> into which that guru had initiated him. To this end, the translator's exegesis, based upon that of the translator's guru, was transmitted to the editor and recorded by the editor herein.</note>

The editor's task is to correlate and systematize and sometimes to expand the notes thus dictated, by incorporating such congenial matter, from widely separated sources, as in his judgement tends to make the exegesis more intelligible to the <term>Occidental</term> <note>A person from the West (Europe or the Americas)</note>, for whom this part of the book is chiefly intended.

The translator felt, too, that, without such safeguarding as this Introduction is intended to afford, the *Bardo Thödol* translation would be peculiarly liable to misinterpretation and consequent misuse, more especially by those who are inclined to be, for one reason or another, inimical to Buddhistic doctrines, or to the doctrines of his particular Sect of Northern Buddhism. He also realized how such an Introduction as is here presented might itself be subject to adverse criticism, perhaps on the ground that it appears to be the outcome of a philosophical <term>eclecticism</term> <note>The practice of deriving ideas or style from a broad and diverse range of sources</note>. However this may be, the editor can do no more than state here, as he has stated in other words in the Preface, that his aim, both herein and in the closely related annotations to the text itself, has been to present the psychology and the teachings peculiar to and related to the *Bardo Thödol* as he has been taught by qualified initiated exponents of them, who alone have the unquestioned right to explain them.

If it should be said by critics that the editor has expounded the *Bardo Thödol* doctrines from the standpoint of the Northern Buddhist who believes in them rather than from the standpoint of the Christian who perhaps would disbelieve at least some of them, the editor has no apology to offer; for he holds that there is no sound reason adducible why he should expound them in any other manner. <term>Anthropology</term> <note>The study of human societies and cultures and their development</note> is concerned with things as they are; and the hope of
