# INTRODUCTION

called, in its own language, *Bardo Thödol* <note>original: *Bardo Thödol*; literally "Liberation by Hearing in the Intermediate State"</note> ('Liberation by Hearing on the After-Death Plane'),¹ is, among the sacred books of the world, unique. As an epitomized exposition of the cardinal doctrines of the <term>Mahāyāna School</term> <note>The "Great Vehicle" of Buddhism, which emphasizes the path of the Bodhisattva who seeks enlightenment for the benefit of all sentient beings</note> of Buddhism, it is of very great importance, religiously, philosophically, and historically. As a treatise based essentially upon the <term>Occult Sciences</term> <note>Here referring to the esoteric or "hidden" spiritual practices meant for advanced practitioners</note> of the <term>Yoga Philosophy</term> <note>A system of mental and physical disciplines intended to achieve spiritual union and liberation</note>, which were fundamental in the curriculum of the great Buddhist University of <term>Nālanda</term> <note>A famous ancient center of learning in India, which flourished from the 5th to 12th century</note>, the Oxford of ancient India, it is, perhaps, one of the most remarkable works the West has ever received from the East. As a mystic manual for guidance through the Otherworld of many illusions and realms, whose frontiers are death and birth, it resembles *The Egyptian Book of the Dead* sufficiently <margin>✓</margin> to suggest some ultimate cultural relationship between the two; although we only know with certainty that the germ of the teachings, as herein made accessible to English readers, has been preserved for us by a long succession of saints and seers of the God-protected Land of the Snowy Ranges, Tibet.

### II. THE SYMBOLISM

The *Bardo Thödol* is unique in that it purports to treat rationally of the whole cycle of <term>sangśāric</term> <note>original: *sangśāric*; relating to *Samsara*, the continuous cycle of birth, death, and rebirth characterized by suffering</note> (i.e. phenomenal) existence intervening between death and birth;—the ancient doctrine of <term>karma</term> <note>The law of cause and effect where one's intent and actions influence their future</note>, or consequences (taught by Emerson as compensation), and of rebirth being accepted as the most essential laws of nature affecting human life. Often, however, its teaching appears to be quite the antithesis of rational, because much of it is recorded in an occult cipher. Dr. L. A. Waddell <note>A British explorer and author known for his 19th-century studies of Tibetan Buddhism</note> has declared, after careful research, that 'the <term>lāmas</term> <note>Spiritual teachers or masters in Tibetan Buddhism</note>

> all sincere researchers into comparative religion devoid of any religious bias ought always to be to accumulate such scientific data as will some day enable future generations of mankind to discover Truth itself—that Universal Truth in which all religions and all sects of all religions may ultimately recognize the Essence of Religion and the Catholicity of Faith.

---

¹ Mr. Talbot Mundy <note>A writer known for his adventure novels set in the East</note>, in his interesting Tibetan romance *Om*, in making reference to this title, *The Tibetan Book of the Dead*, has taken it to be a very free translation of *Bardo Thödol*. It should not, however, so be taken; it has been adopted because it seems to be the most appropriate short title for conveying to the English reader the true character of the book as a whole.

It introduces the core concepts of karma and the cycle of rebirth as the "rational" basis for the book's complex symbolic system.</summary>

# INTRODUCTION

### THE SYMBOLISM

have the keys to unlock the meaning of much of Buddha's doctrine which has been almost inaccessible to Europeans.¹

Some of the more learned <term>lāmas</term> <note>Tibetan Buddhist spiritual masters or high-ranking monks</note>, including the late <term>Lāma Kazi Dawa-Samdup</term> <note>The primary translator of this work and a pioneering scholar of Tibetan Buddhism (1868–1922)</note>, have believed that since very early times there has been a secret international symbol-code in common use among the initiates, which affords a key to the meaning of such <term>occult doctrines</term> <note>Esoteric or "hidden" teachings meant for those who have undergone specific spiritual initiations</note> as are still jealously guarded by religious fraternities in India, as in Tibet, and in China, Mongolia, and Japan.

In like manner, Occidental <note>Western</note> occultists have contended that the hieroglyphical writings of ancient Egypt and of Mexico seem to have been, in some degree, a popularized or <term>exoteric</term> <note>Teachings intended for the general public, as opposed to "esoteric" secrets</note> outgrowth of a secret language. They argue, too, that a symbol-code was sometimes used by Plato and other Greek philosophers, in relation to <term>Pythagorean and Orphic lore</term> <note>Ancient Greek mystical traditions focused on the soul, reincarnation, and mathematical harmony</note>; that throughout the Celtic world the Druids conveyed all their esoteric teachings symbolically; that the use of parables, as in the sermons of Jesus and of the Buddha, and of other Great Teachers, illustrates the same tendency; and that through works like *Aesop's Fables*, and the miracle and mystery plays of medieval Europe, many of the old Oriental symbols have been introduced into the modern literatures of the West.²

---

¹ L. A. Waddell, *The Buddhism of Tibet or Lāmaism* (London, 1895), p. 17.

² There is some sound evidence for supposing that one source of the moral philosophy underlying certain of the *Aesop's Fables* (and, also, by way of comparison, of the Indian *Panchatantra* and *Hitopadesha* <note>Two famous ancient Indian collections of animal fables intended to teach wisdom and worldly conduct</note>) may yet be shown to have been such primitive Oriental folk-tales about animals and animal symbols as scholars now think helped to shape the *Jātaka Tales* <note>Stories concerning the various previous births of the Buddha in both human and animal form</note> concerning the various births of the Buddha (cf. *The Jātaka*, ed. by E. B. Cowell, Cambridge, 1895-1907). Similarly, the Christian mystery plays contain symbolism so much akin to that found in mystery plays still flourishing under ecclesiastical patronage throughout Tibet and the neighbouring territories of Northern Buddhism as to point to another stream of Orientalism having come into Europe (cf. *Three Tibetan Mysteries*, ed. by H. The apparent Romanist canonization of the Buddha, under the medieval character of <term>St. Jehoshaphat</term> <note>A legendary Christian saint whose life story was actually a rewritten version of the Buddha's biography, traveling from India through Persia and Arabia to Europe</note>, is an additional instance of how things Eastern seem to have become things Western (cf. *Baralām and Yĕwâsĕf*, ed. by E. # INTRODUCTION

Be this as it may, it is certain that none of the great systems of ancient thought, nor even vernacular <note>native or common spoken languages</note> literatures, have always found the ordinary work-a-day language of the world adequate to express transcendental doctrines or even to bring out the full significance of moral maxims.

The lamb, the dragon (or serpent), the dove above the altar, the triangle enclosing the all-seeing eye (common to Freemasonry as well), the sacred fish-symbol, the ever-burning fire, or the image of the risen sun upon the receptacle for the consecrated wafer in the Roman Mass, the architectural symbols and the orientation of church and cathedral, the cross itself, and even the colours and designs of the robes of priest and bishop and pope, are a few of the silent witnesses of the survival in the modern Christian churches of the symbolism of paganism. But the key to the interpretation of the inner significance of almost all such Christianized symbols was unconsciously thrown away: uninitiated ecclesiastics, gathered together in heresy-seeking councils, having regarded that primitive Christianity, so deeply involved in symbolism, called <term>Gnosticism</term> <note>An early mystical form of Christianity that emphasized personal spiritual knowledge (gnosis) over orthodox faith</note>, as 'Oriental imagery gone mad', repudiated it as being 'heretical', whereas from its own point of view it was merely <term>esoteric</term> <note>intended for or likely to be understood by only a small number of people with a specialized knowledge</note>.

Similarly, <term>Northern Buddhism</term> <note>Also known as Mahayana Buddhism, prevalent in Tibet, China, and Japan</note>, to which symbolism is so vital, has been condemned by Buddhists of the <term>Southern School</term> <note>Also known as Theravada Buddhism, prevalent in Sri Lanka, Burma, and Thailand</note> for claiming to be the custodian of an esoteric doctrine, for the most part orally transmitted by recognized initiates, generation by generation, direct from the <term>Buddha</term> <note>Siddhartha Gautama, the Awakened One</note>—as well as for teaching (as, for example, in the *Lotus of the True Law* <note>original: "Saddharma-Puṇḍarika", one of the most influential scriptures in Mahayana Buddhism</note>) recorded doctrines not in agreement with doctrines contained

---

*Thödol* <note>The "Hearing" or "Liberation" text</note> and the Egyptian *Book of the Dead*; and, in order to show this, a few of the most striking passages, found in the *Art of Dying* <note>original: "De Arte Moriendi", a genre of medieval Christian literature providing guidance on a "good death"</note> cycle, which parallel textually certain parts of the *Bardo Thödol* <note>The Tibetan Book of the Dead</note>, have been added in foot-notes to the *Bardo Thödol* translation from Mr. Comper's excellent edition in *The Book of the Craft of Dying*.

*Buddhist and Christian Gospels* (Philadelphia, 1908), a pioneer study of the remarkable parallelism which exists between the texts of the *New Testament* and the texts of the Buddhist Canon, by Mr. A. J. Edmunds, suggests, likewise, that one of the most promising fields of research, as yet almost virgin, lies in a study of just such correspondences between Eastern and Western thought and literature as is suggested in this note.

It highlights the historical rejection of "esoteric" interpretations and notes the textual parallels between Tibetan, Egyptian, and Medieval Christian death rites.</summary>

# 'ESOTERIC BUDDHISM'

in the <term>Three Baskets</term> <note>original: *Ti-Pitaka* (Pali) or *Tri-Pitaka* (Sanskrit); the foundational collection of Buddhist scriptures</note>, the collection of scriptures known as the Pali Canon. And yet, though the Southern Buddhist commonly assumes that there cannot be any but a literal interpretation of the Buddha's teachings, the Pali Scriptures contain many parables and metaphorical expressions, some of which the <term>Tibetan teachers</term> <note>original: *lāmas*</note> regard as symbolical and confirmatory of their own esoteric tradition, and to which they thus claim to hold—perhaps not without good reason—the initiate's key.

The Tibetan teachers grant that the Three Baskets ('Three *Pitakas*', or Baskets [of the Law]) are, as the Southern Buddhist holds, the recorded Word (or Doctrine) of the <term>Ancients</term> <note>original: *Theravāda*</note>; but they claim that the Baskets do not contain all the Word, that the Baskets lack much of the Buddha's <term>meditative</term> <note>original: *yogic*</note> teachings, and that it is chiefly these teachings which, in many instances, have been handed down esoterically to the present day. 'Esoteric Buddhism', as it has come to be called—rightly or wrongly—seems to depend in large measure upon 'ear-whispered' doctrines of this character, conveyed according to long-established and inviolable rule, from <term>spiritual teacher</term> <note>original: *guru*</note> to <term>disciple</term> <note>original: *shishya*</note>, by word of mouth alone.

The Pali Canon records that the Buddha held no doctrine secretly 'in a closed fist' (cf. *The Great Scripture of the Buddha's Passing*, from the *Collection of Long Discourses, Volume 2* <note>original: *Mahā Parinibbāṇa Sūttanta*, *Dīgha Nikāya II*</note>), that is to say, withheld no essential doctrine from the members of the <term>Spiritual Community</term> <note>original: *Sangha*</note>, just as no spiritual teacher nowadays withholds a doctrine necessary for the spiritual enlightenment of his initiated or accepted disciples. This, however, is far from implying that all such teachings were intended to be set down in writing for the uninitiated and worldly multitude, or that they ever were so recorded in any of the Canons. The Buddha Himself wrote down nothing of His teachings, and His disciples who after His death compiled the Buddhist Scriptures may not have recorded therein all that their Master taught them. If they did not, and there are, therefore, as the Tibetan teachers contend, certain unwritten teachings of the Buddha which have never been taught to those who were not of the Spiritual Community, then there is, undoubtedly, an extra-canonical, or esoteric, Buddhism. An esoteric Buddhism thus conceived is not, however, to be

<summary>The author discusses the existence of "Esoteric Buddhism," arguing that while the written Pali Canon is essential, it likely lacks the oral "ear-whispered" meditative teachings preserved by Tibetan masters.</summary>

<keywords>Esoteric Buddhism, Three Baskets, Tibetan Teachers, Oral Tradition, Pali Canon, Spiritual Community, Meditative Teachings, Guru-Disciple relationship</keywords>

--- Page 91 ---
<meta>This page concludes the argument that "Esoteric" or hidden Buddhism is not a contradiction of the public teachings, but rather their advanced completion. It introduces the symbolic significance of the forty-nine days in the state between death and rebirth.</meta>

regarded as in any wise in disagreement with canonical, or public, Buddhism, but as being related to it as higher mathematics are to lower mathematics, or as being the apex of the pyramid of the whole of Buddhism.

In short, the evidence provided gives much substantial support to the claim of the <term>Tibetan teachers</term> <note>original: *lāmas*</note>, to whom we refer, that there is—as <term>The Great Liberation Through Hearing in the Intermediate State</term> <note>original: *Bardo Thödol*</note> appears to suggest—an unrecorded body of orally transmitted Buddhistic teachings complementary to canonical Buddhism.¹

### III. THE INNER SIGNIFICANCE OF THE FORTY-NINE DAYS OF THE INTERMEDIATE STATE <note>original: *Bardo*</note>

Turning now to our text itself, we find that structurally it is founded upon the symbolical number Forty-nine, the square of the sacred number Seven; for, according to hidden teachings common to Northern Buddhism and to that Higher Hinduism which the Hindu-born <term>Enlightenment-being</term> <note>original: *Bodhisattva*</note> Who became the Buddha Gautama, the Reformer of the Lower Hinduism and the Codifier of the Secret Lore, never repudiated, there are seven worlds or seven degrees of <term>illusion</term> <note>original: *Māyā*</note>² within the <term>cycle of rebirth</term> <note>original: *Sangsāra*</note>,³ constituted as seven globes of a planetary chain. On each globe there are seven rounds of evolution, making the forty-nine (seven times seven) stations of active existence. As in the

---

¹ It is probably unnecessary for the editor to remind his friends who follow the <term>Way of the Elders</term> <note>original: *Theravāda* Buddhism</note> of the Southern School that, in preparing this Introduction, his aim has necessarily been to present Buddhism chiefly from the standpoint of the Northern Buddhism of the <term>Oral Lineage Sect</term> <note>original: *Kargyutpa Sect*; see page 79</note>, by which the *Bardo Thödol* is accepted as a sacred book and to which the translator belonged. Although the Southern Buddhist may not agree with the *Bardo Thödol* teachings in their entirety, he will, nevertheless, be very apt to find them, in most essentials, based upon doctrines common to all Schools and Sects of Buddhism; and he may even find those of them with which he disagrees interesting and possibly provocative of a reconsideration of certain of his own antagonistic beliefs.

² <term>Illusion</term> <note>original: *Māyā*</note>, the Sanskrit equivalent of the Tibetan <term>magical display</term> <note>original: *Gyūma* (*Sgyūma*)</note>, means a magical or illusory show, with direct reference to the phenomena of Nature. In a higher sense, in the priestly tradition of India <note>original: Brāhmanism</note>, it refers to the <term>Divine Energy</term> <note>original: *Shakti*</note> of the Supreme Spirit <note>original: *Brāhman*; the *Ain Soph* of Judaism</note>.

³ The Sanskrit term <term>Cycle of Existence</term> <note>original: *Sangsāra* (or *Saṁsāra*)</note>, Tibetan <term>The Circle</term> <note>original: *Khorva* (*Hkhorva*)</term>, refers to the phenomenal universe itself, its antithesis being <term>Extinction of Suffering</term> <note>original: *Nirvāṇa*; Tibetan: *Myang-hdas*</note>, which is beyond phenomena (cf. pp. 67-8).

->THE ESOTERICISM<-

embryonic state in the human species the fetus passes through every form of organic structure from the amoeba to man, the highest mammal, so in the state after death—the embryonic state of the psychic world—the Knower or principle of consciousness, before its re-emergence in gross matter, similarly experiences purely psychic conditions. In other words, in both these interdependent embryonic processes—the one physical, the other psychical—the evolutionary and the involutionary achievements, corresponding to the forty-nine stations of existence, are passed through.

Similarly, the forty-nine days of the <term>Intermediate State</term> <note>original: *Bardo*</note> may also be symbolical of the Forty and Nine Powers of the Mystery of the Seven Vowels. In Hindu mythology, from which much of the <term>Intermediate State</term> <note>original: *Bardo*</note> symbolism originated, these Vowels were the Mystery of the Seven Fires and their forty-nine sub-divisional fires or aspects. They are also represented by the <term>auspicious signs</term> <note>original: *Svastika*</note> upon the crowns of the seven heads of the Serpent of Eternity of the Northern Buddhist Mysteries, which originated in ancient India. In <term>Hermetic writings</term> <note>Western esoteric teachings attributed to Hermes Trismegistus</note> they are the seven zones of after-death, or <term>Intermediate State</term> <note>original: *Bardo*</note>, experiences, each symbolizing the eruption in the Intermediate State of a particular seven-fold element of the complex principle of consciousness, thus giving the consciousness-principle forty-nine aspects, or fires, or fields of manifestation¹.

The number seven has long been a sacred number among <term>ancient Indo-European</term> <note>original: Aryan</note> and other races. Its use in the *Book of Revelation* by John illustrates this, as does the conception of the seventh day being regarded as holy. In Nature, the number seven governs the periodicity and phenomena of life, as, for example, in the series of chemical elements, in the physics of sound and color, and it is upon the number forty-nine, or seven times seven, that <term>The Great Liberation Through Hearing in the Intermediate State</term> <note>original: *Bardo Thödol*</note> is thus scientifically based.

->INTRODUCTION<-

### IV. THE ESOTERIC SIGNIFICANCE OF THE FIVE ELEMENTS

Likewise, in a very striking manner, the esoteric teachings concerning the Five Elements, as symbolically expounded in the <term>Liberation Through Hearing in the Intermediate State</term> <note>original: *Bardo Thödol*</note>, parallel, for the most part, certain of the teachings of Western Science, as the following interpretation, based upon that made by the translator himself, indicates:

In the First Round <note>In Theosophical and esoteric cosmology, a "Round" refers to a large cycle of evolutionary time during which life develops across a planetary chain.</note> of our Planet, one element alone—Fire—was evolved. In the fire-mist, which, in accordance with the <term>law of cause and effect</term> <note>original: *karmic law*</note> governing the <term>Cycle of Existence</term> <note>original: *Sangsāra*</note>, or cosmos, assumed a rotary motion and became a blazing globular body of undifferentiated primeval forces, all the other elements lay in embryo. Life first manifested itself clothed in robes of fire; and man, if we conceive him as then existing, was incarnate—as the <term>fire-spirits</term> <note>original: *Salamanders*</note> of medieval occultism were believed to be—in a body of fire. In the Second Round, as the Element Fire assumed definite form, the Element Air separated from it and enwrapped the embryonic Planet as a shell covers an egg. The body of man, and of all organic creatures, thereupon became a compound of fire and air. In the Third Round, as the Planet, bathed in the Element Air and fanned by it, abated its fiery nature, the Element Water came forth from the vaporous air. In the Fourth Round, in which the Planet still is, air and water neutralized the activities of their Parent Fire; and the Fire, bringing forth the Element Earth, became encrusted with it. Esoterically, the same teachings are said to be conveyed by the old Hindu myth of the churning of the <term>Ocean of Milk</term> <note>original: *Sea of Milk*; a reference to the *Samudra manthan*, a central myth in Hindu cosmology where the gods churn the ocean to obtain the nectar of immortality.</note>, which was the Fire-Mist, whence came, like butter, the solid earth. Upon the earth, so formed, the gods are credited with having fed; or, in other words, they, hankering after existence in gross physical bodies, became incarnated on this Planet and so became the Divine Progenitors of the human race.

In the <term>Intermediate State</term> <note>original: *Bardo*</note>, on the first four days these Four Elements manifest themselves, or dawn upon the deceased, in their primordial form, although not in their true occult order.¹

---

¹ It is held, too, that whereas from the Five <term>Buddhas of Meditation</term> <note>original: *Dhyāni Buddhas*</note>, as in our text,

->THE ESOTERICISM<-

The Fifth Element, Ether, in its primal form, symbolized as "the green light-path of the <term>Wisdom of Perfected Actions</term>" <note>In the Bardo teachings, this refers to *Amogha-Siddhi*, though the text here relates Ether to Vairochana; these associations can vary between different tantric traditions.</note>, does not dawn, for, as the text explains, the <term>Enlightenment</term> <note>original: *Bodhic*</note> Faculty of the consciousness of the deceased has not been perfectly developed.

The Ether Element, like the <term>aggregate of matter</term> <note>original: *fire-mist*</note>, is personified in <term>Vairochana</term> <note>The "Illuminator," the central Buddha of the five Dhyānī Buddhas</note>, He Who in Shapes makes visible all things. The psychical attribute of the Ether Element is—to render the <term>Tibetan Buddhist</term> <note>original: *lāmaic*</note> conception in the language of the psychology of the West—that of the subconsciousness; and the subconsciousness, as a transcendental consciousness higher than the normal consciousness in mankind, and as yet normally undeveloped, is—as the vehicle for the manifestation of the <term>Enlightenment</term> <note>original: *Bodhic*</note> Faculty—believed to be destined to become the active consciousness of the humanity of the <term>Fifth Round</term> <note>A future stage of human evolution in esoteric cosmology where spiritual faculties become fully realized.</note>. The memory-records of all past experiences throughout the many states of existence in the <term>Cycle of Rebirth</term> <note>original: *sangsāric*</note> being latent in the subconsciousness, as the Buddha's own teachings imply, the Fifth Round races in whom it becomes active will thus be able to recall all their past existences. In place of faith or mere belief, Man will then possess Knowledge, will come to know himself in the sense implied by the Mysteries of ancient Greece; he will realize the unreality of existence in the <term>Cycle of Rebirth</term> <note>original: *sangsāric*</note>, attaining Enlightenment and Emancipation from the <term>Cycle of Suffering</term> <note>original: *Sangsāra*</note>, from all the Elements; and this will come as a normal process of human evolution. It is, however, the aim in all schools of Indian and Tibetan <term>Spiritual Discipline</term> <note>original: *Yoga*</note> alike—as in the <term>Liberation Through Hearing in the Intermediate State</term> <note>original: *Bardo Thödol*</note>—to outstrip this tedious process of normal evolution and win Freedom even now.

In the body of man as he is—in our present Fourth <insert>Round</insert> emanate the five elements—ether, or aggregate of matter (<term>Vairochana</term>), air, or aggregate of volition (<term>Amogha-Siddhi</term>), fire, or aggregate of feelings (<term>Amitābha</term>), water, or aggregate of consciousness (<term>Vajra-Sattva</term>, esoterically as a reflex of <term>Akṣhobhya</term>), and earth, or aggregate of touch (<term>Ratna-Sambhava</term>)—from the <term>Primordial Buddha</term> <note>original: *Ādi-Buddha*</note> (from whom, according to the <term>Primordial Buddha</term> School, the Five <term>Meditation</term> <note>original: *Dhyānī*</note> Buddhas themselves emanate) emanates the sixth element, which is <term>mind</term> <note>original: *manas*</note>. <term>Vajra-Sattva</term> <note>The "Diamond Being," representing the purity of the mind</note>, as an esoteric deity, sometimes occupies (as does Vairochana)—according to the School and ritual—the place of the <term>Primordial Buddha</term>, and is then synonymous with him.

Round—there are four kingdoms of living creatures: (1) those of the Element of Fire, (2) those of the Element of Air, (3) those of the Element of Water, and (4) those of the Element of Earth. Over this collective life of innumerable millions of lives, man is king. If he is a Great King, filled with the transcendent consciousness of the triumphant <term>spiritual practitioner</term> <note>original: *Yogī*</note> (or Saint), to him the countless multitude of his elemental subjects reveal themselves in their true nature and place in his hand the Sceptre—symbolized by the Tibetan <term>ritual scepter</term> <note>original: *dorje*, meaning "thunderbolt" or "indestructible diamond"</note>—of Universal Dominion over Matter. Then, indeed, he is Lord of Nature, becoming in his turn a Ruler by Divine Right, a <term>Universal Emperor</term> <note>original: *Chakravartin*</note>, God and Creator.¹

### V. THE WISDOM TEACHINGS

Also expressed in symbolic language are the fundamental hidden doctrines of the <term>Liberation Through Hearing in the Intermediate State</term> <note>original: *Bardo Thödol*</note>, which the translator called "The Wisdom Teachings." These—which are essential <term>Great Vehicle</term> <note>original: *Mahāyāna*</note> doctrines—may be outlined as follows:

*The Voidness.*—In all Tibetan systems of <term>meditative discipline</term> <note>original: *yoga*</note>, realization of the <term>Voidness</term> <note>original: *Shūnyatā*; Tibetan: *Stong-pa-ñid*</note> is the one great aim; for to realize it is to attain the unconditioned <term>Truth Body</term> <note>original: *Dharma-Kāya*; Tibetan: *Chos-sku*</note>. This is the primary state of being uncreated, the otherworldly <term>Enlightened</term> <note>original: *Bodhic*</note> All-Consciousness known as Buddhahood. Realization of the Voidness (known as *Suññatā* in the <term>Pali</term> language) is the aim of <term>followers of the Southern Tradition</term> <note>original: *Theravādists*</note> as well.

*The Three Bodies.*—The <term>Truth Body</term> <note>original: *Dharma-Kāya*</note> is the highest of the <term>Three Bodies</term> <note>original: *Tri-Kāya*; Tibetan: *Sku-gsum*</note> of the Buddha and of all Buddhas and beings who have attained Perfect Enlightenment. The other two bodies are the <term>Body of Perfect Bliss</term> <note>original: *Sambhoga-Kāya*; Tibetan: *Longs-spyod-rzogs-sku*</note> and the <term>Emanation Body</term> <note>original: *Nirmāṇa-Kāya*</note>

---

¹ Manu, in *The Laws* (xii, 10–11), says: ‘He, whose firm understanding obtains a command over his words, a command over his thoughts, and a command over his whole body, may justly be called a Triple-Commander.

‘The man who exerts this triple self-command with respect to all animated creatures, wholly subduing both lust and wrath, shall by those means attain <term>Supreme Bliss</term> <note>original: *Beatitude*</note>.’—(Cf. trans. by Sir William Jones.)

<meta>This page continues the technical breakdown of Buddhist metaphysics, moving from the definition of the "Voidness" to the "Three Bodies of the Buddha" (Trikāya). It explains how the absolute, formless reality manifests into the world of form for the sake of helping living beings.</meta>

<header>THE WISDOM TEACHINGS</header>

‘Divine Body of Incarnation’ <note>Tibetan: *Sprul-pahi-sku*, pronounced *Tülpai-ku*; Sanskrit: *Nirmāna-Kāya*</note>.

The <term>Truth Body</term> <note>original: *Dharma-Kāya*</note> is symbolized—for all human word-concepts are inadequate to describe the Qualityless—as an infinite ocean, calm and without a wave, whence arise mist-clouds and rainbow, which symbolize the <term>Enjoyment Body</term> <note>original: *Sambhoga-Kāya*</note>; and the clouds, enhaloed in the glory of the rainbow, condensing and falling as rain, symbolize the <term>Emanation Body</term> <note>original: *Nirmāna-Kāya*</note>.¹

The <term>Truth Body</term> is the primordial, formless <term>Enlightenment</term> <note>original: *Bodhi*</note>, which is true experience freed from all error or inherent or accidental obscuration. In it lies the essence of the Universe, including both the <term>Cycle of Suffering</term> <note>original: *Sangsāra*</note> and <term>Liberation</term> <note>original: *Nirvāna*</note>, which, as states or conditions of the two poles of consciousness, are, in the last analysis, in the realm of the pure intellect, identical.²

In other words, the <term>Truth Body</term> <note>literally "Law Body"</note> being <term>Essential Wisdom</term> <note>original: *Bodhi*</note> unmodified, the <term>Enjoyment Body</term> <note>literally "Compensation Body," or "Adorned Body"</note> embodies, as in the Five <term>Meditation Buddhas</term> <note>original: *Dhyāni Buddhas*</note>, Reflected or Modified Wisdom, and the <term>Emanation Body</term> <note>literally "Changeable Body," or "Transformed Body"</note> embodies, as in the Human Buddhas, Practical or Incarnate Wisdom.³

¹ Mr. Atal Bihari Ghosh <note>see our Preface, page x</note> has added here the following comment: ‘The word <term>Law or Duty</term> <note>original: *Dharma*</note> is derived from the verb-root <term>to support</term> <note>original: *Dhri*</note>, meaning "to Support" or "to Uphold". This Law is that which upholds or supports the Universe, as also the individual. It is in mankind Right Conduct, the result of True Knowledge. Truth according to <term>the Vedic tradition</term> <note>original: *Brāhmanism*</note> is the Absolute, is <term>Liberation</term> <note>original: *Moksha* or *Nirvāna*</note>. <term>Enjoyment</term> <note>original: *Sambhoga*</note> is the Life of Experience. <term>Creation</term> <note>original: *Nirmāna*</note> is the Process of Building. In the Vedic scheme, the Law is the first thing needed. Then comes <term>Purpose/Wealth</term> <note>original: *Artha*</note>, which corresponds with the process of Creation. After this comes Enjoyment; and the last is Liberation.’

² ‘Whatever is visible and invisible, whether the <term>World of Suffering</term> or <term>Liberation</term>, is at base one [that is, the <term>Voidness</term> <note>original: *Shūnyatā*</note>], with two Paths [<term>Ignorance</term> <note>original: *Avidyā*</note> and <term>Knowledge</term> <note>original: *Vidyā*</note>] and two ends. . . . The Foundation of all is uncreated and independent, uncompounded and beyond mind and speech. Of It neither the word Liberation nor Suffering may be said’—*The Good Wishes of the Primordial Buddha* <note>original: *Adi-Buddha*</note>, 1-2 <note>compare the late Lama Kazi Dawa-Samdup’s translation, *Tantric Texts*, volume 7, London, 1919</note>. The Emptiness, synonymous with the <term>Truth Body</term>, is thus beyond all mental concepts, beyond the finite mind with all its imaginings and use of such ultimate terms of the dualistic world as Liberation and Suffering.

³ Compare L. A. Waddell, <italic>in the work cited</italic> <note>original: *op. cit.*</note>, pages 127, 347.

It highlights that in the highest state of reality, the cycle of rebirth and liberation are essentially identical.</summary>

# INTRODUCTION

The Uncreated, the Unshaped, the Unmodified is the <term>Truth Body</term> <note>original: *Dharma-Kāya*</note>. The Offspring, the Modification of the Un-modified, the manifestation of all perfect attributes in one body, is the <term>Enjoyment Body</term> <note>original: *Sambhoga-Kāya*</note>: ‘The embodiment of all that is wise, merciful and loving in the <term>Truth Body</term>—as clouds on the surface of the heavens or a rainbow on the surface of the clouds—is said to be the <term>Enjoyment Body</term>.’¹ The condensation and differentiation of the One Body as many is the <term>Emanation Body</term> <note>original: *Nirmāṇa-Kāya*</note>, or the Divine Incarnations among sentient beings, that is to say, among beings immersed in the Illusion called the <term>Cycle of Existence</term> <note>original: *Saṅgsāra*</note>, in phenomena, in worldly existence. All enlightened beings who are reborn in this or in any other world with full consciousness, as workers for the betterment of their fellow creatures, are said to be <term>Emanation Body</term> incarnates.

With the <term>Truth Body</term> Tantric Buddhism associates the Primordial Buddha <term>Universal Goodness</term> <note>original: *Samanta-Bhadra*; Tibetan: *Kün-tu-bzang-po*, pronounced *Kün-tu-zang-po*</note>, Who is without Beginning or End, the

---

has explained the <term>Doctrine of the Three Bodies</term> <note>original: *Tri-Kāya*</note> in *The Awakening of Faith*, translation by T. Suzuki (Chicago, 1900, pp. 99–103), as follows:
> ‘Because All <term>Those Who Have Thus Gone</term> <note>original: *Tathāgatas*</note> are the <term>Truth Body</term> <note>original: *Dharmakāya*</note> itself, are the <term>highest truth</term> <note>original: *paramārthasatya*</note> itself, and have nothing to do with <term>relative reality</term> <note>original: *saṃvṛtti-satya*</note> and compulsory actions; whereas the seeing, hearing, &c. [i.e. the particularizing senses], of the sentient being diversify [on its own account] the activity of the <term>Buddhas</term>.
> ‘Now this activity [in another word, the <term>Truth Body</term>] has a twofold aspect. The first one depends on the phenomena-particularizing consciousness, by means of which the activity is conceived by the minds of common people, <term>Hearers</term> <note>original: *Śrāvakas*</note>, and <term>Solitary Realizers</term> <note>original: *Pratyekabuddhas*</note>. This aspect is called the <term>Body of Transformation</term> <note>original: *Nirmāṇakāya*</note>.
> ‘But as the beings of this class do not know that the <term>Body of Transformation</term> is merely the shadow [or reflection] of their own <term>evolving-consciousness</term> <note>original: *pravṛtti-vijñāna*</note>, they imagine that it comes from some external sources, and so they give it a corporeal limitation. But the <term>Body of Transformation</term> [or what amounts to the same thing, the <term>Truth Body</term>] has nothing to do with limitation and measurement.
> ‘The second aspect [of the <term>Truth Body</term>] depends on the <term>activity-consciousness</term> <note>original: *karma-vijñāna*</note> by means of which the activity is conceived by the minds of <term>Enlightenment-seekers</term> <note>original: *Bodhisattvas*</note> while passing from their <term>first aspiration</term> <note>original: *cittotpāda*</note> stage up to the height of <term>Enlightenment</term> <note>literally "Bodhisattvahood"</note>. This is called the <term>Body of Bliss</term> <note>original: *Sambhogakāya*</note>. ...
> ‘The <term>Truth Body</term> can manifest itself in various corporeal forms just because it is the real essence of them’ (cf. p. 228⁸).

¹ Cf. A. Avalon, *Tantric Texts*, vii (London and Calcutta, 1919), pp. 36 n., 41 n.

Source of all Truth, the All-Good Father of the Tibetan Buddhist <note>original: "Lāmaistic"</note> Faith. In this same highest Buddha realm, Tibetan Buddhism places the **Thunderbolt-Holder** <note>original: *Vajra-Dhāra*; Tibetan: *Rdorje-Chang*</note>, ‘the Divine Expounder of the Mystic Doctrine called the **Diamond Vehicle** <note>original: *Vajra Yāna*; Tibetan: *Rdorje Theg-pa*</note> or the **Vehicle of Secret Formulas** <note>original: *Mantra Yāna*</note>’; and also the Buddha of **Boundless Light** <note>original: *Amitābha*; Tibetan: *Hod-dpag-med*</note>, who is the Source of Life Eternal. In the <term>Enjoyment Body</term> <note>The body of celestial bliss and perfect attributes; original: *Sambhoga-Kāya*</note> are placed the Five <term>Meditative Buddhas</term> <note>original: *Dhyāni Buddhas*</note>, the Lotus <term>Blood-Drinkers</term> <note>Wrathful deities representing the transformation of passions; original: *Herukas*</note>, and the Peaceful and Wrathful Deities, all of whom will appear in the visions of the <term>Intermediate State</term> <note>The state between death and rebirth; original: *Bardo*</note>. With the <term>Emanation Body</term> <note>The physical manifestation of enlightenment in the world; original: *Nirmāṇa-Kāya*</note> is associated the **Lotus-Born** <note>The 8th-century master who brought Buddhism to Tibet; original: *Padma Sambhava*</note>, who, being the first teacher in Tibet to expound the *Liberation Through Hearing in the Intermediate State* <note>original: *Bardo Thödol*</note>, is the Great **Spiritual Teacher** <note>original: *Guru*</note> for all devotees who follow these teachings.

The opinion commonly held by those not initiated into the higher teachings of the Tibetan monks, that Northern Buddhism recognizes in the <term>Primordial Buddha</term> <note>original: *Ādi-Buddha*</note> a Supreme Deity, is apparently incorrect. The translator held that the <term>Primordial Buddha</term>, and all deities associated with the <term>Truth Body</term> <note>The ultimate, unmanifested nature of reality; original: *Dharma-Kāya*</note>, are not to be regarded as personal deities, but as Personifications of primordial and universal forces, laws, or spiritual influences, which sustain—as the sun sustains the earth’s physical life—the divine nature of all sentient creatures in all worlds, and make man’s liberation from all <term>cyclic existences</term> <note>The repetitive cycle of birth, death, and rebirth; original: *saṅgsāric*</note> possible:

> ‘In the boundless panorama of the existing and visible universe, whatever shapes appear, whatever sounds vibrate, whatever radiances illuminate, or whatever consciousnesses perceive, all are the play or manifestation of the <term>Three Bodies</term> <note>original: *Tri-Kāya*</note>, the Three-fold Principle of the Cause of All Causes, the Primordial Trinity. Pervading all is the All-Pervading Essence of Spirit, which is Mind. It is uncreated, impersonal, self-existing, immaterial, and indestructible.’

->(Lāma Kazi Dawa-Samdup.)<-

# INTRODUCTION

...the higher Buddhism of the Northern School; the <term>Outer Trinity</term> <note>original: "Exoteric Trinity"</note> being, as in the Southern School, the **Awakened One** <note>original: *Buddha*</note>, the **Universal Law** <note>original: *Dharma*</note> (or Scriptures), and the **Community** <note>original: *Saṅgha*</note> (or Priesthood). Regarded in this way—the one trinitarian doctrine as inner <note>original: "esoteric"</note>, the other as outer <note>original: "exoteric"</note>—there are direct correspondences between the two Trinities. Detailed and comprehensive understanding of the **Doctrine of the Three Bodies** <note>original: *Tri-Kāya*</note>, so the <term>high priests</term> <note>original: *lāmas*</note> teach, is the privilege of initiates, who, alone, are fitted to grasp and to realize it.

The translator himself regarded the **Doctrine of the Three Bodies** <note>original: *Tri-Kāya*</note> as having been transmitted by a long and unbroken line of initiates, some Indian, some Tibetan, direct from the days of the **Awakened One**. He considered that the **Awakened One**, having re-discovered it, was merely its Transmitter from preceding **Awakened Ones**; that it was handed on orally, from **spiritual teacher** <note>original: *guru*</note> to **spiritual teacher**, and not committed to writing until comparatively recent times, when Buddhism began to decay, and there were not always sufficient living **teachers** to transmit it in the old way. The theory of Western scholars, that simply because a doctrine is not found recorded before a certain time it consequently did not exist previously, he—as an initiate—laughed at; and the rather strenuous efforts of Christian apologists to claim for the **Doctrine of the Three Bodies** a Christian origin he held, likewise, to be wholly untenable. He had been a close and sympathetic student of Christianity; and, as a young man, he had been much sought after by Christian missionaries, who looked upon him, with his remarkable learning and superior social standing, as an unusually desirable subject for conversion. He carefully examined their claims, and then rejected them, on the ground that, in his opinion, Christianity, as presented by them, is but an imperfect Buddhism, that the Buddhist missionaries sent by King **Ashoka** <note>original: *Aṣokan*</note> to Asia Minor and Syria, as to Alexandria¹, must have profoundly influenced Christianity through some such probable connecting link as the Essenes, that, if Jesus were an historical character, He, being—as the High Priest <note>original: *Lāma*</note> interpreted the Jesus of the *New Testament* clearly to be—an **Enlightened Being** <note>original: *Bodhisattva*</note> (that is <note>original: *i. e.*</note>, a Candidate for Buddhahood), was, undoubtedly, well acquainted with...

## THE WISDOM TEACHINGS 15

Buddhist ethics, and taught them, as in the Sermon on the Mount.

The **Doctrine of the Three Bodies** <note>original: *Tri-Kāya*</note> conveys the inner <note>original: esoteric</note> teachings concerning the Path of the Teachers, their descent from the Higher to the Lower, from the threshold of **Spiritual Emancipation** <note>original: *Nirvāna*</note> to the **Cycle of Rebirth** <note>original: *Sangsāra*</note>; and progression from the Lower to the Higher, from the **Cycle of Rebirth** <note>original: *Sangsāra*</note> to **Spiritual Emancipation** <note>original: *Nirvāna*</note>, is symbolized by the **Five Meditation Buddhas** <note>original: *Dhyāni Buddhas*</note>, each personifying a universal divine attribute. Contained in the **Five Meditation Buddhas** <note>original: *Dhyāni Buddhas*</note> lies the Sacred Way leading to At-one-ment <note>A term used here to mean "becoming one" or reconciliation with the divine</note> in the **Body of Truth** <note>original: *Dharma-Kāya*</note>, to Buddhahood, to Perfect Enlightenment, to **Spiritual Emancipation** <note>original: *Nirvāna*</note>—which is spiritual emancipation through Desirelessness.

*The Five Wisdoms.*—As the All-Pervading Voidness, the **Body of Truth** <note>original: *Dharma-Kāya*</note> is the shape (which is shapelessness) of the Body of Truth; the Thatness constituting it is the **Realm of Truth** <note>original: *Dharma-Dhātu*; Tibetan: *Chös-kyi-dvyings*—pronounced *Chö-kyi-ing*</note>, the Seed or Potentiality of Truth; and this dawns on the First Day of the **Intermediate State** <note>original: *Bardo*</note> as the glorious blue light of the **Meditation Buddha Vairochana** <note>original: *Dhyāni Buddha Vairochana*</note>, the Manifester, 'He Who in Shapes Makes Visible' [the universe of matter]. The **Realm of Truth** <note>original: *Dharma-Dhātu*</note> is symbolized as the **Aggregate of Matter**. From the **Aggregate of Matter** arise the creatures of this world, as of all worlds, in which animal stupidity is the dominant characteristic; and the **illusion** <note>original: *māra*</note> (or illusion of shape) constitutes in all realms of the **Cycle of Rebirth** <note>original: *Sangsāra*</note>—as in the human kingdom where **mind** <note>original: *manas*</note> (or mind) begins to operate—the Bondage, emancipation from which is **Spiritual Emancipation** <note>original: *Nirvāna*</note>. When in man, made as perfect as human life can make him, the stupidity of his animal nature and the illusion of shape, or personality, are transmuted into Right Knowledge, into Divine Wisdom, there shines forth in his consciousness the All-Pervading Wisdom of the **Realm of Truth** <note>original: *Dharma-Dhātu*</note>, or the Wisdom born of the Voidness, which is all-pervading.

As the **Aggregate of Matter**, dawning in the **Intermediate State** <note>original: *Bardo*</note> of the First Day, produces physical bodies, so the Water-Element, dawning on the Second Day, produces the life-stream, the blood; Anger is the obscuring passion, consciousness is the aggregate, and these, when transmuted, become the **Mirror-like Wisdom**, personified in the **Diamond Being** <note>original: *Vajra-Sattva*</note> (the **Body of Perfect Enjoyment** <note>original: *Sambhoga-Kāya*</note>

->INTRODUCTION<-

(reflex of the <term>Meditation Buddha</term> <note>original: *Dhyāni Buddha*</note> <term>Akshobhya</term> <note>the "Immovable" or "Unshakable" One</note>), the ‘Triumphant One of Divine Heroic Mind’.

The Earth-Element of the Third Day, producing the chief solid constituents of the human form, and of all physical forms, gives rise to the passion of Egoism, and the aggregate is Touch; and these, when divinely transmuted, become the <term>Wisdom of Equality</term>, personified in <term>Ratnasambhava</term> <note>original: *Ratna-Sambhava*; the "Gem-born One"</note>, the ‘Gem-born One’, the Beautifier.

The Fire-Element of the Fourth Day, producing the animal-heat of embodied human and animal beings, gives rise to the passion of Attachment, or Lust, and the Aggregate of Feelings. Herein the transmutation gives birth to the <term>All-Discriminating Wisdom</term>, which enables the devotee to know each thing separately, yet all things as one; personified in the <term>Meditation Buddha</term> <note>original: *Dhyāni Buddha*</note> <term>Amitabha</term> <note>original: *Amitābha*; "He of Boundless Light"</note>, ‘He of Boundless Light’, the Illuminator, or Enlightener.

The Element Air, of the Fifth Day, produces the breath of life. Its quality, or passions, in man is Envy, or Jealousy. Its aggregate is Volition <note>The faculty of will or mental formation</note>. The transmutation is into the <term>All-Performing Wisdom</term>, which gives perseverance and unerring action in things spiritual, personified in <term>Amoghasiddhi</term> <note>original: *Amogha-Siddhi*; the "Infallible Success" or "Almighty Conqueror"</note>, the ‘Almighty Conqueror’, the Giver of Divine Power.

As explained above, in Section IV, the last Element, <term>Ether</term> <note>Original: Space or *Akasha*; the subtlest element</note>, which produces the mind, or Knower, and the desire-body of the dwellers in the <term>Intermediate State</term> <note>original: *Bardo*</note>, does not dawn for the deceased, because—as the text tells us—the Wisdom Faculty of the Consciousness, that is to say, the supramundane <term>Awakened</term> <note>original: *Bodhic*</note> consciousness, has not been developed in the ordinary humanity. To it is related—as in our text—<term>Vajrasattva</term> <note>original: *Vajra-Sattva*; the "Diamond Being" or "Indestructible Essence"</note> and the <term>Mirror-like Wisdom</term> and the Aggregate of <term>Awakened Wisdom</term> <note>original: *Bodhic Wisdom*</note>, <term>Vajrasattva</term> being then synonymous, esoterically, with <term>Samantabhadra</term> <note>original: *Samanta-Bhadra*; the "All-Good" or "Universal Goodness"</note> (who, in turn, is often personified in <term>Vairochana</term>, the Chief of the Five <term>Meditation Buddhas</term>), the <term>Primordial Buddha</term> <note>original: *Ādi-Buddha*</note>, the Primordial, the Unborn, Unshaped, Un-modified <term>Body of Truth</term> <note>original: *Dharma-Kāya*</note>.

When the perfection of the Divine Body-Aggregate is attained by man, it becomes the unchanging, immutable <term>Vajrasattva</term>. When the perfection of the Divine Speech-

->3009 D<-

Principle is attained, with it comes the power of divine speech, symbolized by <term>Amitabha</term> <note>original: *Amitābha*; "Infinite Light"</note>. The perfection of the Divine Thought-Principle brings divine infallibility, symbolized by <term>Vairochana</term> <note>original: *Vairochana*; "The Resplendent One"</note>. The perfection of the Divine Qualities of Goodness and Beauty is the realization of <term>Ratnasambhava</term> <note>original: *Ratna-Sambhava*; "The Jewel-Born One"</note>, their producer. With the perfection of Divine Actions comes the realization of <term>Amoghasiddhi</term> <note>original: *Amogha-Siddhi*; "Unfailing Success"</note>, the Omnipotent Conqueror.)

To one after another of these divine attributes, or principles, innate in every human being, the deceased is introduced, as though in a symbolic drama of initiation, to test him and discover whether or not any part of his divine (or *awakened*) <note>original: *bodhic*; referring to the nature of enlightenment</note> nature has been developed. Full development in all the *awakened* powers of the <term>Five Meditation Buddhas</term> <note>original: *Five Dhyāni Buddhas*; the primary celestial buddhas of the mandala</note>, who are the personifications of them, leads to Liberation, to <term>Buddhahood</term> <note>The state of perfect enlightenment</note>. Partial development leads to birth in one of the happier states: the <term>realm of the gods</term> <note>original: *deva-loka*</note>; the <term>realm of the titans</term> <note>original: *asura-loka*; often described as jealous demi-gods</note>; or the <term>realm of mankind</term> <note>original: *nara-loka*</note>.

After the Fifth Day the <term>Intermediate State</term> <note>original: *Bardo*</note> visions become less and less divine; the deceased sinks deeper and deeper into the morass of <term>worldly</term> <note>original: *sangsāric*; relating to the cycle of suffering and rebirth</note> hallucinations; the radiances of the higher nature fade into the lights of the lower nature. Then—the after-death dream ending as the <term>Intermediate State</term> exhausts itself for the percipient, the thought-forms of his mental-content all having shown themselves to him like ghostly spectres in a nightmare—he passes on from the <term>Intermediate State</term> into the equally illusionary state called waking, or living, either in the human world or in one of the many mansions of existence, by being born there. And thus revolves the Wheel of Life, until the one who is bound on it breaks his own bonds through Enlightenment, and there comes, as the Buddha proclaims, the Ending of Sorrow.

In Sections I to V, above, the more prominent occult teachings underlying the <term>Liberation Through Hearing in the Intermediate State</term> <note>original: *Bardo Thödol*</note> have been briefly expounded. In Sections VI to XII, which are to follow, the chief <term>Intermediate State</term> rites and ceremonies, the psychology of this state, and other related doctrines will be explained and interpreted. The last Sections, XIII to XV, will be devoted

# INTRODUCTION

to a consideration of our manuscript, its history, the origin of the <term>Liberation Through Hearing in the Intermediate State</term> <note>original: *Bardo Thödol*</note> texts, and our translating and editing.

In addition to these fifteen sections, there are, as Addenda (see pp. 211-41), six complementary sections, addressed chiefly to the student, who, more than the ordinary reader, will be interested in certain of the more abstruse doctrines and problems which arise from a careful study of the translation and its annotations.

->VI. THE DEATH CEREMONIES<-

When the death-symptoms, as described in the first sections of our text, are completed, a white cloth is thrown over the face of the corpse; and no person then touches the corpse, in order that the culminating process of death, which ends only upon the complete separation of the <term>Intermediate State</term> <note>original: *Bardo*</note> body from its earth-plane counterpart, shall not be interfered with. It is commonly held that normally the process takes from three and one-half to four days, unless assisted by a priest called the <term>extractor of the consciousness-principle</term> <note>original: *hpho-bo*, pronounced 'pho-o'</note>; and that, even if the priest be successful in the extracting, the deceased ordinarily does not wake up to the fact of being separated from the human body until the said period of time has expired.

<margin>✓</margin>
The <term>extractor of consciousness</term> <note>original: *hpho-bo*</note>, upon his arrival, takes a seat on a mat or chair at the head of the corpse; he dismisses all lament-making relatives from the death-chamber and orders its doors and windows to be closed, so as to secure the silence necessary for the right performance of the service. This consists of a mystic chant containing directions for the spirit of the deceased to find its way to the Western Paradise of <term>Infinite Light</term> <note>original: *Amitābha*</note>, and thus escape—if <term>past actions and their consequences</term> <note>original: *karma*</note> permit—the undesirable Intermediate State. After commanding the spirit to quit the body and its attachment to living relatives and goods, the <term>spiritual teacher</term> <note>original: *lāma*</note> examines the crown of the head of the corpse at the line of the sagittal suture, where the two parietal bones articulate, called the <term>Aperture of Brahma</term> <note>original: *Brāhma-randhra*</note>, to determine if the spirit has departed thence, as it should have done; and, if the scalp be not bald, he pulls out a few of the hairs directly over the aperture. If through accident or

<meta>This page continues the description of ritual preparations immediately following death. ### THE DEATH CEREMONIES

...otherwise there be no corpse, the <term>spiritual teacher</term> <note>original: *lāma*; a high-ranking priest or monk in Tibetan Buddhism</note> mentally concentrates upon the deceased, and, visualizing the body of the deceased, imagines it to be present; and, calling the spirit of the deceased, performs the ceremony, which usually lasts about one hour.

Meanwhile, the <term>astrologer-priest</term> <note>original: *tsi-pa*</note> has been engaged to cast a death-horoscope, based upon the moment of death of the deceased, to determine what persons may approach and touch the corpse, the proper method of disposing of the corpse, the time and manner of the funeral, and the sort of rites to be performed for the benefit of the departed. Then the corpse is tied up in a sitting posture, much the same as that in which mummies and skeletons have been found in ancient graves or tombs in various parts of the world, and sometimes called the **embryonic posture**, symbolical of being born out of this life into the life beyond death. The corpse, so postured, is then placed in one of the corners of the death-chamber which has not been assigned to the household daemon <note>A local spirit or deity believed to inhabit and protect the home; specific areas of the house are reserved for such entities.</note>.

Relatives and friends, having been notified of the death, gather together at the house of the deceased; and there they are fed and lodged until the corpse is disposed of. If doubt exists concerning the complete separation of the <term>consciousness-principle</term> <note>The vital spirit or soul that survives the death of the physical body.</note> (or spirit) of the deceased from the body, there is not likely to be any disposal of the corpse until three and one-half to four days after the time of the death. So long as the entertaining of the mourners continues—usually for not less than two, but more often for three days—the spirit of the deceased is offered a part of all food, both solid and liquid, of each meal. This food is placed in a bowl in front of the corpse; and then, after the spirit of the deceased has extracted from the food thus offered the subtle invisible essences <note>It is believed that the deceased consumes the 'aroma' or spiritual essence of the food rather than the physical matter.</note>, the food is thrown away. After the corpse has been removed from the house for final disposal, an effigy of the deceased is put in the corner of the room which the corpse had occupied; and before this effigy food continues thus to be offered until the forty-nine days of the <term>Intermediate State</term> <note>original: *Bardo*; the transitional state between death and rebirth.</note> have expired.

Whilst the funeral rites—including the reading of the <term>Liberation Through Hearing in the Intermediate State</term> <note>original: *Bardo Thödol*</note>—are being performed, in the house of the deceased or

# INTRODUCTION

...at the place of death, other <term>teachers</term> <note>original: *lāmas*; spiritually advanced monks in Tibetan Buddhism</note> chant in relays, all day and night, the service for assisting the spirit of the deceased to reach the <term>Pure Land of Bliss</term> <note>original: *Western Paradise of Amitābha*; a celestial realm presided over by the Buddha of Infinite Light</note>. In Tibetan, this service (which the <term>consciousness-extractor</term> <note>original: *hpho-bo*; a specialized priest who guides the soul out of the body</note> also chants) is called the <term>Prayer of the Pure Land</term> <note>original: *De-wa-chan-kyi-mon-lam*</note>. If the family is well-to-do, another service of a similar nature may be performed at the temple where the deceased used to worship, by all of the assembled monks of that temple.

After the funeral, the <term>teachers</term> who read the *<term>Great Liberation Through Hearing</term>* <note>original: *Bardo Thödol*</note> return to the house of death once a week until the forty-nine-day <term>Intermediate State</term> <note>The transitional period between death and rebirth</note> has ended. It is not uncommon, however, for them to skip one day of the first week and of each of the succeeding periods in order to shorten the service, so that they return after six, five, four, three, two, and one day respectively, thereby concluding the reading in about three weeks.

From the First to the Fourteenth Day, as the arrangement of Book One of our text suggests, the *<term>Intermediate State of Reality</term>* <note>original: *Chönyid Bardo*; the phase where the deceased experiences visions of peaceful and wrathful deities</note> is to be read and re-read, and from the Fifteenth Day onwards the *<term>Intermediate State of Rebirth</term>* <note>original: *Sidpa Bardo*; the phase where the spirit seeks a new physical form</note>. In poorer families, the rites may cease after the Fourteenth Day; for families in better circumstances, it is usual in **Sikkim** <note>A region in the Himalayas known for its distinct Buddhist traditions</note> to continue the rites at least until the expiration of the twenty-one-day period and sometimes during the whole period of the Forty-nine Days of the *Bardo*. On the first day of the funeral rites, if the deceased were a man of wealth or position, as many as one hundred monks may assist; at the funeral of a poor man only one or two monks are likely to be present. After the Fourteenth Day, as a rule for all alike, only one monk is retained to complete the reading.

The effigy of the body of the deceased is made by dressing a stool, block of wood, or other suitable object in the clothes of the deceased; and where the face should be, there is inserted a printed paper called the *<term>name-card</term>* <note>original: *mtshan-spyang* or *spyang-pu*, pronounced *chang-ku*; a ritual tablet that acts as a temporary home for the spirit</note>, of which the following reproduction of a specimen is typical:¹

--- Page 107 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction to the funerary rituals of Tibet, specifically focusing on the physical tools used to guide the deceased through the intermediate state.</meta>

# INTRODUCTION

In this <term>ceremonial image-card<note>original: *spyāng-pu*; a paper portrait used as a temporary residence for the soul of the deceased during funeral rites.</note></term>, the central figure represents the deceased with legs bound and in an attitude of adoration, surrounded by symbols of 'the five excellent sensuous things': (1) a mirror (the first of the three objects on the left and numbered 1), symbolical of the body, which reflects all phenomena or sensations, and of sight as well; (2) a conch (numbered 2) and a lyre (numbered 3), symbolical of sound; (3) a vase of flowers (numbered 4), symbolical of smell; (4) holy cakes in a receptacle like that employed at the Roman Catholic Eucharist (numbered 5), symbolical of essence or nutriment, and of taste; (5) the silk clothes of the central figure and the overhanging royal canopy, symbolical of dress and ornamental art, and of the sense of touch. It is before such a paper figure, inserted in the effigy as a head and face, that the food offerings to the spirit of the deceased continue to be made, and to which, when visualized by the <term>spiritual teacher<note>original: *lāma*</note></term> as the deceased in person, the <term>Liberation Through Hearing in the Intermediate State<note>original: *Bardo Thödol*; commonly known as the Tibetan Book of the Dead.</note></term> is read.

Having begun my Tibetan researches fresh from three years of research in the ancient funeral lore of the Nile Valley, I realized as soon as I gained knowledge of the Tibetan funeral rites—which are very largely pre-Buddhistic—that the effigy of the dead, as now used in Tibet and Sikkim, is so definitely akin to the effigy of the deceased called 'the statue of <term>Osiris<note>The Egyptian god of the afterlife; in funeral rites, the deceased was identified with Osiris to ensure resurrection.</note></term> (or deceased one)', as used in the funeral rites of ancient Egypt, as to suggest a common origin. Furthermore, the <term>image-card<note>original: *spyāng-pu*</note></term> taken by itself alone, as the head-piece for the effigy, has its Egyptian parallel in the images made for the <term>vital essence<note>original: *Ka*; the ancient Egyptian concept of a spiritual double or life-force.</note></term> or spirit. These sometimes were merely heads, complete in themselves, to replace or duplicate the head of the mummy and to furnish additional assistance to the <term>vital essence</term> when seeking—as the Knower in the <term>Intermediate State<note>original: *Bardo*</note></term> seeks—a body to rest in, or that which our text calls a prop for the body (see p. 182). And even as to 'the statue of Osiris' the ancient priests of Egypt read their <term>Book of the Dead</term>, so to the Tibetan effigy the <term>spiritual teachers</term> now read the <term>Liberation Through Hearing in the Intermediate State</term>—both treatises alike being nothing more than guide-books for the traveller in the realm beyond death.

Again, the preliminary rituals of the Egyptian funeral were

### THE DEATH CEREMONIES

These rituals are designed to confer upon the deceased the magical power of rising up in the ghost-body or <term>Ka<note>The ancient Egyptian concept of a "double" or life-force that survives death.</note></term> possessed of all sense faculties, the service having consisted of the "opening of the mouth and eyes" and the restoration of the use of all other parts of the body. Likewise, the aim of the <term>spiritual teachers<note>original: *lāmas*</note></term>, at the outset, is to restore complete consciousness to the deceased after the state of swoon that immediately follows death, and to accustom them to the unfamiliar environment of the Otherworld. This assumes that the deceased is, like the majority of people, one of the unenlightened, and thus incapable of immediate liberation from the cycle of rebirth.

In conformity with our own view—that this part of the Tibetan funeral rites concerning the effigy and the <term>ceremonial image-card<note>original: *spyang-pu*</note></term> has survived from pre-Buddhist, likely ancient times—Dr. L. A. Waddell writes the following: "This is essentially a <term>Bön<note>The indigenous, shamanistic religion of Tibet that existed before the arrival of Buddhism.</note></term> rite, and is referred to as such in the histories of <term>Guru Padma Sambhava<note>The 8th-century Indian master credited with bringing Buddhism to Tibet.</note></term>, as being practiced by the Bön [that is, the religion prevalent in Tibet before the advent of Buddhism, which, in its belief in a transcendent reality, is much like Taoism], and as having incurred the displeasure of Guru Padma Sambhava, the founder of <term>Tibetan Buddhism<note>original: *Lāmaism*; a historical term for the form of Buddhism practiced in Tibet.</note></term>."

Of the image-card itself, Dr. Waddell adds: "Its inscription [as in our copy above] usually runs:

> "I, the world-departing One, . . . (and here the name of the deceased is inserted), adore and take refuge in my <term>spiritual teacher<note>original: *lāma-confessor*</note></term> and all the deities, both peaceful and wrathful;^1 and [may] 'the Great Pitier'^2 forgive my accumulated sins and impurities of former lives, and show me the way to another good world!"^3

At the left shoulder of the central figure of the image-card, as seen in our copy, and sometimes down the middle in other versions...

## INTRODUCTION

copies, are inscribed phonetic symbols referring to the six worlds of the <term>cycle of rebirth<note>original: *sangsāric*; the Buddhist concept of the beginningless cycle of birth, death, and reincarnation.</note></term> existence, translated as follows:
**S** = <term>godly being<note>original: *sura*</note></term>, referring to the <term>heavenly realm<note>original: *deva-world*</note></term>;
**A** = <term>titan<note>original: *asura*</note></term>, referring to the world of the warring titans;
**Na** = <term>man<note>original: *nara*</note></term>, referring to the human world;
**Tri** = <term>brute animal<note>original: *trisan*</note></term>, referring to the animal world;
**Pre** = <term>unhappy ghost<note>original: *preta*</note></term>, referring to the realm of the "hungry ghosts";
and ***Hung*** (from the term for "fallen") = hell, referring to the hell-world.^1

At the termination of the funeral rites, the <term>ceremonial image-card<note>original: *spyang-pu*; a paper effigy or face-card representing the deceased.</note></term> is ceremoniously burned in the flame of a butter-lamp, and the spirit of the deceased is given a final farewell. By the color of the flame and the way in which the flame acts, the after-death fate which the deceased has met with is determined.

The ashes of the burned <term>image-card<note>original: *spyang-pu*</note></term> are collected in a plate and then, upon being mixed with clay, are made into miniature stupas called <term>votive tablets<note>original: *sa-tschha*; small clay models of sacred monuments or plaques with inscriptions.</note></term>, usually in molds leaving impressions either of symbolical ornamentation or of sacred letters. One is kept for the family altar in the home of the deceased, and the rest are deposited in a sheltered place at a cross-roads or on a hill-top, usually under a projecting ledge of rock, or in a cave if there happens to be a cave.

With the burning of the paper, the rest of the effigy of the deceased is taken apart, the clothes going to the <term>spiritual teachers<note>original: *lāmas*</note></term>, who carry them off and sell them to the first purchaser, keeping the proceeds as part of their fee. When one year has elapsed after the death, a feast in honor of the deceased is usually given and the service of the Medical Buddhas <note>A group of eight Buddhas associated with healing and the prevention of misfortune.</note> is performed.^2 Thereafter, a widow of the deceased is free to remarry.^3

Connected with the Tibetan funeral itself there is much interesting ritual. Thus, when the officiating <term>spiritual teacher<note>original: *lāma*</note></term> is preparing to assist at the removal of the corpse from the house,

These feasts are given 'in the name of' the dead, to whom also the merit is offered. This, under certain circumstances, helps the dead to attain a higher rebirth." —Cassius A. Pereira.

he presents a 'scarf of honour' to the corpse and, addressing the corpse as the deceased, advises it to partake freely of the food offered, warns it that it is dead and that its ghost must not haunt the place or trouble living relatives, saying in conclusion, 'Remember the name of thy spiritual <term>lama-teacher<note>original: *lāma*; a spiritual guide or high-ranking monk in Tibetan Buddhism.</note></term>, which is . . . [so and so], and by his aid take the right path— the white one. Come this way!'¹

Then, as the <term>lama<note>original: *lāma*</note></term> begins to lead the funeral procession, he takes hold of one end of the long scarf, the other end having been tied to the corpse, and begins to chant a liturgy to the accompaniment of a miniature <term>hand-drum<note>original: *damaru*; a ritual drum made of two skulls or wood, sounded by strikers on cords when twirled.</note></term> (having loose-hanging knotted cords attached, which, striking the drum as it is twirled by the hand of the <term>lama</term>, cause it to sound) and of a <term>trumpet made of a human thigh-bone<note>original: *rkang-gling*; a ritual instrument used to summon spirits or signify the impermanence of the body.</note></term>. When there are a number of priests, the chief priest, going before the rest, rings a handbell (as the Breton priest does in a Breton peasant funeral procession), and the other priests assist with the chanting and the music, one blowing at intervals the sacred <term>conch-shell<note>original: *dung-kar*; used in Buddhist ritual to represent the far-reaching voice of the Dharma.</note></term>, another clashing brass cymbals, and perhaps another twirling the small drum, or blowing the thigh-bone trumpet. From time to time the chief <term>lama</term> looks back to invite the spirit to accompany the body and to assure it that the route is in the right direction. After the corpse-bearers come the main body of mourners, some bearing refreshments (to be in part cast on the funeral-pyre for the benefit of the deceased and in part partaken of by the priests and mourners), and last of all the weeping and wailing relatives. Such priestly guiding of the deceased's spirit is for the laity alone, for the spirits of deceased <term>lamas</term>, having been trained in the doctrines of the <term>Bardo Thodol<note>original: *Bardo Thödol*; literally "Liberation Through Hearing in the Intermediate State," the text guiding the consciousness through the afterlife.</note></term>, know the right path and need no guidance.

In Tibet itself all known religious methods of disposing of a corpse are in vogue; but, owing to lack of fuel for purposes of cremation, ordinarily the corpse, after having been carried to a hill-top or rocky eminence, is chopped to pieces and, much after the <term>Parsee custom<note>A reference to "sky burial," where the body is offered to vultures, similar to the Zoroastrian "Towers of Silence" in Persia and India.</note></term> in Persia and Bombay, given to the birds and beasts of prey. If the corpse be that of a nobleman, whose family can well afford

<page-num>26</page-num>
->INTRODUCTION<-

a funeral pyre, it may be cremated. In some remote districts earth burial is customary; and it is commonly employed everywhere when death has been caused by a very contagious and dangerous disease, like small-pox for example. Otherwise, Tibetans generally object to earth burial, for they believe that when a corpse is interred the spirit of the deceased, upon seeing it, attempts to re-enter it, and that if the attempt be successful a <term>vampire<note>original: *ro-lang*; literally "a risen corpse," these are spirits in Tibetan folklore that reanimate a body if it is not properly disposed of.</note></term> results, whereas cremation, or other methods of quickly dissipating the elements of the dead body, prevent vampirism. Sometimes, too, as among the Hindus, corpses are cast into rivers or other bodies of water. In the case of the <term>Dalai Lama<note>original: *Dalai Lāma*; the supreme spiritual leader and temporal ruler of Tibet.</note></term> and the <term>Tashi Lama<note>original: *Tashi Lāma*; also known as the Panchen Lama, the second-highest spiritual authority in the Gelug school.</note></term>, and of some very great man or saint, embalming is practised; and the corpse, in a way somewhat resembling the ancient Egyptian embalming process, is packed in a box of marsh salt, usually for about three months, or until the salt has absorbed all the watery parts of the corpse. Then, after the corpse is well cured, it is coated with a cement-like substance made of clay, pulverized sandal-wood, spices, and drugs. This adheres and hardens; and all the sunken or shrivelled parts of the body, such as the eyes, cheeks, and stomach, having been rounded out by it to their natural proportions, a very Egyptian-like mummy is produced. Finally, when thoroughly dried and then covered with a paint made of dissolved gold, the mummy is set up like an image in a sort of Tibetan Westminster Abbey <note>The author uses "Westminster Abbey" as a Western analogy for a sacred national shrine where the illustrious dead are interred.</note>.

At Shigatze <note>A major city and the traditional seat of the Panchen Lama.</note>, the seat of the Tashi Lama, there are five such funereal temples. With their double roofs, resplendent with gold, they resemble the palaces or royal shrines of China. In size and embellishment they differ, in accordance with the rank and wealth of the mummies occupying them, some being inlaid with gold, some with silver. <note>original: *Cf. Ekai Kawaguchi, Three Years in Tibet (Madras, 1909), p. 394.* This refers to the account of a Japanese monk who traveled to Tibet.</note> Before these enshrined mummies prayer is offered up, incense burnt, and elaborate rituals are performed, as in the ancestral cults of the Chinese and Japanese.

# THE DEATH CEREMONIES

books of the <term>Hindus<note>Followers of Hinduism, the predominant religious tradition of India, which shares several philosophical concepts with Tibetan Buddhism regarding the elements and rebirth.</note></term>: a human body is said to consist of four elements—earth, water, air, and fire—and it should be returned to these elements as quickly as possible. Cremation is considered the best method to adopt. Earth-burial, as among Christians also, is the returning of the body to the element Earth; water-burial is the returning of the body to the element Water, air-burial, to the element Air—the birds which devour the corpse being the denizens of the air; and fire-burial, or cremation, the returning of the body to the element Fire.

When air-burial is adopted in Tibet, even the bones of the corpse, after the birds have stripped them of flesh, are disposed of by being hammered to bits in small cavities in the rocks of the funereal hill, then mixed with flour and formed into a dough and given to the birds to devour.<gloss>The men who perform this part of the burial belong to a special caste, and, being regarded as unclean, are ordinarily shunned by other Tibetans.</gloss> The Tibetan air-burial is thus more thorough than that of the <term>Parsees<note>A Zoroastrian community in India who practice the "Tower of Silence" ritual, where bodies are exposed to vultures, though unlike the Tibetan method, they do not traditionally crush the bones.</note></term>, who allow the bones of their dead to remain in the air and slowly decompose.

In a Tibetan funeral of the ordinary sort, neither a coffin nor any corpse-receptacle is used. The corpse after being laid upon its back on a sheet or piece of cloth spread over a framework, commonly made of a light material like wicker affixed to two poles, is covered with a pure white cloth. Two men, inserting their heads between the projecting ends of the two poles, act as pall-bearers. In <term>Sikkim<note>A Himalayan region situated between Nepal and Bhutan, historically influenced by Tibetan religious culture.</note></term>, however, the corpse is carried thus sitting, in the <term>embryonic posture<note>The fetal position, symbolizing the soul's preparation for a new birth just as a child is positioned in the womb.</note></term> described above.

Both in Sikkim and in Tibet every funeral is conducted in strict accordance with the directions which have been given by the <term>astrologer<note>original: *tsipa*; a vital figure in Tibetan society who calculates the spiritual and elemental influences affecting a person's life and death.</note></term> who cast the <term>death-horoscope<note>original: *gshin-rtsis*; a calculation made at the time of death to determine the causes of the demise and the specific rituals required for a favorable rebirth.</note></term>, indicating who shall touch or handle the corpse, who shall carry it, and the form of the burial. The astrologer also declares what kind of evil spirit caused the death, for in popular belief—as also among the Celtic peoples of Europe—no death is natural, but is always owing to interference by one of the innumerable <term>death-demons<note>original: *shin-dre*; harmful spirits believed to cause sickness or "steal" the life-force of the living.</note></term>. The astrologer announces, too, what ceremonies are necessary to exorcize the death-demon

->INTRODUCTION<-
