In Sikkim, on the space of ground levelled for the funeral-pyre, a mystic diagram, symbolical of the **Happy Realm of Sukhavati** <note>original: *Sukhavatī*, the 'Pure Land' of the West</note>, or the **Red Western Realm of Happiness** (see text, p. 113), is outlined with flour and divided into compartments, the central space (upon which the funeral-pyre is built) being dedicated to the <term>Amitābha<note>The Buddha of Infinite Light, who presides over the Western Paradise and represents the transformation of passion into discriminating wisdom.</note></term>. At the beginning of the cremation ceremonies the chief *lama* <note>a high-ranking spiritual teacher or priest</note> visualizes the funeral-pyre as being the <term>mandala<note>A sacred geometric diagram representing the celestial residence of a deity.</note></term> of Amitābha, and the fire as being Amitābha himself, who, as in our text (see p. 113), personifies the element Fire. Then the corpse itself, when laid upon the pyre, is visualized as the *mandala* of Amitābha and its heart as the dwelling-place of Amitābha. As the fire begins to grow in volume, sweet-smelling oils and spices and sandal-wood and incense-sticks are cast into it in sacrifice, as in the <term>Homa<note>A traditional fire ritual found in both Hindu and Buddhist traditions where offerings are burnt to convey them to the divine.</note></term> ritual of the Indians. Finally, as the cremation ceremonies end, the priests and the mourners visualize the spirit of the departed as being purged of all <term>karmic<note>Relating to the law of cause and effect where past actions influence future existence.</note></term> obscurations by the fire which is Amitābha, the **Incomprehensible Light**.

Such, in brief, is the mysticism underlying the beautiful rites performed for the dead at the place of cremation in Sikkim.

In all other forms of burial, throughout Tibet or territories under Tibetan influence, a parallel or corresponding funeral service, based on the same symbolical rituals, is performed, with variations according to sect and province.

### VII. THE *BARDO*¹ OR AFTER-DEATH STATE

From the moment of death and for three and one-half or sometimes four days afterwards, the Knower, or principle of consciousness...

---

¹ *Bar-do* literally means 'between' (*Bar*) 'two' (*do*), that is, 'between two [states]'—the state between death and rebirth—and, therefore, the **Intermediate** or **Transitional [State]**. The translator, in certain instances, favoured '**Uncertain [State]**' as its English rendering. It might also be rendered as the '**Twilight [State]**'.

<summary>This page describes the symbolic cremation rituals in Sikkim, where the fire and the pyre are visualized as the Buddha Amitābha and his celestial realm. It also introduces the concept of the Bardo as the transitional state between death and rebirth.</summary>

<keywords>Amitābha, Sukhavati, Mandala, Homa, Bardo, Sikkim, cremation, funeral rites, karmic purification</keywords>

--- Page 114 ---
<meta>This page continues the transition from the physical funeral rites described on page 28 to a technical explanation of the psychological and spiritual stages experienced by the deceased after death, specifically defining the three phases of the Bardo.</meta>

<page-num>29</page-num>

->THE AFTER-DEATH STATE<-

The consciousness of an ordinary person who has died is believed to enter a sleep-like or trance state. In this condition, the person is usually unaware that they have been separated from their physical, human body. This initial period is known as the **First Intermediate State**, called the **Intermediate State of the Moment of Death** <note>original: *Hchi-khahi Bar-do*</note>. During this stage, the **Clear Light** <note>A fundamental, pure state of mind that is said to be the natural condition of all beings, appearing clearly at the moment the body's senses cease.</note> dawns. At first, it appears in its primordial purity; however, if the deceased is unable to recognize it—meaning they cannot hold onto or remain in this state of pure, unmodified mind—the light becomes obscured by their own past actions. When this first state ends, the "Knower" (the conscious essence of the person) awakens to the fact that death has occurred and begins to experience the **Second Intermediate State**, called the **Intermediate State of Glimpsing Reality** <note>original: *Chös-nyid Bar-do*</note>. This eventually merges into the **Third Intermediate State**, called the **Intermediate State of Seeking Rebirth** <note>original: *Srid-pahi Bar-do*</note>. This final stage concludes when the consciousness takes a new birth in the human world, another realm of existence, or one of the paradises.

As explained in the previous section, the transition from one *Bardo* <note>The Tibetan word for an "intermediate" or "transitional" state between death and a new life.</note> to another is similar to the process of birth. The "Knower" wakes up from one swoon or trance state only to enter another, until the third state ends. Upon awakening in the Second State, the deceased experiences symbolic visions. These are essentially hallucinations created by **karmic reflexes** <note>The habitual patterns or "echoes" created by one's actions and thoughts during life.</note> of things done while alive. What the person thought and did now becomes objective: these thought-forms, having been nurtured during life, now pass by in a powerful, solemn parade as the contents of the person's personality.¹

In the Second State, the deceased—unless they have been spiritually trained—tends to suffer under the delusion that, although they are dead...

---

¹ Some of the more learned <term>lamas<note>high-ranking spiritual teachers or priests</note></term>—primarily those of the <term>Gelugpa<note>Also known as the "Yellow-Hat Sect," this is a major school of Tibetan Buddhism to which the Dalai Lama belongs.</note></term>—believe that the highly symbolic visions of the one hundred and ten principal deities of the "Reality" state are seen only by advanced practitioners who have studied <term>Tantra<note>A complex system of esoteric Buddhist practice involving visualization, ritual, and meditation.</note></term>. They suggest that an ordinary person will instead experience visions more like those described in the state of seeking rebirth.

<summary>This page defines the three stages of the Bardo (the intermediate state between death and rebirth), explaining how a deceased person moves from the initial "Clear Light" of death into a realm of symbolic visions shaped by their past karma.</summary>
<keywords>Bardo, Chikhai Bardo, Chönyid Bardo, Sidpa Bardo, Clear Light, Karma, Rebirth, Gelugpa, Tantra</keywords>

--- Page 115 ---
<meta>This page continues the explanation of the transition through the afterlife stages, specifically detailing the Third Intermediate State (Bardo) where the consciousness seeks a new physical form based on its past actions.</meta>

<page-num>30</page-num>

->INTRODUCTION<-

Although he is deceased, he still feels as though he possesses a body of flesh and blood. When he comes to realize that he truly has no such body, he begins to develop an overwhelming desire to possess one; and, as his natural preference for **cyclic existence** <term>sangsāric: the continuous cycle of birth, death, and rebirth in the world of suffering</term> becomes the deciding factor, he enters into the **Third Intermediate State** <note>original: *Bardo*</note> of seeking Rebirth. Eventually, with his rebirth in this or some other world, the after-death state comes to an end.

For the common person, this is the normal process; but for those very exceptional minds, possessed of great knowledge of **spiritual discipline** <note>original: *yogic*</note> and enlightenment, only the more spiritual stages of the **Intermediate State** <note>original: *Bardo*</note> of the first few days will be experienced. The most enlightened of **practitioners** <note>original: *yogis*</note> may escape the **Intermediate State** <note>original: *Bardo*</note> entirely, passing into a paradise realm, or else reincarnating in this world as soon as the human body has been discarded, maintaining all the while an unbroken continuity of consciousness.¹ As men think, so they are, both here and hereafter; thoughts are real things, the parents of all actions, both good and bad; and as one has sown, so will the harvest be.

If escape from the **Intermediate State** <note>original: *Bardo*</note> is not achieved through rebirth into some other state—rebirth in **Hell** being possible for those who have done exceptional evil, though not for the ordinary person, who pays for normal moral failings by being reborn as a human being—within the symbolic period of **Forty-nine Days** (a period whose actual duration is determined by **the consequences of one's past actions** <note>original: *karma*</note>), the deceased remains subject to all the illusions of the **Intermediate State** <note>original: *Bardo*</note>. These may be blissful or miserable depending on the individual, and progress is impossible during this time. Apart from reaching **ultimate liberation** <term>Nirvāna: the state of being freed from the cycle of suffering and rebirth</term> after death—thus cutting forever the bonds of worldly existence in an illusory body of habits—the only hope for the ordinary person to reach the **state of a Buddha** <note>original: *Buddhahood*</note> lies in being reborn as a human being. Birth in any realm other than the human world causes a delay for someone desiring to reach the Final Goal.

Pereira.</note>

<header>THE AFTER-DEATH PSYCHOLOGY</header>

### VIII. THE PSYCHOLOGY OF THE **INTERMEDIATE STATE** VISIONS <note>original: *Bardo*</note>

Definite psychological significance attaches to each of the deities appearing in the **Liberation Through Hearing in the Intermediate State** <note>original: *Bardo Thödol*; commonly known as the Tibetan Book of the Dead</note>; but, in order to grasp it, the student must bear in mind that—as suggested above—the apparitional visions seen by the deceased in the **Intermediate State** <note>original: *Bardo*</note> are not visions of reality, but nothing more than the hallucinatory embodiments of the thought-forms born of the mental-content of the percipient; or, in other words, they are the intellectual impulses which have assumed personified form in the after-death dream-state.

Accordingly, the **Peaceful Deities** <note>original: *Z'i-wa*</note> are the personified forms of the sublimest human sentiments, which proceed from the psychic heart-centre. As such, they are represented as the first to dawn, because, psychologically speaking, the heart-born impulses precede the brain-born impulses. They come in peaceful aspect to control and to influence the deceased whose connexion with the human world has just been severed; the deceased has left relatives and friends behind, works unaccomplished, desires unsatisfied, and, in most cases, he possesses a strong yearning to recover the lost opportunity afforded by human embodiment for spiritual enlightenment. But, in all his impulses and yearnings, **the law of cause and effect** <term>karma: the principle that every physical or mental action has a corresponding consequence</term> is all-masterful; and, unless it be his **merited** <note>original: *karmic*</note> lot to gain liberation in the first stages, he wanders downwards into the stages wherein the heart-impulses give way to brain-impulses.

Whereas the **Peaceful Deities** are the personified forms of the feelings, the **Wrathful Deities** <note>original: *To-wo*</note> are the personifications of the reasonings and proceed from the psychic brain-centre. Yet, just as impulses arising in the heart-centre may transform themselves into the reasonings of the brain-centre, so the **Wrathful Deities** are the **Peaceful Deities** in a changed aspect.

As the intellect comes into activity, after the sublime heart-born impulses subside, the deceased begins to realize more and more the state in which he is; and with the supernormal faculties of the **Intermediate State body** <note>original: *Bardo*-body</note> which he begins to make use of—

<summary>This page explains that the deities encountered after death are actually personifications of the deceased's own thoughts and feelings, emerging from different psychic centers of the body. It distinguishes between Peaceful Deities (heart-centered emotions) and Wrathful Deities (brain-centered reasonings).</summary>

<meta>catchword: appari-</meta>

in much the same manner as an infant new-born in the human world begins to employ the human plane sense-faculties—he is enabled to think how he may win this or that state of existence. **The Law of Cause and Effect** <note>original: *Karma*</note> is, however, still his master, and defines his limitations. As on the human plane the sentimental impulses are most active in youth and often lost in mature life, wherein reason commonly takes the place of them, so on the after-death plane, called the **Intermediate State** <note>original: *Bardo*</note>, the first experiences are happier than the later experiences.

From another aspect, the chief deities themselves are the embodiments of universal divine forces, with which the deceased is inseparably related, for through him, as being the **small world reflecting the great universe** <note>original: *microcosm of the macrocosm*</note>, penetrate all impulses and forces, good and bad alike. (**The All-Good** <note>original: *Samanta-Bhadra*</note>, thus personifies Reality, the Primordial Clear Light of the Unborn, Unshaped **Body of Truth** <note>original: *Dharma-Kāya*; the highest state of enlightened consciousness</note> (cf. p. 95). **The Illuminator** <note>original: *Vairochana*</note> is the Originator of all phenomena, the Cause of all Causes. As the Universal Father, **the Illuminator** manifests or spreads forth as seed, or semen, all things; his **feminine energy** <note>original: *shakti*</note>, the Mother of Great Space, is the Universal Womb into which the seed falls and evolves as the world-systems. **The Diamond Being** <note>original: *Vajra-Sattva*</note> symbolizes Immutability. **The Jewel-Born** <note>original: *Ratna-Sambhava*</note> is the Beautifier, the Source of all Beauty in the Universe. **Boundless Light** <note>original: *Amitābha*</note> is Infinite Compassion and Love Divine, the **Anointed One** <note>original: *Christos*</note>. **The Unfailing Success** <note>original: *Amogha-Siddhi*</note> is the personification of Almighty Power or Omnipotence. And the minor deities, heroes, **sky-goers** <note>original: *dākinīs*; often described as 'fairies' or female spirits of wisdom</note>, goddesses, lords of death, **demons** <note>original: *rākshasas*</note>, spirits, and all others, correspond to definite human thoughts, passions, and impulses, high and low, human and sub-human and superhuman, in **karmic** <note>relating to the law of cause and effect</note> form, as they take shape from the seeds of thought forming the percipient's consciousness-content (cf. p. 219). )

As the **Liberation Through Hearing in the Intermediate State** <note>original: *Bardo Thödol*</note> text makes very clear by repeated assertions, none of all these deities or spiritual beings has any real individual existence any more than have human beings: 'It is quite sufficient for thee [i. e. the deceased percipient] to know that these apparitions are [the reflections of] thine own thought-forms' (p. 104). They are merely the consciousness-content visualized, by **causal** <note>original: *karmic*</note> agency, as appari-

## THE AFTER-DEATH PSYCHOLOGY

<note>This section completes the sentence from the previous page.</note>
...apparitional appearances in the **Intermediate State** <note>original: *Bardo*; the transitional state between death and the next rebirth</note>—airy nothings woven into dreams.

The complete recognition of this psychology by the deceased sets him free into Reality. Therefore is it that the ***Great Liberation by Hearing and by Seeing*** <note>original: *Bardo Thödol*</note>, as the name implies, is The Great Doctrine of Liberation by Hearing and by Seeing.

The deceased human being becomes the sole spectator of a marvellous panorama of hallucinatory visions; each seed of thought in his consciousness-content **through the law of cause and effect** <note>original: *karmically*</note> revives; and he, like a wonder-struck child watching moving pictures cast upon a screen, looks on, unaware, unless previously an **adept in mental discipline** <note>original: *yoga*</note>, of the non-reality of what he sees dawn and set.

At first, the happy and glorious visions born of the seeds of the impulses and aspirations of the higher or divine nature awe the uninitiated; then, as they merge into the visions born of the corresponding mental elements of the lower or animal nature, they terrify him, and he wishes to flee from them; but, alas, as the text explains, they are inseparable from himself, and to whatsoever place he may wish to flee they will follow him.

It is not necessary to suppose that all the dead in the **Intermediate State** <note>original: *Bardo*</note> experience the same phenomena, any more than all the living do in the human world, or in dreams. The ***Great Liberation by Hearing and by Seeing*** <note>original: *Bardo Thödol*</note> is merely typical and suggestive of all after-death experiences. It merely describes in detail what is assumed will be the **Intermediate State** <note>original: *Bardo*</note> visualizations of the consciousness-content of the ordinary devotee of the **Nyingma School** <note>original: "Red Hat School"; the oldest of the four major schools of Tibetan Buddhism</note> of **Padmasambhava** <note>The 8th-century master who established Buddhism in Tibet</note>. As a man is taught, so he believes. Thoughts being things, they may be planted like seeds in the mind of the child and completely dominate his mental content. Given the favourable soil of the will to believe, whether the seed-thoughts be sound or unsound, whether they be of pure superstition or of realizable truth, they take root and flourish, and make the man what he is mentally.

Accordingly, for a Buddhist of some other School, as for a Hindu, or a Moslem, or a Christian, the **Intermediate State** <note>original: *Bardo*</note> experiences would be appropriately different: the Buddhist's or the Hindu's thought-forms, as in a dream state, would give rise to corresponding visions of the deities of the Buddhist or Hindu

<summary>This page argues that the visions of the Bardo are subjective, karmic projections of the deceased's own mind, which vary based on one's religious and cultural conditioning during life.</summary>
<keywords>Bardo Thödol, Intermediate State, Karma, Psychology of Death, Padmasambhava, Nyingma School, Subjective Reality, Religious Conditioning</keywords>

--- Page 119 ---
<meta>This page continues the explanation of how the subjective mind shapes the experience of the afterlife. It concludes a sentence from page 33 regarding how different religious or philosophical backgrounds—such as those of a Muslim, a Christian, a Native American, or a materialist—determine the specific imagery encountered in the state between death and rebirth.</meta>

<page-num>34</page-num>

->INTRODUCTION<-

pantheon; a Muslim's <note>original: "Moslem's"</note>, to visions of the Muslim Paradise; a Christian's, to visions of the Christian Heaven, or an American Indian's to visions of the Happy Hunting Ground. And, similarly, the materialist will experience after-death visions as negative and as empty and as deityless as any he ever dreamt while in the human body. Rationally considered, each person's after-death experiences, as the ***Great Liberation through Hearing in the Intermediate State*** <note>original: *Bardo Thödol*</note> teaching implies, are entirely dependent upon his or her own mental content. In other words, as explained above, the after-death state is very much like a dream state, and its dreams are the children of the mentality of the dreamer. This psychology scientifically explains why devout Christians, for example, have had—if we are to accept the testimony of Christian saints and seers—visions (in a trance or dream state, or in the after-death state) of God the Father seated on a throne in the New Jerusalem, and of the Son at His side, and of all the Biblical scenery and attributes of Heaven, or of the Virgin and Saints and Archangels, or of Purgatory and Hell.

In other words, the ***Great Liberation through Hearing*** <note>original: *Bardo Thödol*</note> seems to be based upon verifiable data of human physiological and psychological experiences; and it views the problem of the after-death state as being purely a psycho-physical problem; and is, therefore, in the main, scientific. It asserts repeatedly that what the <term>percipient (the person experiencing the vision)</term> on the <term>Intermediate plane (the state between death and rebirth)</term> <note>original: *Bardo*</note> sees is due entirely to his own mental-content; that there are no visions of gods or of demons, of heavens or of hells, other than those born of the hallucinatory <term>karmic (the law of cause and effect based on past actions)</term> thought-forms constituting his personality, which is an impermanent product arising from the thirst for existence and from the will to live and to believe.

From day to day the **Intermediate State** <note>original: *Bardo*</note> visions change, concomitant with the eruption of the thought-forms of the percipient, until their **causal** <note>original: *karmic*</note> driving force exhausts itself; or, in other words, the thought-forms, born of habitual propensities, being mental records comparable as has already been suggested to records on a cinema-film, their reel running to its end, the after-death state ends, and the Dreamer, emerging from the womb, begins to experience anew the phenomena of the human world.

<summary>This page argues that afterlife experiences are subjective psychological projections shaped by an individual's earthly beliefs and "karmic" history, comparing the process to a cinema film playing until its conclusion. It asserts that the Bardo Thödol is a scientific psychological study of the transition from death to a new birth.</summary>

<keywords>subjective afterlife, psychological projection, Bardo Thödol, karma, religious visions, reincarnation, mental content, dream state</keywords>

--- Page 120 ---
<meta>This page continues the psychological analysis of the afterlife state. The previous page established that a person's cultural and religious background dictates the imagery they see after death—whether it be the Christian Heaven or a materialist's void—concluding that these visions are subjective "dreams" of the deceased's own mind.</meta>

<page-num>35</page-num>

<header>THE AFTER-DEATH PSYCHOLOGY</header>

The *Bible* of the Christians, like the *Koran* of the Muslims <note>original: "Moslems"</note>, never seems to consider that the spiritual experiences in the form of hallucinatory visions by prophet or devotee, reported therein, may, in the last analysis, not be real. But the ***Great Liberation through Hearing in the Intermediate State*** <note>original: "Bardo Thödol"</note> is so sweeping in its assertions that it leaves its reader with the clear-cut impression that every vision, without any exception whatsoever, in which spiritual beings, gods or demons, or paradises or places of torment and purgation play a part, in an <term>intermediate state <note>This refers to the "Bardo," the transitional period between death and the next rebirth.</note></term> or any dream-like ecstasy, is purely illusionary, being based upon phenomena of the <term>cycle of rebirth <note>original: "sangsāric"</note></term>.

The whole aim of the teaching of the ***Great Liberation***, as otherwise stated elsewhere, is to cause the Dreamer to awaken into Reality, freed from all the obscurations of illusions caused by <term>past actions <note>original: "karmic"</note></term> or the cycle of existence, in a state beyond the world or a state of <term>ultimate liberation <note>original: "Nirvānic"</note></term>, beyond all phenomenal paradises, heavens, hells, purgatories, or worlds of embodiment. In this way, then, it is purely Buddhistic and unlike any non-Buddhist book in the world, secular or religious.

### IX. THE JUDGEMENT

The Judgement Scene as described in our text and that described in the Egyptian *Book of the Dead* seem so much alike in essentials as to suggest that common origin, at present unknown, to which we have already made reference. In the Tibetan version, the <term>King of Truth <note>original: "Dharma-Rāja"</note></term> (Tibetan: the <term>Religious King of the Dead <note>original: "Shinje-chho-gyal"</note></term>), King of the Dead (commonly known to <term>followers of the School of the Elders <note>original: "Theravādists"</note></term> as the <term>King of the Dead <note>original: "Yama-Rāja"</note></term>), the Buddhist and Hindu <term>Pluto <note>The Roman god of the underworld, used here as a familiar comparison for Western readers.</note></term>, as a Judge of the Dead, corresponds to Osiris in the Egyptian version. In both versions alike there is the symbolical weighing: before the King of Truth there are placed on one side of the balance black pebbles and on the other side white pebbles, symbolizing evil and good deeds; and similarly, before Osiris, the heart and the feather (or else in place of the feather an image of the Goddess of Truth which it symbolizes) are weighed one against the other, the heart representing the conduct or conscience of the deceased and the feather righteousness or truth.

In the Egyptian *Book of the Dead*, the deceased, addressing his heart, says: 'Raise not thyself in evidence against me.'

<summary>The author explains that Tibetan Buddhism views all afterlife visions as mental illusions and compares the Tibetan scene of weighing good and evil deeds to the judgement of Osiris in Egyptian mythology.</summary>

<keywords>Bardo Thödol, afterlife psychology, Samsara, Nirvana, Judgement Scene, Dharma-Rāja, Osiris, Egyptian Book of the Dead, karma, Buddhist philosophy</keywords>

<page-num>36</page-num>
<header>INTRODUCTION</header>

‘Be not mine adversary before the Divine Circle; let there be no fall of the scale against me in the presence of the great god, Lord of <term>Amenta <note>The ancient Egyptian underworld or realm of the dead.</note></term>.’ In the Egyptian Judgement Scene it is the ape-headed (less commonly the ibis-headed) Thoth, god of wisdom, who supervises the weighing; in the Tibetan Judgement Scene it is the monkey-headed <term>Shinje <note>Tibetan: Gshin-rje. The Lord of Death who presides over the judgment of souls.</note></term>; and in both scenes there is the jury of deities looking on, some animal-headed, some human-headed.^1 In the Egyptian version there is a monstrous creature waiting to devour the deceased should the deceased be condemned, whilst in the Tibetan version devils wait to conduct the evil-doer to the hell-world of purgation; and the record-board which Thoth is sometimes depicted as holding corresponds to the Mirror of ***Karma*** <note>The law of moral cause and effect.</note> held by <term>Dharma-Rāja <note>Sanskrit: "King of Truth" or "Righteous King," another name for the judge of the dead.</note></term> or, as in some versions, by one of the divine jury. Furthermore, in both Books of the Dead, the deceased when first addressing the Judge pleads that he has done no evil. Before Osiris, this plea seems to be accepted in all the texts now known; before Dharma-Rāja it is subject to the test of the Mirror of ***Karma***, and this seems to be distinctly an Indian and Buddhist addition to the hypothetical prehistoric version, whence arose the Egyptian and the Tibetan versions, the Egyptian being the less affected.

Plato, too, in recording the other-world adventures of Er, in the tenth book of the *Republic*, describes a similar Judgement, in which there are judges and <term>karmic <note>Related to the consequences of one's past actions.</note></term> record-boards (affixed to the souls judged) and paths—one for the good, leading to Heaven, one for the evil, leading to Hell—and demons waiting to take the condemned souls to the place of punishment, quite as in the ***Great Liberation through Hearing in the Intermediate State*** <note>original: "Bardo Thödol"</note> (see p. 49).^2

> ^1 Such animal-headed deities as appear in the ***Great Liberation*** <note>original: "Bardo Thödol"</note> are, for the most part, derived from the pre-Buddhistic religion of Tibet called <term>Bön <note>The indigenous, shamanistic religious tradition of Tibet that preceded Buddhism.</note></term>, and, therefore, probably of very great antiquity. Like their Egyptian parallels, they seem to be more or less totemistic; and, through their impersonation by masked priests, as in the Ancient Egyptian Mysteries and surviving Tibetan mystery plays, may be—as our text also suggests—symbolic of definite attributes, passions, and propensities of <term>embodied beings <note>original: "saṅsāric"</note></term>—human, sub-human, and superhuman. (See p. 140^1.)
> ^2 The student is here referred to Section VII of our Addenda (pp. 238-41), concerning the Christianized version of the Judgement contained in the curious medieval treatise entitled *The Lamentation of the Dying Creature*.

<header>THE AFTER-DEATH JUDGEMENT</header>

The lore of purgatory—now Christianized and associated with St. Patrick at the site of the originally pre-Christian St. Patrick’s Purgatory in Ireland—points to a universal human belief. This includes the legends of the Otherworld and rebirth found among Celtic peoples, the myths of the Greek underworld <note>Original: "Proserpine lore," referring to the goddess Persephone/Proserpina who rules the realm of the dead.</note> recorded in sacred books worldwide, and the Middle Eastern <note>Original: "Semitic."</note> doctrines of heaven, hell, and judgment. It also encompasses the concept of resurrection, viewed here as a Christian adaptation of older Jewish rebirth doctrines. These accounts, along with the writings of the philosopher Plato, all testify to beliefs shared across humanity, likely far older than the most ancient records from Babylon or Egypt.¹

The painting of the Tibetan Judgment Scene reproduced in this book (see the image facing page 166) was created in strict accordance with monastic tradition in Gangtok, Sikkim, during 1919. It was painted by Lharipa-Pempa-Tendup-La, a Tibetan artist who was staying there at the time. A much earlier version of this scene was, until recently, preserved as a mural within the "Wheel of Life" at the Tashiding temple in Sikkim. Dr. L. A. Waddell has described that version as follows: ‘The judgment is in every case delivered by the impartial <term>Religious King of the Dead <note>Original Tibetan: *Shinje-chho-gyal*. The judicial aspect of the Lord of Death.</note></term> or <term>Righteous King <note>Original Sanskrit: *Dharma-Rāja*.</note></term>. He is a form of <term>Yama <note>The Hindu god of the dead, adopted into the Buddhist tradition.</note></term>, the lord of the deceased, who holds a mirror in which the naked soul is reflected. Meanwhile, his servant <term>Shinje <note>A class of death-spirit or the Lord of Death himself.</note></term> weighs the person’s good and bad deeds on a pair of scales; good deeds are represented by white pebbles, and bad deeds by black ones.’² And Dr. Waddell has traced back

---

¹ In my book *Fairy-Faith in Celtic Countries* (Oxford, 1911), Chapter 10, I suggested that the lore of purgatory—which centered on a cave used for ancient pre-Christian initiation rites on an island in Loch Derg, Ireland—likely gave rise to the Roman Catholic doctrine of Purgatory. This cave is now a famous Catholic pilgrimage site known as St. Patrick’s Purgatory. The original cave was destroyed by order of the English government in Ireland to stop what they called "pagan superstition."

Furthermore, the underground places of worship and initiation dedicated to the Sun-God Mithras, still found as ancient ruins across Southern Europe, bear a close resemblance to the original Irish Purgatory and other underground sites in Celtic countries, such as New Grange in Ireland and Gavrinis in Brittany. This suggests a common prehistoric origin, essentially religious and connected to a cult of the <term>intermediate state <note>Original: *Bardo*-world. The state between death and the next life.</note></term> and its inhabitants.

# INTRODUCTION

the origin of the picture to a similar <term>Wheel of Life <note>Original Sanskrit: *Bhavachakra*. A symbolic circle representing the cycle of birth, death, and rebirth.</note></term>, commonly, though incorrectly, known as ‘the Zodiac’ in the verandah of the Ajaṇṭā Cave No. XVII, India. (See p. 56.) This, then, establishes the antiquity of the Judgement Scene, of which our text contains one version.

Throughout the canonical and apocryphal literature of Northern Buddhism <note>Refers to the Mahayana tradition, which spread to Tibet, China, and Japan.</note> other versions are numerous. In the Pali canon of Southern Buddhism <note>Refers to the Theravada tradition, predominant in Sri Lanka and Southeast Asia.</note> there are parallel versions, for example in the *Section on Divine Messengers* <note>Original Pali: *Devadūta Vagga*.</note> of the *Numbered Discourses* <note>Original Pali: *Aṅguttara Nikāya*.</note>, and in the *Discourse on Divine Messengers* <note>Original Pali: *Devadūta Sūttam*.</note> of the *Middle-Length Discourses* <note>Original Pali: *Majjhima Nikāya*.</note>.

The latter version may be summarized as follows: The Exalted One, the Buddha, while sojourning at the Jetavana Monastery, addresses the monks assembled therein concerning the after-death state of existence. Like a man of clear vision, sitting between two houses, each with six doors, He beholds all who come and go; the one house symbolizing the <term>Intermediate State <note>Original Tibetan: *Bardo*. The state of existence between death and the next rebirth.</note></term> or state of disembodied existence, the other the embodied state of existence, and the twelve doors the six entrances and the six exits of the six <term>realms <note>Original Sanskrit: *lokas*. The six worlds of rebirth: gods, demi-gods, humans, animals, hungry ghosts, and hell-beings.</note></term>. Then, after explaining the manner in which <term>actions and consequences <note>Original Sanskrit: *karma*.</note></term> governs all states of existence, the Buddha describes how the evil-doer is brought before the King of Death and questioned about the Five Messengers of Death.

The first messenger is symbolized by a new-born babe lying on its back; and the message is that even for it, as for all living creatures, old age and death are inevitable.

The second messenger comes in the guise of an aged person, eighty, ninety, or a hundred years of age, decrepit, crooked as the curved rafter of a gabled roof, leaning on a staff, trembling as he walks, pathetically miserable, with youth entirely gone, broken-toothed, grey-haired and nearly bald, and with wrinkled brow; and his message is that the babe but grows up and matures and decays to become a victim of Death.

The third messenger, a person confined by illness, rolling in his own filth, unable to rise or to lie down without the aid of an attendant, brings the message that disease, too, is inevitable, even as death.

The fourth messenger, a thief undergoing most terrible punishment, bears the message that the punish-

<summary>This page explores the historical and scriptural origins of the "Wheel of Life" and the "Judgement Scene," linking Tibetan traditions to the Ajanta Caves in India and early Buddhist scriptures that describe the symbolic "Five Messengers of Death."</summary>

--- Page 124 ---
<meta>This page concludes the discussion of the "Five Divine Messengers" of death. It describes the trial of the soul before the Lord of Death and transitions into the philosophical doctrine of rebirth.</meta>

->THE AFTER-DEATH JUDGEMENT<-

Punishment for evil-doing in this world is as nothing compared to the punishment which <term>the law of cause and effect <note>Original Sanskrit: *karma*. The principle that every intentional action has an inevitable consequence.</note></term> inflicts after death. The fifth messenger, sent to emphasize the same message of death and the decay of the body, is a corpse—swollen, discoloured, and rotting.

In each instance, <term>the Lord of Death <note>Original Sanskrit: *King Yama*. In Buddhist and Hindu mythology, he is the deity who judges the dead and oversees the various realms of rebirth.</note></term> asks the deceased if he had seen the messenger and receives the reply, "No." Then the King explains to him who the messenger was and the meaning of the messages. The deceased person, thereby remembering, is obliged to confess that, not having done good deeds, he had not acted upon the messages but had done evil instead, forgetting the inevitability of death. Thereupon, the Lord of Death pronounces the judgement: since the deceased had failed to do good, he must suffer the consequences of his own actions. Accordingly, the hell-furies take the deceased and cause him to suffer five sorts of purgatorial punishments; and, though he suffers most unbearable pains, he is, as <term>the Tibetan Book of the Dead <note>Original Tibetan: *Bardo Thödol*. A funerary text intended to guide the consciousness through the state between death and rebirth.</note></term> makes clear, incapable of dying.

In the version from <term>the Numbered Discourses <note>Original Pali: *Aṅguttara Nikāya*. A collection of the Buddha's teachings organized by numerical groups.</note></term>, wherein there are but three messengers—the aged person, the man or woman overcome with disease, and the corpse—the Buddha concludes the discourse thus:

> "If men who have been warned by heavenly messengers have been indifferent as regards religion, they suffer long, being born in a low condition.
> 
> "If virtuous men have been warned by heavenly messengers in this world, they do not neglect to profess the holy doctrines. Seeing the danger of attachment, which is the cause of birth and death, they have in this life extinguished the miseries of existence by arriving at a condition free from fear, happy and free from passions and sins."¹

### X. THE REBIRTH DOCTRINE

In examining the Rebirth Doctrine, more particularly as it presents itself in our text, two interpretations must be taken into account: the literal or popular interpretation,

which is the popular interpretation; and the symbolical or esoteric interpretation, which is held to be correct by the initiated few, who claim not scriptural authority or belief, but knowledge. With respect to Tibet, these few are chiefly <term>learned teachers <note>Original Tibetan: *lāmas*. High-ranking spiritual guides or masters in Tibetan Buddhism.</note></term> who are said to have made successful application of methods like those which the Buddha expounded for <margin>/</margin>remembering past incarnations, and for acquiring the <term>meditative power <note>Original Sanskrit: *yogī*. > ‘If he desireth to be able to call to mind his various temporary states in days gone by, such as one birth, two births, three, four, five, ten, twenty, thirty, forty, fifty, a hundred, a thousand, or a hundred thousand births, his births in many an <term>aeon <note>Original Sanskrit: *kalpa*. The law of moral causality where every deed influences the quality of future lives.</note></term> they inherit—in that state of self-concentration, if the mind be fixed on the acquirement of any object, that object will be attained’ (<term>The Chapter on the Grain of Salt <note>Original Pali: *Lonaphala Vagga*.</note></term>, <term>The Numbered Discourses <note>Original Pali: *Anguttara Nikāya*. # INTERPRETATION OF REBIRTH DOCTRINE

[psycho]logy—the science of the West which has now proven ‘is the abode of everything that is latent’ <note>A reference to the subconscious or unconscious mind as described by William James.</note>—is likewise described, there is this additional passage:

> ‘Thus he calleth to mind the various appearances and forms of his previous births. This is the first stage of his knowledge; his ignorance [as regards prior births] hath vanished, and his knowledge [as regards prior births] hath arisen: darkness hath departed, and light hath arrived, the result due to one who liveth in meditation, subduing his passions promptly.’ <note>Quoted from the *Anguttara Nikaya*, a collection of the Buddha's numbered discourses.</note>

Nowhere, to our knowledge, are there nowadays—as there are said to have been in <term>Buddhaghosa’s <note>A 5th-century Indian scholar whose commentaries are the foundation of Theravada Buddhist doctrine.</note></term> time—**yogis** among <term>Southern Buddhists <note>Refers to the Theravada tradition practiced in Sri Lanka, Burma, and Thailand.</note></term> who have carried this practice to a successful issue. It is only among <term>Northern Buddhists <note>Refers to the Mahayana and Vajrayana traditions of Tibet and East Asia.</note></term> (as among Hindus) that such **yoga** seems to be, according to trustworthy evidence from well-informed Tibetans and Indians, a practically applied science even until now, producing modern saints, some few of whom are believed worthy to be called perfected saints, or ***Arhants*** <note>Original Sanskrit: *Arhat*. One who has attained spiritual perfection and release from the cycle of rebirth.</note>.

As the question, What is and is not the right interpretation of the **Rebirth Doctrine**? is by no means settled among the Oriental peoples who hold the Doctrine, it is necessary for us frankly to recognize the problem as highly controversial. Consequently in this Section we should try to weigh both interpretations carefully; and, if possible, arrive at a sound conclusion, in order to guide the student aright in what is the most fundamental doctrine underlying the <term>Tibetan Book of the Dead <note>Original Tibetan: *Bardo Thödol*. A guide for the consciousness of the deceased during the interval between death and rebirth.</note></term>. In doing so, it seems desirable to invoke the aid of such facts of Western Science as appear to be directly applicable.

As to the esoteric interpretation, the editor has discovered that the initiates who hold to it invariably follow the Buddha’s command as contained in the <term>Discourse to the Kalamas <note>Original: *Kalama Sutta*. A famous teaching where the Buddha encourages testing the truth of doctrines through personal experience rather than blind faith.</note></term>, found in the <term>Collection of Numerical Discourses <note>Original: *Anguttara Nikaya*.</note></term>,

# INTRODUCTION

...or else the Hindu equivalent in works on <term>Yoga <note>A system of spiritual practice aimed at inner realization and liberation.</note></term>, which advises not to accept any doctrine as true until it has been tested and proven so, even if it is "found written in the Scriptures." These practitioners hold that no scriptures—whether written in <term>Pali <note>The language of the earliest Buddhist scriptures, used primarily in the Theravada tradition.</note></term>, <term>Sanskrit <note>The ancient liturgical language of India, used for many Mahayana Buddhist texts.</note></term>, <term>Tibetan</term>, or others—are infallible or free from corruptions, whether they concern this or any other doctrine.

The public interpretation—namely, that the human stream of consciousness (the "human life-flux") not only can, but very often does, take on a new body as a lower animal immediately after a human life—is accepted universally by <term>Buddhists <note>Followers of the teachings of the Buddha.</note></term> of both the Northern and Southern Schools, as well as by <term>Hindus <note>Followers of the diverse religious traditions of India.</note></term>. Relying on the authority of written records, they regard this as undeniable. This belief, based on the authority of text and the untested theories of <term>spiritual teachers <note>original: "gurus"</note></term> and priests who consider literally interpreted records to be infallible and who are not masters of <term>yoga <note>The practical application of meditative techniques to gain direct spiritual insight.</note></term>, is nowadays considered to be the orthodox interpretation.

In contrast to this public interpretation, which the <term>Liberation Through Hearing in the Between-State <note>original: "Bardo Thödol", commonly known in the West as the *Tibetan Book of the Dead*.</note></term> certainly conveys if read literally, the hidden interpretations may be stated—based on the authority of various Hindu and Buddhist philosophers from whom the editor has received instruction—as follows:

The human form (though not the divine nature within man) is a direct inheritance from the kingdoms of lower life-forms; it has evolved from the lowest forms of life, guided by a constantly growing and changing life-flux. This flux is potential consciousness, which figuratively may be called the "seed" of the life-force, connected with or overshadowing each sentient creature and being psychological in its essence. As such, it is the evolving principle, the principle of continuity, and the principle capable of acquiring knowledge and understanding of its own nature—the principle whose normal goal is <term>Enlightenment <note>A state of perfect knowledge and freedom from suffering, known in Sanskrit as *Nirvana*.</note></term>. And, just as the physical seed of a vegetable or animal organism—even a human seed—is seen by the eye to be capable of producing only its own kind, so it is with that which we might figuratively call the psychological seed of the life-flux (which the eye cannot see). If it belongs to a human being, it cannot incarnate in, or overshadow, or be intimately bound up with a body...

<summary>This page explains that advanced practitioners do not view scriptures as infallible, preferring personal testing. It also contrasts the literal belief in human-to-animal reincarnation with a more philosophical, evolutionary view of the "psychical seed" or life-flux.</summary>

# ESOTERICISM OF REBIRTH DOCTRINE

foreign to its evolved characteristics, either in this world, in the <term>intermediate state <note>original: "Bardo"; the transitional state of existence between death and the next rebirth.</note></term>, or in any realm or world of <term>cyclic existence <note>original: "sangsāric"; referring to the continuous cycle of birth, death, and rebirth in the material world.</note></term>. This is held to be a natural law governing the manifestation of life, as inviolable as the law of <term>cause and effect <note>original: "karma"; the principle that every intentional action has a corresponding moral consequence.</note></term>, which sets it into operation.

For a human life-flux <note>The continuous stream of consciousness that moves from one life to the next.</note> to flow into the physical form of a dog, or fowl, or insect, or worm, is, therefore, held to be as impossible as would be—let us say—the transferring of the waters of Lake Michigan into the depression occupied by the waters of Lake Killarney, or—as the Indian would say—as putting into the bed of the Ganges River the waters of the Indian Ocean.

Degeneration, in a highly developed flowering plant, or apple, or vegetable, or wheat, or animal, is, of course, concomitant with cultural neglect; but within this creation period—at least so far as the physical vision of science has penetrated therein—the flowering plant does not degenerate into the apple, nor into the corn, nor one species of animals into another, nor does man degenerate into anything but the savage man—never into a sub-human creature. As to the processes affecting the life-flux which the human eye cannot see, the esoteric teaching <note>The "inner" or secret teachings reserved for initiated students, as opposed to "exoteric" or public teachings.</note> coincides with that of the ancient Greek and Egyptian mystics: 'As below, so above'; which implies that there is one harmonious <term>law of cause and effect <note>original: "karmic law"</note></term> governing with unwavering and impartial justice the visible as well as the invisible operations of nature.

From this follows the corollary, which the Oriental advocates of the esoteric interpretation give out: Progression or retrogression—never an unchanging neutral state of inactivity—are the alternatives within the <term>cycle of existence <note>original: "Sangsāra"</note></term>; and the one or the other, within any of the mansions of existence, cannot lead the life-flux to the threshold of that mansion—neither the sub-human to the human, nor the human to the sub-human—save step by step. And retrogression and progression alike are time-processes: ages pass ere the fire-mist becomes the solidified planet; an <term>Enlightened One <note>An individual who has attained the state of a Buddha, having reached full spiritual awakening.</note></term> is the rare fruit of unknown myriads of embodiments; and man, the highest of the animal-beings, cannot become the lowest

It draws parallels between spiritual laws and the physical laws of nature, asserting that progress or decline happens step-by-step over vast periods of time.</summary>

...of the animal-beings, no matter how heinous his sins, at one bound. <note>This sentence completes the thought from the previous page, asserting that a human cannot instantly "jump" into an animal rebirth regardless of their negative actions.</note>

Given ages of continual retrogression, the life-flux <note>The continuous stream of consciousness that moves from one life to the next.</note> which is now human may cease to be human, the human constituents of it becoming atrophied or latent through lack of exercise, in much the same way as atrophy overcomes the activity of a bodily organ or function which is not used. Thereupon, being no longer kinetically, but merely potentially human—just as a dog or horse or elephant are potentially, but not kinetically, human—that life-flux can and ordinarily would fall back into the sub-human kingdoms, whence it may begin anew to rise upwards to the human state or continue to retrograde even below the brute world.

The late <term>Spiritual Teacher <note>original: "Lāma"; a title for a high-ranking teacher or master in Tibetan Buddhism.</note></term> Kazi Dawa-Samdup, the translator, has left on record his own complementary opinion, as follows:

> 'The forty-nine days of the <term>Intermediate State <note>original: "Bardo"; the transitional state of existence between death and the next rebirth.</note></term> symbolize ages either of evolution or of degeneration. Intellects able to grasp Truth do not fall into the lower conditions of existence.
>
> 'The doctrine of the transmigration of the human to the sub-human may apply solely to the lower or purely brutish constituents of the human principle of consciousness; for the Knower itself neither incarnates nor re-incarnates—it is the Spectator.
>
> 'In the <term>Liberation Through Hearing in the Intermediate State <note>original: "Bardo Thödol"; the Tibetan title of the text often called "The Tibetan Book of the Dead."</note></term>, the deceased is represented as retrograding, step by step, into lower and lower states of consciousness. Each step downwards is preceded by a swooning into unconsciousness; and possibly that which constitutes his mentality on the lower levels of the <term>Intermediate State <note>original: "Bardo"</note></term> is some mental element or compound of mental elements formerly a part of his earth-plane consciousness, separated, during the swooning, from higher or more spiritually enlightened elements of that consciousness. Such a mentality ought not to be regarded as on a par with a human mentality; for it seems to be a mere faded and incoherent reflex of the human mentality of the deceased. And perhaps it is some such thing as this which incarnates in sub-human animal bodies—if anything does in a literal sense.'

### ESOTERICISM OF THE DOCTRINE OF REBIRTH

orders <note>This fragment completes the sentence from the previous page: "The doctrine of the transmigration of the human to the sub-human may apply solely to the lower orders [of beings]..."</note>, for he expressed it while quite unaware of its similarity to the theory held secretly <note>original: "esoterically"; referring to knowledge intended only for a small, specialized group.</note> by the Egyptian priests and publicly <note>original: "exoterically"</note> recorded by Herodotus, who, apparently, became their pupil in the monastic college at Heliopolis <note>An ancient Egyptian city, a center for the worship of the sun god Ra.</note>. Judging from what Herodotus and others of the ancient Greeks, and Romans as well, have written touching thereon, we arrive at the following summary: The human soul was believed to remain in the after-death state during a period of three thousand years. Its physical body <note>original: "human-plane body"</note> of the moment of death disintegrating, the constituents went to form the bodies of animals and plants, moving from one to another <note>original: "transmigrating"</note> during the three thousand years. At the end of that period the soul gathers together the identical particles of matter which had thus been continually moving through other forms and which had constituted its former earth-plane body of the moment of death, and from them rebuilds—through habit, as a bird builds its nest—a new body and is reborn in it as a human being.¹

---

¹ *Compare* <note>original: "Cf."</note> Herodotus, ii. 123; Lucretius, *On the Nature of Things* <note>original: "De Rerum Natura"</note>, iii. 843-61. In common with ancient historians and philosophers, Herodotus (ii. 171) refuses to divulge, in a literal manner, the higher or secret teachings of the Mysteries of Antiquity:
> 'On this lake [within the sacred precinct of the temple at Sais] the Egyptians perform by night the representation of his adventures [meaning the symbolic adventures regarding the birth, life, death, and rebirth of Osiris—'whose name', writes Herodotus, 'I consider it a sin <note>original: "impious"</note> to reveal'], which they call Mysteries. On these matters, however, though accurately acquainted with the particulars of them, I must [as an initiate] observe a discreet silence. So, too, with regard to the Mysteries of Demeter [celebrated at Eleusis, in Greece], which the Greeks term "The Thesmophoria" <note>An ancient festival held in honor of Demeter, the goddess of agriculture, and her daughter Persephone.</note>, I know them [as an initiate], but I shall not mention them, except so far as may be done without sin [or done lawfully].'

It has now been proven by archaeological and other research that the Mysteries consisted of symbolic dramatic performances open only to those who had been formally admitted <note>original: "initiates and neophytes"</note>, illustrating the universally spread secret teachings concerning death and resurrection (meaning rebirth); and that the doctrine of the movement of the human soul into animal bodies—if depicted at all—was not intended to be taken literally (as it has been by those outside the circle of the initiated), but symbolically as in Plato's *Republic*, detailed reference to which follows herein. (*Compare* Herodotus, ii. 122.)

Herodotus in the last-mentioned passage gives a symbolic account of the descent into the underworld <note>original: "Hades"</note> and the return to the human world of King Rhampsinitus, in whose honour the priests of Egypt therefore instituted what was probably—when interpreted through reason—a rebirth festival. The most ancient recorded parallel now known exists in the *Rig Veda* <note>The oldest of the sacred books of Hinduism.</note> (*Section* <note>original: "Maṇḍala"</note> 10, *Hymn* <note>original: "Sūkta"</note> 135), wherein,

<summary>This page compares Tibetan concepts of rebirth with ancient Egyptian and Greek "Mysteries," suggesting that stories of humans reincarnating as animals were intended as symbolic lessons rather than literal truths. It highlights a 3,000-year cycle of the soul described by Herodotus and hints at even older parallels in the Hindu Rig Veda.</summary>

# INTRODUCTION

And this theory, when amended with certain necessary modifications, helps to illustrate the symbolical or hidden <note>original: "esoteric"</note> interpretation of the *Bardo* <term>Bardo: In Tibetan Buddhism, the "intermediate state" between death and the next rebirth.</term> Rebirth Doctrine.

In further illustration, applicable to the higher Hinduism as to the higher Buddhism, advocates of this interpretation point out that even before the final dissolution of the human body at the moment of death there is incessant movement <note>original: "transmigration"</note> of the bodily atoms. So long as the body is the receptacle of the consciousness-principle, it is said to renew itself completely every seven years. And even as the constituents of the physical man thus move through all organic and inorganic kingdoms and the mind remains unchangeably human during the brief cycle of one life-time, so, normally, it likewise remains human during the greater evolutionary cycle—that is <note>original: "i. e."</note>—until it reaches the end of all worldly <note>original: "sangsāric"</note> evolution, namely, Enlightenment in Nirvana <note>original: "Nirvāṇic Enlightenment"</note>.

The hidden <note>original: "esoteric"</note> teaching concerning this may be stated literally: That which is common to the human and to the sub-human worlds alike, namely, matter in its varied aspects as solids, liquids, and gases, eternally moves from form to form <note>original: "transmigrates"</note>. That which is specifically human and specifically sub-human remains so, in accordance with the law of nature that like attracts like and produces like, that all forces ever follow the line of least <note>This sentence is interrupted by the end of the text block and likely continues on the next page with the word "resistance."</note>

as Sayana <note>A famous 14th-century commentator on the Vedas.</note> in his Commentary in the *Atharva Veda* <note>One of the four sacred Vedas of Hinduism, containing hymns and incantations.</note> (book nineteen) seems to explain, the boy mentioned is the same as the boy Nachiketas of the [*Taittirīya*] *Brāhmaṇa* <note>An ancient Hindu ritual text.</note>, who went to the realm of Yama, the King of the Dead, in the Realm of Yama <note>original: "Yama-Loka"</note>, and then returned to the realm of men. That this primeval legend of the underworld <note>original: "Hades legend"</note> was interpreted symbolically <note>original: "esoterically"</note> as teaching a rebirth doctrine is confirmed by the ancient *Katha Upanishad*, the story of Nachiketas being used therein as a literary vehicle to convey the highest teachings of the Vedanta <note>A school of Hindu philosophy based on the Upanishads.</note> concerning birth, life, and death. (Compare <note>original: "Cf."</note> *Katha Upanishad*, ii. 5; iii. 8, 15; iv. 10–11; vi. 18.)

Preserved in an Old Javanese manuscript of the fourteenth century is a very similar legend of the underworld in which the Spirit <note>original: "Yaksha"</note> Kuñjarakarṇa is commanded by the Lord Vairochana <note>The "Illuminator" Buddha, central to many Eastern traditions.</note> "to go to Yama's kingdom to see what is prepared for all evil-doers." Peculiar interest attaches to this version, because it records a doctrine—akin to that referred to by the Greek and Roman writers—of thousand-year periods of moving through <note>original: "transmigration into"</note> plants, animals, and defective human beings, prior to rebirth in a human body free from the stains of past actions <note>original: "karmic blemishes"</note>. It mentions, too, that from Yama's kingdom Pūrṇavijaya was recalled to human life. (Compare <note>original: "Cf."</note> *The Legend of Kuñjarakarṇa*, translated from the Dutch of Prof. Kern by Miss L. A. Thomas, in the *Indian Antiquary*, Bombay, 1903, vol. xxxii, pp. 111–27.)

<summary>This page explores the "esoteric" interpretation of rebirth, arguing that while physical atoms constantly recycle, the core human consciousness remains stable until achieving Nirvana. It supports this by referencing ancient Hindu scriptures like the Katha Upanishad and Javanese legends regarding the soul's journey after death.</summary>

---

¹ An examination of *The Laws of Manu* <note>An ancient and foundational legal and ethical text of Hinduism, also known as the *Mānavadharmaśāstra*.</note>, the authority of which is unquestioned by orthodox Hindus, seems to confirm the symbolic <note>original: "esoteric"</note> interpretation. This follows the translation by Sir William Jones <note>An 18th-century linguist who was one of the first Europeans to study Sanskrit.</note> as revised by G. C. Haughton (in *Institutes of Hindu Law or the Ordinances of Menu*, London, 1825), and that by G. Bühler (in *The Sacred Books of the East*, vol. xxv, Oxford, 1886).

Manu at first sets forth the fundamental laws that '*Action*' <note>Refers to the concept of Karma.</note>, 'which springs from the mind, from speech, and from the body, produces either good or evil results; by action are caused the [various] conditions of man, the highest, the middling, and the lowest'; and that '[A man] obtains [the results of] a good or evil mental [act] in his mind, [that of] a verbal [act] in his speech, [that of] a bodily [act] in his body'. (Bühler's trans., xii. 3, 8.)

Manu then proceeds to expound how man is not a simple but a complex being:

> 'That substance, which gives a power of motion to the body, the wise call <term>kshētrajna: the knower of the field; the conscious principle that experiences life through the body</term>, or <term>jīvātman: the vital spirit or individual living soul</term>; and that body, which thence derives active functions, they name <term>bhūtātman: the elemental soul; the physical body formed from the elements</term>:
> 'Another internal spirit, called <term>mahat: the great soul; the principle of universal intelligence</term>, attends the birth of all creatures embodied, and thence in all mortal forms is conveyed a perception either pleasing or painful.
> 'These two, the vital spirit and reasonable soul, are closely united with *five* elements, but connected with the supreme spirit, or divine essence, which pervades all beings high and low.' (Jones's trans., xii. 12-14.)

<margin>✓</margin>
From what follows, Manu apparently implies that it is this 'vital spirit', or animal soul, which alone is capable of moving <note>original: "transmigrating"</note> into sub-human forms, and not 'the reasonable soul', or super-animal principle:

> 'When the vital soul has gathered the fruit of sins, which arise from a love of sensual [i.e. animal or brutish] pleasure, but must produce misery, and, when its taint has thus been removed, it approaches again those two most effulgent essences, *the intellectual soul and the divine spirit*:
> 'They two, closely conjoined, examine without remission the virtues and vices of that sensitive [or animal] soul, according to its union with which it acquires pleasure or pain in the present and future worlds.
> If the vital spirit had practised virtue for the most part, and vice in a small degree, it enjoys delight in celestial abodes, clothed with a body formed of pure elementary [i.e. ethereal] particles;
> 'But, if it had generally been addicted to vice, and seldom attended to virtue, then shall it be deserted by those pure elements, and, *having a coarser body of sensible nerves*, it feels the pains to which <term>Yama: the Hindu lord of death and judge of the deceased</term> shall doom it:
> 'Having endured those torments according to the sentence of Yama, and its taint being almost removed, it again reaches those five pure elements in the order of their natural distribution.' (Jones's trans., xii. 18-22.)

<summary>This page explores the "Laws of Manu" to support the idea that only the "lower" animal-like parts of the human soul undergo rebirth into lower forms, while the "higher" intellectual spirit remains distinct. It breaks down the human constitution into several parts, including the vital spirit, the elemental body, and the universal intelligence.</summary>

# INTRODUCTION

Accordingly, the seekers of hidden meaning <note>original: "esotericists"</note> hold it to be unscientific to believe that a human flow of life <note>original: "life-flux"</note> or consciousness-principle could reincarnate in the body of a lower animal creature within forty-nine days after its extraction from the human form, as the literalist <note>original: "exotericist"</note> believes who accepts such a rebirth doctrine at face value.

After further exposition of the science of rebirth in its hidden or rational aspect, Manu <note>A legendary figure in Hindu mythology, the progenitor of humanity and author of the foundational legal code.</note> arrives at the following summary:

<margin>✓</margin>
> 'Thus, by indulging the sensual [i.e. animal or brutish] appetites, and by neglecting the performance of duties, the basest of men, ignorant of sacred acts of atonement <note>original: "expiations"</note>, assume <insert>in their vital spirit form, but not in their reasonable soul form</insert> the basest forms.
> 'What particular bodies the vital spirit enters in this world, and in consequence of what sins here committed, now hear at large and in order.' (Jones's translation, xii. 52-3.)

Manu evidently exerts himself apart from the main subject of his treatise, as the whole of *The Laws* suggests, to invest with legal and divine sanction the dogma that the person of a Priest <note>original: "Brāhmin"</note> is peculiarly sacred and cannot be harmed, and so gives prominence to the sin of slaying a Priest by mentioning it first; then to the sin of a priest drinking spirituous liquor, and then to the sin of stealing the gold of a priest. In all such instances, as in all which follow them, the implication is, as we have above observed, that the 'vital spirit', or animal soul, separated from the two higher elements of man's complex constitution, which are 'the reasonable soul' and 'the divine essence', suffers the penalty of migrating in lower animal creatures:

<margin>✓</margin>
> 'The slayer of a *Priest* <note>original: "Brāhmen"</note> <insert>i. e. the vital spirit, or the irrational animal soul, of a Priest-slayer</insert> must enter, according to the circumstances of the crime, the body of a dog, a boar, an donkey <note>original: "ass"</note>, a camel, a bull, a goat, a sheep, a stag, a bird, an outcaste <note>original: "Chandāla"</note>, or a low-born person <note>original: "Puccasa"</note>'; and so on for other crimes (Jones's translation, xii. 55-7).

In this connection, it is interesting to observe a few of the correspondences between cause and effect which other parts of *The Laws* suggest. Thus, if a man steal precious things he 'shall be born in the tribe of goldsmiths <insert>considered to be of very low social status</insert>, or among birds called gold-makers <note>original: "hēmacāras"</note>. If a man steal grain in the husk, he shall be born a rat; if a yellow mixed metal, a gander <insert>which is of like mixed color</insert>; if water, a diver-bird <note>original: "plava"</note>. . . . If he steal flesh-meat, a vulture; . . . if oil, a cockroach <note>original: "blatta", or oil-drinking beetle</note>; . . . if exquisite perfumes, a musk-rat'. (Jones's translation, xii. 61-5.)

Understanding Manu in the sense which this note aims to set forth, the seekers of hidden meaning <note>original: "esotericists"</note> disallow such popular and literal interpretation of *Manu's Laws* as the Priests <note>original: "Brāhmins"</note> in their own interests promote—according to the esotericists—among the common people concerning the doctrines of rebirth and the law of cause and effect <note>original: "karma"</note>.

The student should observe that the italicized non-English words in the passages quoted in this note from the translation by Sir William Jones mark his additions <note>original: "interpolations"</note> from commentaries on Manu, especially from the *Explanatory Note of Culluca* <note>original: "Gloss of Culluca"</note>, and that the bracketed words indicate our own additions. Because the italicized additions tend to bring out the more obscure meanings of the passages cited, preference has been given to the Jones translation, although that by Bühler, which is more literal and therefore more technical, is, in all essentials, substantially the same.

<summary>This page explores the esoteric interpretation of the Laws of Manu, arguing that "rebirth" into animal forms refers specifically to the lower "vital spirit" rather than the higher human soul. It suggests that literal interpretations were promoted by the priestly caste to maintain social order and protect their status.</summary>

->### SYMBOLISM OF REBIRTH DOCTRINE<-

->H<-

<meta>3009</meta>

...as the *Liberation Through Hearing in the Intermediate State* <note>original: "Bardo Thödol"</note>, when viewed outerly <note>original: "exoterically"</note>, or literally, presents.

The rebirth symbols of the intermediate state <note>original: "Bardo"</note> themselves ought now to be considered from the standpoint of the inner, hidden interpretation <note>original: "esoteric interpretation"</note>; and to clarify them, countless parallels could be chosen from widely separated sources. However, because of its recognized authority, no parallel seems more appropriate than that contained in the tenth book of Plato's *Republic*, describing certain of the Greek Heroes in the intermediate state of seeking rebirth <note>original: "Sidpa Bardo"</note> choosing their bodies for their next incarnations:

The legend of the intermediate state <note>original: "Bardo"</note> as recorded in the *Republic* concerns Er the son of Armenius, a Pamphylian by birth, who, as Plato tells us,

> 'was slain in battle, and ten days afterwards, when the bodies of the dead were taken up already in a state of corruption, his body was found unaffected by decay, and carried home to be buried. And on the twelfth day, as he was lying on the funeral pile, he returned to life and told them what he had seen in the other world. He said that when his soul left the body he went on a journey with a great company, and they came to a mysterious place at which there were two openings in the earth; they were near together, and over against them were two other openings in the heaven above. In the intermediate space there were judges seated, who commanded the just, after they had given judgement on them and had bound their sentences in front of them, to ascend by the heavenly way on the right hand; and in like manner the unjust were bidden by them to descend by the lower way on the left hand; these also bore the symbols of their deeds, but fastened to their backs.'

Having thus described the otherworld Judgement—which closely resembles the Judgement described in our text—Plato goes on to describe the souls of the Greek Heroes in their intermediate state of seeking rebirth <note>original: "Sidpa Bardo"</note> preparing for reincarnation:

> 'Most curious, he said, was the spectacle—sad and laughable and strange; for the choice of the souls was in most cases based on their own experience of a previous life. There he saw the soul which had once been Orpheus <note>A legendary Greek musician and poet who attempted to rescue his wife from the underworld.</note> choosing the life of a swan out of enmity to the race of women, hating to be born of a woman...'

It emphasizes an "esoteric" or hidden interpretation of these rebirth symbols over a literal one.</summary>

# INTRODUCTION

because they had been his murderers; he beheld also the soul of Thamyras <note>A legendary Thracian musician who challenged the Muses and was subsequently blinded.</note> choosing the life of a nightingale; birds, on the other hand, like the swan and other musicians, wanting to be men. The soul which obtained the twentieth lot chose the life of a lion, and this was the soul of Ajax the son of Telamon <note>A Greek hero of the Trojan War; his choice reflects his pride and eventual fall.</note>, who would not be a man, remembering the injustice which was done him in the judgement about the arms <note>The contest where the armor of the fallen Achilles was awarded to Odysseus rather than Ajax, leading to Ajax’s suicide.</note>. The next was Agamemnon <note>The king who led the Greeks in the Trojan War, later murdered by his wife.</note>, who took the life of an eagle, because, like Ajax, he hated human nature by reason of his sufferings. About the middle came the lot of Atalanta <note>A famous huntress and athlete in Greek myth.</note>; she, seeing the great fame of an athlete, was unable to resist the temptation: and after her there followed the soul of Epeius the son of Panopeus <note>The engineer who built the Trojan Horse.</note> passing into the nature of a woman cunning in the arts; and far away among the last who chose, the soul of the jester Thersites <note>A soldier in the Greek army known for his vulgarity and mocking nature.</note> was putting on the form of a monkey. There came also the soul of Odysseus having yet to make a choice, and his lot happened to be the last of them all. *Now the recollection of former toils had disenchanted him of ambition, and he went about for a considerable time in search of the life of a private man who had no cares; he had some difficulty in finding this, which was lying about and had been neglected by everybody else; and when he saw it he said that he would have done the same had his lot been first instead of last, and that he was delighted to have it.* And not only did men pass into animals, but I must also mention that there were animals tame and wild which changed into one another and into corresponding human natures—the good into the gentle and the evil into the savage, in all sorts of combinations.’

If read superficially, this Platonic account of the rebirth process <note>original: "reincarnation"</note> may be understood literally—even as the *Liberation Through Hearing in the Intermediate State* <note>original: "Bardo Thödol"</note> may be; and it is not impossible to imagine that Plato, as an initiate into the Greek Mysteries <note>Secret religious rites of ancient Greece that offered guidance on the afterlife and the soul's journey.</note>, who, like Herodotus, never refers to their inner teachings <note>original: "esoteric teachings"</note> openly, but only in figurative and very often intentionally misleading phraseology, intended that it should be understood so by the uninitiated. Nevertheless, when the passage is examined closely, the outer doctrine <note>original: "exoteric doctrine"</note> of transmigration <note>The movement of a soul from a human body into a non-human animal body.</note> of the human into the sub-human, or vice versa, is evidently not the meaning underlying it.

<summary>The author concludes the Myth of Er, detailing how legendary Greek figures chose their next incarnations based on past traumas, and argues that Plato, like the Tibetan masters, intended these animal rebirths to be read as symbols rather than literal transformations.</summary>
<keywords>Myth of Er, Plato, Bardo Thödol, Reincarnation, Transmigration, Greek Mysteries, Esotericism, Odysseus, Ajax, Symbolism</keywords>
