<meta>This page continues the analysis of Plato's "Myth of Er," specifically interpreting the animal transformations described in the previous section. It argues that these descriptions should not be taken as literal biological changes, but rather as symbolic representations of the soul's character and past experiences, aligning with an esoteric understanding of the rebirth doctrine.</meta>

# SYMBOLISM OF REBIRTH DOCTRINE

The reference to the choice made by Odysseus <note>The cunning hero of the *Odyssey* who spent ten years trying to return home to Ithaca after the Trojan War.</note>, as italicized by us, gives the clue to the real meaning intended. Odysseus' choice was last; each of the heroes preceding him in choosing their lot had neglected the lot of "the life of a private man who had no cares," and Odysseus chooses this lot as the best of all.

If we consider the sort of life chosen by each of the Greeks who preceded Odysseus, we find it to be definitely symbolical of the character of the chooser:

Thus, Orpheus <note>A legendary musician and prophet; his music was said to be so beautiful it could charm all living things and even stones.</note>, the founder of the Orphic Mysteries <note>A set of religious beliefs and practices in ancient Greece associated with the soul's journey, purification, and the afterlife.</note>, a divine teacher sent to instruct mankind by the god of song and music, Apollo, and held by the Greeks to have been the greatest of harp-players and the most enlightened of poets and singers, very appropriately chooses "the life of a swan"; for since immemorial time the swan has symbolized—as it still does—song and music; and Plato's figurative language, correctly interpreted, implies that Orpheus was to reincarnate as a great poet and musician, as was but natural. To assume—as the <term>exotericist<gloss>One who interprets religious or philosophical texts literally or based only on their outward, public meaning.</gloss></term> may—that such a being as Orpheus could be born as a swan in reality thus appears to the <term>esotericist<gloss>One who looks for hidden, symbolic, or inner spiritual meanings within a text or tradition.</gloss></term> to be untenable.

Likewise, Thamyras <note>An ancient Thracian singer who, according to myth, was so proud of his skill that he challenged the Muses and was punished with the loss of his sight and his ability to sing.</note>, an ancient Thracian bard, renowned as a harp-player and singer, symbolically chooses the life of the sweet-singing nightingale.

Ajax, the Homeric hero <note>Ajax the Great, a formidable warrior in the *Iliad* known for his immense strength and courage.</note>, who, next to Achilles, was the bravest of the Greeks, most fittingly chooses the life of a lion; for the king of beasts is, and has been for unknown ages, the symbol denoting bravery or fearlessness, which almost all nations and races of men have recognized.

Agamemnon <note>The king of Mycenae and commander of the Greek forces during the Trojan War.</note>, the next to choose, selects the life of an eagle; for among Greek heroes he was the chief, as Zeus <note>The king of the Olympian gods.</note> was among the gods of Olympus; and, he having been regarded as an incarnation of Zeus and worshipped as one of the divinities, there is assigned to him the symbol of Zeus, which is the eagle.

Atalanta <note>A famous huntress and athlete who refused to marry any man who could not beat her in a footrace.</note>, the most swift-footed of mortals and famous for her foot-races with her many suitors, very naturally is reborn as a great athlete; and, in her case, Plato uses no symbol.

# INTRODUCTION

Nor is a symbol used in connexion with Epeius <note>The Greek engineer famous for building the Trojan Horse; though brilliant in craft, he was often depicted in later traditions as lacking physical courage.</note>, who, noted for his cunning in constructing the wooden horse at the siege of Troy, and whose cowardice afterwards became proverbial, is seen ‘passing into the nature of a woman cunning in the arts’.

As to the jester Thersites <note>A soldier of the Greek army during the Trojan War, known for his ugliness and for mocking his superiors.</note>, who puts on the form of a monkey, comment is unnecessary.

Accordingly, the expressions concerning the heroes’ hatred of being born of woman seem to be purely metaphorical and employed to carry out logically the literary use of the animal-symbols, just as the passages are concerning ‘animals tame and wild which changed into one another and into corresponding human natures—*the good into the gentle and the evil into the savage, in all sorts of combinations*’, and of ‘birds, on the other hand, like the swan and other musicians, wanting to be men’.

Even the ordinary soul, the first seen by Er <note>The protagonist of Plato's myth, a soldier who died in battle and returned to life to explain the journey of the soul and the process of reincarnation.</note> to make choice—although neither an incarnate divinity like Orpheus or Agamemnon, nor a hero like Ajax—and though possessed of a mind obscured by animal propensities, is not assigned by Plato, as he would be by a believer in the <term>exoteric<gloss>The public or literal interpretation of a doctrine, as opposed to its hidden, "esoteric" meaning.</gloss></term> rebirth doctrine, to birth in sub-human form. In his case, too, no animal-symbol is made use of:

> ‘He who had the first choice came forward and in a moment chose the greatest tyranny; his mind having been darkened by folly and sensuality, he had not thought out the whole matter before he chose, and did not at first perceive that he was fated, among other evils, to devour his own children. . . Now he was one of those who came from heaven, and in a former life had dwelt in a well-ordered State, but his virtue was a matter of habit only and he had no philosophy.’

And, as the *Bardo Thödol* <note>The Tibetan Book of the Dead, or "Liberation Through Hearing in the Intermediate State."</note> teaches, in other language, in its insistence on the need of Right Knowledge to the devotee who follows the Path of Enlightenment <note>original: "Bodhic Path"; the spiritual journey toward becoming a Buddha.</note>, so Plato teaches:

> ‘For if a man had always on his arrival in this world dedicated himself from the first to sound philosophy, and had been moderately fortunate in the number of the lot, he might, as the messenger reported, be happy here, and also his

<summary>This page argues that Plato's descriptions of souls choosing animal or different human forms are metaphors for character traits rather than literal biological transformations. It highlights the danger of "habitual virtue" without philosophical understanding, drawing a parallel between Greek thought and the Tibetan Bardo Thödol.</summary>

# SYMBOLISM OF REBIRTH DOCTRINE

With the assistance of symbols and metaphors, Pindar <note>An ancient Greek lyric poet known for his odes; he was one of the first to articulate the idea of the soul's judgment and reincarnation.</note>, Empedocles, Pythagoras, and Socrates, like Plato and the Greek Mysteries, also taught the rebirth doctrine.

On a golden funereal tablet dug up near the site of Sybaris <note>An ancient Greek city in southern Italy known for its wealth; such tablets were often buried with initiates of the Orphic mysteries to guide them in the afterlife.</note> there is the following line of an inscription: 'And thus I escaped from the cycle, the painful, the misery-laden.'² This, like known Orphic <note>Refers to Orphism, a set of religious beliefs and practices in the ancient Greek and Hellenistic world associated with the mythical poet Orpheus.</note> teachings, is purely Buddhistic and Hindu, and suggests that in ancient Greece the rebirth doctrine was widespread, at least among Greeks of culture who had been initiated into the Mysteries.

Symbolism similar to that used by Plato has been used by the recorders of the Buddhist Scriptures as well, as, for example, in the account of the Northern School of the birth of the Buddha Himself. This latter from the Tibetan *<term>Vinaya Pitaka<gloss>The 'Basket of Discipline,' the portion of the Buddhist canon dealing with monastic rules and the life of the Buddha.</gloss></term>* or *Dulva* (the most trustworthy and probably oldest part of the *<term>Bkah-hgyur<gloss>The Kangyur, or the 'Translated Word' of the Buddha, which forms the first part of the Tibetan Buddhist canon.</gloss></term>*), III, folio 452ª of the copy in the East India Office, Calcutta, runs thus :

> 'Now the future Buddha was in the <term>Tushita Heaven<gloss>The 'Joyful' realm where enlightened beings reside before their final rebirth on Earth.</gloss></term>, and knowing that his time had come, he made the five preliminary examinations: first, of the proper family [in which to be born]; second, of the country; third, of the time; fourth, of the race; fifth, of the woman. And having decided that Mahāmāyā was the right mother, in the midnight watch he entered her womb under the appearance of an elephant. Then the queen had four dreams: first, she saw a six-tusked white elephant <note>A powerful symbol in Buddhism representing purity, sovereignty, and the descent of a divine being into the world.</note> enter her womb; second, she moved in space above; third, she ascended a great rocky mountain; fourth, a great multitude bowed down to her.

> 'The soothsayers predicted that she would bring forth a son with the thirty-two signs of the great man. "If he stay at home, he will become a universal monarch; but if he shave his hair and beard, and, putting on an orange-coloured robe, leave his home for the homeless state and

<header>INTRODUCTION</header>

Again, the *<term>Jātaka<gloss>The "Birth Stories"—a collection of legends detailing the Buddha's previous lives as both humans and animals.</gloss></term>*,—of the Southern School <note>The Theravāda tradition, primarily found in Sri Lanka, Myanmar, Thailand, Laos, and Cambodia.</note>,—a compilation of folk-lore, folk-belief, and popular mythology touching the Buddha and His many incarnations, which crystallized round about His personality in much the same way as the matter of the Arthurian Legend <note>The body of stories and medieval romances centering on the legendary King Arthur of Britain.</note> crystallized round King Arthur, during the third century after His death¹—attributes to Him many previous births in sub-human form; and although the esotericist <note>One who seeks the hidden or inner spiritual meaning behind external religious doctrines.</note> would concede that in remote aeons of evolution such incarnations could possibly have been really sub-human, he would give to such of them as may have occurred in this world-period a symbolical significance, whereas the orthodox <term>Theravādist<gloss>A follower of the "School of the Elders," which emphasizes a literal and historical interpretation of the Pali scriptures.</gloss></term> interprets all of them literally.

In any case, a literal interpretation of the *Jātaka*—seeing that it is, according to the esotericist, essentially an exoteric treatise <note>A teaching intended for the general public rather than a small group of initiates.</note> designed for the people²—appears to be more plausible than that of the *<term>Dulva<gloss>The Tibetan version of the Vinaya, or the "Basket of Discipline," which outlines monastic rules and the Buddha's life story.</gloss></term>* account of the Buddha's birth. Furthermore, since there is a parallel account in the <term>Pali Scriptures<gloss>The standard collection of scriptures in the Theravāda Buddhist tradition, written in the Pali language.</gloss></term> wherein the same animal symbol, namely, the six-tusked white elephant, is employed, we have here an example of the use of symbolism, definite in purpose, common to both Northern and Southern Buddhism, which even the exotericist could not but interpret symbolically.

Similarly, as the popular interpretation appears to have fundamentally shaped the *Jātaka*, so it may have also affected the compilation of the *<term>Bardo Thödol<gloss>Literally "Liberation Through Hearing in the Intermediate State," popularly known as the Tibetan Book of the Dead.</gloss></term>*; for like all treatises which have had at least a germ-origin in very ancient times and then grown up by the ordinary process of amalgamating congenial material, the *Bardo Thödol*, as a **Doctrine of Death and Rebirth**, seems to have existed at first unrecorded, like almost all sacred books now recorded in <term>Pali</term>, <term>Sanskrit</term>, or <term>Tibetan</term>, and was a growth of unknown centuries. # SYMBOLISM OF REBIRTH DOCTRINE

There is no doubt it had lost something of its primitive purity. By its very nature and religious usage, the *<term>Bardo Thödol<gloss>Literally "Liberation Through Hearing in the Intermediate State," the Tibetan funerary text popularly known in the West as the "Tibetan Book of the Dead."</gloss></term>* would have been very susceptible to the influence of the popular or <term>exoteric<gloss>Teachings intended for the general public, focusing on the literal or outer meaning rather than hidden spiritual truths.</gloss></term> view; and in our opinion it did fall under it, in such manner as to attempt the impossible, namely, the harmonizing of the two interpretations. Nevertheless, its original <term>esotericism<gloss>The inner, hidden meaning of a doctrine intended only for those with specialized spiritual training.</gloss></term> is still discernible and predominant. Let us take, for example, the animal-thrones of the <term>Five Dhyāni Buddhas<gloss>Also known as the "Five Wisdom Buddhas," they represent the five qualities of the Buddha and the transformation of the five poisons/negative emotions into five wisdoms.</gloss></term> as it describes them, in harmony with Northern Buddhist symbology:

*   The **Lion-throne** is associated with **Vairochana** <note>The "Illuminator," the central Buddha who represents the wisdom of the universal law.</note>.
*   The **Elephant-throne** with **Vajra-Sattva** <note>The "Diamond Being," representing the indestructible nature of the mind and the purification of anger.</note>.
*   The **Horse-throne** with **Ratna-Sambhava** <note>The "Jewel-Born One," representing the wisdom of equality and the transformation of pride.</note>.
*   The **Peacock-throne** with **Amitābha** <note>The Buddha of "Infinite Light," representing discerning wisdom and the transformation of attachment.</note>.
*   The **Harpy-throne** <note>original: "Shang-shang." A mythical creature often depicted as half-human and half-bird, similar to a Garuda.</note> with **Amogha-Siddhi** <note>The "Infallible Success," representing the wisdom of action and the transformation of envy or jealousy.</note>.

And, in interpreting the symbols, we find them to be poetically descriptive of the peculiar attributes of each deity: the **Lion** symbolizes courage or might, and sovereign power; the **Elephant**, immutability; the **Horse**, sagacity and beauty of form; the **Peacock**, beauty and power of transmutation, because in popular belief it is credited with the power of eating poisons and transforming them into the beauty of its feathers; the **Harpy**, mightiness and conquest over all the elements. The deities, too, in the last analysis, are symbolical of particular **Bodhic** <note>Related to "Bodhi" or Enlightenment.</note> attributes of the *<term>Dharma-Kāya<gloss>The "Truth Body," representing the ultimate, unmanifested nature of reality and the Buddha's mind.</gloss></term>* and of supramundane forces of Enlightenment emanating thence, upon which the devotee may depend for guidance along the Path to Buddhahood.

In attempting the esoteric interpretation of the animal symbols used in the *<term>Sidpa Bardo<gloss>The final stage of the intermediate state (Bardo) where the soul seeks a new womb for rebirth.</gloss></term>*—and this interpretation finds its parallel in the esoteric interpretation obviously intended by the *Sidpa Bardo* episode in Plato <note>Referring to the Myth of Er in Plato's Republic, where souls choose their next lives in various forms.</note>, as in the *<term>Dulva<gloss>The Tibetan translation of the Vinaya, the Buddhist canonical texts concerning monastic discipline and the historical life of the Buddha.</gloss></term>* account of the birth of the Buddha—we have sufficient Buddhist rebirth symbols whose esoteric interpretation is clearly known and generally accepted to guide us.

emblems that the *<term>spiritual teachers<gloss>Tibetan: lāmas</gloss></term>* have preserved for us. And by its means I have been able to restore the fragment of a cycle in the verandah of <note>A famous complex of ancient Buddhist cave temples in India</note> Ajaṇṭā Cave No. XVII, hitherto uninterpreted, and merely known as "the Zodiac". <note>Early Western explorers often called the "Wheel of Life" a zodiac because of its circular shape and symbolic figures.</note> This picture portrays in symbolic and concrete form the three original sins and the recognized <term>causes of rebirth<gloss>Sanskrit: Nidānas; the twelve links in the chain of existence that trap beings in the cycle of suffering.</gloss></term>, so as to ensure their being vividly perceived and avoided; while the evils of existence in its various forms and the tortures of the damned are intended to intimidate evil-doers. In it, the three original sins are depicted as a pig, a cock, and a snake, and their <term>inner meaning<gloss>original: "esoteric significance"</gloss></term> is given by Dr. Waddell thus: "The pig symbolizes the ignorance of stupidity; the cock, animal desire or lust; and the snake, anger."¹ In the accompanying symbolic illustrations of the Twelve <term>Causes of Rebirth<gloss>original: "Nidānas"</gloss></term>, only the third is an animal symbol, the others being human and figurative symbols; and this is a monkey eating fruit, symbolizing <term>consciousness<gloss>Tibetan: nam-she; Sanskrit: Vijñāna. This refers to the dualistic mind that distinguishes between self and object.</gloss></term> of good and evil fruits, through tasting every fruit or sensuous experience in the manner of a roving <note>meaning someone who acts on impulse without spiritual discipline</note> non-philosophically guided libertine, thus engendering consciousness.²

Accordingly, the animal forms and environments named in the Second Book of the *<term>Tibetan Book of the Dead<gloss>Tibetan: Bardo Thödol, "Liberation Through Hearing in the Intermediate State."</gloss></term>* (see pp. 178-9, 185) as possible forms and environments to be entered by the <term>soul<gloss>original: "human consciousness-principle"</gloss></term> upon rebirth in this world may be interpreted as follows:

(1) The dog-form (like that of the cock in 'The Wheel of Life') symbolizes excessive sexuality or sensuality.³ It also symbolizes, in popular Tibetan lore, jealousy. And the dog-kennel environment symbolizes abiding in, or living in, a state of sensuality.

(2) The pig symbolizes (as in 'The Wheel of Life') the ignorance of stupidity dominated by lust; and selfishness

and uncleanness as well. The pigsty environment symbolizes worldly existence dominated by these characteristics.

(3) The ant symbolizes (as it does amongst the nations of the West) industry, and the lust for worldly possessions; and the ant-hill environment the dwelling under the corresponding conditions of life.

(4) The insect or grub symbolizes an earthly or grovelling disposition, and its hole the dwelling in an environment dominated by such disposition (see text, p. 179).

(5) The calf, kid, lamb, horse, and fowl forms mentioned (see text, pp. 178-9) symbolize, in like manner, corresponding characteristics common to those animals and to the highest of the animal beings, man, such as almost all civilized races have associated therewith, and popularly illustrated in animal mythology like that which Aesop <note>An ancient Greek storyteller credited with a number of fables now collectively known as Aesop's Fables.</note> made the basis of his *Fables*. In the *Old Testament* the visions of the prophet Ezekiel and in the *New Testament* the *Revelation* of John show how similar animal symbolism affected even the *Bible*. And, in our view, should the Buddhist and Hindu <term>exotericists<gloss>Those who follow the outer or literal meaning of religious teachings rather than the hidden, inner meanings.</gloss></term> re-read their own Scriptures in the light of the *Science of Symbols* their opposition to <term>Esotericism<gloss>Inner or hidden spiritual knowledge intended for those who have been specially initiated.</gloss></term> would probably be given up.

Accordingly, the animal symbols in the <term>Bardo of Seeking Rebirth<gloss>Tibetan: Sidpa Bardo; the final stage of the afterlife where the consciousness is drawn toward a new womb.</gloss></term>—despite evident corruptions of the text and of the inner <term>rebirth doctrine<gloss>original: "esoteric rebirth doctrine"</gloss></term> denoted by these symbols—should rightly be taken to imply that, in accordance with its <term>actions and their consequences<gloss>Sanskrit: karma; the law of cause and effect governing the cycle of rebirth.</gloss></term>, a human principle of consciousness, unless winning Emancipation <note>Liberation from the cycle of death and rebirth (Nirvana).</note>, will, under the normal conditions of gradual progression which govern the majority of mankind, continue to be born in a human form in this creation-period, with the mental traits or characteristics symbolized by animals. Under exceptional or abnormal <term>conditions of retrogression<gloss>original: "karmic conditions of retrogression"</gloss></term>, it may, on the other hand, during the course of ages, gradually lose its human nature and fall back into sub-human kingdoms.

As the translator explained, we need but look round us in the human world to find the bloodthirsty tiger-man, the murderer; the lustful swine-man; the deceitful fox-man; the thieving and imitating monkey-man; the grovelling worm-man; the industrious and oft-times miserly ant-man;

It argues that animalistic traits described in sacred texts represent human psychological states and moral conditions rather than literal transformations into sub-human species. It also describes how a spiritually developed individual can navigate the state between death and rebirth to achieve a higher existence or return as a teacher.</meta>

<header>INTRODUCTION</header>

the ephemeral—sometimes professedly aesthetic—butterfly-man; the strong ox-man; or the fearless lion-man. Human life is far richer in possibilities for the workings out of evil <term>consequences of one’s actions<gloss>The spiritual principle of cause and effect where intent and actions influence the future.</gloss> <note>original: "karma"</note></term>—no matter how animal-like those <term>consequences<note>original: "karma"</note></term> may be—than any sub-human species could possibly be. The illiterate folk-beliefs so common in Buddhist and Hindu lands, that a human murderer must inevitably be reborn as a ferocious beast of prey, or a sensualist as a pig or dog, or a miser as an ant, are, therefore, like many other popular beliefs, evidently based upon false analogies—some which have crept into Oriental Scriptures—and upon an unduly limited view of the innumerable conditions offered by human embodiment, from the saint to the criminal, from the King-Emperor to the slum-dweller, or from the man of culture to the lowest savage.

In accordance with our findings, that higher and rational teaching concerning rebirth, which in the <term>Liberation Through Hearing in the Intermediate State<note>original: "Bardo Thödol," the Tibetan funerary text popularly known as the "Tibetan Book of the Dead."</note></term> is, perhaps, confused because of corruptions of text, may now be summarized. If, on the Plane of Uncertainty, the influence of innate or <term>karmic<note>original: "karmic"</note></term> propensities of desire for the grosser sensations of <term>worldly<note>original: "sangsāric"</note></term> existence, such as govern life in a human body, can be dominated through the exercise of the more powerful influence of Right Knowledge, that part of the consciousness-principle capable of realizing <term>Enlightenment<note>original: "Buddhahood"</note></term> triumphs, and the deceased, instead of being obsessed with the frightful hallucinatory spectres of his lower or animal nature, passes the interval between human death and rebirth in one of the paradise realms instead of in the <term>Intermediate State<note>original: "Bardo"</note></term>. If such a more enlightened one be very unusually developed spiritually, that is to say if he be a great <term>spiritual<note>original: "yogic"</note></term> saint, he may gain even the highest of the paradises and be reborn among mankind under the guiding power of the ‘Lords of <term>Action<note>original: "Karma"</note></term>’, who, though still <term>worldly<note>original: "sangsāric"</note></term> beings, are described by the <term>venerable teachers<note>original: "lāmas"</note></term> as being immeasurably higher in evolution than man. When thus directed by the ‘Guardians of the Great Law’, the earth-returning one is said to reincarnate out of compassion, to assist human kind; he comes as a Teacher, as a Divine Missionary, as an <term>Emanation Body<gloss>A physical manifestation of an enlightened being who takes human form to guide others.</gloss> <note>original: "Nirmāṇa-Kāya"</note></term>

It explains that through "Right Knowledge," a person can avoid negative visions in the intermediate state after death and instead achieve a paradise realm or return as an enlightened teacher.</summary>

# THE HIGHER REBIRTH DOCTRINE

incarnate. Normally, however, rebirth is of the lower or ordinary sort, unendowed—because of the lack of enlightenment of the one undergoing it—with consciousness of the process. Even as a child knowing not the higher mathematics cannot measure the velocity of light, so the animal-man cannot profit by the higher law governing the rebirth of the divine-man; and, drinking of the <note>Likely a reference to the River Lethe in Greek mythology, used here as a metaphor for the loss of memory between incarnations.</note> River of Forgetfulness, he enters the door of the womb and is reborn, direct from the desire-world called the <term>Intermediate State<gloss>The state of existence between death and a new birth.</gloss><note>original: "Bardo"</note></term>. This lower rebirth, almost brutish in many instances, because controlled chiefly by animal propensities such as sub-human and human creatures have in common, differs, however, from that of brutes in virtue of the functional activity of the purely human element of the consciousness, which in all sub-human creatures is latent and not active; and for this element, even in the lowest of mankind, to become latent instead of active requires approximately as long a period of cyclic time as it does for the sub-human consciousness to evolve its latent human element into full human activity. The popular misunderstanding of this aspect of the higher or esoteric Doctrine of Rebirth thus appears to have assisted in no small measure to give rise to the obviously irrational belief, found almost everywhere throughout the Scriptures of both Buddhism and Hinduism, that the brute principle of consciousness in its entirety and the human principle of consciousness in its entirety are capable of alternately exchanging places with one another.

It was the late <note>Sir Edward Burnett Tylor (1832–1917), a founding figure of modern cultural anthropology.</note> Dr. E. B. Tylor, father of the modern science of <term>Anthropology<gloss>The scientific study of humanity, focusing on cultural practices and beliefs across time.</gloss></term>, who after a very careful examination of the data pronounced the higher doctrine of rebirth to be the more reasonable:

> 'So it may seem that the original idea of transmigration was the straightforward and reasonable one of human souls being reborn in new human bodies. . . . The beast is the very incarnation of familiar qualities of man; and such names as lion, bear, fox, owl, parrot, viper, worm, when we apply them as epithets to men, condense into a word some leading feature of a human life.'¹

# INTRODUCTION

That this is the true interpretation is confirmed—so far as Europe is concerned—by the teachings of the **Druids**, the learned Brahmin-like priests of Europe’s scientific pre-Christian religion, held by the Celtic nations.1 <note>The Druids were the high-ranking professional class in ancient Celtic cultures, serving as priests, judges, and lore-keepers.</note>

In *The Fairy-Faith in Celtic Countries*, in the year 1911, I suggested that the rebirth doctrine, in its straightforward, Druidic form, accords, in its essentials, with the psychological science of the West—that the subconscious mind is the store-house of all latent memories; that these memories are not limited to one lifetime; that these memory-records, being recoverable, prove the doctrine to be based upon demonstrable facts. Since the year 1911 the whole trend of Western psychological research in the realm of the subconscious and in psycho-analysis has tended to confirm that view.

<margin>✓</margin>
I was unaware when I wrote *The Fairy-Faith* that **Huxley** <note>Thomas Henry Huxley (1825–1895) was a famous English biologist known as "Darwin's Bulldog" for his advocacy of evolution.</note> held—as he did—the theory of human reincarnation to offer the best explanation of even ordinary physiological and biological phenomena. And since the testimony of Huxley, as one of the greatest biologists, coincides with that, as above given, of the late **Dr. Tylor**, the foremost of modern anthropologists, and also confirms from the standpoint of our own Western Science the higher or esoteric interpretation of the Rebirth Doctrine as offered by the Occult Sciences of the East, we here record it as a fitting conclusion to this Section:

> ‘Everyday experience familiarizes us with the facts which are grouped under the name of heredity. Every one of us bears upon him obvious marks of his parentage, perhaps of remoter relationships. More particularly, the sum of tendencies to act in a certain way, which we call “character”, is often to be traced through a long series of progenitors and collaterals. So we may justly say that this “character”—this moral and intellectual essence of a man—does veritably pass over from one fleshly tabernacle to another, and does really transmigrate

from generation to generation. In the new-born infant, the character of the stock lies latent, and the **Ego** <note>The "Ego" here refers to the individual sense of self or the "I" that persists through experiences.</note> is little more than a bundle of potentialities. But, very early, these become actualities; from childhood to age they manifest themselves in dullness or brightness,—weakness or strength, viciousness or uprightness; and with each feature modified by confluence with another character, if by nothing else, the character passes on to its incarnation in new bodies. > ‘As Prof. Rhys-Davids <note>Thomas William Rhys Davids (1843–1922) was an English scholar of the Pāli language and founder of the Pāli Text Society.</note> aptly says [in *Hibbert Lectures*, p. 114], the snowdrop “is a snowdrop and not an oak, and just that kind of snowdrop, because it is the outcome of the *Karma* of an endless series of past existences”.’¹

### XI. THE COSMOGRAPHY

Buddhist cosmography <note>Cosmography is the science that describes the features of the universe or cosmos.</note> as understood by the **lāmas** <note>Lamas are spiritual teachers or masters in Tibetan Buddhism.</note>, and continually referred to throughout our text, more especially in connexion with the **Doctrine of Rebirth**, is a very vast and complex subject; and to consider it here in any detail would involve the esoteric <note>Esoteric refers to knowledge intended for or likely to be understood by only a small number of people with a specialized interest or enlightenment.</note> as well as the exoteric <note>Exoteric refers to knowledge intended for or likely to be understood by the general public.</note> interpretation of an enormous mass of doctrines, more or less of **Brahmanic** <note>Brahmanic refers to the ancient Vedic and priestly traditions of India that preceded and influenced Buddhism.</note> origin, concerning the many states of sentient existence within the **Sangsāra** <note>original: "Sangsāra". This is the "cycle of existence," the beginningless series of births, deaths, and rebirths.</note>, or cosmos—some planetary as in this world, some

# INTRODUCTION

in the many heavens and paradises, and others in the numerous states of purgation called hells. Generalizing, it may be said that when the Brahmanic <note>Referring to the ancient Indian religious system from which Hinduism evolved.</note> and Buddhist teachings concerning cosmography <note>The study of the structure and mapping of the universe.</note> are carefully examined from the standpoint of the initiated Oriental, and not from the too-often prejudiced standpoint of the Christian philologist, it seems to suggest far-reaching knowledge, handed down from very ancient times, of astronomy, of the shape and motion of planetary bodies, and of the interpenetration of worlds and systems of worlds, some solid and visible (such as are alone known to Western Science) and some ethereal and invisible existing in what we may perhaps call a fourth dimension of space.

Esoterically <note>Knowledge intended for or likely to be understood by only a small number of people with a specialized knowledge.</note> explained, Mount Meru <note>original: *Ri-rab*, the "Great Mountain" at the center of the world.</note>, the central mountain of Hindu and Buddhist cosmography, round which our cosmos is disposed in seven concentric circles of oceans separated by seven intervening concentric circles of golden mountains, is the universal hub, the support of all the worlds. We may possibly regard it, like the Central Sun of Western astronomy, as the gravitational centre of the known universe. Outside the seven circles of oceans and the intervening seven circles of golden mountains lies the circle of continents.

In illustration, an onion of fifteen layers may be taken to represent roughly the **Tibetan Buddhist** <note>original: "lāmaic"</note> conception of our universe. The core, to which the fifteen layers cohere, is Mount Meru. Below, are the various hells; above, supported by Mount Meru, are the heavens of the gods, the more sensuous, like the thirty-three heavens ruled by Indra <note>The King of the Gods in Buddhist and Hindu mythology, associated with the sky and lightning.</note>, and those under the sway of Mara <note>The demonic figure who represents temptation and the cycle of death and rebirth.</note>, being ranged in their own regular gradation beneath the less sensuous heavens of Brahma <note>The creator god in the Indian tradition, representing the highest levels of the form-based heavens.</note>. As apex over all, is the final heaven, called 'The Supreme' <note>original Tibetan: *'Og-min*, meaning "The Unexcelled" or "None Below"</note>. Being the last outpost of our universe, **The Supreme Realm**, as the vestibule to **Nirvana** <note>The state of liberation from suffering and the cycle of rebirth.</note>, is the transitional state leading from the mundane to the supramundane; and thus there presides over it the divine influence of 'The Best of All' <note>original Tibetan: *Kuntu-zangpo*; Sanskrit: *Samanta-Bhadra*, meaning the "All-Good" or "Universal Goodness"</note>, the Tibetan Buddhist personification of **Nirvana**.

On a level with Indra's realm dwell, in their own heaven—

<page-num>63</page-num>

->THE COSMOGRAPHY<-

worlds, the eight **Mother Goddesses** <note>original: *Hlāmo*</note>, all of whom appear in our text. They are the Mother Goddesses of the early Hindus, called in Sanskrit the *Mātris* <note>A group of mother goddesses in Hindu mythology, often depicted as the female counterparts of major gods.</note>.

Within **Mount Meru** <note>The sacred mountain at the center of the universe in Buddhist and Hindu cosmology.</note> itself, upon which the Heavens rest, there are four realms, one above another. Of these, the three lower are inhabited by various orders of spirits; and in the fourth, immediately beneath the Heavens, from which, like the fallen angels of Christian belief, they were expelled on account of their pride, dwell the **‘Ungodly Spirits’**, the <note>original: *Asuras* (Tib. *Lha-ma-yin*)</note>, or Titans, who, as rebels, live and die waging unending war with the gods above.

The innermost layer of the onion <note>The author uses an onion as a metaphor for the concentric layers of the universe.</note> is the Ocean surrounding Mount Meru. The next layer, outwardly expanding, is that of the **Golden Mountains**; the next beyond is another Ocean; and so on, a circle of Golden Mountains always coming after a circle of Ocean until the fifteenth layer containing the outermost Ocean, in which float the Continents and their satellites <note>The smaller islands surrounding the four main continents.</note>. The skin of the onion is a wall of iron enclosing the one universe.

Beyond one such universe there lies another, and so on to infinity.¹ Each universe, like a great cosmic egg, is enclosed within the iron-wall shell, which shuts in the light of the sun and moon and stars, the iron-wall shell being symbolical of the perpetual darkness separating one universe from another. All universes alike are under the domination of natural law, with which <term>Karma: the law of cause and effect, where every action has a corresponding consequence.</term> is commonly made synonymous; for, in the Buddhist view, there is no scientific necessity to affirm or to deny the existence of a supreme God-Creator, the **Karmic Law** furnishing a complete explanation of all phenomena and being of itself demonstrable.

Each universe, like our own, rests upon ‘a warp and woof’ of blue air (i. e. ether), symbolized by crossed **thunderbolt-scepters** <note>original: *dorjes*; ritual objects used in Tibetan Buddhism to represent indestructibility and the irresistible force of enlightenment.</note> (such as are depicted by the emblem on the cover of our book). Upon

¹ Could we take the **Tibetan Buddhist** <note>original: "lāmaic"</note> conception of a universe to be that of a world-system, and of a plurality of universes to be that of a plurality of world-systems forming a universe, we should then be able better to correlate the cosmography of Northern Buddhism (and of Brahmanism, from which it appears to have originated) with the cosmography of Western Science.

# INTRODUCTION

this rests **'the body of the waters'** of the outer Ocean. Each Ocean symbolizes a stratum of air (or ether), and each of the intervening mountains a stratum of congealed air (or ether), that is to say, material substance; or, from a more occult view-point, the Oceans are the Subtle and the Mountains the Gross, the one alternating with the other as Opposites.

Like the **Seven Days** of the Mosaic <note>Referring to the tradition of Moses as the author of the creation story in the Book of Genesis.</note> version of Creation, the numerical dimensions which the **spiritual teachers** <note>original: *lāmas*</note> assign to our universe are more often to be taken as suggestive or symbolical than literal. <term>Mount Meru</term> <note>The sacred mountain at the center of the universe in Buddhist and Hindu cosmology.</note>, they say, towers 80,000 miles above the **Central Enchanted Ocean** and extends below the surface of the waters the same distance, the Central Ocean itself being also 80,000 miles deep and 80,000 miles wide. The succeeding girdle of **Golden Mountains** is just half that number of miles in height and width and depth, and the next Ocean, correspondingly, 40,000 miles deep and 40,000 miles wide. The consecutive circles of alternating pairs composed of **Golden Mountains** and an **Enchanted Ocean** gradually diminish as to width, depth, and height, being respectively 20,000, 10,000, 5,000, 2,500, 1,250, and 625 miles. This brings us to the **Continents** in the **Outer Ocean of Space**.

Of these **Continents**, the four chief ones—as described in the **Second Book** of our ***Liberation Through Hearing in the Intermediate State*** <note>original: *Bardo Thödol*</note>—are situated in the **Four Directions**. On either side of each of these **Four Continents** are smaller or satellite **Continents**, thus making the total number of Continents twelve, which, again, is a symbolical number, like the number seven of the cosmographical arrangement.

The **Eastern Continent** is called in Tibetan **‘Vast Body’** <note>original: *Lü-pah* (*Lus-hpags*); Sanskrit: *Virāt-deha*</note>. Its symbolical shape is like that of a crescent moon; and, accordingly, the colour white is assigned to it, and crescentic faces are ascribed to its inhabitants, who are said to be tranquil-minded and virtuous. Its diameter is given as being 9,000 miles.

The **Southern Continent** is our **Planet Earth**, called **‘Rose-Apple Island’** <note>original: *Jambu-ling*; Sanskrit: *Jambudvīpa*</note>, probably an onomatopoeic word—as the translator held—descriptive of the fruit of a rose-apple tree falling into water, **‘region’** <note>original: *ling*</note> itself meaning 'place', or 'region'.

<meta>This page continues the detailed description of the four major continents of the Buddhist world-system, moving from the human realm of the south to the western and northern continents, and concludes with the mystical symbolism of Mount Meru's faces.</meta>

<page-num>65</page-num>
<header>THE COSMOGRAPHY</header>

The name <term>Jambuling</term> <note>From the Sanskrit "Jambudvipa," referring to the Rose-Apple tree which grows at its center.</note> would thus mean the region or continent wherein rose-apple fruit fall into the water. Its symbolic shape is like that of the shoulder-blade of a sheep, that is sub-triangular, or rather pear-shaped, to which the faces of its inhabitants conform. Blue is the colour assigned to it. Riches and plenty abound in it, along with both good and evil. It is said to be the smallest of the Four Continents, being but 7,000 miles in diameter.

The Western Continent is called <term>Balongchōd</term> <note>original: *Ba-glang-spyöd*</note>, literally meaning *cow+ox+action* (Sanskrit <term>Godhana</term> <note>Literally "Wealth of Oxen."</note>, or 'Wealth of Oxen'). In shape it is like the sun, and red of colour. Its inhabitants, whose faces are round like the sun, are believed to be very powerful and to be addicted to eating cattle, as the literal meaning of its name itself may suggest. Its diameter measures 8,000 miles.

The Northern Continent is <term>Daminyan</term>, or <term>Graminyan</term> <note>original: *Sgra-mi-snyan*, meaning "Land of the Unpleasant Sound."</note>, equivalent to the Sanskrit <term>Uttara Kuru</term>, meaning 'Northern Kuru [Race]'. It is of square shape and green colour. Its inhabitants have corresponding faces, square like those of horses. Trees supply all their sustenance and wants, and the Kuru, on dying, haunt the trees as tree-spirits. This is the largest of the Continents, being 10,000 miles in diameter.

Each satellite Continent resembles the Continent to which it is attached, and is one-half its size. The left Satellite of our world (<term>Jambuling</term>), called <term>Ngāyabling</term> <note>The "Tail-fan Continent," often associated with the realm of demons.</note>, is, for example, the world of the <term>Rākṣhasas</term> <note>A class of powerful, often carnivorous spirits or demons in Buddhist and Hindu mythology.</note>, to which <term>Padma Sambhava</term> <note>The "Lotus-Born" master, a historical and legendary figure who brought Buddhism to Tibet in the 8th century.</note>, the Great *Guru* <note>Spiritual teacher.</note> of <term>Lāmaism</term> <note>A historical term for Tibetan Buddhism.</note>, is believed to have gone to teach the <term>Rākṣhasas</term> goodness and salvation, and to be there now as their king.¹

Underlying this *Tibetan Buddhist* <note>original: *lāmaic*</note> cosmology there is, as research will show, an elaborate symbolism. Take, for instance, the description of <term>Mt. Meru</term> <note>The sacred mountain at the center of the universe.</note> as given by Dr. Waddell: 'Its eastern face is of silver, the south of jasper, the west of ruby, and the north of gold'²—which illustrates a use of ancient symbols very similar to that in the *Revelation* of John <note>Referring to the descriptions of the New Jerusalem in the Christian New Testament.</note>. ->INTRODUCTION<-

Hindu and in turn Buddhist <term>cosmography</term> <note>The study of the structure and origin of the universe.</note> would be—even if it were possible for us—quite beyond the scope of an introduction. Suffice it to say that the possession of a key to such explanation is claimed by expert professors of the <term>Occult Sciences</term> <note>In this historical context, "Occult Sciences" refers to esoteric spiritual traditions and meditative disciplines rather than magic in the modern sense.</note> in India and in Tibet—compared to which, in the realm of mind and matter, our Western Science is, so they maintain, but at the Threshold of the Temple of Understanding.

### XII. THE FUNDAMENTAL TEACHINGS SUMMARIZED

Ere passing on to the final Sections of this Introduction, touching the Manuscript itself, we may now summarize the chief teachings upon which the whole of the *Bardo Thödol* <note>Literally, "Liberation Through Hearing in the Intermediate State," commonly known as the *Tibetan Book of the Dead*.</note> is based, as follows:

1. That all possible conditions, or states, or realms of <term>sangsāric</term> <note>Pertaining to *Samsara*, the cycle of birth, suffering, and death.</note> existence, heavens, hells, and worlds, are entirely dependent upon phenomena, or, in other words, are nought but phenomena;
2. That all phenomena are transitory, are illusionary, are unreal, and non-existent save in the <term>sangsāric mind</term> <note>The unenlightened mind that perceives the world as solid and separate from itself.</note> perceiving them;
3. That in reality there are no such beings anywhere as gods, or demons, or spirits, or sentient creatures—all alike being phenomena dependent upon a cause;
4. That this cause is a yearning or thirsting after sensation, after the unstable <term>sangsāric</term> existence;
5. That so long as this cause is not overcome by Enlightenment death follows birth and birth death, unceasingly—even as the wise Socrates believed; <note>Referring to the Greek philosopher's views on the immortality of the soul and reincarnation (metempsychosis) as discussed in the *Phaedo*.</note>
<margin>✓</margin>
6. That the after-death existence is but a continuation, under changed conditions, of the phenomena-born existence of the human world—both states alike being <term>karmic</term> <note>Governed by the law of cause and effect based on one's past intentions and actions.</note>;
<margin>✓</margin>
7. That the nature of the existence intervening between death and rebirth in this or any other world is determined by antecedent actions;
8. That, psychologically speaking, it is a prolonged dream-like state, in what may be called the fourth dimension of space, filled with hallucinatory visions directly resultant from

<summary>This page concludes the discussion on cosmography and introduces a summary of the eight fundamental Buddhist teachings that underpin the Bardo Thödol, emphasizing that all states of existence are mental phenomena.</summary>
<keywords>cosmography, Occult Sciences, Bardo Thödol, Samsara, phenomena, Enlightenment, Socrates, Karma, fourth dimension, rebirth</keywords>

<page-num>67</page-num>

<header>THE FUNDAMENTAL TEACHINGS</header>

the mental-content of the percipient, happy and heaven-like if the <term>karma</term> <note>original: "karma"; the law of cause and effect where one’s past actions determine future circumstances.</note> be good, miserable and hell-like if the <term>karma</term> be bad;

9. That, unless <term>Enlightenment</term> <note>A state of perfect spiritual awakening and liberation from ignorance.</note> be won, rebirth in the human world—directly from the <term>Bardo</term> <note>original: "Bardo"; the intermediate state or "gap" between death and the next rebirth.</note> world or from any other world or from any paradise or hell to which <term>karma</term> has led—is inevitable;

10. That Enlightenment results from realizing the unreality of the <term>samsara</term> <note>original: "sangsāra"; the beginningless cycle of birth, suffering, death, and rebirth.</note>, of existence;

11. That such realizing is possible in the human world, or at the important moment of death in the human world, or during the whole of the after-death or <term>intermediate state</term>, or in certain of the non-human realms;

12. That training in <term>yoga</term> <note>original: "yoga"; here referring specifically to meditative disciplines used to control the thinking processes.</note>, i.e. in control of the thinking processes so as to be able to concentrate the mind in an effort to reach Right Knowledge, is essential;

13. That such training can best be had under a human <term>guru</term> <note>original: "guru"; a spiritual guide or teacher who provides personal instruction.</note>, or teacher;

14. That the Greatest of <term>Teachers</term> known to mankind in this cycle of time is Gautama the Buddha;

15. That His Doctrine is not unique, but is the same Doctrine which has been proclaimed in the human world for the gaining of Salvation, for the Deliverance from the Circle of Rebirth and Death, for the Crossing of the Ocean of <term>samsara</term>, for the Realization of <term>Nirvana</term> <note>original: "Nirvāṇa"; the ultimate state of liberation and the end of suffering.</note>, since immemorial time, by a long and illustrious Dynasty of Buddhas, who were Gautama's Predecessors;

16. That lesser spiritually enlightened beings, <term>Bodhisattvas</term> <note>original: "Bodhisattvas"; beings who have reached a high level of enlightenment but choose to remain in the cycle of existence to help others.</note> and <term>teachers</term>, in this world or in other worlds, though still not freed from the Net of Illusion, can, nevertheless, bestow divine grace and power upon the <term>disciple</term> <note>original: "shishya" or "chela"; a student or follower on the spiritual path.</note> who is less advanced upon the Path than themselves;

17. That the Goal is and can only be Emancipation from the <term>samsara</term>;

18. That such Emancipation comes from the Realization of <term>Nirvana</term>;

19. That <term>Nirvana</term> is non-worldly, being beyond all paradises, heavens, hells, and worlds;

<summary>This page lists the final eleven fundamental tenets of the Bardo Thödol, emphasizing that rebirth is inevitable without enlightenment and that the goal of the spiritual path is liberation from the cycle of existence (Samsara) through the realization of Nirvana.</summary>

<header>INTRODUCTION</header>
<page-type>text</page-type>

20. That it is the Ending of Sorrow;
21. That it is Reality.

He who realized <term>Nirvāṇa</term> <note>original: "Nirvāṇa"; the ultimate state of liberation, peace, and the extinction of suffering and the cycle of rebirth.</note>, the <term>Buddha Gautama</term> <note>The historical Buddha, Siddhartha Gautama, who lived approximately 2,500 years ago.</note> Himself, has spoken of it to His own disciples thus:

> ‘There is, disciples, a Realm devoid of earth and water, fire and air. It is not endless space, nor infinite thought, nor nothingness, neither ideas nor non-ideas. Not this world nor that is it. I call it neither a coming nor a departing, nor a standing still, nor death, nor birth; it is without a basis, progress, or a stay; it is the ending of sorrow.

> ‘For that which clingeth to another thing there is a fall; but unto that which clingeth no fall can come. Where no fall cometh, there is rest, and where rest is, there is no keen desire. Where keen desire is not, naught cometh or goeth; and where naught cometh or goeth there is no death, no birth. Where there is neither death nor birth, there neither is this world nor that, nor in between—it is the ending of sorrow.

> ‘There is, disciples, an Unbecome, Unborn, Unmade, Unformed; if there were not this Unbecome, Unborn, Unmade, Unformed, there would be no way out for that which is become, born, made, and formed; but since there is an Unbecome, Unborn, Unmade, Unformed, there is escape for that which is become, born, made, and formed.’¹

### XIII. THE MANUSCRIPT

Our manuscript copy of the <term>Bardo Thödol</term> <note>original: "Bardo Thödol"; literally "The Great Liberation through Hearing in the Intermediate State," known popularly in the West as the Tibetan Book of the Dead.</note> was procured by the editor early in the year 1919 from a young <term>lāma</term> <note>original: "lāma"; a spiritual teacher or monk in the Tibetan tradition.</note> of the <term>Kargyütpa Sect</term> <note>original: "Kargyütpa"; the Kagyu school, one of the four main lineages of Tibetan Buddhism, emphasizing meditation and oral transmission.</note> of the <term>Red Hat School</term> <note>A term used to distinguish the older lineages (like the Kagyu and Nyingma) from the "Yellow Hat" Gelug school.</note> attached to the <term>Bhutia Basti Monastery</term> <note>A prominent Buddhist temple located in Darjeeling, India.</note>, Darjeeling, who said that it had been handed down in his family for several generations. The manuscript is unlike any other seen by the translator or editor, in that it is illustrated by paintings in colour painted on the folios of the text. All other similarly illustrated Tibetan manuscripts seen by us have had the illustrations made on separate pieces of manuscript paper or else of cotton cloth, pasted to the folios. When procured, the

---

¹ <term>Udāna</term> <note>original: "Udāna"; a scripture from the Pali Canon containing "inspired utterances" attributed to the Buddha.</note>, viii. 1, 4. 3 ; based on a translation from the original <term>Pali</term> <note>An ancient Middle Indo-Aryan language used in the earliest Buddhist scriptures.</note> by Mr. Francis J. Payne, London, England.

<page-num>69</page-num>
<header>THE MANUSCRIPT</header>

The manuscript was in a very ragged and worn condition, now remedied by each folio <note>a leaf of a book, numbered on only one side</note> being inserted in a protective frame of **Tibetan paper** of the same sort as that upon which the manuscript is written. Fortunately, all of the illuminated <note>decorated with gold, silver, or colored illustrations</note> folios, though faded, were in a fair state of preservation. One of the ordinary folios, folio number 111, was missing, but this has now been replaced by a faithful copy of the same passage found in a <term>Block-Print</term> <note>a book produced using carved woodblocks for printing, a common traditional method in Tibet</note> version of the *<term>Bardo Thödol</term>* <note>original: "Bardo Thödol"; translated as "Liberation through Hearing in the Intermediate State," popularly known in the West as the Tibetan Book of the Dead</note> belonging to **Dr. Johan Van Manen**, Secretary of the **Asiatic Society** in Calcutta, who is well known as a Tibetan scholar. Reference to this Block-Print version is made throughout our translation. In all essentials, and, generally, word for word, our manuscript and Dr. Van Manen's Block-Print were found to be identical. In some spellings of proper names of deities of **Sanskrit** <note>the ancient liturgical and scholarly language of India</note> origin there are variations in the two versions, and in both books a number of clerical errors. The manuscript is far older than the modern Block-Print and seems to have been copied from an earlier manuscript.

The manuscript itself is undated, but the translator judged it to be from 150 to 200 years old. It has seen very much service, having been read many times over the dead; its ragged and worn condition is, therefore, no criterion—as it might seem to be—of its age. It is written in an excellent hand on the ordinary paper used for manuscripts among the Tibetans and Himalayan peoples, made from the pulped bark of the <term>Hdal</term> <note>original: "Hdal", pronounced *Dāā*; a plant of the Daphne genus</note>, otherwise known as **Daphne**, a kind of laurel of which one species bears a purplish white blossom, another a yellowish white. It is usually by <term>lāmas</term> <note>spiritual teachers or monks in the Tibetan tradition</note> in a monastery that the paper is manufactured. On account of the bark of the *Hdal* being extremely tough, the <term>Sikkimese</term> <note>the people of Sikkim, a Himalayan region</note> used it as ropes.

The total number of folios composing the manuscript is 137, each measuring about 9½ by 3¼ inches. Excepting the first folio and the first half of the second, the space actually occupied by the text on each measures on an average 8¼ by 2¼ inches. Most of the folios contain five lines of matter, a few contain four lines. The title-page contains two lines.

On leaf <note>original: "folio"</note> 64, the Eight <term>Kerima</term> <note>a group of eight wrathful, cemetery-dwelling goddesses</note> and the Eight <term>Htamenma</term> <note>animal-headed "half-human" goddesses</note> of the Thirteenth Day; and the Four Female Door-keepers <note>deities guarding the four cardinal directions of the sacred circle</note> of the Fourteenth Day;

Each deity is depicted in conformity with the description given in the text as to colour, position, posture, <term>mudrā</term> <note>symbolic hand gestures used in Buddhist ritual</note>, and symbols.

All of the illustrations in the manuscript thus belong to the **Interim State of Reality** <term>Chönyid Bardo</term> <note>the second stage of the afterlife where the deceased encounters visions of peaceful and wrathful deities</note> of the First Book. In our translation, copious annotations contain the textual name of each deity and the Sanskrit equivalent when, as in most cases, there is one.

No attempt has been made to compare <note>original: "collated"</note> our manuscript with other manuscripts of the same text, none having been available. Such manuscripts are, no doubt, numerous in Tibet, and the production of a standard or uniform text would require years of careful labour—a task remaining for scholars of the future. The only comparison of texts attempted was with Dr. Van Manen's Block-Print, which is probably not more than about twenty to thirty years old. The translator said that, so far as he was aware, Block-Prints of the **Liberation by Hearing in the Intermediate State** <term>Bardo Thödol</term> have appeared—at least in Sikkim and Darjeeling—rather recently, although probably known in Tibet itself much longer, block-type printing having been carried on for unknown centuries in China, and thence brought to Tibet, long before printing was done in Europe.¹

# INTRODUCTION

Each Buddhist Sect in Tibet, according to the opinion of the translator, probably has its own version of the **Liberation through Hearing in the Intermediate State** <term>Bardo Thödol</term> <note>a funerary text read to the deceased to guide them through the afterlife</note> more or less changed in some details, but not in essentials, from our version. The version used by the reformed **Virtuous Way** <term>Gelugpa</term> <note>the sect of the Dalai Lamas, known for its emphasis on logic and monastic discipline</note>, otherwise known as the Yellow-Hat School, is the most altered. It has had all references to **Padma Sambhava** <note>the 8th-century Indian master who brought Buddhism to Tibet</note>, the Founder of the **Ancient Ones** <term>Ñingmapa</term> <note>the oldest school of Tibetan Buddhism</note>—the Red-Hat School of Tibetan Buddhism—as well as the names of deities peculiar to the Red-Hats, removed <note>original: "expurgated"</note>.

Major W. L. Campbell, who was the British Political Representative in Sikkim during my residence there, wrote to me, from the Residency in Gangtok, under date of the twelfth of July, 1919, concerning the various versions of the **Liberation through Hearing**, as follows: 'The Yellow Sect have six, the Red Sect seven, and the **Oral Lineage** <term>Kar-gyut-pas</term> <note>a school emphasizing meditation and yoga, often associated with Milarepa</note> five.'

Our text being of the primitive or Red-Hat School and attributed to the Great **Teacher** <term>Guru</term> Padma Sambhava himself, who introduced **Vajrayana** <term>Tantric</term> <note>a specialized path of Buddhism involving complex rituals and visualizations</note> Buddhism into Tibet, has been deemed by us to be substantially representative of the original version, which, on the basis of internal evidence derived from our [manuscript, includes]:

6. 'The Confronting of the **Intermediate State of Rebirth** <term>Sidpa-Bardo</term>';
7. 'The Salvation by Attaching [whereby] the Physical Form <note>original: "Body Aggregate"</note> is Self-Liberated'—a version of the **Liberation by Wearing** <term>Tadhol</term> <note>the practice of placing sacred diagrams or texts on the body of the deceased</note> Doctrine—(see pp. 136¹, 152¹, 194 of our text);
8. 'The Prayer to Protect [One] from the Fears in the **Intermediate State** <term>Bardo</term>';
9. 'The Self-Liberating Diagnosis of the Symptoms of Death'—(cf. pp. 86, 89-97 of our text);
10. 'The Direct Confrontation called "The Unveiled Insight" <note>original: "The Naked Vision"</note>, and the Self-Liberation [by that]';
11. 'The Special Teaching showing the Forms of Merit or Demerit, while in the **Intermediate State of Rebirth**, called "The Self-Liberating in the **Intermediate State of Rebirth**"';
12. 'The Addenda <note>additional material</note> [to the above, "The Special Teaching"]';
13. 'Prayer to the Line [of **Teachers**] of the Divine Self-Liberating Doctrine';
14. 'The Ransoming of the Dying';
15. 'Self-Liberation called "Purification by Confession" <note>original: "Absolution by Confession"</note>';
16. 'The Best Wish-Granting **Liberation by Wearing**'—another form of the **Liberation by Wearing** Doctrine;
17. 'The Ritual called "The Self-Liberation from Habitual Propensities"'.

Herein, the treatises numbered 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 8 correspond—in slightly different versions—to the matter contained in our manuscript. The manuscript, moreover, contains much matter in the Appendix not contained in this Block-Print. The Block-Print itself is quite new, but the blocks from which it was printed may be quite old—how old, we have been unable to ascertain.

<header>THE MANUSCRIPT</header>

manuscript text, was probably, at least in essentials, pre-Buddhistic <note>suggesting that parts of the text may stem from the indigenous Bön traditions of Tibet before Buddhism arrived</note>.

As elsewhere noted, our manuscript is arranged as one work in two parts or books, with thirteen folios of texts of **Intermediate State** <term>Bardo</term> prayers as an appendix at the end. The Block-Print <note>a text printed from hand-carved wooden blocks</note> is arranged as two distinct books and lacks the appendix of prayers. But at the end of the first book of the Block-Print there comes a very important account of the origin of the **Liberation through Hearing in the Intermediate State** <term>Bardo Thödol</term>, which is not contained in our manuscript, and this is given in translation in the following Section.

### XIV. THE ORIGIN OF THE BARDO THÖDOL

Thus, from the Block-Print, and also from other Tibetan sources, we learn that the **Liberation through Hearing** <term>Bardo Thödol</term> text originated, or, what is perhaps more correct, was first committed to writing in the time of **Padma Sambhava** <note>the 8th-century Indian master who established Tantric Buddhism in Tibet</note>, in the eighth century A. D.; was subsequently hidden away, and then, when the time came for it to be given to the world, was brought to light by **Rigzin Karma Ling-pa**. The Block-Print account is as follows :

> ' This has been brought from the Hill of **Gampodar** <note>original Tibetan: Gampo-dar</note>, on the bank of the **Golden** <term>Serdan</term> <note>original Tibetan: Gser-ldan, meaning "Possessing Gold" or "Golden"</note> River, by **Rigzin Karma Ling-pa** <note>original Tibetan: Rigs-hdzin Kar-ma Gling-pa</note>.'

**Knowledge-Holder** <term>Rigzin</term>, as herein given, is a personal title, and **Karma Ling-pa** the name of a place in Tibet meaning 'Karma Land'. The translator has pointed out that *Rigs* is an erroneous spelling of *Rig*; for, if *Rigs* were correct, the name *Rigzin* would mean Class-Holder <note>original Tibetan: Rigs+hZin</note>. That *Rig* is intended—thus making the name mean **Knowledge-Holder** <term>Rigzin</term> <note>original Tibetan: Rig+hdzin</note>, a caste or class designation<sup>1</sup>—was confirmed by a small section of a **Liberation through Hearing** <term>Bardo Thödol</term> manuscript in the possession of the translator, in which Rigzin Karma Ling-pa is otherwise called **Discoverer of Treasures** <term>Tertön</term> <note>original Tibetan: Gter-bston</note>, or 'Taker-Out of Treasures'. The **Liberation through Hearing** <term>Bardo Thödol</term> is, therefore, one of the Tibetan Lost

---

<sup>1</sup> **Knowledge-Holder** <term>Rig-hdzin</term>, a translation into Tibetan of the Sanskrit term **Bearer of Knowledge** <term>Vidyā-Dhara</term>, used, as herein, of a learned person, such as a pandit, also denotes a class of supernatural beings like certain orders of fairies.

<summary>This page discusses the historical and legendary origins of the Bardo Thödol, identifying it as a "treasure text" hidden in the 8th century by Padma Sambhava and later discovered by Rigzin Karma Ling-pa.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the historical contextualization of the Bardo Thödol, moving from the 14th-century discovery by Karma Ling-pa back to the 7th and 8th-century foundations of Tibetan Buddhism and the arrival of the "Precious Guru," Padma Sambhava.</meta>

# INTRODUCTION

Books recovered by Rigzin of Karma Ling-pa <note>a "treasure-revealer" or tertön believed to have found hidden scrolls from antiquity</note>, who is held to be an emanation or incarnation of Padma Sambhava <note>the Indian master credited with bringing Tantric Buddhism to Tibet</note>, the Founder of **Lāmaism** <term>a term historically used to describe Tibetan Buddhism, referencing the "Lamas" or spiritual masters</term>.

<insert>(</insert>It was in the eighth century A.D. that **Lāmaism**, which we may define as **Tantric Buddhism** <term>a form of Buddhism focused on ritual, meditation, and yoga to achieve enlightenment</term>, took firm root in Tibet.<insert>)</insert> A century earlier, under the first king to rule over a united Tibet, King **Srong-Tsan-Gampo** (who died in A.D. 650), Buddhism itself entered Tibet from two sources: from Nepal, the land of the Buddha's ancestors, through the Tibetan King's marriage with a daughter of the royal family of Nepal; and from China, through his marriage—in the year 641—with a princess of the Chinese Imperial Family. The King had been nurtured in the old **Bön** <term>the indigenous, shamanistic spiritual tradition of Tibet that existed before Buddhism</term> faith of Tibet, which, with its primitive doctrine of rebirth, was quite capable of serving as an approach to Buddhism; and under the influence of his two Buddhist wives he accepted Buddhism, making it the state religion; but it made little headway in Tibet until a century later, when his powerful successor, **Thi-Srong-Detsan**, held the throne from A.D. 740 to 786. <insert>(</insert>It was **Thī-Srong-Detsan** who invited **Padma Sambhava** (Tibetan: *Pēdma Jungnē*, i.e. 'The Lotus-Born'), better known to the Tibetans as *Guru Rin-po-ch'e* <term>literally "The Precious Guru" or "The Precious Master"</term>, to come to Tibet. The famous Guru was at that time a Professor of *Yoga* <note>here referring to the systematic practice of spiritual and physical discipline</note> in the great Buddhist University of **Nālanda**, India, and far-famed for expert knowledge of the Occult Sciences. He was a native of **Udyāna** or Swat, in what is now a part of Afghanistan.<insert>)</insert>

The Great Guru saw the wonderful opportunity which the King's invitation offered, and promptly accepted the call, passing through Nepal and arriving at **Samye** (*Sam-yas*), Tibet, in the year 747. It was to Samye that the King had invited him, in order to have exorcized the demons of the locality; for as soon as the walls of a monastery which the King was having erected there were raised they were over-thrown by local earthquakes, which the *demons* opposing Buddhism were believed to have caused. When the Great Guru had driven out the demons, all the local earthquakes ceased, much to the wonder of the people; and he himself supervised the completion of the royal monastery, and

<summary>This page outlines the historical introduction of Buddhism to Tibet, detailing the roles of King Srong-Tsan-Gampo and King Thi-Srong-Detsan in inviting the Indian master Padma Sambhava to establish the faith.</summary>

->ORIGIN OF THE *THE LIBERATION THROUGH HEARING IN THE INTERMEDIATE STATE* <note>original: *Bardo Thödol*</note> 75<-

established therein the first community of Tibetan Buddhist spiritual masters <note>original: *lāmas*</note>, in the year 749.

During his stay in Tibet at that time, and during subsequent visits, Padma Sambhava had many esoteric <term>Tantric</term> books translated into Tibetan out of ancient Indian <note>original: "Sanskrit"</note> originals—some of which have been preserved in the monasteries of Tibet—and hidden away with appropriate mystic ceremonies in various secret places. He also endowed certain of his disciples with the meditative <term>yogic</term> power of reincarnating at the proper time, as determined by the study of the stars <note>astrology</note>, in order to take them out, along with the treasures hidden away with them and the requisites needed for properly performing the rites described in the texts. This is the generally accepted tradition; but according to another tradition the **Treasure-revealers** <note>original: *Tertöns*</note> are to be regarded as various incarnations of the **Great Guru** <note>the "Great Master" Padma Sambhava</note> himself. According to a rough estimate, the religious texts already taken out by such **Treasure-revealers**, from century to century, would form an encyclopædia of about sixty-five volumes of woodblock prints, each, on an average, consisting of about four hundred ordinary-sized pages <note>folios</note>.

Our text, *The Liberation Through Hearing in the Intermediate State* <note>original: *Bardo Thödol*</note>, being one of these recovered "hidden" <term>apocryphal</term> books, should, therefore, be regarded as having been compiled (for the internal evidence suggests that it was a Tibetan compilation rather than a direct translation from some unknown Indian <note>Sanskrit</note> original) during the first centuries of **Tibetan Buddhism** <note>original: "Lāmaism"</note>, either—as it purports to have been—in the time of Padma Sambhava or soon afterwards. Its present general use all over Tibet as a funeral ritual and its acceptance by the different sects, in varying versions, could not have been the outcome of a few generations; it testifies rather convincingly to its antiquity, bears out the pre-Buddhistic and at least partially **Bön** <term>the indigenous, shamanistic spiritual tradition of Tibet</term> origin which we attribute to it, and suggests some validity in the claims made for the **Treasure-revealers**.

We are well aware of the adverse criticisms passed by European critics on the **Treasure-revealer** tradition. There is not lacking, nevertheless, sound reason for suspecting that the European critics are not altogether right. Therefore, it seems to us that the only sound attitude to assume towards the **Treasure-revealer** problem is to keep an open mind until sufficient data accumulate to

Though the claim of the **Treasure-revealer** <note>original: *Tertōn*</note> be proven false, the fact that the *Liberation Through Hearing in the Intermediate State* <note>original: *Bardo Thödol*</note> is now accepted as a sacred book in Tibet and has for some considerable time been used by the **spiritual masters** <note>original: *lāmas*</note> for reading over the dead would, of course, not be affected; only the theory concerning the textual compilation of what, in its essentials, is apparently a prehistoric ritual would be subject to revision.

As for Padma Sambhava's <note>The "Lotus-Born" master who brought Buddhism to Tibet</note> own sources, apart from such congenial traditional teachings as no doubt he incorporated in some of his Tibetan treatises, we are told, by oral tradition now current among the spiritual masters, that he had eight **preceptors** <note>original: *gurus*</note> in India, each representing one of the eight chief <term>Tantric</term> <note>Esoteric systems of practice involving ritual, meditation, and yoga</note> doctrines.

In a Tibetan block-print, which belonged to the translator, purporting to record the history—though much mixed with myth—of the **Great Master** <note>original: *Guru*</note>, entitled *The Abridged Testament made by the Lotus-Born of Orgyan* <note>original Tibetan: *Orgyan-Padmas-mzad-pahi-bkah-thang-bsdūd-pa*</note>, meaning 'The Abridged Testament made by the Lotus-Born of Orgyan' (or 'by the Lotus-Born of Orgyan'—Padma Sambhava), consisting of but seventeen folios, there is recorded on the twelfth folio, sixteenth section, the following passage, confirming the historical tradition touching the origin of the *Liberation Through Hearing in the Intermediate State* text:

> 'The Eight Incarnations of the Great **Enlightened Beings** <note>original: *Bodhisattvas*</note> are:
> '**Ugyan-ling-pa**, in the centre;
> '**Dorje-ling-pa**, in the east;

> 'These Eight Great **Treasure-revealers** <note>original: *Tertōns*</note> shall come;
> 'Mine own incarnations alone are they.'

<header>ORIGIN OF THE LIBERATION THROUGH HEARING IN THE INTERMEDIATE STATE <note>original: *Bardo Thödol*</note></header>

be his own incarnations. According to this account, the **Treasure-revealer** <note>original: *Tertön*; a person who discovers hidden spiritual "treasures" intended for a later time</note> of our book, the *Liberation Through Hearing in the Intermediate State* <note>original: *Bardo Thödol*</note>, is the fifth, named after the place called Karma Land, thus confirming the Block-Print of the *Liberation Through Hearing in the Intermediate State*; and Karma Land is in the northern quarter of Tibet. We have been unable to ascertain the exact time in which this **Treasure-revealer** lived, although he is a popular figure in the traditional history of Tibet. The name **Knowledge-Holder** <note>original: *Rigzin*</note>, given to him in the Block-Print first above quoted, meaning ‘Knowledge-Holder’, refers to his character as a religious devotee or **spiritual master** <note>original: *lāma*</note>; **The One from Karma Land** <note>original: *Karma ling-pa*</note>, as given in both accounts, refers also to an ancient Tibetan monastery of primitive **spiritual mastership** <note>original: Lāmaism</note> in the Kams Province, northern Tibet.

According to our view, the best attitude to take touching the uncertain history and origin of the *Liberation Through Hearing in the Intermediate State* is that of a critical truth-seeker who recognizes the anthropological significance of the passing of time, and of the almost inevitable reshaping of ancient teachings handed down at first orally and then, after having crystallized, being recorded in writing. As in the case of the Egyptian *Liberation Through Hearing in the Intermediate State*, popularly known as ‘The Egyptian Book of the Dead’, so in ‘The Tibetan Book of the Dead’, there is, no doubt, the record of the belief of innumerable generations in a state of existence after death. No one scribe could have been its author and no one generation its creator; its history as a book, if completely known, could only be the history of its compilation and recording; and the question, Whether this compilation and recording were done within comparatively recent times, or in the time of the **Lotus-Born Master** <note>original: *Padma Sambhava*; the 8th-century figure credited with bringing Buddhism to Tibet</note> or earlier? could not fundamentally affect the ancient teachings upon which it is based.

Although it is remarkably scientific in its essentials, there is no need to consider it as being accurate in all its details; for, undoubtedly, considerable corruption has crept into the text. In its broad outlines, however, it seems to convey a sublime truth, heretofore veiled to many students of religion, a philosophy as subtle as that of **Plato** <note>The Greek philosopher (c. 427–347 BC) who described the soul’s journey and reincarnation in his "Myth of Er"</note>, and a psychical science far in advance of that, still in its infancy, which forms the study of the **Society for Psychical Research** <note>A famous organization founded in London in 1882 to study paranormal and psychological phenomena scientifically</note>. And, as such, it deserves the serious attention of the Western World, now
