<meta>This page continues the transition from the previous discussion of neutral states of being into the specific emotional and psychological challenges of the Sidpa Bardo (the Intermediate State of Seeking Rebirth). It describes the moment of realization when the deceased recognizes they are truly dead and explains the restless, "wind-blown" nature of the consciousness during this phase.</meta>

# [PART I: THE BARDO OF SEEKING REBIRTH]
<page-num>164</page-num>

...your intellect has been separated from your physical body <note>original: "earth-plane body."</note>. <note>Like a traveler walking alone at night who notices prominent landmarks—large trees, houses, bridges, or temples—the deceased experiences the afterlife in a similar way. They are drawn to familiar places in the human world by their past habits and desires, known as **<term>karmic propensities</term>**. However, because they now possess a body made of mind and desire rather than flesh, they cannot stay in one place for long. As our text explains, they are driven about by the winds of their own desires, much like a feather caught in a storm.</note>

Because you are unable to stay still, you will often feel troubled, frustrated, and panicked. At times, your **<term>Knower</term>** <note>The conscious awareness or intellect that survives death.</note> will be clouded; at other times, it will be flighty and confused. Then, the thought will strike you: "Alas! I am dead! What shall I do?" Because of this realization, your **<term>Knower</term>** will become heavy with sadness and your heart will feel chilled; you will experience an endless, miserable sorrow. <note>It is important to remember that these terrifying experiences and feelings of unhappiness are entirely the result of one's own **<term>karma</term>** (the law of cause and effect). If the deceased had reached a high level of spiritual development, their experience in this intermediate state would be peaceful and happy from the start. This text, the **<term>Bardo Thödol</term>** <note>original: "Bardo Thödol"; literally, "Liberation Through Hearing in the Intermediate State," often called the Tibetan Book of the Dead.</note>, is primarily written for average people rather than highly realized masters who pass directly into spiritual Reality.</note> Since you cannot rest in any one place and feel forced to move on, do not let your mind wander through various thoughts. Instead, allow your intellect to rest in its own natural, unaltered state.

As for food, you can only consume what has been specifically dedicated to you; you cannot eat any other food. <note>Similar to the spirits mentioned in ancient Greek and Celtic folklore, those in the **<term>Bardo</term>** are said to live on subtle, "unseen" essences. They extract these either from food offerings made by the living or from the natural world. Other texts, such as the **<term>Six Doctrines</term>** <note>A set of advanced tantric practices also known as the Six Dharmas of Naropa.</note>, explain that these beings "live on odors"—extracting the spiritual essence of physical things.</note> As for friends at this time, there is no guarantee you will find any. <note>While friends and companions may exist in this intermediate state just as they do on earth, they are unable to change the deceased person's **<term>karma</term>**. Every individual must follow the path created by their own past actions.</note>

These are the signs that your **<term>mental body</term>** is wandering through the **<term>Sidpa Bardo</term>**. During this time, your happiness or misery will depend entirely on your **<term>karma</term>**.

You will see your own home, your attendants, your relatives, and even your own corpse. You will think, "Now I am dead! What shall I do?" Overwhelmed by deep sorrow, the thought will...

<meta>This page continues the guidance for the deceased soul during the Sidpa Bardo, the "Intermediate State of Seeking Rebirth." <header>PART I] THE DESIRE FOR A BODY</header>

It will occur to you, "Oh, what would I not give to possess a body!" And with this thought, you will wander here and there seeking a physical form.

Even if you could enter your old dead body nine times over, too much time has passed while you were in the **<term>Intermediate State of Reality</term>** <note>original: "Chönyid Bardo." The stage of the afterlife where the true nature of reality appears in the form of peaceful and wrathful deities.</note>. If it is winter, the body will have frozen; if it is summer, it will have rotted. Otherwise, your relatives will have cremated it, buried it in the earth, cast it into the water, or given it to the birds and beasts of prey. <note>In Tibet, all these methods of disposing of a corpse are practiced, including mummification for high-ranking individuals.</note> Because you will find no place to enter, you will feel dissatisfied and have the sensation of being squeezed into narrow cracks and crevices amidst rocks and boulders. <note>This sensation symbolizes the soul being drawn into a womb, particularly those that lead to unfortunate or "animal-like" human rebirths.</note> This kind of misery occurs in the **<term>Intermediate State</term>** <note>original: "Bardo."</note> when one is seeking rebirth. Even though you seek a body, you will find nothing but trouble. Put aside this desire for a physical form; let your mind rest in a state of calm resignation, and strive to remain in that peace.

By being shown the truth in this way, a person can find liberation and escape the *Intermediate State*.

### [THE JUDGEMENT]

**[Instructions to the Priest or Reader]:** Even now, it is possible that because of the influence of negative **<term>karma</term>** <note>The accumulation of past actions and intentions that shapes one's future.</note>, the deceased will not recognize the truth. Therefore, call them by name and speak these words:

> O nobly-born [Name], listen. Your suffering comes from your own **<term>karma</term>**; it is not caused by anyone else. It is the result of your own past actions. Therefore, pray earnestly to the **<term>Three Jewels</term>** <note>original: "Precious Trinity." This refers to the Buddha, the Teachings (Dharma), and the Spiritual Community (Sangha).</note>; they will protect you. If you do not know how to pray or how to meditate upon the **<term>Great Seal</term>** <note>original: "Great Symbol" or "Mahamudra." A profound system of meditation on the nature of the mind.</note> or upon a personal guardian deity, then the **<term>Good Genius</term>** <note>original: "Lhan-chig-skyes-pahi-lha." Literally the "simultaneously-born god," this is a personification of a human's higher or divine nature. In local Himalayan traditions, it is sometimes called the "Little White God."</note>, who...

<header>BARDO OF SEEKING REBIRTH [BOOK II</header>

who was born simultaneously with you, will come now and count out your good deeds with white pebbles, and the **Evil Genius**,¹ who was also born simultaneously with you, will come and count out your evil deeds with black pebbles. Thereupon, you will be greatly frightened, awed, and terrified, and will tremble; and you will attempt to tell lies, saying, ‘I have not committed any evil deed’.

Then the **<term>Lord of Death</term>** <note>original: Yama-Rāja. The king of the underworld and judge of the dead in Buddhist and Hindu tradition.</note> will say, ‘I will consult the Mirror of **<term>Karma</term>** <note>The law of cause and effect, where one's past actions determine future circumstances.</note>’.

So saying, he will look in the Mirror, wherein every good and evil act is vividly reflected. Lying will be of no avail.

Then [one of the fierce attendants of] the Lord of Death will place round your neck a rope and drag you along; he will cut off your head, extract your heart, pull out your intestines, lick up your brain, drink your blood, eat your flesh, and gnaw your bones;² but you will be incapable of dying. Although your body be hacked to pieces, it will revive again. The repeated hacking will cause intense pain and torture.

Even at the time that the pebbles are being counted out, be not frightened, nor terrified; tell no lies; and fear not the Lord of Death.

Because your body is a mental body, it is incapable of dying even though beheaded and quartered. In reality, your body is of the nature of **<term>voidness</term>** <note>original: shunyata. The philosophical concept that all things are empty of a permanent, independent self; here used to reassure the deceased that their "body" is an illusion.</note>;³ you need not be afraid. The

---

<note>¹ Original text: *Lhan-chig-skyes-pahi-hdre*. This means the "simultaneously-born demon" or evil spirit. It is the personification of a human being’s lower or carnal nature. In the local language of Sikkim, it is known as the "Little Black Demon" (*Bdud-nag-chung*), often associated with the tempter-figure Mara.</note>

<note>² These tortures symbolize the pangs of the deceased’s conscience. The Judgment described here is a symbolic representation of one's own conscience acting as an impartial judge. The Mirror represents memory. According to the teachers (lāmas), different elements of the deceased's own consciousness act out the roles: one part tries to make excuses for its actions, another identifies the selfish motives behind those actions (the "black" deeds), while a friendlier element seeks justification and pardon. The entire scene is a projection of the mind's own internal moral struggle.</note>

<note>³ This means that the "astral" or desire-body is incapable of suffering physical death in the way a biological body does.</note>

<summary>This page describes the Judgment of the Dead, where the deceased's good and evil deeds are tallied with white and black pebbles before the Mirror of Karma. It explains that the terrifying tortures that follow are symbolic projections of the conscience, emphasizing that the soul's "mental body" cannot actually be destroyed.</summary>

<keywords>Judgment, Lord of Death, Mirror of Karma, Good Genius, Evil Genius, Conscience, Mental Body, Voidness, Karma</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the instructions for the deceased during the Sidpa Bardo (the Intermediate State of Seeking Rebirth). After the soul has failed to find a physical body to re-enter, it now faces the psychological and spiritual trial of judgment.</meta>

<page-type>illustration</page-type>

## THE JUDGEMENT

->*Described on pages xxx-xxxiii, 35-8, 165-7, 240*<-

<note>The following describes a traditional thangka painting depicting the Buddhist afterlife. In the center sits Yama, the Lord of Death, a terrifying figure who acts as a mirror to one's own conscience. Above him are the five enlightened Buddhas, representing the potential for liberation that exists even in the darkest moments. Below, the scene shows the "Mirror of Karma" and the weighing of souls, followed by the various symbolic punishments of the hell realms—depictions of the mental suffering caused by one's own past negative actions.</note>

<page-num>166</page-num>
<header>BARDO OF SEEKING REBIRTH [BOOK II]</header>

The **Good Genius**,¹ <note>original: Lhan-chig skyes-pai lha. A "born-with-one" deity or innate guardian spirit that records one's virtuous acts.</note> who was born simultaneously with you, will come now and count out your good deeds with white pebbles, and the **Evil Genius**,¹ <note>original: Lhan-chig skyes-pai 'dre. An innate demon or shadow-self that records one's harmful acts.</note> who was also born simultaneously with you, will come and count out your evil deeds with black pebbles. Thereupon, you will be greatly frightened, awed, and terrified, and will tremble; and you will attempt to tell lies, saying, ‘I have not committed any evil deed’.

Then the **<term>Lord of Death</term>** <note>original: Yama-Rāja. The king of the underworld and judge of the dead in Buddhist and Hindu tradition.</note> will say, ‘I will consult the Mirror of **<term>Karma</term>** <note>The law of cause and effect, where one's past actions determine future circumstances.</note>’.

So saying, he will look in the Mirror, wherein every good and evil act is vividly reflected. Lying will be of no avail.

Then [one of the fierce attendants of] the Lord of Death will place round your neck a rope and drag you along; he will cut off your head, extract your heart, pull out your intestines, lick up your brain, drink your blood, eat your flesh, and gnaw your bones;² but you will be incapable of dying. Although your body be hacked to pieces, it will revive again. The repeated hacking will cause intense pain and torture.

Even at the time that the pebbles are being counted out, be not frightened, nor terrified; tell no lies; and fear not the Lord of Death.

Because your body is a mental body, it is incapable of dying even though beheaded and quartered. In reality, your body is of the nature of **<term>voidness</term>** <note>original: shunyata. The philosophical concept that all things are empty of a permanent, independent existence; they are essentially projections of the mind.</note>. Therefore, you need not fear. The Lords of Death are your own hallucinations. Your body of propensities is a body of <unclear>habitual</unclear> tendencies, and it is void. Voidness cannot injure voidness; the qualityless cannot injure the qualityless.

<summary>This page describes the terrifying "Judgment of the Dead," where the deceased's actions are weighed using white and black pebbles and reflected in a cosmic mirror. It emphasizes that these experiences, though painful, are actually mental projections and that the soul's "mental body" cannot be truly harmed because its true nature is void.</summary>

<keywords>Yama, Mirror of Karma, Good and Evil Geniuses, Bardo, judgment, mental body, voidness, shunyata, afterlife trials</keywords>

### THE MANTRA OF THE LORD OF COMPASSION <note>original: Chenrazee. Also known by the Sanskrit name Avalokiteshvara, he is the patron deity of Tibet and the embodiment of universal mercy.</note>

## THE JUDGMENT

->*Described on pages 30-33, 35-38, 165-167, 240*<-

<note>The following describes a traditional thangka painting depicting the Buddhist afterlife. In the center sits Yama, the Lord of Death, a terrifying figure who acts as a mirror to one's own conscience. Above him are the five enlightened Buddhas, representing the potential for liberation that exists even in the darkest moments. Below, the scene shows the "Mirror of Karma" and the weighing of souls, followed by the various symbolic punishments of the hell realms—depictions of the mental suffering caused by one's own past negative actions.</note>

<page-num>166</page-num>
<header>BARDO OF SEEKING REBIRTH [BOOK II]</header>

The **Good Guardian**,¹ <note>original: Lhan-chig skyes-pai lha. Literally the "innate deity" or a guardian spirit born alongside the individual to record their virtuous acts.</note> who was born at the same moment as you, will come now and count out your good deeds with white pebbles. Meanwhile, the **Evil Spirit**,¹ <note>original: Lhan-chig skyes-pai 'dre. An innate demon or shadow-self that records one's harmful or selfish acts.</note> who was also born at the same moment as you, will come and count out your evil deeds with black pebbles. At this sight, you will be seized by great fear, awe, and terror; you will tremble and attempt to lie, saying, ‘I have not committed any evil deed.’

Then the **<term>Lord of Death</term>** <note>original: Yama-Rāja. The king of the underworld and judge of the dead in Buddhist and Hindu tradition, often interpreted as a projection of one's own mind.</note> will say, ‘I will consult the Mirror of **<term>Karma</term>** <note>The law of cause and effect, where every intentional action of body, speech, or mind leads to a corresponding result.</note>.’

As he speaks, he looks into the mirror where every past action is reflected clearly...

<summary>This page illustrates the sacred mantra of Avalokiteshvara and begins the dramatic narrative of the Judgment, where a deceased person's deeds are weighed by innate spirits using white and black pebbles.</summary>
